**Why this matters:** React is the single most-asked framework in frontend and full-stack loops, and the difference between a mid and a senior candidate is almost entirely about *mechanism*. Mid-level candidates memorize hook lists. Senior candidates can explain why a component re-rendered, why an effect looped, why a memo didn't help, and where the boundary between "React's job" and "your job" sits.

**The one-sentence mental model:** React is a **state-driven UI description**. You declare what the UI should look like for a given state; React computes the cheapest path to make the DOM match, and it may do that work at a lower priority or in chunks so the browser stays responsive.

**Resources for the whole phase:** react.dev (the rewritten docs — treat as primary), the legacy reactjs.org docs for architecture internals, the React source on GitHub (`packages/react-reconciler`), Kent C. Dodds' *Testing Library* philosophy articles, "A (Mostly) Complete Guide to React Rendering Behavior" by Mark Ryan, overreacted.io by Dan Abramov, and the React 19 release notes / RFC repository.

# 4.1.1 The rendering model: elements, phases, reconciliation, and Fiber
🟡 Intermediate → 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 3.3 (JS Core)

**Definition:** *Rendering* in React means calling your component functions to produce a tree of React elements, comparing that tree with the previous one, and committing the minimal set of DOM mutations. *Reconciliation* is the algorithm for that comparison. *Fiber* is the data structure and scheduling engine that make it possible to interrupt, resume, and prioritize that work.

**Step 1: elements, not DOM.** A React element is a plain immutable object describing "what should exist", not a live instance:

```jsx
const el = <button className="primary" onClick={save}>Save</button>;
// roughly:
{
  type: 'button',
  key: null,
  ref: null,
  props: { className: 'primary', onClick: save, children: 'Save' },
  $$typeof: Symbol.for('react.element')
}
```

Elements are cheap, comparable, and serializable-ish. That is why React can diff them, why you can return arrays, and why `===` on elements is meaningful (each JSX call creates a **new** object, so elements are never reference-equal).

**Step 2: the component types.**
- **Function components** are called and return elements. They may also be `async` in a Server Component context only.
- **Class components** are instantiated (`new`) and have lifecycle methods; React still re-renders them with `this.setState`.
- **Host components** are lowercase strings and map to DOM/host elements.
- **`React.Fragment`/`<>`** groups children without adding a node.
- **`memo(Component)`** returns a special element type; React skips re-invoking it when props are shallow-equal.
- **`forwardRef(fn)`** wraps a function component so it receives `ref` as a third argument.
- **Context providers/consumers** are also element types and participate in the tree.

**Step 3: the two-phase commit.** React always separates *what* from *how*:

```
      render phase (may be interrupted, restarted, or thrown away)
      ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
setState│ 1. begin work on root  →  schedule callback            │
 /props│ 2. walk down: render components, build the work-in-    │
 change│    progress tree; call component fns; diff children;    │
       │    call hooks in order; collect effects                 │
       └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                              │
                              ▼
      commit phase (synchronous, not interruptible)
      ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
       │ 3a. before-mutation lifecycle (getSnapshotBeforeUpdate)│
       │ 3b. DOM mutations (insert/update/remove nodes, refs)   │
       │ 3c. layout effects (useLayoutEffect) — sync, before    │
       │     paint; can read layout, blocks paint               │
       │ 3d. paint (browser renders)                             │
       │ 3e. passive effects (useEffect) — async, usually after │
       │     paint; can be interrupted                          │
       └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

Consequences you must be able to state:
1. **Render must be pure.** Since the render phase can run twice (StrictMode, or after an interruption in concurrent mode), side effects in render will happen twice. Never mutate external state, never `fetch`, never write to a ref that render reads.
2. **Render can be aborted.** A transition (4.1.12) may start, get interrupted by a higher-priority update, and re-render. So the same component function can run with the same props twice and produce different results *only* if it read something impure like `Date.now()` or `Math.random()`.
3. **Effects are not part of rendering.** They run after commit; if the render is thrown away, the effect never runs.
4. **All mutations happen in one synchronous commit**, so the DOM is never half-updated from a single render.

**Step 4: reconciliation rules.** The algorithm is simple in description and heavily optimized in implementation:

1. If the element type changed → **unmount** the old subtree, mount the new one (no reuse of children).
2. If the type is the same:
   - Host component: recurse over children and diff attributes, updating only what changed.
   - Function/class component: React re-invokes the function (unless `memo` + equal props can bail out), then recurses into the resulting children.
3. Keys are used to match siblings at the same level. If keys match, React reorders the *existing* fiber nodes; new keys mount, missing keys unmount.
4. Old props are retained so effects can diff them, and so class lifecycles and `getDerivedStateFromProps` can run.

**Reconciliation is O(n) in the number of nodes, not O(n·m)**, but it is *heuristic*: it assumes the tree shape and element types match by position. That heuristic is why keys exist and why a conditional before a list (`{cond && <div/>}`) is dangerous — it shifts positions, so React will reuse the wrong fibers.

**Step 5: Fiber.** Before Fiber (React ≤15, Stack Reconciler), the work was a recursive synchronous call stack: once you started reconciling you could not stop. Fiber converted that into:

- **Fiber node** = a unit of work object holding `{tag, type, key, stateNode, memoizedProps, memoizedState, child, sibling, return, updateQueue, flags}`. Fibers form a **doubly-linked tree** (`return` points to the parent fiber, `child`/`sibling` to children).
- **Work loop:** React maintains a stack/queue of fibers with work and processes them one at a time, yielding between fibers (`shouldYield()` checks frame time). That is what makes interruption possible: it can stop anywhere between fibers.
- **Two fiber trees:** a `current` tree (committed, on screen) and a `workInProgress` tree (being built). Interruptions just stop mutating the WIP tree and throw it away; the current tree is untouched. This is the heart of "concurrent" React.
- **Scheduling:** lanes and priority levels. Updates get a lane; the scheduler picks the highest-priority non-empty lane set. Input events, transitions, suspense retries, and offscreen work all have their own lanes. This is why a transition can't block a keystroke.
- **Double buffering** means React doesn't need to copy the tree to try something.

Practical takeaways for an interview:
- "The virtual DOM" is a *description of the desired output* that enables diffing. It's not a copy of the DOM, and it's not the reason React is fast — the reason is **batching updates into one commit, avoiding layout thrash, and skipping DOM writes for unchanged nodes**.
- React is not necessarily faster than direct DOM for small, targeted updates (`element.textContent = x` beats a React render for a text node). React wins when updates are frequent, spread across a declarative tree, or when the alternative is error-prone manual bookkeeping.
- A render is **pure computation + reconciliation**, not DOM mutation. The DOM mutation happens in commit. That's why you can render on the server at all.

**Questions**
1. What is the virtual DOM, and is it the reason React is fast?
2. Describe the render and commit phases. What can happen in each?
3. Can rendering be interrupted? Can it be discarded? Why does that matter for purity?
4. What are the reconciliation rules when types differ?
5. What is a Fiber, and what does it buy us?
6. Why can a render run twice in development but not production?
7. What's the difference between "re-render" and "re-mount"? Which is more expensive and which bugs are more subtle?
8. Where do refs get attached, and why does that make `useRef` in an effect always non-null?

**Model answer, Q1:** "The virtual DOM is an in-memory description of what the UI should be, as plain objects. It lets React compute a diff against the previous description and apply only the real DOM mutations needed. It's not the reason React is fast — React is fast because it batches multiple state updates into one commit, avoids interleaved read/write layout thrash, and skips DOM writes entirely for unchanged subtrees. For a single text-node update, direct DOM manipulation is faster than a React render."

**Model answer, Q7:** "Re-render means React calls the component function again and diffs the returned elements; state and DOM nodes are preserved. A re-mount means React throws away the old fiber and its state and creates new instances, which loses state, refires effects, and is far more expensive. Remount bugs are nastier because they usually come from unstable `key`s, changing element types in a position (a `<div>` becoming a `<span>`), or conditional rendering, and they show up as 'my form state resets' or 'this effect runs way too often' rather than as an obvious error."

**Mistakes:** treating effects as lifecycle hooks; doing data fetching and derived-state work in effects; assuming `useMemo` makes things fast without measuring; putting a `console.log` in render and trusting its count.

**Mini task:** Build a 10,000-row list and profile it: (a) with plain `setState` on every keystroke, (b) with a debounced filter, (c) with a manual `useMemo` filter, (d) virtualized with a fixed row height. Record commit counts and long-task durations using the React DevTools profiler and `performance.measure`.

# 4.1.2 Keys and identity: why index keys break
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.1.1

**What a key is:** a string identity assigned to an element inside an array (or, in practice, to a sibling in a list) that lets React match a new element to a *previous* element. Keys are stored on the fiber and are part of the matching algorithm.

**The rule:** keys must be **stable across renders** (derived from data), **unique among siblings**, and **consistent in type** (`number 1` and `string "1"` are different keys).

**Why index keys break.** React assumes list structure is mostly positional, so it falls back on the index. When you reorder, filter, or insert, positions change but data doesn't, and React happily reuses the wrong fibers:

```jsx
// BROKEN: keys are positions
const rows = items.map((item, i) => <Row key={i} text={item.text} />);

// Reorder "a, b, c" → "c, a, b":
// key 0 previously held "a" (uncontrolled <input> with a draft value),
// now holds "c". React reuses that fiber, so the DOM node and its
// *uncontrolled* state (the typed text, focus, selection) persist.
```

Symptoms of index-key bugs:
- Input values "follow the row" instead of the item.
- Animations and CSS transitions apply to the wrong nodes.
- Checked/focused state sticks to a position.
- With `AnimatePresence`-style libraries, elements exit with the wrong content.

**The subtler case: item IDs that aren't stable.** Using `Math.random()` or `Date.now()` as a key is worse than an index, because every render looks like a brand-new list: everything unmounts and remounts, state is destroyed, effects refire, and animations restart. `crypto.randomUUID()` in a `useState` initializer is fine because it's created once per item instance.

```jsx
// GOOD: stable data identity
const rows = items.map((item) => <Row key={item.id} text={item.text} />);
```

**Keys and conditional rendering.**

```jsx
// Fragile: inserting/removing the banner shifts positions
<div>
  {showBanner && <Banner />}
  {list.map((i) => <Row key={i.id} />)}
</div>
```

This specific case is usually fine for the *list* because the list's own keys are stable; the danger is when conditionally rendered siblings have *no* keys or index keys and the condition toggles, e.g. `items.map((it, i) => cond ? <A key={i}/> : <B key={i}/>)`.

**Keys in lists that reorder often: alternatives**
- Sort/compare by a stable identity column.
- Use a `Map` keyed by id and pass the item in as a prop instead of relying on position.
- If you must keep order stable and content changes, use a key composed of stable identity plus version only when you genuinely want a remount: `key={`${item.id}:${item.version}`}` — but be aware that's a deliberate remount hammer.

**Keys across different parents don't need to be unique** — only siblings. Two different lists can both use `key="1"`.

**`key` is not `id` and not `ref`.** Common confusions:
- `key` participates in reconciliation; `id` is just an HTML attribute for the DOM/links/accessibility.
- Passing `key` as a prop doesn't work (React strips it) — if a child needs it, pass it under another name.
- `key` does not stop an effect from refiring when the *component* remounts for another reason.

**Questions**
1. What is a key for, and what breaks without one?
2. Why is `key={index}` worse than no key at all in a reorderable list?
3. Why is `key={Math.random()}` catastrophic?
4. Do keys need to be globally unique?
5. How do you force a component to remount on purpose?
6. A child component's input loses its text when the parent reorders a list. Diagnose it.

**Model answer, Q6:** "The parent is likely using index keys, so after the reorder React matched the fiber at position 0 — which used to be item A — to item C. Because inputs are uncontrolled, their DOM value (and focus) lives on that reused node, so the text sticks to the position instead of the item. Fix: key by a stable `item.id`. Then check whether the id is truly stable (don't derive it from the array index anywhere upstream)."

**Mistakes:** index keys in sortable/filterable lists; random keys; reaching for `key={Math.random()}` to force a remount instead of restructuring; copying keys from the array index because "the order never changes" and then adding sorting.

**Mini task:** Write a sortable, filterable, addable/deletable list twice — once with index keys, once with id keys — and add two uncontrolled inputs per row. Show the bug, then fix it. Then explain which state was destroyed by the remount and how you would preserve it.

# 4.1.3 What actually causes a re-render
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** (the single most-asked React question)

**Definition:** A re-render is React calling your component function again and diffing the new output. It happens when React has new work for a fiber: a state update on that fiber, a props change from the parent, or a context read that changed.

**The complete list of triggers:**
1. `setState` (or a dispatch) in this component.
2. A parent re-renders **and** creates new props for this element. (Note: a parent re-render does *not* by itself force children to re-render if they are `memo`'d and props are shallow-equal. But an unmemoized child *always* re-renders when its parent re-renders, even if props are identical objects.)
3. `useContext` returning a new value (see 4.1.14) — this propagates to all consumers below the provider, bypassing `memo`.
4. A store subscription in the component (Zustand, Redux `useSelector`, Jotai) emits a value that changed for that slice.
5. The root re-renders (new element from `root.render`, or a parent of the tree).
6. `forceUpdate` (class) / `useReducer` dispatch.
7. `useSyncExternalStore` detects a store change.
8. HMR in development.

**What does *not* cause a re-render:**
- Mutating an object or array in place (`items.push(x)` then `setItems(items)`): the identity is unchanged, so `Object.is` says "equal" and React bails out. This is why "mutate then set" bugs feel random — other triggers (parent render) later expose the change, so it looks non-deterministic.
- Re-rendering because of an `useMemo` recompute — a memo recompute causes no re-render; it's just a cache miss.
- An effect running. Effects *follow* renders.
- Changing a ref. Refs don't drive renders.

**Bailout mechanics.** When you call `setState`, React doesn't re-render immediately. It enqueues an update on the fiber's `updateQueue` and schedules work. During the next render it processes the queue, computes the next state, and compares with `Object.is`:
- Equal → React can **bail out**, and may skip re-rendering that subtree entirely (it can even skip the component and its children if no children have pending work). Note React may still re-render the component *once* ("React may still need to render that specific component before bailing out").
- Different → continue, re-render children.

```jsx
// Bails out: same value
const [n, setN] = useState(0);
setN(0);   // React bails out; component function may not run

// Bails out if the array identity is unchanged (the classic bug)
const [rows, setRows] = useState([]);
setRows([...rows, newRow]);          // OK, new identity → renders
setRows(rows); rows.push(x);         // same identity → no render

// Updater functions are safer because they avoid stale reads
setRows(prev => [...prev, newRow]);
```

**Children re-render even when props are identical.** This is the #1 source of "why does everything re-render":

```jsx
function Parent() {
  const [n, setN] = useState(0);
  const style = { color: 'red' };          // new object every render
  return (
    <>
      <button onClick={() => setN(n + 1)}>{n}</button>
      <Memoized style={style} />           // memo compares props shallowly → NOT equal
      <Plain style={style} />               // always re-renders
    </>
  );
}
const Memoized = React.memo(function Memoized({ style }) { return <div style={style} />; });
```

**"Re-render" is cheap; "re-render of an expensive subtree" is not.** React's own cost is roughly proportional to nodes diffed. The cost that matters is what *your* components do during render: computation, `JSON.parse`, creating large objects, and especially *children* that are expensive because they aren't memoized.

**Diagnosing re-renders.**
- React DevTools Profiler → "Record why each component rendered" (enable the setting), which shows the prop/state/context diff that triggered it.
- Add a `console.count` or a render-phase log; but prefer the Profiler because logs lie under StrictMode double-render.
- Bisect: comment out `<Profiler>`-wrapped subtrees and see where the cost is.

**Questions**
1. List every thing that causes a re-render.
2. My `setState` with the same value still logs a render. Why?
3. Why do memoized children re-render when the parent re-renders?
4. Mutating an array and calling `setState` doesn't update the UI. Explain.
5. Does a context consumer re-render if the provider value is `Object.is`-equal but a new object?
6. How do you find why a component re-renders, precisely?
7. What's the cost of a re-render, really?

**Model answer, Q2:** "React enqueues the update and compares the next state with the current state using `Object.is`. If they're equal it bails out of re-rendering that subtree. But the spec allows React to render the component one more time before bailing out — it 'may still need to render that specific component again before bailing out' — so you can see one extra render. Also, in StrictMode dev, renders are intentionally double-invoked. So one extra render log is normal; a loop is not."

**Model answer, Q7:** "Re-running a component function is cheap: it's a plain function call plus a diff of the returned elements. What costs is the subtree below it and whatever you compute during render. So the optimization question isn't 'how do I stop this render' but 'is this subtree expensive, and if so is it expensive because of an O(n) computation, an unmemoized child, or a large DOM update'. Measure with the Profiler, then decide."

**Mistakes:** assuming `memo` stops the parent from re-rendering; assuming one log line equals one render; using an object/array literal as a prop or in a context value and then wondering why `memo` doesn't help; treating context reads as scoped.

**Mini task:** Take any 200-line component and count renders for every component on: initial mount, a state update, a context value change, a parent-driven prop change with identical values, and a store update. Write down which triggers which, and confirm with React DevTools' "why did this render".

# 4.1.4 `useState`: queue, updater, bailout, equality, batching
🟡 Intermediate → 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition:** `useState` creates a single state slot whose value persists across renders and is private to the component (or shared only via props/context).

**How it works (mechanism).** On mount, React creates the hook record in the fiber's `memoizedState` linked list, with the initial value (evaluated eagerly — see the laziness note below). On update, `setState`:
1. Creates an update object `{lane, payload}` and appends it to `fiber.updateQueue` (a circular list with `next`/`circle` pointers).
2. Schedules work on the fiber.
3. During the next render, React processes the queue from the position it last stopped at (`baseQueue` for skipped updates), computing `newState = reducer(prevState, action)`.
4. Compares `newState` with `memoizedState` using `Object.is`. Equal → bailout. Different → new `memoizedState`, mark children for update, re-render.

**Key rules that follow from that mechanism:**

1. **State is a snapshot.** The variable in your closure is the value from *that* render. `setN(n + 1)` three times in one handler sets the same value three times.
   ```jsx
   setN(n + 1); setN(n + 1); setN(n + 1);   // n + 1, not n + 3
   setN(p => p + 1); setN(p => p + 1); setN(p => p + 1);  // n + 3
   ```
2. **Functional updates are queued and applied in order.** Use them whenever the next value depends on the previous one, especially inside async callbacks, intervals, and event handlers that may batch.
3. **Batching.** React 18+ auto-batches updates from promises, timeouts, and native event handlers, not just React events. Updates in the same tick produce one render.
   ```jsx
   // React 17 (legacy root): only React events batch
   // React 18+ createRoot: everything batches
   ```
   `flushSync` opts *out* of batching (rarely needed; sometimes needed for third-party integration and for measuring layout synchronously).
4. **Lazy initial state.** `useState(() => expensive())` runs the initializer exactly once. `useState(expensive())` runs it on *every* render and throws the result away.
5. **Equality is `Object.is`**, not shallow equality. `NaN` equals `NaN` under `Object.is` (unlike `===`); `+0` and `-0` differ. There is no built-in deep comparison, and you should not want one — see 4.1.7.
6. **State must be immutable.** Mutating nested objects in place is the same bug as mutating arrays: the identity of the state object is unchanged, so `Object.is` bails out and the UI never updates.
   ```jsx
   // Broken
   const [state, setState] = useState({ user: { name: 'a' }, tags: [] });
   state.tags.push('x'); setState(state);          // no render
   // Fixed
   setState(s => ({ ...s, tags: [...s.tags, 'x'] }));
   // Structural sharing matters: only `tags` is new, `user` is the same reference
   ```
7. **Setting state on an unmounted component.** React 18 removed the warning; the update is simply ignored. It no longer warns, but you can still leak if the async work itself holds resources — use `AbortController` in 4.1.5.
8. **Multiple state hooks vs one object.** Multiple `useState` calls let React skip the unaffected slots during the render-phase queue processing (a bailout). One big object means any change re-renders on all of it and you lose referential equality benefits. Prefer multiple hooks, or a reducer when the states are interdependent.
9. **Don't mirror props into state.** `const [name, setName] = useState(props.name)` creates a second source of truth that drifts. Either use the prop directly (with a `key` to reset) or lift the state (4.1.7).

**Questions**
1. Why does `setN(n + 1)` three times only increment once?
2. When does the initializer of `useState` run?
3. What is `Object.is` and why does React use it? Why no deep compare?
4. How does batching work in React 18 vs 17, and how do you opt out?
5. Why can't you mutate state? What exactly goes wrong?
6. When would you use `useReducer` instead of `useState`?
7. What happens if you `setState` after unmount?
8. Multiple `useState` in a component — how does React know which one to update?

**Model answer, Q1:** "Because state is a snapshot, `n` is the value from this render and doesn't change until the next render. All three calls enqueue the same computed value, `n + 1`. React coalesces them into one render. If I need the accumulated result, I pass an updater function, and React applies each updater to the latest state in the queue in order, giving `n + 3`."

**Model answer, Q5:** "Two failures. First, `Object.is(next, current)` is true when you mutate in place, so React bails out and never re-renders. Second, even if a render happens for another reason, the previous element tree references the *same* objects, so the diff sees no change and the DOM isn't updated. It works until the day it doesn't, which is worse. Immutability also gives you structural sharing for free, cheap change detection via `===`, and undo/redo."

**Mistakes:** `useState(new Date())` (runs every render); `useEffect(() => setX(props.x), [props.x])` loops; treating state as mutable; assuming `setState` returns the new value synchronously; mixing `n` and `p => p` styles in one place.

**Mini task:** Implement a `useReducer`-free counter hook with: `increment()`, `incrementBy(n)`, `reset()`, and history via `undo`. Write it twice — once naively with object state, once with independent slots — and compare re-render behavior with the Profiler.

---

# 4.1.5 `useEffect`: mental model, dependencies, cleanup, when *not* to use it
🟡 Intermediate → 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition:** `useEffect(effect, deps)` schedules a *side effect* after React commits: it runs after the DOM is updated, and React runs cleanup before re-running it and again on unmount.

**The mental model that makes effects click.** An effect is not "do this when the component mounts". It is:

> **"Keep this external system in sync with my state."**

The external system is anything outside React: a subscription (WebSocket, store, media query), a timer, a network connection, an imperative third-party library, the DOM (`title`, `scrollTop`, canvas). If the "external system" is just React state, you probably don't need an effect (4.1.7).

**Timing, precisely:**
```
commit → DOM mutations → useLayoutEffect (sync, before paint)
      → paint
      → useEffect (usually async, after paint; may be deferred if the browser is busy)
      → cleanup of the *previous* effect runs before the next effect, immediately before it
      → cleanup runs on unmount
```
React flushes pending passive effects before starting a new render if the update would otherwise be surprising, and always before `flushSync`. Also, effects queued during an interrupted render are skipped entirely.

**Cleanup functions and their three jobs:**
1. **Undo** the effect: unsubscribe, `clearInterval`, `close()`, `abort()`.
2. **Reset state** before the next run so you don't get a race (see stale responses below).
3. **Guard against races** with a `cancelled`/`ignore` flag or `AbortController`.

```jsx
useEffect(() => {
  const controller = new AbortController();
  const id = setInterval(tick, 1000);
  window.addEventListener('resize', onResize);
  fetch(`/api/items`, { signal: controller.signal })
    .then(r => r.json())
    .then(data => { if (!controller.signal.aborted) setItems(data); })
    .catch(err => { if (err.name !== 'AbortError') throw err; });
  return () => {
    controller.abort();
    clearInterval(id);
    window.removeEventListener('resize', onResize);
  };
}, [query]);   // a new query aborts the old request: no stale-response race
```

**The dependency array.**
- Omitted → runs after **every** render.
- `[]` → runs after the first render only, cleanup on unmount. "Mount/unmount", not "first time the value is truthy".
- `[a, b]` → runs when the identity of `a` or `b` changes (`Object.is` per slot) *compared to the last committed render*, and cleanup runs before re-running.

Dependency pitfalls:
- **Missing deps** → stale closure (4.1.6).
- **Extra deps** → effect churn: a new object/array/function identity every render re-runs the effect every render (a subscription leak, a re-animation, an infinite loop).
- **Derive during render, not in the effect.** If `fullName` is just `first + last`, compute it in render.
- **Lint:** `eslint-plugin-react-hooks` enforces exhaustive-deps. Turning it off should be a deliberate, commented decision (a legit use: fire-and-forget analytics where a stale value is harmless, or a ref-based "latest value" pattern).
- **Object dependencies:** either list the fields you use (`[user.id, user.name]`) or store the object in a ref and depend on `user.id`.

**The infinite loop recipe.** Almost always one of these:

```jsx
// 1. Setting state unconditionally
useEffect(() => { setCount(count + 1); }, []);            // deps missing
// 2. Object/array/function identity changes every render
useEffect(() => { setData({ ...data, ts: Date.now() }); }, [data]);
// 3. Two effects synchronizing each other
useEffect(() => setA(b), [b]);  useEffect(() => setB(a), [a]);
// 4. Custom hook with an unstable internal dep
useEffect(() => { doThing(); }, [options]);   // options = {} literal every render
```

Fix by (a) making the computation pure and running it during render, (b) stabilizing the dependency with `useMemo`/`useCallback` or by depending on primitives, (c) moving the sync into an *event handler*, (d) using a functional update so the effect doesn't depend on the value.

**Three questions to ask before writing an effect:**
1. Can this be computed during render from props/state? → Do that.
2. Is it caused by a user interaction? → Put it in the **event handler**, not an effect.
3. Is it syncing with something outside React? → Effect with proper cleanup.

**Questions**
1. When exactly do effects run relative to paint?
2. What is a cleanup function for? Give three reasons.
3. Why do I need to clean up a subscription? What breaks if I don't?
4. What does `[]` actually mean? Is it "run once forever"?
5. Why did my effect run twice even with `[]`?
6. How do I write an effect that doesn't loop forever?
7. What causes an infinite effect loop and how do I debug it?
8. Should I `await` inside an effect?
9. Why is an effect not a good place to derive state?
10. How do I fetch data in an effect, and what's the modern answer?

**Model answer, Q4:** "`[]` means 'this effect has no dependencies, so run it after the first commit and never re-run for state/prop changes'. Cleanup still runs on unmount. It is not 'run once per component instance forever' in the sense of ignoring all future renders — it *is* run after every commit where dependencies changed, and with `[]` dependencies never change. Under StrictMode in dev, React mounts, unmounts, and remounts to surface missing cleanups, so you'll see it run twice."

**Model answer, Q8:** "You can, but if you `await` in the body the cleanup function can't be returned, so you lose cancellation and unmount safety. Options: define an inner `async` function and call it, returning the cleanup synchronously; or use `AbortController`. And in most production apps the right answer is a data-fetching layer (4.4) that handles caching, dedupe, retries, and staleness — because hand-rolled effects re-implement all of it badly."

**Model answer, Q9:** "Because an effect writes state *after* render, you get an extra render pass and a frame where the UI shows stale data. If a value is derivable from props and state, deriving it during render is synchronous, correct under concurrent rendering (which can re-run render), and cheaper. Effects are for synchronizing with systems outside React, not for computing values inside it."

**Mistakes:** fetching in effects without cancellation; returning an object from cleanup (React throws); async cleanup functions (they're ignored — you must not `await` in cleanup); forgetting to remove event listeners; using effects as lifecycle equivalents of class `componentDidMount` for data derivation; using an effect for analytics on every render instead of on the specific action.

**Mini task:** Write five effects: (1) a resize observer with correct cleanup, (2) an interval with a pause/resume control, (3) a debounced search with `AbortController` and stale-response protection, (4) a document-title sync that you'd argue should be an effect, and (5) a document-title sync that you first write as an effect and then rewrite as *not* one. For each, name the external system and what happens without cleanup.

---

# 4.1.6 Stale closures and how to fix them
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition:** A stale closure is when a callback (timer, event listener, subscription, async continuation) captures a render's `props`/`state` variables, and by the time it runs, those values are from an older render — so it reads old data.

**Why it happens:** each render creates a brand-new function with a brand-new lexical scope. Functions handed to `setTimeout`, `addEventListener`, `Promise.then`, or `useEffect`'s dependency array keep pointing at the scope they were created in. If they were created in render #1, they see render #1's values forever.

```jsx
function Counter() {
  const [count, setCount] = useState(0);

  // ❌ setInterval captures count === 0 forever
  useEffect(() => {
    const id = setInterval(() => setCount(count + 1), 1000);
    return () => clearInterval(id);
  }, []);                       // "I don't want to re-subscribe every second"

  // ✅ read the latest value at call time
  useEffect(() => {
    const id = setInterval(() => setCount(c => c + 1), 1000);
    return () => clearInterval(id);
  }, []);
}
```

**The four fixes, and when each is right:**

1. **Functional update** (`setCount(c => c + 1)`) — best when you only *write*. No extra deps, no re-subscription.
2. **Functional ref** (read the current value at invocation time) — best when you need to *read* too:
   ```jsx
   function useLatest<T>(value: T) {
     const ref = useRef(value);
     // write during commit, not render (see 4.1.8 note on render purity)
     useLayoutEffect(() => { ref.current = value; });
     return ref;
   }
   ```
   ```jsx
   const countRef = useLatest(count);
   useEffect(() => {
     const id = setInterval(() => {
       setCount(prev => countRef.current + 1);   // read latest
     }, 1000);
     return () => clearInterval(id);
   }, [countRef]);
   ```
3. **Include the dependency and re-subscribe** — correct but costly; don't re-subscribe every second just to see the new count. Fine for cheap subscriptions (a media query).
4. **`useEffectEvent` (experimental) / an event-scoped callback** — the React team's preferred end state: effects declare *what* they depend on for lifecycle, but read values through a stable "event" function. In React 19 this is still experimental, so pattern 2 is the practical answer today.

**The classic stale-closure bugs:**
```jsx
// Debounced search that always searches the first query
const onChange = (e) => {
  clearTimeout(timer.current);
  timer.current = setTimeout(() => search(e.target.value), 300); // e.target.value may be null by then!
};
```
Two bugs here: the captured `e` is a *pooled/persistent* synthetic event whose `target` may have changed (React no longer pools events in 17+, but `e.target.value` at call time is still the live DOM value — read `e.target.value` **first**), and if `search` is a prop that changes, you capture the old one.

```jsx
// Correct version
const query = e.target.value;              // read synchronously
const searchRef = useLatest(search);
clearTimeout(timer.current);
timer.current = setTimeout(() => searchRef.current(query), 300);
```

```jsx
// WebSocket onmessage closure
useEffect(() => {
  ws.onmessage = (ev) => setRoom(JSON.parse(ev.data));  // fine, no state read
}, []);
useEffect(() => {
  ws.onmessage = (ev) => setMessages(m => [...m, JSON.parse(ev.data)]);
}, []);
// ❌ vs
useEffect(() => {
  ws.onmessage = (ev) => setMessages([...messages, JSON.parse(ev.data)]); // stale
}, [messages]);   // and now it re-subscribes on every message
```

**Detection:** turn on `eslint-plugin-react-hooks` (exhaustive-deps + the `react-hooks/exhaustive-deps` warning), and check every callback you pass to something that outlives the render. The tell-tale signs: a timer that never sees new data, a listener that reads an old prop, an event handler registered once that logs a stale ID.

**Questions**
1. What is a stale closure and what causes it?
2. Your `setInterval` always increments by the wrong amount. Explain and fix two ways.
3. Why doesn't adding `count` to the dependency array fix it well?
4. What is a ref-based "latest value" pattern and when is it the right fix?
5. `useEffectEvent` would change which rule?
6. How do you avoid the stale `e.target.value` in a debounce?
7. Why can you never just "fix it by adding deps" for a chat message handler?

**Model answer, Q2:** "The interval callback was created during the first render, so it closes over `count === 0`. Every tick it computes `0 + 1`. Two fixes: use a functional update, `setCount(c => c + 1)`, so I never read the outer variable; or keep a ref holding the latest count and read `countRef.current` inside the callback. Adding `count` to the deps would technically fix correctness but would clear and re-create the interval every second, resetting the timer and possibly skipping ticks — that's a bug factory."

**Mistakes:** adding every variable to the dependency array to "fix" staleness and unknowingly tearing down a subscription every render; reading `e.target` asynchronously; capturing a whole object and depending on its identity; using a ref during render to initialize state.

**Mini task:** Build a 200-line "live dashboard" with: a 1s poller, a resize listener, a keyboard shortcut, and a debounced input. Force each callback to log its captured values; demonstrate the staleness; then fix all four with functional updates / latest-value refs / stable subscription deps, and explain each choice.

---

# 4.1.7 Effects vs events, and "you might not need an effect"
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** (React team canonical topic; a favorite in senior rounds)

**The shift in thinking.** The old mental model — "effects are lifecycle hooks; do things on mount" — produced code that fights React. The current model:

- **Effects are for synchronizing with systems outside React.** External stores, subscriptions, timers, the document, the network, imperative libraries.
- **Events are for responding to user actions.** A click, submit, keystroke. The handler runs, and any state updates it causes are React's business.
- **Rendering is pure.** Compute derived values during render.

**The pattern table** — the single most useful thing to memorize:

| You want to… | Do this | Not this |
|---|---|---|
| Reset state when `id` changes | Give the component a `key={id}` so React remounts it | `useEffect(() => setX(props.x), [props.x])` |
| Transform data for display | Compute during render: `const visible = items.filter(...)` | `useEffect` + `setState` |
| Reset state on a user action | Do it in the event handler: `onSubmit` → `setForm(initial)` | `useEffect` watching a flag |
| Fetch on a dependency change | A framework/hook that owns fetching (4.4), or an effect with a cancel flag | effect with no cancellation |
| Notify a parent of a change | `useEffect(() => { onChange(v) }, [v])` is sometimes right, but prefer raising state | a callback stored in a ref and invoked from an effect |
| Sync with an external store | `useSyncExternalStore` | manual subscribe + setState |
| Update the document title / scroll | Effect (it *is* an external system), or a framework's metadata API | render-phase DOM writes (illegal) |
| Log analytics | In the event handler, or in an effect keyed on the specific value | `useEffect` with no deps (logs every render) |

**The "adjusting state when a prop changes" problem.** React's official guidance: you usually don't need an effect. Three options, in order of preference:

```jsx
// ❌ Effect that mirrors a prop: extra render, and it can flash
function List({ items, filter }) {
  const [visible, setVisible] = useState([]);
  useEffect(() => { setVisible(items.filter(i => i.ok(filter))); }, [items, filter]);
  return <ul>{visible.map(i => <li key={i.id}>{i.label}</li>)}</ul>;
}

// ✅ 1. Derive during render (best when it's cheap)
function List({ items, filter }) {
  const visible = items.filter(i => i.ok(filter));
  return <ul>{visible.map(i => <li key={i.id}>{i.label}</li>)}</ul>;
}

// ✅ 2. Adjust state during render with the "previous value" pattern (when you must keep state)
function List({ items, filter }) {
  const [visible, setVisible] = useState(items);
  const [prevFilter, setPrevFilter] = useState(filter);
  if (filter !== prevFilter) {           // React re-runs this component immediately, before children
    setPrevFilter(filter);
    setVisible(items.filter(i => i.ok(filter)));
  }
  return <ul>{visible.map(i => <li key={i.id}>{i.label}</li>)}</ul>;
}

// ✅ 3. Reset with a key so the child remounts with fresh state
function List({ items, filter }) {
  return <Results key={filter} items={items} filter={filter} />;
}
```

Option 2 is legitimate and React documents it: setting state during render is allowed *for this specific case* and React re-runs the component immediately without committing. It is not "impure render" in the harmful sense, because it only sets state, never touches the outside world.

**The 7 anti-patterns to recognize on sight** (from the React docs' "You Might Not Need an Effect"):
1. Transforming data for rendering.
2. Handling a user event (a reset button shouldn't need an effect).
3. Prop-driven state (reset with `key`).
4. Resetting state with a prop (same fix).
5. Adjusting state when a prop changes (prev-value pattern).
6. Avoiding a "recalculation" effect (just compute).
7. Handling a "failed network request" (handle it in the fetch code, not by mirroring `loading` in state).

**A worked refactor.** This is a classic interview prompt:

```jsx
// ❌ Given: a "chat" that shouldn't connect until the user opens it
function Chat({ roomId }) {
  const [ws, setWs] = useState(null);
  useEffect(() => {
    const socket = connect(roomId);
    setWs(socket);                 // extra render; also the connection may fire before setState commits
    return () => socket.close();
  }, [roomId]);
  useEffect(() => { if (!ws) return; ws.on('message', onMessage); return () => ws.off('message', onMessage); }, [ws]);
  // ... and now onMessage is stale, so another dep...
}

// ✅ Connect-on-demand: no effect, no ws state
function Chat({ roomId }) {
  const socketRef = useRef<WebSocket | null>(null);
  const connect = () => {
    if (socketRef.current) return;
    const s = new WebSocket(url(roomId));
    s.onmessage = ev => handle(ev.data);   // `handle` is stable (useCallback/useLatest or a module fn)
    socketRef.current = s;
  };
  const disconnect = () => { socketRef.current?.close(); socketRef.current = null; };
  return <>
    <button onClick={connect}>Join</button>
    <button onClick={disconnect}>Leave</button>
  </>;
}
```

This is *not* "effects are bad". It's "if the trigger is an event, do it in the event handler". Effects are still the only correct place to react to something React doesn't know about.

**Questions**
1. Why is `useEffect` a poor replacement for lifecycle methods?
2. Give four examples of "you might not need an effect" with the fix for each.
3. How do you reset state when a prop changes? Give three approaches and their trade-offs.
4. When *is* setting state during render allowed?
5. Your component connects a WebSocket twice. Explain.
6. Where should analytics `track()` calls live?
7. How do you decide effect vs event at design time?
8. Why does "extra render" matter for performance and for correctness?

**Model answer, Q6:** "In the handler that represents the user's intent, not in an effect. If the semantic action is 'user clicked Save', track it in `onSave`. If the semantic action is 'the item became persisted', that's a state transition, and an effect keyed on the persisted value is defensible — but then dedupe it and make sure it fires once per transition, not once per render. The rule of thumb: if you can't name the user intent, you probably shouldn't be tracking it."

**Model answer, Q8:** "Correctness first: an effect writing state means the UI has already committed with the old value, so anything reading the DOM or the screen reader sees the stale state, and you get a visible flash plus a double commit. Performance second: each such effect adds a render pass, and a chain of them can cascade into several renders per interaction. Correctness is the real argument; the render count is a symptom."

**Mistakes:** the "fetch and setState" effect copied from 2018 tutorials; using an effect to reset a form; using an effect to log an analytics event that fires on every render; arguing that effects are "bad" rather than "scoped".

**Mini task:** Take any component with ≥2 effects and classify each: external system / event / derivation / anti-pattern. Rewrite the anti-patterns. For each rewrite, state how many renders you removed and what bug class you eliminated (flash, double fetch, lost input, stale UI).

---

# 4.1.8 `useRef`: the mutable box
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** `useRef(initial)` returns a stable `{ current }` object that lives for the component's lifetime. Mutating `.current` does **not** trigger a render.

```jsx
const ref = useRef(0);       // { current: 0 }
ref.current = 5;             // no re-render
```

**Legitimate uses:**
1. **DOM references** (`ref={node => ...}` or `useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null)`), used for measurement, focus, scroll, imperative third-party libraries.
2. **Mutable values that shouldn't cause renders**: the latest props in an async callback (4.1.6), a timer id, an AbortController, a WebSocket instance, a "have we mounted" flag, a previous-value tracker for comparing in an effect.
3. **Storing an instance of a non-React system** where a state variable would be wrong (a `<canvas>` context, an animation controller).

**Anti-uses:**
- As state (it doesn't render — you'll read `ref.current` in render and get a stale value).
- To "avoid re-renders" for values the UI must display.
- To replace `useState` for things that need to trigger renders.

**Ref identity and rules:**
- The ref object is created once per component instance (per fiber), so it's stable across renders and safe in dependency arrays.
- Don't write `ref.current` **during render** and read it in the same render — under concurrent rendering and StrictMode's double-invoke, render runs twice and the second run sees your mutation. If you need "the value from the previous committed render", write in `useLayoutEffect` or `useEffect`, or use React's official "adjust state in an effect" pattern:

```jsx
// ✅ Official pattern: track a previous value
function usePrevious<T>(value: T) {
  const ref = useRef<T | undefined>(undefined);
  useEffect(() => { ref.current = value; }, [value]);
  return ref.current;   // value from the last committed render
}
```

- Don't pass the same ref to two nodes (last write wins) and don't write and read `.current` in the same render.

**Refs and cleanup:** a ref is the right home for anything that needs `.close()`/`.abort()` on unmount, but do the acquiring in an effect so cleanup is paired:

```jsx
useEffect(() => {
  const ws = new WebSocket(url);
  wsRef.current = ws;
  return () => { ws.close(); wsRef.current = null; };
}, [url]);
```

**Callback refs vs `useRef`:** a callback ref runs with `null` on detach, which is how you can react to a node being mounted/unmounted or replaced — useful for measuring or integrating imperative libraries that must know the element exists.

**Questions**
1. Why doesn't mutating a ref cause a render? Where does React read it?
2. When is a ref better than state?
3. What's wrong with writing `ref.current` during render?
4. How do you track the previous value of a prop?
5. Should you ever put React state in a ref? *(Yes, for "is this the initial value" guards and for values that must be read synchronously in a callback, but you must be careful: state changes won't re-render, so it's for bookkeeping, not display.)*
6. How do you forward a ref to a DOM node from a function component?
7. When would you need a ref to an object (not a DOM node)?

**Model answer, Q3:** "Because render can run twice (StrictMode) and can be interrupted and restarted, anything a render writes could be written twice or read from a discarded render pass. Refs are mutable boxes with no corresponding Fiber update, so writing to them during render breaks the model that render is pure computation over props and state. The safe place is after commit: `useLayoutEffect` (before paint) or `useEffect`."

**Mistakes:** `useRef` for displayed data; reading `.current` in render; expecting `useRef(fn())` to be lazy (it's not lazy — the argument is evaluated every render, so pass `useRef(null)` and set it in an effect, or use `useState(() => fn())`); returning a ref object from a custom hook and exposing it where a value is expected.

**Mini task:** Build `useLatest`, `usePrevious`, `useIsMounted`, and `useEventCallback` (a stable callback that always calls the latest version — the workhorse for event handlers passed to memoized children), and write a test for each showing it doesn't cause re-renders.

---

# 4.1.9 `useReducer`: when state transitions are complex
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** `useReducer(reducer, initialState, init?)` centralizes state transitions in a pure function. React owns the queue; you own the logic.

**When it's better than `useState`:**
- The next state depends on the previous state in several places (a state machine, a wizard, a form with cross-field rules).
- You want every transition named and logged (the action log is a free audit trail and makes debugging much easier).
- You want to co-locate related state that must change together.
- You want to test transitions as a pure function without rendering anything.

```jsx
type State =
  | { status: 'idle' }
  | { status: 'loading' }
  | { status: 'success'; data: Item[] }
  | { status: 'error'; error: Error };

type Action =
  | { type: 'load' }
  | { type: 'success'; data: Item[] }
  | { type: 'error'; error: Error }
  | { type: 'reset' };

function reducer(state: State, action: Action): State {
  switch (action.type) {
    case 'load':    return { status: 'loading' };
    case 'success': return { status: 'success', data: action.data };
    case 'error':   return { status: 'error', error: action.error };
    case 'reset':   return { status: 'idle' };
    default:        return assertNever(action);   // exhaustive
  }
}
const assertNever = (x: never): never => { throw new Error('unhandled: ' + x); };

function useItems() {
  const [state, dispatch] = useReducer(reducer, { status: 'idle' } as State);
  const load = useCallback(async (signal: AbortSignal) => {
    dispatch({ type: 'load' });
    try {
      const res = await fetch('/api/items', { signal });
      if (!res.ok) throw new Error(`HTTP ${res.status}`);
      dispatch({ type: 'success', data: await res.json() });
    } catch (e) {
      if ((e as Error).name !== 'AbortError') dispatch({ type: 'error', error: e as Error });
    }
  }, []);
  return { ...state, load };
}
```

**Why the discriminated union matters:** it makes impossible states unrepresentable. `status: 'success'` cannot coexist with `error: undefined`. Compare:

```jsx
// ❌ Three independent booleans = 8 states, 6 of them nonsense
const [loading, setLoading] = useState(false);
const [data, setData] = useState(null);
const [error, setError] = useState(null);
```

**Reducer rules:**
- Must be **pure**: no mutation of `state` or `action`, no network calls, no `Date.now()`/`Math.random()` inside (put them in the action payload).
- Must be **complete**: every action handled, with `assertNever` for exhaustiveness.
- `dispatch` is **stable for the component's lifetime**. Never put `dispatch` in a dependency array (harmless but noise), and never rely on `dispatch` changing to trigger an effect.
- **`init` runs once** and must be pure; use it to avoid re-creating the initial state.
- Lazy initialization: `useReducer(reducer, expensiveArg, init)` beats `useReducer(reducer, expensiveArg())`.
- StrictMode double-invokes reducers in dev. That's another reason reducers must be pure.

**Debugging win:** `dispatch` an action from the console via React DevTools, and you can replay a transition with exact inputs. That's how you get deterministic bug repros.

**`useReducer` vs `useState` vs a state-machine library (XState).** XState (5.x, 11.4) is right when the process has *many* states and *guards/transitions* that form a real machine — wizards, uploads, multi-step forms, media players, connection protocols with illegal transitions. A reducer is right when transitions are simple; XState when the state graph is complex enough that you need visualization, persisted state, and explicit guards.

**Questions**
1. When do you choose `useReducer` over `useState`?
2. Why must reducers be pure, and what breaks if they aren't?
3. Is `dispatch` referentially stable? What does that enable?
4. How do you get exhaustive type checking on actions?
5. What's the `init` third argument for, and when does it matter?
6. Why is `status: 'loading' | 'done'` better than two booleans?
7. When would you reach for a state machine library instead?
8. How would you implement undo/redo with `useReducer`?

**Model answer, Q8:** "Keep a history array in state: `{ past: State[], present: State, future: State[] }`. On each action, push `present` to `past`, set `present` to the new state, and clear `future`. Undo pops from `past` into `present` and pushes the old `present` onto `future`. Redo is the mirror. Keep the history bounded (last N) so memory doesn't grow, and skip entries for high-frequency, non-meaningful changes like a text field keystroke — or coalesce them by action type."

**Mistakes:** side effects inside a reducer; `Date.now()` in a reducer (breaks replayability); mutating state instead of returning a new object; expecting the reducer to be called once per dispatch in dev; using a reducer where a ref would do.

**Mini task:** Model a 4-step checkout wizard as a reducer with an explicit state union, guards for "can go back", and a `MAX_STEPS`-aware progress field. Then write unit tests that drive the reducer directly with action sequences — no rendering — including illegal transitions.

---

# 4.1.10 `useMemo`, `useCallback`, `memo`: cost, benefit, and measurement
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definitions.**
- `React.memo(Component, areEqual?)` — skips re-rendering the component when props are shallow-equal (or equal per `areEqual`).
- `useMemo(fn, deps)` — caches the **return value** of `fn` until deps change.
- `useCallback(fn, deps)` — is `useMemo(() => fn, deps)` for a function. It returns a stable function identity.

**What `memo` actually does:** when a parent re-renders and produces an element for a memoized child, React compares the new props to `memoizedProps` using `Object.is` per key (or your `areEqual`). If equal **and** the element type is identical and no context changed and no state update is pending inside, React bails out and reuses the previous subtree. Crucially, **`memo` still creates a new element object**; the bail-out happens in reconciliation.

**The cost model — you must know this to use these correctly:**

| | Cost per render | Benefit |
|---|---|---|
| `useMemo`/`useCallback` | Allocate a deps array, compare deps, keep the previous value alive in memory | Avoid recompute; provide stable identity |
| `memo` | Compare all props shallowly on every parent render | Skip a subtree render + diff |
| Both | Add code, add a deps list to maintain, risk staleness bugs | — |

React's own guidance (and Dan Abramov's article) is essentially: **don't memoize by default; memoize based on a measurement.** Reasons: (1) the comparison cost is real, (2) the deps arrays are a correctness liability, (3) most "slow renders" are actually slow *computations* or slow DOM updates that memo doesn't help, (4) React Compiler (4.1.21) automates exactly this and makes hand memoization less necessary.

**When memoization genuinely helps:**
1. **Expensive computation** that runs on every render of a hot component:
   ```jsx
   const rows = useMemo(() => expensiveFilter(items, query), [items, query]);
   ```
   Use when the compute is genuinely heavy (>1ms). For small arrays, `filter` on 1000 items is microseconds — memo is *slower*.
2. **Stable identity to prevent effect churn**: passing a stable `submit` to a `<form onSubmit>` that a child subscribes to; a memoized context value (4.1.14); a stable object passed to a dependency array.
3. **`memo` on a genuinely expensive leaf** whose props are usually stable — a chart, a markdown renderer, a big table, a third-party widget.
4. **Referential stability as an API contract**: a context value, a store selector, a `useSyncExternalStore` snapshot, a `Map`/`Set` you return from a hook. If consumers compare with `Object.is`, you must return a stable reference.

**When it does not help (and hurts):**
- Props that change identity every render (`style={{}}`, inline arrow functions, `data={[1,2]}`). `memo` will compare and fail every time — pure overhead plus code noise.
- A cheap component. `memo` makes it slower.
- A component that reads context whose value changes often (4.1.14): the bailout is skipped.
- As a substitute for fixing an architecture problem: a 5,000-row list of unmemoized rows and one memoized wrapper is not a performance strategy.

**Custom comparison.**
```jsx
const Row = memo(function Row({ row, onPick }: Props) { ... }, (prev, next) => {
  return prev.row.id === next.row.id && prev.onPick === next.onPick;
});
```
This lets you ignore a changing `row` object when only the id matters. But you own correctness now: if `row.name` changed and the ids matched, your UI is stale. Prefer `useMemo` on the *data* instead, or stable normalized state (5.3.6).

**Correctness rules for deps:**
- The deps array must contain **every** reactive value used inside. Lint it.
- Don't put values in the deps that the function shouldn't depend on; instead push them into the hook body.
- A dep must be a value whose identity semantically matches "when should this change".
- `useMemo(fn, [])` on a function that closes over props = a stale function (4.1.6) — the most common `useCallback` bug.

**React DevTools Profiler workflow (the honest answer to "is it faster?"):**
1. Record a before/after with the "why did this render" setting on.
2. Check the *commits* and their durations, not just the count.
3. Verify the memoized component actually bailed out (it won't appear in the commit).
4. Watch the render count of the component itself — memo removes it from commits entirely.

**Questions**
1. What is `memo`, `useMemo`, and `useCallback`, and how do they differ?
2. Why does `useCallback` need a deps array if it just returns the function?
3. My `memo` component re-renders anyway. List every possible reason.
4. Is `useMemo` free? What does it cost?
5. When is memoization the wrong tool?
6. How do you decide whether a component is worth memoizing?
7. Why does the React team recommend against default memoization?
8. What's the difference between memoizing a value and memoizing a component?
9. Explain `areEqual` and its risks.
10. What does React Compiler change about this?

**Model answer, Q3:** "Any of: the props aren't actually equal — an inline object/array/function literal; the component reads a context whose value changed (context propagation skips the memo bailout); there is a pending state update inside the memoized subtree; the component type changed (a new component identity, e.g. defining it inside another component); the parent itself re-rendered and the element's type is different from last time; StrictMode is double-invoking in dev; or you're looking at a remount caused by a key change rather than a re-render."

**Model answer, Q6:** "Measure. React DevTools Profiler with 'record why each component rendered' tells me whether a component is re-rendering at all and why. If it's not in the commits, it's fine. If it is, I check whether its render is slow or it's just re-rendering a cheap subtree — because an expensive *DOM update* below it won't be helped by memo. Then, and only then, I memoize the specific expensive computation or wrap the specific expensive leaf."

**Model answer, Q10:** "React Compiler does automatic memoization at build time: it inserts the equivalent of `useMemo`/`useCallback`/`memo` where it can prove they're correct, based on its rules of React. So the manual effort mostly goes away for code that compiles cleanly. Until then — or for code the compiler bails on (violating the rules, side effects in render, mutating props) — manual memoization still matters, and you still have to know *why* you're adding it."

**Mistakes:** wrapping everything in `memo`; `useCallback` for a function used once; memoizing a cheap `filter`; unstable props defeating `memo`; `useCallback(fn, [])` capturing stale props; memoizing an effect's dependency instead of the value; assuming `useMemo` guarantees referential stability (React may discard the cache in the future — it's a performance hint, not a semantic guarantee; if you *need* stability, build it yourself).

**Mini task:** Take a search page with 5,000 rows. Measure: (a) baseline, (b) `useMemo` the filter, (c) `memo` the row, (d) both, (e) virtualize. Record commit counts and durations. Then find the point where the cost of memoization exceeds the benefit, and explain why in numbers.

---

# 4.1.11 `useLayoutEffect`, `useInsertionEffect`, and measurement
🟡 Intermediate → 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definitions.**
- `useEffect` — passive effect, runs *after* the browser has painted (usually).
- `useLayoutEffect` — runs synchronously after DOM mutations, *before* paint. React runs all layout effects, then the browser paints. Because it blocks paint, it must be fast.
- `useInsertionEffect` — runs before *any* layout work, intended for CSS-in-JS libraries to inject styles so the browser never computes an intermediate layout with missing styles. Never use it in app code.

**When you need `useLayoutEffect`:**
1. **Reading layout after mutation and synchronously re-rendering**: measure an element, then set state to adjust a tooltip position, a carousel translate, or a virtualized scroll offset.
2. **Preventing a visual jump**: for example, resetting `scrollTop` to 0 on route change after the new content has been committed.
3. **Keeping two DOM nodes in sync imperatively** where a state update would be too slow.

```jsx
function Tooltip({ anchor, children }) {
  const [pos, setPos] = useState({ top: 0, left: 0 });
  const ref = useRef<HTMLSpanElement>(null);

  useLayoutEffect(() => {
    const a = anchor.getBoundingClientRect();
    const t = ref.current!.getBoundingClientRect();
    setPos({ top: a.bottom + 8, left: a.left + (a.width - t.width) / 2 });
  }, [anchor, children]);

  return <span ref={ref} style={{ position: 'fixed', top: pos.top, left: pos.left }}>{children}</span>;
}
```

**Why this works where `useEffect` doesn't:** with `useEffect`, the browser has already painted the un-positioned tooltip (usually at the wrong place) and *then* React re-renders and repositions it. The user sees a flash. With `useLayoutEffect`, the measurement and the correction both happen before the first paint of that commit, so only the final position is painted.

**The infinite `useLayoutEffect` loop.** `setState` in a layout effect re-runs render → re-runs the layout effect. It only settles if the state converges (or you guard it):

```jsx
useLayoutEffect(() => {
  const next = computePosition();
  setPos(p => (Math.abs(p.left - next.left) < 0.5 && Math.abs(p.top - next.top) < 0.5 ? p : next));
}, [deps]);   // the functional update makes it idempotent
```

**`useLayoutEffect` and SSR.** It warns during server rendering because there's no layout to read. Options: make the component client-only (`'use client'` in RSC), gate it behind a mounted flag, or use `useEffect` and accept the flash. Better: compute what you can from known geometry and avoid measuring entirely.

**Do not measure in render** (`getBoundingClientRect` during render): the DOM isn't up to date yet, and render purity is violated (side-effecting read, plus it can't be run twice safely). Also avoid `window` access during SSR for the same reason.

**Measurement toolbelt:**
```jsx
const el = ref.current!;
const { width, height, top, left } = el.getBoundingClientRect();  // forces layout if styles are dirty
const { scrollWidth, clientWidth } = el;                            // size vs viewport
const ro = new ResizeObserver(([entry]) => setWidth(entry.contentRect.width));
ro.observe(el);
return () => ro.disconnect();
```
Remember `getBoundingClientRect` forces a synchronous layout if you have pending style writes — batch reads before writes, or you'll cause thrashing (3.5.1, 3.6.3).

**Questions**
1. `useLayoutEffect` vs `useEffect` — when each?
2. Why does measuring a tooltip cause a flash with `useEffect`?
3. Why can't you call `useLayoutEffect` on the server?
4. Your layout effect runs forever. Diagnose and fix.
5. Why should you avoid measuring during render?
6. When do you need `useInsertionEffect`, and who should actually use it?
7. When is ResizeObserver better than a resize listener?
8. What's the cost of a `useLayoutEffect` that does heavy work?

**Model answer, Q4:** "Most likely I call `setState` with a fresh object each time, so the state identity always changes and re-triggers the effect. Fixes: return the previous state when the value is effectively unchanged (a functional update with a tolerance check), depend on primitives rather than objects, or move the whole thing to an event handler with `requestAnimationFrame`."

**Model answer, Q7:** "ResizeObserver fires when an element's box changes for any reason — content, siblings, CSS load, font load, container queries, orientation — and it fires before paint. A `resize` listener only fires on window resize, fires *after* layout in a way that's easy to thrash, and needs debouncing."

**Mistakes:** using `useLayoutEffect` for anything that isn't synchronous layout work (it blocks paint, hurting INP); measuring during render; storing DOM nodes in state; ignoring SSR warnings; layout effects with heavy computation or network calls.

**Mini task:** Build a tooltip, a sticky header that toggles a shadow at `scrollY > 8`, and a virtualized list that must read `scrollTop` synchronously. Implement each with `useLayoutEffect` and explain, in terms of paint timing, what a visible flash would look like with `useEffect`.

---

# 4.1.12 Concurrent React: `useTransition`, `useDeferredValue`, `startTransition`
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**The problem being solved.** React 18 made rendering **interruptible and priority-based**. Some updates must be urgent (typing, clicking, dragging) and must not be delayed by anything. Some updates are *transitions*: they can be interrupted, split across frames, or delayed while keeping the previous UI visible. The old model couldn't express that: `setState` was always synchronous-ish and blocked the main thread while React rendered.

**`useTransition`**
```jsx
function SearchPage({ query, setQuery, results, isStale }) {
  const [isPending, startTransition] = useTransition();
  const [deferredQuery] = useDeferredValue(query);

  function onChange(e: QueryChangeEvent) {
    const next = e.target.value;
    setQuery(next);                                   // urgent: the input must reflect the keystroke NOW
    startTransition(() => {
      setDeferredQuery(next);                         // non-urgent: results can catch up
    });
  }

  return (
    <>
      <input value={query} onChange={onChange} />
      {isPending && <Spinner />}
      <Results query={deferredQuery} results={results} stale={query !== deferredQuery} />
    </>
  );
}
```
Semantics:
- `startTransition(scope)` marks **all** state updates inside `scope` as transition updates (same lane).
- The input's own `setQuery` is urgent; the expensive `setDeferredQuery` is not. React renders the urgent update first, so typing stays at 60fps, and the results update after (or are interrupted and restarted when the next keystroke arrives).
- `isPending` is `true` from the moment the transition starts until the transition's render *commits* — a genuinely reliable "loading" flag, unlike `isLoading` state you manage yourself (it flickers if you forget to reset it).
- **Rule:** the transition scope must be synchronous. No `await` inside it — you cannot `startTransition(async () => {...})`. For async work, either set pending state in an event handler before awaiting, or wrap the state updates *after* the await:
  ```jsx
  async function onSubmit() {
    setStatus('saving');                 // urgent
    const data = await save(form);       // network
    startTransition(() => setItems(data));  // the expensive re-render is deferrable
  }
  ```
  (React 19 adds async transitions: `startTransition(async () => { await save(); setItems(...) })` with `isPending` covering the await — see 4.1.21.)
- **Rule:** you cannot transition a controlled input's own value to something slower. If `setQuery` itself triggered an expensive synchronous render, typing would still block. Transitions only help if the urgent part is cheap.

**`useDeferredValue`**
```jsx
const deferredSearch = useDeferredValue(search);
const deferredItems = useDeferredValue(items);
```
It returns the previous value on renders where the input changed, then schedules a low-priority re-render with the new value. Perfect when you *don't control* the urgent state (e.g. it comes from a URL, a store, or a third-party input) but you want the expensive derived rendering to be deferrable.

**`Suspense` + transitions** are the other half: a transition that suspends **keeps the old UI on screen** instead of showing a fallback, avoiding the flash. See 4.1.17.

**What transitions do *not* do:** they don't make the urgent render faster; they don't parallelize rendering; they don't reduce total work. They reprioritize and, crucially, allow React to *interrupt and discard* work. React also may not interrupt at all if the work is small — that's fine.

**Measurement:** use the Profiler with the "priority" track enabled; look at transition commits being dropped (`<Suspense>` or a discarded render) when you type fast. Also check INP in the field — that's the metric this feature exists to improve.

**Questions**
1. What problem do transitions solve that `useState` + `isLoading` doesn't?
2. Why can't you put `await` in a transition scope (React 18)? What changes in React 19?
3. `useTransition` vs `useDeferredValue` — when each?
4. Why can't you make a controlled input's value itself a transition?
5. What does `isPending` guarantee that a `loading` boolean doesn't?
6. How do transitions interact with Suspense?
7. Does `startTransition` make the work faster, cheaper, or just later?
8. What would you do if the urgent render itself is expensive?

**Model answer, Q3:** "They're two sides of the same idea. `useTransition` is for state you own: you wrap the expensive state update and get a `pending` flag. `useDeferredValue` is for a value you don't own — a prop, a store value, a URL param — where you want the expensive part of the tree to render against a slightly stale version instead of blocking. Use both together when you control the input and also want to derive expensive content from it."

**Model answer, Q7:** "Later, and interruptible. Total work is unchanged, but because it's interruptible React can throw away a render that has already been invalidated by a newer keystroke, so under fast typing the expensive tree effectively renders less often. That's a real CPU saving, not just a reordering. But the urgent render must still be cheap, or the input will lag."

**Mistakes:** wrapping everything in `startTransition` including the input's own value; awaiting inside a transition in React 18; assuming `isPending` is a network flag (it's a render-scheduling flag); using a transition where the change must be reflected synchronously (e.g. a destructive confirm); assuming transitions work without `createRoot`.

**Mini task:** Build a filter panel over a 20,000-row list with a text input, a facet select, and a sort dropdown. Use a transition for the results, `useDeferredValue` for the query, and render a subtle "updating…" affordance instead of a spinner (which is the better UX). Then type as fast as you can and record dropped renders in the Profiler and INP.

---

# 4.1.13 `useId`, `useSyncExternalStore`, `useImperativeHandle`, `useDebugValue`
🟡 Intermediate → 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**`useId`** — generates a unique, stable-per-component-instance ID that is **hydration-safe**:
```jsx
const id = useId();     // e.g. «r1»
<label htmlFor={id}>Name</label><input id={id} />
```
Why not `Math.random()` or a counter? Server-rendered markup must match the client's first render exactly, and a global counter differs between server and client. `useId` derives the ID from the tree position + a per-render root prefix, so server and client agree. Use it for `htmlFor`/`id`, `aria-describedby`, `aria-controls`, and SVG `href` references. Don't use it for data keys (it changes if the component moves) or for anything you persist.

**`useSyncExternalStore`** — the correct primitive to subscribe to a mutable external store (Redux, Zustand, an XState machine, a browser API like `localStorage` or `matchMedia`):
```tsx
function useSnapshot<T>(subscribe: (cb: () => void) => () => void, getSnapshot: () => T): T {
  return useSyncExternalStore(subscribe, getSnapshot, getServerSnapshot);
}
```
It guarantees the value read during render is consistent and triggers a re-render if the store changed between render and commit, which fixes the "tearing" problem in concurrent rendering (two components rendering with different values from the same store). It also handles SSR with `getServerSnapshot`. Most libraries (Zustand, Redux Toolkit, React Router) use it internally, which is why their selectors are safe under concurrent features.

**`useImperativeHandle`** — customize what a parent receives from a ref of a component wrapped in `forwardRef`:
```tsx
const Input = forwardRef<InputHandle, InputProps>(function Input(props, ref) {
  const inputRef = useRef<HTMLInputElement>(null);
  useImperativeHandle(ref, () => ({
    focus: () => inputRef.current?.focus(),
    clear: () => { if (inputRef.current) inputRef.current.value = ''; },
  }), []);
  return <input ref={inputRef} {...props} />;
});
```
Keep the handle minimal (a few commands, no data). In React 19 you can accept `ref` as a normal prop in function components, but `useImperativeHandle` is still the way to expose a custom handle. Prefer controlled props for data; reserve handles for genuinely imperative things (focus, scroll, measurement, canvas).

**`useDebugValue`** — labels a value for DevTools inspection without affecting rendering. Almost never needed; it exists for custom hook libraries. It formats on every render in dev (a cost), so libraries usually gate it: `useDebugValue(value, formatter)` or only call it when a debug flag is set.

**Other hooks worth knowing:** `useImperativeHandle`, `useMemo`/`useCallback`, `use` (4.1.21), `useOptimistic`, `useFormStatus`, `useActionState`, plus `useSyncExternalStore`. React 19 also added `use(promise)` and `use(context)` readable during render, and deprecated `forwardRef` in favor of ref-as-prop.

**Questions**
1. Why can't you use a counter or random number for element IDs in SSR?
2. What problem does `useSyncExternalStore` solve?
3. When do you need `useImperativeHandle`?
4. What's wrong with exposing everything through a ref handle?
5. When is `useDebugValue` justified?
6. Which hook would you use to sync a component with `window.matchMedia`?

**Model answer, Q2:** "Concurrent rendering can interleave and retry renders, so a component might read a store during a render that gets thrown away and another component might read it after it changed — that's tearing, where the tree shows inconsistent data. `useSyncExternalStore` makes React re-check the snapshot after commit and re-render if it changed, so every component in a pass agrees on one value, and it provides the `getServerSnapshot` hook needed for SSR."

**Model answer, Q4:** "A ref handle is an escape hatch from controlled React. If you put state or data in it, callers can desync the UI from the source of truth, and the component's invariants (validation, formatting, state machines) get bypassed. Handles should expose *commands* — focus, scrollTo, open, reset, measure — and the component should remain the source of truth."

**Mistakes:** using `useId` for business keys; hand-rolling a store subscription with `useState` + `useEffect` and getting tearing or missed updates; exposing data through `useImperativeHandle`; using `useDebugValue` in app code.

**Mini task:** Write `useMediaQuery` using `useSyncExternalStore` (not `useState` + `addEventListener`), with an SSR fallback. Then write a `Dialog` with `useImperativeHandle` exposing `open`/`close`/`isOpen`, plus tests using RTL (Phase 9) for the handle's behavior.

---

# 4.1.14 Context: propagation, performance, and splitting
🟡 Intermediate → 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**How it works (mechanism).** Each Provider creates a fiber with `type = Context.Provider` and a `memoizedProps.value`. During reconciliation, React *pushes* the value onto a context stack, then when it encounters a consumer, it compares the value it reads against the value on the fiber that rendered it (`dependencies` list on the fiber). If different, the consumer and its subtree are marked for update.

Consequences:
- **A context value change re-renders every consumer below the provider**, even across `memo` boundaries. `memo` does not stop context propagation.
- The value is read **during render** (`useContext` / `use(Ctx)`), so consumers always see a consistent value for that render pass.
- Splitting contexts works because each Provider is a separate fiber: `<ThemeProvider>` and `<AuthProvider>` are separate nodes, so a theme change doesn't touch auth consumers.

**The classic performance bug:**
```jsx
// ❌ one new object per render → every consumer re-renders on every App render
const AppContext = createContext({ theme: 'dark', user: null, setUser: () => {} });
function App() {
  const [user, setUser] = useState(null);
  const value = { theme: 'dark', user, setUser };   // NEW identity every render
```
