```jsx
  return <AppContext.Provider value={value}><Layout /></AppContext.Provider>;
}
```
Every `useContext(AppContext)` consumer re-renders on every App render, even if `theme` never changed. This is the #1 context performance complaint.

**Fixes, in order of preference:**
1. **Split by update frequency**: `<ThemeContext>`, `<AuthContext>`, `<DispatchContext>`. Put the setter in a separate context whose value is stable (`useCallback` or the raw `setUser`, which is always stable).
2. **Split by mutation**: a read-only context and a write-only actions context. Consumers that only dispatch subscribe to a value that never changes.
3. **Memoize the value** — a stopgap, but necessary when the value genuinely must be one object:
   ```jsx
   const value = useMemo(() => ({ theme, user }), [theme, user]);
   ```
   This only helps if the *dependencies* are stable. `useMemo(() => ({ user }), [user])` is safe; `useMemo(() => ({ theme, user }), [{ theme, user }])` is nonsense.
4. **Select from a store instead of context** — Zustand/Redux with selectors means a component only re-renders for the slice it reads. This is why state libraries exist alongside context.
5. **Don't put frequently-changing state in a high-level provider.** Put it as low in the tree as possible.

**Context vs state library vs props**

| | Context | Zustand/Redux/Jotai | Local state |
|---|---|---|---|
| Consumer granularity | Whole value (or split contexts) | Selector-based, per-slice | Component only |
| Devtools/time-travel | No | Yes (Redux) | No |
| Boilerplate | Low | Low–medium | None |
| Cross-cutting actions | Awkward (dispatch context) | First-class middleware/thunks | Awkward |
| Best for | Theme, i18n, router, low-churn services | Server state + large shared app state | Everything local |

**Best practice: context is a *service* container, not a state container.** Pass stable functions and rarely-changing config; keep mutable state in the store or nearest owner.

**Also:** context doesn't work outside React (no DOM events), it isn't a state manager for high-frequency updates (each change re-renders a subtree), and it can't be read in event handlers outside the tree — for that, use a ref/store.

**Questions**
1. How does React know a context consumer needs to re-render?
2. Does `memo` protect a component from context updates?
3. Why does creating the value object inline destroy context performance?
4. How many contexts do you need, and how do you decide to split?
5. Context vs a state library — when each?
6. Why is context bad for high-frequency updates (e.g. mouse position)?
7. How do you pass a lot of values through many layers without prop drilling?
8. How would you debug "my context consumer re-renders too much"?

**Model answer, Q6:** "Every context value change marks every consumer for update, and React re-renders those subtrees. If the value changes per mouse-move event, that's a React render per event at 60–120Hz, plus a diff of the whole subtree. A ref updated in the event handler (no render) or a transient subscription (`useSyncExternalStore` with a selector, or a third-party observable) is the right tool. Context is for relatively rare, meaningful changes."

**Model answer, Q7:** "Three options, in preference order: compose components so you don't need to pass anything (children/slots), pass the data through a single `useMyThing()` hook backed by a module-scoped store or context (so each component asks for what it needs), or split and memoize the context value. Prop drilling through five layers is a signal that a boundary or abstraction is missing — not that props are the wrong tool."

**Mistakes:** one giant app context; putting dispatch and state in the same value; `useMemo` with an object literal in the deps; assuming `memo` shields consumers; context for form input state.

**Mini task:** Build an app with a 30-component tree consuming one context, and instrument render counts. Refactor to three split contexts plus a Zustand-style selector store; record the render-count reduction. Explain which refactor gave the win and why `useMemo` alone was insufficient.

---

# 4.1.15 Component patterns: controlled vs uncontrolled, compound, headless, render props, HOCs
🔟 🟡 Intermediate → 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

### Controlled vs uncontrolled inputs

**Controlled:** React owns the value; the input is a function of props.
```jsx
<input value={name} onChange={e => setName(e.target.value)} />
```
**Uncontrolled:** the DOM owns the value; React only seeds it and can read it imperatively.
```jsx
<input defaultValue={name} ref={inputRef} />           // form-level reset works for free
<input defaultValue={name} key={resetKey} />           // or remount to reset
```

| | Controlled | Uncontrolled |
|---|---|---|
| Source of truth | React state | DOM |
| Validation & formatting | Trivial | Manual / `setCustomValidity` |
| Reset | `setValue('')` | `form.reset()` or a `key` |
| Read without a re-render | No | Yes (read on submit) |
| Performance at scale | Re-renders the input per keystroke | Fine, zero renders |
| Debounce live preview | Awkward (state must update) | Natural |

**Use uncontrolled for big forms**, files, rich text, and third-party widgets. Hybrid pattern: uncontrolled inputs plus a `FormData` read on submit, with `useState` only for the fields that need live reactivity. That's what React Hook Form does (Phase 4.4 / 9).

**Forms and identity.** A `<form>` with `key={userId}` resets all uncontrolled children when the user changes — the cheapest "reset everything" implementation.

### Compound components
```tsx
const TabsContext = createContext<{ value: string; setValue: (v: string) => void } | null>(null);
function useTabs() { const ctx = useContext(TabsContext); if (!ctx) throw new Error('Tabs.* outside <Tabs>'); return ctx; }

function Tabs({ defaultValue, children }: TabsProps) {
  const [value, setValue] = useState(defaultValue);
  const ctx = useMemo(() => ({ value, setValue }), [value]);
  return <TabsContext.Provider value={ctx}>{children}</TabsContext.Provider>;
}
function TabsList({ children }) { return <div role="tablist" className="flex gap-1">{children}</div>; }
function TabsTrigger({ value, children }: { value: string; children: ReactNode }) {
  const { value: current, setValue } = useTabs();
  const selected = current === value;
  return <button
    role="tab"
    aria-selected={selected}
    tabIndex={selected ? 0 : -1}                 // roving tabindex
    onClick={() => setValue(value)}
    onKeyDown={e => { /* ArrowLeft/Right/Home/End move + activate */ }}
    className={selected ? 'tab tab--active' : 'tab'}
  >{children}</button>;
}
function TabsContent({ value, children }: { value: string; children: ReactNode }) {
  const { value: current } = useTabs();
  if (current !== value) return null;
  return <div role="tabpanel" tabIndex={0} aria-labelledby={`tab-${value}`}>{children}</div>;
}

export { Tabs, TabsList, TabsTrigger, TabsContent };
```
Why compound components beat a config-prop component: consumers compose their own markup (so design isn't locked in), state is shared without prop drilling, and the API stays small. Add the error-throwing hook so misuse fails loudly. Combine with **headless** primitives (`@radix-ui`, Headless UI, Ark UI) which give you state + a11y + keyboard behavior with **zero styles** — then style them yourself or wrap them in your design system.

### Render props vs children-as-function vs HOC vs hooks

| Pattern | Shape | Use when | Cost |
|---|---|---|---|
| Render prop / children-as-function | `<Comp>{(x) => ...}</Comp>` | You need the component's internals in your markup | New function identity each render; less tree-shakeable |
| HOC | `withThing(Component)` | Cross-cutting concern, needs no markup control; legacy libs | Prop collisions, `ref`/hoist-statics issues, naming |
| Compound + context | `Tabs.Root`/`Tabs.Trigger` | Shared state + caller owns markup | More boilerplate |
| Custom hook | `const x = useThing()` | You own the markup entirely; need composability | Nothing (this is why hooks won) |
| Slot / `asChild` | `<Trigger asChild><button/></Trigger>` | Radix/Ark style: component renders its child | Requires `Slot` cloning logic |

Practical advice: prefer custom hooks + composition; use headless libs for accessibility; use HOCs only for legacy/analytics wrappers; avoid render props for hot paths.

**Composition over configuration.** The smell to avoid:
```tsx
<Table showToolbar showFilters showPagination expandable rowActions={['edit','delete','archive']} />
```
Every new requirement adds a boolean. Compound components and slots scale better:
```tsx
<Table.Root>
  <Table.Toolbar><Table.Filter /><Table.Search /></Table.Toolbar>
  <Table.Body>{rows.map(r => <Table.Row key={r.id}><Table.Cell>{r.name}</Table.Cell></Table.Row>)}</Table.Body>
  <Table.Pagination />
</Table.Root>
```

**Questions**
1. When do you choose uncontrolled inputs, and how do you reset them?
2. Why do controlled inputs re-render per keystroke, and does that matter?
3. Build the mental model for compound components. Why is a throwing hook important?
4. Headless UI vs component library vs design-system wrapper?
5. Render props vs HOC vs custom hook — pick per scenario and justify.
6. What's the "boolean-prop explosion" smell and what's the fix?
7. How does `asChild`/Slot work?
8. How would you test a compound component?

**Model answer, Q2:** "Because the value comes from state, and `onChange` updates state, so each keystroke schedules a render. For a single input that's trivial. It matters for large forms (use uncontrolled + refs/FormData), for inputs that trigger expensive validation or re-renders of siblings (defer with `useDeferredValue` or uncontrolled + debounced validation), and for third-party widgets that manage their own value."

**Model answer, Q8:** "Test from the consumer's perspective: render the composed component (`<Tabs><TabsList><Tabs.Trigger/></TabsList><TabsContent/></Tabs>`) and drive it with RTL's `userEvent`, asserting roles and `aria-selected`, keyboard arrow navigation, and the error thrown when a `Tabs.Trigger` is rendered without `Tabs`. Testing the internal context directly couples tests to implementation."

**Mistakes:** controlled inputs for a 100-field form; HOCs that swallow props; context defaults that hide the "used outside provider" bug; library components that lock in markup; testing internal context instead of behavior.

**Mini task:** Build a headless `Combobox` (no styles) with `useListbox` + `useCombobox` semantics: `aria-expanded`, `aria-activedescendant`, typeahead, Home/End, Escape, and click-outside. Then wrap it twice: once with your own CSS classes, once with Radix primitives. Write the accessibility tests for both.

---

# 4.1.16 Portals, refs, `forwardRef`, imperative handles
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Portals** let a component render DOM elsewhere in the document while remaining logically part of the React tree.

```jsx
function Modal({ open, onClose, title, children }) {
  const ref = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null);

  useEffect(() => {
    if (!open) return;
    const previous = document.activeElement as HTMLElement | null;
    ref.current?.focus();                       // move focus into the dialog
    const onKey = (e: KeyboardEvent) => {
      if (e.key === 'Escape') { e.stopPropagation(); onClose(); }
      if (e.key === 'Tab') {                     // focus trap
        const focusables = ref.current!.querySelectorAll<HTMLElement>(
          'a[href],button:not([disabled]),input:not([disabled]),select,textarea,[tabindex]:not([tabindex="-1"])'
        );
        if (focusables.length === 0) return;
        const first = focusables[0], last = focusables[focusables.length - 1];
        if (e.shiftKey && document.activeElement === first) { e.preventDefault(); last.focus(); }
        else if (!e.shiftKey && document.activeElement === last) { e.preventDefault(); first.focus(); }
      }
    };
    document.addEventListener('keydown', onKey, true);
    const { overflow } = document.body.style;
    document.body.style.overflow = 'hidden';     // scroll lock
    return () => {
      document.removeEventListener('keydown', onKey, true);
      document.body.style.overflow = overflow;
      previous?.focus();                          // restore focus on close
    };
  }, [open, onClose]);

  if (!open) return null;
  return createPortal(
    <div className="modal-backdrop" onMouseDown={e => e.target === e.currentTarget && onClose()}>
      <div ref={ref} role="dialog" aria-modal="true" aria-labelledby="modal-title" tabIndex={-1}
           className="modal-panel">
        <h2 id="modal-title">{title}</h2>
        {children}
        <button onClick={onClose}>Close</button>
      </div>
    </div>,
    document.body
  );
}
```

Portals are for modals, tooltips, dropdowns, and toasts — anything that must escape `overflow: hidden` and stacking contexts. Key properties:
- **Events bubble through the React tree, not the DOM tree.** A click inside a portal rendered deep in your tree will bubble to ancestors in React even though the DOM parent is `document.body`. This is a classic bug ("my outside-click handler fires when I click the modal") — stop propagation, or check `e.currentTarget` vs `e.target`.
- **`document` doesn't exist during SSR** — guard with a mounted check, or render `null` on the server and mount on the client (hydration-safe if you don't render content that differs).
- **Server rendering portals** needs a framework-provided target.

**Refs, three flavors:**
```jsx
// 1. object ref
const box = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null);
// 2. callback ref (called with node on mount and null on unmount; also fires when the ref callback identity changes)
const listRef = useRef<Set<HTMLLIElement>>(new Set());
// 3. useImperativeHandle to expose a command API (see 4.1.13)
```
**`forwardRef` (and React 19's ref-as-prop):**
```tsx
// React 18 and earlier
type Props = { label: string; ref?: React.Ref<HTMLInputElement> };
const Input = forwardRef<HTMLInputElement, Props>(function Input({ label, ref, ...rest }, forwardedRef) {
  return <label>{label}<input ref={forwardedRef} {...rest} /></label>;
});

// React 19: ref is just a prop for function components
function Input({ label, ref, ...rest }: Props) {
  return <label>{label}<input ref={ref} {...rest} /></label>;
}
```
Rules: don't put `ref` on a function component yourself (warns and doesn't work); don't spread `props` before `ref` (or the inner ref wins); `forwardRef` doesn't fix a component that renders multiple nodes.

**`useImperativeHandle`** should be small and command-shaped. Good handles: `{ focus, blur, scrollToIndex, getSelected, open, close, measure }`.

**Questions**
1. What problem do portals solve?
2. Do events inside a portal bubble to the React parent? Why does that surprise people?
3. What must you handle when opening a modal? *(focus move, focus trap, Escape, restore focus, scroll lock, `aria-modal`, labelling, inert background, portal target on SSR)*
4. `forwardRef` vs ref-as-prop?
5. When should a component expose a handle instead of props?
6. Why can a component only accept one ref?
7. How do you render a tooltip that doesn't get clipped by `overflow: hidden`?

**Model answer, Q2:** "Yes. React attaches its event listeners at the root container and simulates propagation using the *React* tree, so a click inside a portal bubbles to the React ancestors of the component that created the portal, even though its DOM parent is `document.body`. That's why an overlay-level click-outside handler in the tree will fire for clicks inside the portal. Fix: check `e.target === e.currentTarget` for backdrop clicks, or call `stopPropagation` inside the portal content."

**Model answer, Q3:** "Move focus into the dialog on open, trap Tab/Shift+Tab within it, close on Escape, return focus to the trigger on close, lock body scroll, mark it `role="dialog" aria-modal="true"` with an accessible name, and consider the `inert` attribute on background content so screen readers and Tab don't wander out. Also restore everything in the effect cleanup — that's what the cleanup function is for, and it's why mount/unmount symmetry matters."

**Mistakes:** forgetting the focus trap; not restoring focus; no `aria-modal`; portal content without scroll-lock compensation (layout shift when the scrollbar disappears); assuming `document` exists on the server.

**Mini task:** Build a portal-based modal that is fully keyboard accessible, restores focus, locks scroll without layout shift, closes on backdrop click, and announces its title. Then a tooltip positioned via `getBoundingClientRect` in `useLayoutEffect` inside a portal so it escapes clipping and overflow.

---

# 4.1.17 Error boundaries and Suspense
🟡 Intermediate → 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Error boundaries.** A boundary is a **class component** (or, with frameworks, an error boundary component) that catches render/lifecycle errors thrown by its descendants and shows a fallback instead of unmounting the whole app.

```tsx
class ErrorBoundary extends React.Component<
  { children: ReactNode; fallback: ReactNode | ((err: Error, reset: () => void) => ReactNode); onError?: (e: Error, info: React.ErrorInfo) => void },
  { error: Error | null }
> {
  state = { error: null as Error | null };
  static getDerivedStateFromError(error: Error) { return { error }; }
  componentDidCatch(error: Error, info: React.ErrorInfo) {
    this.props.onError?.(error, info);
    reportToSentry(error, info.componentStack);
  }
  reset = () => this.setState({ error: null });
  render() {
    if (this.state.error) {
      return typeof this.props.fallback === 'function'
        ? this.props.fallback(this.state.error, this.reset)
        : this.props.fallback;
    }
    return this.props.children;
  }
}
```

Key facts to state in an interview:
- **They must be class components** (React 19 adds no hook equivalent — this is a well-known API gap). A framework may offer one (Next.js `error.tsx`, Remix `ErrorBoundary`).
- They catch errors thrown **during render, in lifecycle methods, and in constructors of children** — including errors thrown inside a component rendered within them.
- They **do not** catch: errors in event handlers, in async code/`setTimeout`, in promise rejections handled elsewhere, errors thrown in the boundary itself, and errors during SSR (the boundary runs on the server).
- Event handler and async errors must be caught at the source (`try/catch`) and reported to your error tracker.
- **Where to place them:** at meaningful feature boundaries (per route, per widget), with a top-level fallback as the last resort. A boundary that wraps the entire app is a fallback of last resort only; it will hide most bugs.
- Report to Sentry with the `componentStack` and a user-action breadcrumb trail (Phase 4.6).

```jsx
<ErrorBoundary fallback={<FullPageError />}>
  <ErrorBoundary fallback={<WidgetError name="Chart" />}><Chart /></ErrorBoundary>
  <ErrorBoundary fallback={<WidgetError name="Feed" />}><Feed /></ErrorBoundary>
</ErrorBoundary>
```

**Suspense** renders a fallback while a subtree's data isn't ready, and supports transitions that keep the old UI visible.

```jsx
function Page() {
  return (
    <Suspense fallback={<Skeleton />}>
      <SlowSection />          {/* suspends while loading */}
    </Suspense>
  );
}
```
- A component suspends by throwing a promise (React 19 also supports throwing an async function's promise with `use()`; data frameworks like Relay/Apollo/SWR adapters, and Next.js's `<Suspense>` + streaming RSC, use this).
- Only the **nearest** boundary shows a fallback. Nested boundaries let you keep the shell interactive.
- **`use(promise)`** (React 19) reads a promise or context during render and suspends on a pending promise. The promise must be created outside the component (or be cached) — creating it inside the component loops.
- **Transitions + Suspense = no fallback flash.** Wrapping a state update in `startTransition` means React keeps showing the old content instead of swapping to a spinner. This is the main UX reason for both APIs.
- React 18 also introduced `<SuspenseList>` and streaming SSR; in SSR, Suspense boundaries let the server stream HTML with out-of-order completion, which is how Next.js improves TTFB/FCP.
- **`Suspense` is not for everything:** it's for *already-started slow work* that should not block interaction. It isn't a data-fetching API by itself (that's Phase 4.4), and it isn't a substitute for a loading state in a small component where a spinner is fine.

**Choosing skeletons vs spinners:** skeletons preserve layout, so CLS stays ~0 and the perceived wait is shorter. Reserve spinners for sub-second waits where the skeleton would flash.

**Questions**
1. Why must error boundaries be class components, and what is the React 19 state of play?
2. What do error boundaries *not* catch?
3. Where would you place boundaries in a real app?
4. Why is a top-level boundary alone insufficient?
5. How does Suspense + `startTransition` avoid spinners?
6. What throws a promise in a Suspense world?
7. Suspense vs a `loading` state variable?
8. How does Suspense help SSR/TTFB?

**Model answer, Q2:** "Not event handler errors, not errors inside `setTimeout` or promise callbacks that you don't rethrow during render, not errors thrown by the boundary itself, and not server-render errors. Those must be caught with `try/catch` at the call site and reported. The reason is that the boundary mechanism is implemented by React's render/commit machinery: it can only catch a throw that happens while React is rendering a child fiber."

**Model answer, Q5:** "A transition update is non-urgent, so if the new content suspends, React can keep showing the existing UI and keep the main thread responsive to input, instead of committing a fallback. Without a transition, React must show the fallback immediately, which is why typing in a search box with a Suspense boundary produces a flash of spinner on every keystroke. That's the canonical 'Suspense + transition' pairing."

**Mistakes:** wrapping the whole app in one boundary; expecting boundaries to catch fetch errors; putting a data-fetching side effect in a component that suspends without a cache; using Suspense for sub-100ms loads; forgetting `key` to reset a boundary's error state (a common pattern: reset on navigation).

**Mini task:** Build a `QueryErrorBoundary` with: `reset()`, retry-with-backoff, a "report" affordance that sends the `componentStack`, and a `resetKeys` prop so navigation clears the error. Simulate a thrown error in a child, then in an event handler (show that the boundary does *not* catch it), then in an async callback.

---

# 4.1.18 Lists, virtualization, and large forms
🟡 Intermediate → 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Lists.** Rendering 10,000 rows creates 10,000 elements and DOM nodes: memory, style recalc, and layout all suffer. Fixes, in increasing order of effort:
1. **Reduce the data** — pagination, infinite scroll, filters.
2. **Reduce per-row cost** — memoized row, no inline objects, CSS containment (`contain: strict`), fixed row height, `will-change` used sparingly.
3. **Virtualize** — render only the visible window plus overscan.

```jsx
function VirtualList({ items, rowHeight, overscan = 6 }) {
  const ref = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null);
  const [start, setStart] = useState(0);
  const [viewportH, setViewportH] = useState(0);

  useLayoutEffect(() => {
    const el = ref.current!;
    const ro = new ResizeObserver(([e]) => setViewportH(e.contentRect.height));
    ro.observe(el);
    const onScroll = () => setStart(Math.max(0, Math.floor(el.scrollTop / rowHeight) - overscan));
    el.addEventListener('scroll', onScroll, { passive: true });
    onScroll();
    return () => { ro.disconnect(); el.removeEventListener('scroll', onScroll); };
  }, [rowHeight, overscan]);

  const visibleCount = Math.ceil(viewportH / rowHeight) + overscan * 2;
  const slice = items.slice(start, start + visibleCount);
  const padTop = start * rowHeight;

  return (
    <div ref={ref} style={{ height: '100%', overflow: 'auto' }}>
      <div style={{ height: items.length * rowHeight, position: 'relative' }}>
        <div style={{ transform: `translateY(${padTop}px)` }}>
          {slice.map((item, i) => <Row key={item.id} item={item} />)}
        </div>
      </div>
    </div>
  );
}
```
Production libraries (`@tanstack/react-virtual`, `react-window`, `react-virtuoso`) also handle **dynamic row heights**, scroll anchoring (content above the viewport changing size), sticky headers, bidirectional/infinite loading, and accessibility (only rendered rows exist in the a11y tree — which is a real trade-off, so provide an accessible alternative or `aria-setsize`/`aria-posinset`).

**Keys for lists:** stable ids (4.1.2). For infinite scroll, key by item id and never by page index — pages change content.

**Forms at scale.** A 500-field controlled form re-renders the whole form on every keystroke. Approaches:
- **Uncontrolled** + refs + `FormData` on submit (best for large forms).
- **Field-level subscriptions**: a form library that only re-renders the changed field (RHF's `register`, Formik's `useField`).
- **Split components per field** so each field's state change re-renders only that subtree.
- **Schema validation** on blur and on submit, not on every keystroke (Phase 4.4).
- For very large forms: dynamic import per section, virtualization of non-field sections, and progressive disclosure.

**Uncontrolled form + `FormData`:**
```jsx
function onSubmit(e: React.FormEvent<HTMLFormElement>) {
  e.preventDefault();
  const fd = new FormData(e.currentTarget);            // zero re-renders
  const payload = Object.fromEntries(fd.entries());
}
<form key={userId} onSubmit={onSubmit}>               {/* key resets all children */}
  <input name="email" type="email" required />
  <input name="profile.name" />                        {/* dot paths map to nested fields */}
  <input name="tags" multiple defaultValue={['a']} />
</form>
```
Note `FormData` quirks: file inputs need `files`, checkbox `value` defaults to `"on"`, multiple files are `FileList`, and a `name` with dots/brackets needs manual mapping (RHF does this).

**Infinite scroll**: `IntersectionObserver` on a sentinel, cursor-based pagination, guard against duplicate fetches, and an accessible "Load more" button as the fallback (auto-scroll loading is an accessibility problem — see WCAG 2.2 "Focus Not Obscured" and motion/timeout criteria).

**Questions**
1. Why is a 10k-row list slow? Name the three costs.
2. What does virtualization buy, and what does it cost (a11y, find-in-page, print, dynamic heights)?
3. Why must an infinite list use cursor pagination with stable keys?
4. How do you keep a 500-field form fast?
5. Why is `FormData` a good fit for large forms, and what are its pitfalls?
6. When is a sentinel-based infinite scroll an accessibility problem?
7. What is scroll anchoring and why does it matter for virtualized lists?

**Model answer, Q2:** "Virtualization removes DOM nodes, which reduces memory, style recalculation, layout, and paint. The costs are: only rendered rows exist in the accessibility tree and for find-in-page; dynamic row heights need measurement; scroll position can jump when content above changes size unless you anchor; print/export and "select all" need full-data handling; and server-side rendering the whole list isn't possible. So it's a rendering optimization, not a data model change — keep the full array in memory and render a window."

**Mistakes:** `key={index}` in an infinite list; unbounded DOM growth; auto-loading with no button fallback; measuring row heights in `useEffect` instead of `useLayoutEffect` (causes a jump); keeping a controlled form for 500 fields.

**Mini task:** Implement a virtualized list from scratch (fixed height), then a dynamic-height version using `ResizeObserver`, then wire it to a cursor-paginated API with an `IntersectionObserver` sentinel, a `Load more` button for keyboard users, and a "select all 10,000" checkbox that operates on the data model rather than the DOM.

---

# 4.1.19 Custom hooks: composition and the four you'll actually write
🟡 Intermediate → 🔴 · **Must-know**

**What a custom hook is:** a function whose name starts with `use`, that may call other hooks, that owns *logic* (not markup), and whose state is private to each call site. Rules of hooks apply inside it.

**When a custom hook is the right abstraction:**
- The same **logic** is needed in ≥2 places (the smell test is copy-pasted `useEffect`, not "it feels reusable").
- The logic is a state machine with transitions (debounce, async request lifecycle, upload progress).
- It lets a component stay declarative: `const { query, setQuery, results } = useSearch()` instead of 60 lines of effect and ref bookkeeping.
- It provides a **stable API surface** that can be tested independently.

**When it isn't:** wrapping a single line; hiding which hooks it calls in a way that makes debugging hard; a hook that returns 12 values (that's a component or a store).

**Rules:** call hooks unconditionally, at the top level; name the return values explicitly; return a stable tuple/object with `useMemo` if consumers use it as a dependency; document the side effects it owns; expose `reset`/`cancel`/`abort`.

### `useDebounce` (value + callback forms, with cancel)
```tsx
export function useDebounce<T>(value: T, delay = 300): T {
  const [debounced, setDebounced] = useState(value);
  useEffect(() => {
    const id = setTimeout(() => setDebounced(value), delay);
    return () => clearTimeout(id);                 // cancel previous on every change/unmount
  }, [value, delay]);
  return debounced;
}

export function useDebouncedCallback<A extends unknown[]>(
  fn: (...args: A) => void, delay = 300, options: { leading?: boolean } = {}
) {
  const fnRef = useLatest(fn);                       // always the latest closure
  const timer = useRef<ReturnType<typeof setTimeout> | null>(null);
  const leadingRef = useRef(false);

  const cancel = useCallback(() => {
    if (timer.current) { clearTimeout(timer.current); timer.current = null; }
    leadingRef.current = false;
  }, []);

  useEffect(() => cancel, [cancel]);                // cancel on unmount

  return useCallback((...args: A) => {
    const callNow = options.leading && !leadingRef.current;
    cancel();
    if (callNow) { leadingRef.current = true; fnRef.current(...args); }
    timer.current = setTimeout(() => { timer.current = null; fnRef.current(...args); }, delay);
  }, [cancel, delay, fnRef, options.leading]);
}
```
Edge cases: debouncing `''` (usually you want to *not* search for empty); leading vs trailing; cancelling on unmount; `delay` changes; a stable returned function identity so it's safe in child deps.

### `useFetch` / a minimal query hook (the *shape* every data library shares)
```tsx
type State<T> = { status: 'idle' | 'loading' | 'success' | 'error'; data?: T; error?: Error };

export function useQuery<T>(
  queryKey: readonly unknown[],
  fetcher: (signal: AbortSignal) => Promise<T>,
  options: { enabled?: boolean; staleTimeMs?: number; retry?: number } = {}
) {
  const { enabled = true, staleTimeMs = 30_000, retry = 2 } = options;
  const [state, setState] = useState<State<T>>({ status: 'idle' });
  const cache = useRef(new Map<string, { at: number; data: T }>());
  const key = useMemo(() => JSON.stringify(queryKey), [queryKey]);
  const fetcherRef = useLatest(fetcher);

  const run = useCallback(async () => {
    const hit = cache.current.get(key);
    if (hit && Date.now() - hit.at < staleTimeMs) { setState({ status: 'success', data: hit.data }); return; }
    const controller = new AbortController();
    setState(s => ({ ...s, status: 'loading' }));
    for (let attempt = 0; attempt <= retry; attempt++) {
      try {
        const data = await fetcherRef.current(controller.signal);
        cache.current.set(key, { at: Date.now(), data });
        setState({ status: 'success', data });
        return;
      } catch (err) {
        if ((err as Error).name === 'AbortError') return;
        if (attempt === retry) setState({ status: 'error', error: err as Error });
        else await sleep(2 ** attempt * 200 + Math.random() * 100);   // exponential backoff + jitter
      }
    }
  }, [key, fetcherRef, retry, staleTimeMs]);

  useEffect(() => {
    if (!enabled) return;
    run();
    return () => { /* abort the in-flight request via a ref'd controller */ };
  }, [run, enabled]);

  return { ...state, refetch: run };
}
```
The real lesson is the **shape**, not the code: cache by key, dedupe in-flight requests, stale-while-revalidate, abort on unmount/key change, retry with backoff and jitter, stable keys, and `enabled` for conditional fetching. That's TanStack Query (Phase 4.4). Building this yourself is the best way to understand why.

### `useOnClickOutside` and `useIntersection`
```tsx
export function useOnClickOutside<T extends HTMLElement>(
  handler: (e: MouseEvent | TouchEvent) => void, enabled = true
) {
  const ref = useRef<T>(null);
  const handlerRef = useLatest(handler);
  useEffect(() => {
    if (!enabled) return;
    const listener = (e: MouseEvent | TouchEvent) => {
      const el = ref.current;
      if (!el || el.contains(e.target as Node)) return;
      handlerRef.current(e);
    };
    // capture:true so we see the event before React handlers can stopPropagation
    document.addEventListener('mousedown', listener, true);
    document.addEventListener('touchstart', listener, true);
    return () => {
      document.removeEventListener('mousedown', listener, true);
      document.removeEventListener('touchstart', listener, true);
    };
  }, [enabled, handlerRef]);
  return ref;
}
```
```tsx
export function useIntersection<T extends Element>(
  { root = null, rootMargin = '0px', threshold = 0 } = {},
  once = false
) {
  const ref = useRef<T>(null);
  const [entry, setEntry] = useState<IntersectionObserverEntry | null>(null);
  useEffect(() => {
    const el = ref.current;
    if (!el) return;
    const io = new IntersectionObserver(([e]) => {
      setEntry(e);
      if (once && e.isIntersecting) io.disconnect();
    }, { root, rootMargin, threshold });
    io.observe(el);
    return () => io.disconnect();
  }, [root, rootMargin, threshold, once]);
  return [ref, entry] as const;
}
```

### `useLocalStorage` (SSR-safe, cross-tab, validated)
```tsx
export function useLocalStorage<T>(key: string, initial: T, validate?: (v: unknown) => v is T) {
  const [value, setValue] = useState<T>(() => {
    if (typeof window === 'undefined') return initial;              // SSR
    try {
      const raw = window.localStorage.getItem(key);
      if (raw == null) return initial;
      const parsed = JSON.parse(raw);
      return validate ? (validate(parsed) ? parsed : initial) : (parsed as T);
    } catch { return initial; }
  });
  useEffect(() => {
    try { window.localStorage.setItem(key, JSON.stringify(value)); } catch { /* quota / private mode */ }
  }, [key, value]);
  useEffect(() => {                                                       // cross-tab sync
    const onStorage = (e: StorageEvent) => { if (e.key === key && e.newValue) setValue(JSON.parse(e.newValue)); };
    window.addEventListener('storage', onStorage);
    return () => window.removeEventListener('storage', onStorage);
  }, [key]);
  return [value, setValue] as const;
}
```
Also handle: quota exceeded, Safari private mode throwing on `setItem`, JSON parse failure, and validation (never trust storage).

**More hooks worth having in your toolbox:** `useToggle`, `useMediaQuery` (via `useSyncExternalStore`), `useOnEscape`, `useEventCallback`, `useCopyToClipboard`, `useLongPress`, `useIsomorphicLayoutEffect` (layout effect on client, effect on server), `useVirtualList`, `useFocusTrap`, `useForm` (tiny controlled/uncontrolled hybrid), `useCountUp`, `useOnlineStatus`, `useKeyPress`.

**Questions**
1. When should you extract a custom hook? What's the real smell test?
2. When is a custom hook the wrong abstraction?
3. Implement `useDebounce` with `cancel`, and explain leading vs trailing.
4. What are the five responsibilities of any data-fetching hook?
5. Why must a debounce timer be cleared in cleanup?
6. Why `capture: true` in a click-outside listener?
7. Why does `useLocalStorage` need validation?
8. How do you test a custom hook that has timers and network calls?

**Model answer, Q2:** "When it hides a single trivial call, when it returns an enormous grab-bag of values (that's a store or a component), when it needs to be called conditionally, or when it exists purely to have a name. Also when a hook returns JSX — at that point you have a component, and you should use one so the state has a lifecycle you can reason about."

**Model answer, Q8:** "With React Testing Library's `renderHook` and `act`. For timers: `vi.useFakeTimers()`/`jest.useFakeTimers()` and `advanceTimersByTime`, plus `waitFor` for assertions after async effects. For network: MSW (Phase 9) rather than mocking `fetch`, so you test the real fetch path. And assert on *observable behavior* — the returned value over time — rather than on how many times an internal setter was called."

**Mistakes:** hooks that call hooks conditionally; hooks returning a new object every render (breaks memoized consumers); debounce without cancellation; not aborting requests on unmount; `useLocalStorage` without try/catch (Safari/quotas); forgetting that `useRef(fn())` isn't lazy.

**Mini task:** Write `useDebounce`, `useDebouncedCallback`, `useQuery` (mini), `useIntersection`, `useOnClickOutside`, `useLocalStorage`, and `useIsomorphicLayoutEffect`. For each: document the external system it owns, its cleanup, and write 3 RTL tests including the unmount case. Then replace your `useQuery` with TanStack Query and explain which of your responsibilities it moved to the library.

---

# 4.1.20 StrictMode, hydration, and the double-invoke
🟡 Intermediate → 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**StrictMode** is a development-only wrapper that intentionally surfaces unsafe patterns. In React 18+ it does three things on mount:

1. **Re-renders components an extra time** (render-phase double invocation).
2. **Re-runs effects** an extra time: mount → cleanup → mount.
3. **Re-runs reducers** and (in dev) re-runs some initializer functions.

**Why:** it's a rehearsal for what concurrent rendering and future behaviour can do (remounting, interrupting, re-running impure logic). If a component only works when effects run exactly once, it has a bug: an uncleaned subscription, a double-charged request, a non-idempotent side effect.

```jsx
// BROKEN — the double effect exposes it
useEffect(() => {
  const id = crypto.randomUUID();
  fetch('/api/track', { method: 'POST', body: id });   // fires twice in dev
}, []);

// FIXED — idempotent, or clean up properly
useEffect(() => {
  const controller = new AbortController();
  track({ controller, dedupe: true });
  return () => controller.abort();
}, []);
```

**What StrictMode does *not* do:** it does not double-fire event handlers, it does not double-invoke `useMemo` bodies in a way you should rely on (the docs explicitly say you should not rely on memo caches being preserved), and it does not detect impure render beyond a dev-only double call.

**Purify your render phase.** Because render runs twice in dev, and can be re-run in production under concurrency, render must be a pure function of props and state. Common impurities:
- `Date.now()`, `Math.random()`, `crypto.randomUUID()` in render → use `useState(() => ...)` for a once-per-instance value, or move to an effect.
- Mutating props or state objects.
- Reading and writing refs during render (4.1.8).
- Dispatching to a store during render.
- Network calls or logging with side effects (logging is fine but noisy).

**Hydration.** SSR sends HTML produced by running your components on the server. On the client React must adopt that DOM without re-running the same rendering assumptions. Hydration fails (React logs "Text content does not match" / "Hydration failed") when the first client render produces different output than the server HTML. Usual causes:
- `Date`, `Math.random()`, locale or timezone differences between server and client.
- `window`/`localStorage`/`navigator` access during render.
- Browser-only CSS-in-JS injecting styles differently.
- Invalid HTML nesting that the browser "fixes" (a `<div>` inside a `<p>`).
- Rendering different content based on feature detection or viewport size.

Fixes: `suppressHydrationWarning` for a single unavoidable text node (e.g. a timestamp) and nothing more; defer browser-only reads to an effect; gate with a mounted flag and render a stable placeholder; move client-only rendering into a component loaded with `next/dynamic` with `ssr: false`; use a framework's proper data APIs (dehydrated server state) rather than ad-hoc client fetches.

**Time to interactive vs time to hydrate.** Hydration costs JS parse/compile/execute on the main thread. Techniques that reduce its cost: less client-side JavaScript, `React.lazy` for below-the-fold code, avoiding hydration of static content (partial hydration/ Islands), streaming SSR so the browser can start work earlier, and RSC to ship less component code.

**Questions**
1. Why does StrictMode double-invoke effects, and what bug class is it designed to expose?
2. My API call fires twice in development. Is it a StrictMode bug? What is the real fix?
3. Why is reading `Date.now()` in render a bug even though it "only happens in dev twice"?
4. What causes hydration mismatches? Which fix is legitimate and which is a hack?
5. What does `suppressHydrationWarning` actually do, and when is it acceptable?
6. How do you reduce hydration cost?
7. Should you ship StrictMode to production? What does it do to your bundle?

**Model answer, Q2:** "StrictMode is doing its job: it remounts effects so that a missing cleanup shows up. The real bug is that my effect performs a non-idempotent side effect with no cleanup. If the effect created a subscription, timer, or socket, StrictMode's cleanup would tear it down and it would be recreated — fine. My `fetch` had no abort, so both calls completed. The fix is to make the effect abortable and idempotent, and for writes to carry an idempotency key. Production StrictMode does not double-invoke, so this only shows up in development, which is exactly why it is valuable."

**Model answer, Q4:** "In order of likelihood: time- or locale-dependent rendering, direct `window`/`localStorage`/`navigator` reads during render, invalid HTML nesting the parser repairs, and content that depends on client-only feature detection. The legitimate fix is to make the first client render match the server — defer browser-only reads to an effect, pass server-resolved values down, or render a stable placeholder. `suppressHydrationWarning` is legitimate for a single genuinely unavoidable value like a formatted timestamp on a text node; using it broadly hides real bugs, and it does not fix mismatched attributes or extra/missing elements."

**Mistakes:** treating the double-invoke as a bug to suppress; adding a `didMount` ref guard around every effect instead of cleaning up properly; using `suppressHydrationWarning` on a wrapper element to hide structural mismatches; reading `window` in render in an SSR app.

**Mini task:** Take an SSR-rendered page and intentionally introduce four hydration mismatches (a timestamp, a `localStorage` read, a `navigator.userAgent` branch, and invalid nesting). Observe each error message, then fix all four properly and explain why `suppressHydrationWarning` is not the answer.

---

# 4.1.21 React 19: Actions, `use`, `useOptimistic`, `useFormStatus`, ref-as-prop, React Compiler
🔴 Advanced → senior · **Must-know** (React 19 is stable; expect it in interviews)

### Actions
An **Action** is an async function passed to React that lets you perform a state transition with pending state, error handling, and optimistic updates handled for you. Two integration points:

```jsx
// 1. Form Actions (progressive enhancement; works without JS for the submit)
function ChangeName({ user }) {
  async function submit(formData) {                 // receives FormData
    'use server';                                    // optional in RSC frameworks
    const name = String(formData.get('name'));
    await db.updateUser(user.id, { name });          // revalidatePath/revalidateTag live here
  }
  return <form action={submit}><input name="name" defaultValue={user.name} /></form>;
}

// 2. useActionState: React manages the transition + returned state
function ChangeName({ user }) {
  const [state, submit, isPending] = useActionState(
    async (prevState, formData) => {
      const name = String(formData.get('name')).trim();
      if (!name) return { error: 'Name is required' };      // returned state, no throw
      try {
        await db.updateUser(user.id, { name });
        return { ok: true, name };
      } catch (e) {
        return { error: 'Could not save. Try again.' };
      }
    },
    { ok: false, error: null }
  );
  return (
    <form action={submit}>
      <input name="name" defaultValue={user.name} required />
      <SubmitButton>{isPending ? 'Saving…' : 'Save'}</SubmitButton>
      {state.error && <p role="alert">{state.error}</p>}
    </form>
  );
}
function SubmitButton({ children }) {
  const { pending } = useFormStatus();              // only valid INSIDE a <form>
  return <button disabled={pending}>{children}</button>;
}
```
Key React 19 additions:
- **`startTransition` accepts async functions** and `isPending` now stays true for the whole await.
- **Errors thrown in Actions** propagate to the nearest error boundary (before, they were swallowed).
- **`useFormStatus`** gives a child component (`pending`, `data`, `method`, `action`) — it must be rendered inside a `<form>` that has an action; it cannot be used in the component that renders the form. That's the most common mistake.
- **Programmatic submission** via `useActionState`'s third return value (`submit` with no event) allows custom buttons.

### `use`
```jsx
function Page({ promise }) {           // e.g. a server-passed promise, or a cached resource
  const data = use(promise);           // suspends while pending, reads when resolved
  return <Profile user={data} />;
}
function ThemeLabel() {
  const theme = use(ThemeContext);     // ALSO reads context, conditionally!
  return <span>{theme.label}</span>;   // no useContext hook needed → conditional reads allowed
}
```
Rules and gotchas:
- `use` can be called **conditionally** or inside loops (unlike hooks) because it is not a hook that stores state on the fiber's hook list.
- In a client component, the promise must be **created outside** the component and must be stable across renders (create it in a module scope, a cache, or pass it from a server component). Creating it inside render causes an infinite suspend loop.
- React re-suspends and retries when the promise resolves; the promise should be cached so re-renders don't create new promises (this is what Suspense caches and what frameworks implement with resource caching).
- Reading context with `use` inside a condition that changes between renders is allowed, but the *shape* of the tree must remain consistent for the underlying data.

### `useOptimistic`
```jsx
function Conversation({ messages, sendAction }) {
  const [optimistic, addOptimistic] = useOptimistic(
    messages,
    (state, newMsg: Message) => [...state, { id: 'temp', pending: true, ...newMsg }]
  );
  return (
    <>
      {optimistic.map(m => <Bubble key={m.id} msg={m} />)}
      <form action={sendAction} onSubmit={e => { /* optimistic update */ }}>
        ...
      </form>
    </>
  );
}

// server action or transition:
startTransition(async () => {
  addOptimistic(msg);                  // shown immediately
  await send(msg);                     // real state arrives via revalidation
});                                     // if it fails, the optimistic entry disappears
```
Semantics: the optimistic state is **derived** from the real state plus pending actions, so it disappears automatically when the real state updates or the action errors — no manual rollback. The second argument must be **pure**. You can pass `{ state }` or `(state, payload)`. It requires the update to happen inside a transition or an action.

### Ref as a prop
React 19 lets function components receive `ref` as a normal prop, so `forwardRef` is no longer needed for new code:
```jsx
function MyInput({ placeholder, ref }) {
  return <input ref={ref} placeholder={placeholder} />;
}
```
`forwardRef` still works and is still needed in some interop cases; React 19 also lets you pass **ref as a prop to function components** in third-party libs. Related changes: `ref` can be a cleanup function (returning a value from a ref callback unsets it, which removes the need for manual null handling), and `createElement` no longer needs the extra `forwardRef` argument.

### Other React 19 items worth knowing
- **Document metadata**: `<title>`, `<meta>`, `<link>` rendered anywhere in the tree are hoisted to `<head>` (so you can set metadata from a component, and Server Components can set it per route).
- **Stylesheets**: stylesheet precedence and async stylesheet support; `<link rel="stylesheet" precedence>`.
- **Static/dynamic APIs**: `cache`, `use cache` (framework-dependent), prerendering hints.
- **Improved hydration error messages** and better error diffs.
- **Removed/deprecated**: `ReactDOM.render` (use `createRoot`), `ReactDOM.hydrate` (use `hydrateRoot`), string refs, legacy Context (`contextTypes`), `propTypes`, defaultProps for function components, and `React.FC`.

### React Compiler
A build-time compiler (via the Babel plugin or frameworks) that automatically applies the equivalent of `useMemo`/`useCallback`/`memo` where it can prove it is safe. It analyses your components against a set of rules of React and bails out of components that violate them (mutating props/state during render, reading refs during render, calling `useMemo` with unstable deps, side effects in render).

**What changes for you:**
- Hand-written `useMemo`/`useCallback` become less necessary for correctly written components.
- Performance reasoning shifts from "did I memoize?" to "do I violate the rules of React?" (mutating, impure render, unstable deps).
- It does not fix algorithmic problems: an O(n²) computation inside a memo is still O(n²).
- It does not eliminate the need to understand re-render causes.
- Adopt gradually: enable it, fix the bailouts it reports, and measure.

**Questions**
1. What is an Action and what does React give you for free?
2. `useFormStatus` returns `pending` but my button never disables. Why?
3. What does `use(promise)` do, and what's the one rule that makes it loop forever?
4. How does `useOptimistic` know to remove an optimistic entry on failure?
5. When would you still use `useState` instead of an Action?
6. What changes with ref-as-a-prop, and what still needs `forwardRef`?
7. What does React Compiler actually do, and what does it not fix?
8. What breaks if you mutate state during render? How does React 19/18 behave?

**Model answer, Q3:** "If the promise is created inside the component's render, each render creates a new pending promise, so `use` suspends again forever — an infinite suspense loop. The promise must be created outside the component (module scope, a cache keyed by the request, or passed in from a server component) so it stays referentially stable while pending."

**Model answer, Q7:** "React Compiler statically analyses components and inserts memoization where it can prove correctness, so it removes most of the manual `useMemo`/`useCallback`/`memo` work. It does not fix algorithmic complexity — memoizing an O(n²) filter still costs O(n²) — and it does not fix components that violate the rules of React; it bails out of those and reports why. And it doesn't remove the need to understand *why* something re-renders: if a prop identity changes, the computation genuinely has to run again."

**Mistakes:** calling `useFormStatus` in the component that renders the `<form>`; passing a freshly created promise to `use`; making the `useOptimistic` reducer impure; assuming React Compiler makes `memo` unnecessary in code that violates its rules; putting `await` inside `useOptimistic`'s reducer.

**Mini task:** Build a comment composer using a form Action with `useActionState` for validation errors, `useFormStatus` in a nested submit button, `useOptimistic` for instant feedback, and an error boundary for thrown errors. Then flip a switch to fail the server action and verify the optimistic comment disappears and an error is surfaced accessibly.

# 4.1.22 MUST-IMPLEMENT-FROM-SCRATCH (12 React utilities)
Write each from memory. These are the utilities you should be able to produce in an interview without thinking.

### 1. `useDebouncedValue` with cancel and flush
```tsx
export function useDebouncedValue<T>(value: T, delayMs = 300) {
  const [debounced, setDebounced] = useState(value);
  const timer = useRef<ReturnType<typeof setTimeout> | null>(null);
  const flush = useCallback(() => {
    if (timer.current) { clearTimeout(timer.current); timer.current = null; }
    setDebounced(value);
  }, [value]);
  useEffect(() => {
    clearTimeout(timer.current);
    timer.current = setTimeout(() => { timer.current = null; setDebounced(value); }, delayMs);
    return () => { if (timer.current) clearTimeout(timer.current); };
  }, [value, delayMs]);
  return [debounced, flush] as const;
}
```
*Edge cases:* cleanup on unmount; `flush()` for a "search now" button; not debouncing `''` if that is your product rule (do it in the caller).

### 2. `useLatest` (stable callback/value)
```tsx
export function useLatest<T>(value: T) {
  const ref = useRef(value);
  useLayoutEffect(() => { ref.current = value; });
  return ref;
}
export function useEventCallback<A extends unknown[], R>(fn: (...a: A) => R) {
  const ref = useLatest(fn);
  return useCallback((...args: A) => ref.current(...args), [ref]);
}
```
*Edge cases:* writing during render is a bug — write after commit; stable identity is the whole point.

### 3. `usePrevious`
```tsx
export function usePrevious<T>(value: T) {
  const ref = useRef<T | undefined>(undefined);
  useEffect(() => { ref.current = value; }, [value]);
  return ref.current;
}
```

### 4. Shallow equality hook (the basis of `memo`)
```ts
export function shallowEqual(a: Record<string, unknown>, b: Record<string, unknown>): boolean {
  if (Object.is(a, b)) return true;
  if (typeof a !== 'object' || a === null || typeof b !== 'object' || b === null) return false;
  const ka = Object.keys(a), kb = Object.keys(b);
  if (ka.length !== kb.length) return false;
  return ka.every(k => Object.prototype.hasOwnProperty.call(b, k) && Object.is(a[k], b[k]));
}
```

### 5. `useMediaQuery` (SSR-safe, via `useSyncExternalStore`)
```tsx
export function useMediaQuery(query: string): boolean {
  const subscribe = useCallback((cb: () => void) => {
    const mql = window.matchMedia(query);
    mql.addEventListener('change', cb);
    return () => mql.removeEventListener('change', cb);
  }, [query]);
  return useSyncExternalStore(
    subscribe,
    () => window.matchMedia(query).matches,          // client snapshot
    () => false                                       // server snapshot
  );
}
```

### 6. `useOnClickOutside`
```tsx
export function useOnClickOutside<T extends HTMLElement>(handler: (e: MouseEvent | TouchEvent) => void, enabled = true) {
  const ref = useRef<T>(null);
  const handlerRef = useLatest(handler);
  useEffect(() => {
    if (!enabled) return;
    const listener = (e: MouseEvent | TouchEvent) => {
      if (!ref.current || ref.current.contains(e.target as Node)) return;
      handlerRef.current(e);
    };
    document.addEventListener('mousedown', listener, true);
    document.addEventListener('touchstart', listener, true);
    return () => {
      document.removeEventListener('mousedown', listener, true);
      document.removeEventListener('touchstart', listener, true);
    };
  }, [enabled, handlerRef]);
  return ref;
}
```
*Edge case:* `capture: true` so a handler that stops propagation elsewhere cannot hide the click from you.

### 7. `useIntersection` with `once`
```tsx
export function useIntersection<T extends Element>(opts: IntersectionObserverInit = {}, once = false) {
  const ref = useRef<T>(null);
  const [entry, setEntry] = useState<IntersectionObserverEntry | null>(null);
  useEffect(() => {
    const el = ref.current;
    if (!el || typeof IntersectionObserver === 'undefined') return;
    const io = new IntersectionObserver(([e]) => {
      setEntry(e);
      if (once && e.isIntersecting) io.disconnect();
    }, opts);
    io.observe(el);
    return () => io.disconnect();
  }, [opts.root, opts.rootMargin, String(opts.threshold), once]);  // primitives to avoid re-subscribing
  return [ref, entry] as const;
}
```
*Edge case:* an options object literal in the deps re-subscribes every render — depend on primitives.

### 8. `useIsMounted` / `useIsomorphicLayoutEffect`
```ts
export const useIsomorphicLayoutEffect = typeof window !== 'undefined' ? useLayoutEffect : useEffect;
export function useIsMounted() {
  const mounted = useRef(false);
  useEffect(() => { mounted.current = true; return () => { mounted.current = false; }; }, []);
  return useCallback(() => mounted.current, []);
}
```

### 9. `useToggle` with functional semantics
```ts
export function useToggle(initial = false) {
  const [on, setOn] = useState(initial);
  const toggle = useCallback(() => setOn(o => !o), []);
  return [on, toggle, setOn] as const;
}
```

### 10. `useCopyToClipboard` (clipboard + fallback + feedback)
```tsx
export function useCopyToClipboard(timeoutMs = 2000) {
  const [copied, setCopied] = useState(false);
  const copy = useEventCallback(async (text: string) => {
    try {
      if (navigator.clipboard?.writeText) await navigator.clipboard.writeText(text);
      else {
        const ta = document.createElement('textarea');
        ta.value = text; ta.setAttribute('readonly', ''); ta.style.position = 'absolute'; ta.style.left = '-9999px';
        document.body.appendChild(ta); ta.select();
        const ok = document.execCommand('copy');
        document.body.removeChild(ta);
        if (!ok) throw new Error('copy failed');
      }
      setCopied(true);
      setTimeout(() => setCopied(false), timeoutMs);
      return true;
    } catch { return false; }
  });
  return { copied, copy };
}
```

### 11. A normalized "store" hook with selectors and referential stability
```tsx
type State = { users: Record<string, User>; ids: string[] };
function useUsersStore(initial: User[]) {
  const [state, setState] = useState<State>(() => normalize(initial));
  const selectById = useCallback((id: string) => state.users[id], [state.users]);
  const add = useEventCallback((u: User) => setState(s => ({ users: { ...s.users, [u.id]: u }, ids: [...s.ids, u.id] })));
  const remove = useEventCallback((id: string) => setState(s => {
    const users = { ...s.users }; delete users[id];
    return { users, ids: s.ids.filter(x => x !== id) };
  }));
  return { state, selectById, add, remove };
}
```
*Lesson:* normalized state + stable selectors is what makes granular re-renders possible (5.3.6 / 4.3).

### 12. A tiny event-driven store (Redux-shaped, ~25 lines)
```ts
type Listener = () => void;
export function createStore<S, A extends { type: string }>(reducer: (s: S, a: A) => S, initial: S) {
  let state = initial;
  let listeners: Listener[] = [];
  const getState = () => state;
  const dispatch = (a: A) => { state = reducer(state, a); listeners.forEach(l => l()); };
  const subscribe = (l: Listener) => {
    listeners.push(l);
    return () => { listeners = listeners.filter(x => x !== l); };
  };
  return { getState, dispatch, subscribe };
}

// component binding (what Redux's useSelector does)
export function useStoreSelector<S, T>(store: ReturnType<typeof createStore<S, any>>, select: (s: S) => T): T {
  const [, force] = useReducer(c => c + 1, 0);
  const selected = select(store.getState());
  const selectedRef = useRef(selected);
  useEffect(() => store.subscribe(() => {
    const next = select(store.getState());
    if (!Object.is(next, selectedRef.current)) { selectedRef.current = next; force(); }
  }), [store, select]);
  return selected;
}
```
*Edge cases:* unsubscribe on unmount; selector identity stability; no tearing thanks to reading during render and re-checking after commit.

---

# 4.1.23 RENDER/EFFECT TRACE DRILLS (20)
Predict the console order, the number of renders per component, and the number of effect invocations, before running.

**Setup for most drills:** a `Parent` with `const [n, setN] = useState(0)` rendering `<Child label={`n=${n}`} />`, plus `<Console name="..." />` with `console.log` in the component body and `useEffect(() => { console.log('mount'); return () => console.log('cleanup'); }, deps)`.

**Drill 1** — initial mount only. Expected: `Child render`, `Child effect mount`. Order of effects is child-before-parent (children's effects run first on mount).
**Drill 2** — `setN(1)` in a click handler. Expected: `Child render` (once), then effect cleanup + effect if deps changed.
**Drill 3** — `setN(n + 1)` twice in the same handler. Expected: **one** render with `n=1` (batched + equal value).
**Drill 4** — `setN(n + 1)` then `setN(p => p + 1)` in the same handler. Expected: one render with `n=2`.
**Drill 5** — `Child` has `useEffect(..., [])` and the parent re-renders 5 times. Expected: child renders 6 times, effect runs once (plus twice in StrictMode dev).
**Drill 6** — `useEffect(..., [n])` and parent re-renders without changing `n`. Expected: effect does not re-run (deps compared to last committed render).
**Drill 7** — parent passes `style={{color:'red'}}` (new object each render) to a `memo` child. Expected: memo child re-renders every parent render.
**Drill 8** — provider with `value={{a:1}}` and a memoized consumer. Expected: consumer re-renders every provider render despite `memo`.
**Drill 9** — two effects in one component: does effect 1 run before effect 2? Yes, in declaration order. Does cleanup run in declaration order too? Yes.
**Drill 10** — an effect that sets state unconditionally with `[]`. Expected: infinite loop; StrictMode makes it obvious. Fix with a functional update guarded by a previous-value ref, or derive instead.
**Drill 11** — `useMemo(() => console.log('compute'), [n])` with `n` set to the same value. Expected: `compute` runs once; `setN(same)` bails out so no render, so no recompute.
**Drill 12** — `useRef` mutation during render. Expected: value is written twice in StrictMode and reads are inconsistent if render is retried — an impure render.
**Drill 13** — conditional `return null` before a hook. Expected: "Rendered fewer hooks than expected" error. Fix: early return *after* all hooks.
**Drill 14** — a component that unmounts while its fetch is in flight, no abort. Expected: a React 18+ "no-op" update plus a possible memory leak of the closure; the classic fix is `AbortController` in cleanup.
**Drill 15** — `key` changes from `"a"` to `"b"` on the same element type. Expected: unmount + fresh mount (state reset, effects re-run) even though the type is identical.
**Drill 16** — changing a component's `type` (a ternary between two different components). Expected: full remount, state lost.
**Drill 17** — a `Suspense` boundary whose child throws a promise; parent re-renders. Expected: the fallback stays visible and no new commit happens until the promise resolves; the transition version keeps the old UI instead.
**Drill 18** — two sibling effects both adding a `window` listener with different handlers; cleanup correctness. Expected: both removed on unmount; if one lacks cleanup, the handler survives.
**Drill 19** — `useLayoutEffect` that reads layout and sets state. Expected: the state update happens before paint, so no flash; with `useEffect` you'd see one.
**Drill 20** — a context provider whose value is `useMemo`'d over `[user]` where `user` is fetched. Expected: three renders (loading → user → children), and consumers only re-render when `user` changes.

**Answers to the trickiest ones:**
- **Q13 fix:** all hooks must run on every render, so put the early return after the hook calls, or split the component.
- **Q15 vs Q16:** both remount. Keys express *identity* across a list; type changes express *different* components. Both destroy state, which is why "use a key to reset" is the idiomatic fix for "reset state when the prop changes".

---

# 4.1.24 REACT INTERVIEW Q&A (32 questions with model answers)

**Q1. Explain how React decides to re-render, and what does NOT cause a re-render.**
Model answer: "A re-render happens when React has work for a fiber: a state update on it, a new props object from a parent re-render, a changed context value read by it, or an external store change. Mutating state in place does not cause one, because `Object.is` sees the same reference; effects do not cause renders; and a `useMemo` cache miss is not itself a render."

**Q2. Why does my component render twice in development?**
Model answer: "StrictMode deliberately double-invokes render and re-runs effects in development to surface impure renders and missing cleanups. It's not a production behaviour, so if double-invoking breaks something, the underlying side effect should be idempotent and abortable rather than guarded with a 'has mounted' ref."

**Q3. `useEffect` vs `useLayoutEffect` — when each?**
Model answer: "`useLayoutEffect` runs after DOM mutation and before paint; use it when you must read layout and synchronously correct it, or when a visual jump would be visible. `useEffect` runs after paint; use it for subscriptions, logging, analytics, and anything not paint-critical. Because layout effects block paint, they must be fast, and they warn during SSR."

**Q4. What is a stale closure and how do I fix it?**
Model answer: "A callback created in an earlier render captures that render's variables. Fix by (a) functional state updates when you only write, (b) a ref holding the latest value when you must read, (c) including the value in deps and re-subscribing when re-subscription is cheap, or (d) moving the logic into an event handler so it doesn't need to survive."

**Q5. When do you NOT need `useEffect`?**
Model answer: "When the work is derivable from props/state (compute during render), when it's caused by a user interaction (put it in the handler), when it's a prop change that should reset state (use a `key`), or when it's a failed request (handle the error where it happens). Effects are for external systems: subscriptions, timers, the DOM, the network."

**Q6. Why doesn't `memo` stop the re-render?**
Model answer: "Most often the props aren't shallow-equal — an inline object, array, or arrow function. Other causes: the component reads a context whose value changed, there's a pending state update inside it, its type changed, or it's being remounted because a `key` changed."

**Q7. Is `useMemo` worth it?**
Model answer: "Only when the computation is measurably expensive or when the identity is a contract (a dependency, a context value, a store selector). Memoization itself costs an array allocation, a comparison, and retained memory; React's guidance is to measure and memoize deliberately."

**Q8. Why does my context consumer re-render constantly?**
Model answer: "Because the provider's `value` is a new object on every render, so `Object.is` fails for every consumer, and context propagation bypasses `memo`. Fix by splitting contexts by update frequency, separating the dispatch from the state, memoizing the value, or moving to a store with per-slice selectors."

**Q9. What's the difference between `key` and `id`?**
Model answer: "`key` participates in reconciliation and tells React which previous element a new element is; it must be stable and unique among siblings. `id` is just an HTML attribute for the DOM, links, labels and accessibility, and has no effect on React."

**Q10. Why does my list lose input values when I reorder?**
Model answer: "Index keys. React matched fibers by position, so after reordering it reused the DOM nodes (and their uncontrolled values) for different items. Key by a stable data id."

**Q11. How do I avoid an effect running on every render?**
Model answer: "One of the deps is a new object/array/function every render. Depend on primitives, `useMemo` the object, or restructure so the effect doesn't need it. Also check that the effect isn't setting state unconditionally."

**Q12. Explain concurrent rendering and `useTransition`.**
Model answer: "Concurrent React lets rendering be interrupted, resumed, or discarded, and lets updates be prioritized. `useTransition` marks state updates as non-urgent, so React renders the urgent update first and can throw away the transition's work if a newer update arrives. `isPending` is true until the transition commits, so it gives you a real pending flag. Transitions don't make work cheaper in total — they reorder it and allow wasted work to be discarded."

**Q13. `useDeferredValue` vs `useTransition`?**
Model answer: "`useTransition` is for state you own — you wrap the expensive update and get a pending flag. `useDeferredValue` is for a value you don't own (a prop, store value, URL param) and defers the expensive rendering of everything derived from it. Use both when you control an input and also render expensive content from it."

**Q14. Why can't an error boundary be a function component?**
Model answer: "Because React implements error boundaries by catching throws during render, and it identifies a boundary by class lifecycle methods (`getDerivedStateFromError` and `componentDidCatch`). There's no hook equivalent; React 19 still requires a class component, so frameworks like Next.js provide their own boundary components."

**Q15. What does an error boundary NOT catch?**
Model answer: "Event handler errors, errors in `setTimeout` or unhandled promise rejections, errors in the boundary itself, and SSR errors. Those need `try/catch` at the call site plus reporting."

**Q16. How do I measure whether my optimization worked?**
Model answer: "With the React DevTools Profiler: record the interaction before and after, compare commit counts and durations, enable 'record why each component rendered', and confirm the memoized subtree no longer appears in commits. Also check field metrics (INP) for real-user impact, and remember that lab numbers don't replace RUM."

**Q17. Why is `key={Math.random()()` a terrible idea?**
Model answer: "A new key every render means every child unmounts and remounts each render: state is destroyed, effects re-run, animations restart, focus is lost, and it's slow. If you need a stable id per item instance, create it once in a `useState` initializer or derive it from data."

**Q18. What's the right way to fetch data in a component?**
Model answer: "In production, use a data layer with caching, dedupe, retries, invalidation and staleness (TanStack Query or your framework's server-side fetching), because hand-rolled effects reimplement all of it badly. In an interview or a tiny component, an effect with `AbortController` cleanup is acceptable — and you should say so."

**Q19. What's `StrictMode` for, and should it ship to production?**
Model answer: "It surfaces unsafe patterns in development by double-invoking render and re-running effects. It is a no-op in production builds, so there's no cost; keep it on in development always."

**Q20. Explain hydration and how to avoid mismatch.**
Model answer: "SSR sends HTML produced on the server; the client must produce matching output on first render. Mismatches come from time/locale/randomness, direct browser API reads in render, invalid HTML nesting, or client-only conditionals. Fix by making the first client render deterministic and deferring browser-only reads to effects. `suppressHydrationWarning` is only for a single unavoidable text node."

**Q21. How do you share state between two sibling components that are far apart?**
Model answer: "In order of preference: lift state to the nearest common ancestor, use a custom hook if both need the same logic, use context if the value changes rarely, and use a store if the value changes often or many components need slices. Do not reach for a global store before trying lifting — it usually makes the data flow harder to follow."

**Q22. What's the difference between a controlled and uncontrolled component, and when do you care?**
Model answer: "Controlled means React owns the value; you can validate, format, and reset it programmatically, at the cost of a render per keystroke. Uncontrolled means the DOM owns it; reads happen at submit time, resets are trivial, and it's much faster for large forms. Hybrid: uncontrolled fields plus a controlled subset for live feedback."

**Q23. Why does my effect cause an infinite loop even though I have a dependency array?**
Model answer: "Because a dependency's identity changes every render — usually an object or array literal, or a value you set inside the effect. Or because the effect sets state unconditionally. Fix by depending on primitives, memoizing objects, using functional updates, or deriving the value instead."

**Q24. What's the deal with `React.memo` and context?**
Model answer: "`memo` compares props; context propagation is separate. When a provider's value changes, React marks consumers for update regardless of their props, so `memo` doesn't protect them. Splitting the provider into multiple contexts, or memoizing/splitting the value, is the fix."

**Q25. Explain portals and one surprising behaviour.**
Model answer: "A portal renders children into a different DOM node while keeping them in the React tree, which is how modals escape `overflow: hidden` and stacking contexts. The surprise: React events bubble according to the React tree, so a click inside a portal bubbles to React ancestors that are not DOM ancestors — which breaks naive outside-click handlers."

**Q26. When would you use `useReducer` over `useState`?**
Model answer: "When transitions depend on previous state in several places, when you want named transitions for logging and testing, when related state must change together, or when modelling a state machine with explicit statuses (which removes impossible states). Also when you want to unit-test transitions without rendering."

**Q27. What causes a "Maximum update depth exceeded" error?**
Model answer: "Usually a `setState` during render (directly or in a `useMemo`/reducer), a `useEffect` that sets state unconditionally, or a dependency that changes every render. The fix is to derive instead of synchronizing, use functional updates, or stabilize the dependency."

**Q28. How do you make a large list performant?**
Model answer: "In order: reduce the data (pagination/filters), reduce per-row cost (memoized rows, stable props, fixed row height, CSS containment), and virtualize so only the visible window is in the DOM. Then measure with the Profiler and long-task/INP data."

**Q29. What's `useId` for and what is it not for?**
Model answer: "It's for stable, hydration-safe DOM ids: `htmlFor`, `aria-describedby`, `aria-controls`, SVG references. It is not for data keys (it changes if the component moves) and not for anything you persist, because it changes across renders and sessions."

**Q30. How would you decide between Context and a state library?**
Model answer: "Context for low-churn services and rarely-changing config (theme, locale, router, a dispatch function). A store (Zustand/Redux/Jotai) when state changes often and many components need different slices, when you need devtools/time-travel, or when you need actions/middleware. Measure render counts before and after either choice."

**Q31. Explain the Rules of Hooks and why ESLint enforces them.**
Model answer: "Hooks rely on stable call order because React stores hook state on the fiber's hook linked list, keyed positionally. Conditional, early-return-before-hook, or looped calls corrupt that mapping — React detects a mismatch in dev, but nested hooks in loops silently break ordering guarantees. ESLint's plugin statically enforces the rule."

**Q32. What's the hardest React bug you've had to diagnose, and how did you solve it?**
*(Candidate-specific. Strong answers describe symptoms → narrowing → tooling → root cause → prevention.)* Example frame: "Users reported the cart total flickering. I used the Profiler's 'why did this render' to find a context value recreated every render, which forced every consumer to re-render, and one consumer derived a total with an O(n) reduce in render. Fixing the context split plus memoizing the derived value removed the flicker. I added a render-count assertion in a test to prevent regression."
