# 4.1.25 MACHINE-CODING PROBLEMS (with full solutions)

### Problem 1 — Accessible Modal with focus trap, portal, and restore
Requirements: opens on trigger click, `Esc` closes, backdrop click closes, focus moves in on open, Tab is trapped, focus returns to the trigger, body scroll locked without layout shift, announced via `aria-modal` + labelled title, and it works when the trigger is inside a scrollable container.
Key points: portal to `document.body`; `useLayoutEffect` for focus/scroll work so there's no flash; restore everything in cleanup; guard `document` for SSR.
*Rubric:* focus management 2, keyboard correctness 2, cleanup/restore 2, scroll lock without CLS 1, SSR guard 1.

### Problem 2 — Typeahead autocomplete
Requirements: debounced (250ms) fetch, cancellation with `AbortController`, stale-response protection, keyboard navigation (ArrowUp/Down/Enter/Escape/Home/End), `aria-activedescendant` + `role="combobox"`/`listbox`/`option`, loading and error states, minimum query length, and Enter selects.
Key implementation details: keep the highlighted index in state but reset it when results change; use `aria-activedescendant` with stable option ids (option ids must be stable across renders so the active descendant doesn't dangle); announce result counts with a polite live region; don't use `role="listbox"` on a `<ul>` without option semantics.
*Rubric:* cancellation/race handling 2, a11y semantics 2, keyboard behaviour 2, edge cases (empty query, no results, slow network) 2, code clarity 2.

### Problem 3 — Infinite list with virtualization
Requirements: 10,000 rows, fixed row height, only the visible window in the DOM, `IntersectionObserver` sentinel for the next page, cursor pagination, an accessible "Load more" button, and no duplicate items when a page is refetched.
Key details: `translateY` offsetting vs absolute positioning; overscan for smooth scrolling; dedupe by id when appending; `AbortController` on unmount; expose total count for screen readers via `aria-rowcount` or a text summary.
*Rubric:* virtualization math 2, scroll handling 1, pagination/dedupe 2, a11y 2, robustness 3.

### Problem 4 — Tabs (WAI-ARIA authoring practices)
Requirements: roving tabindex, arrow-key navigation with wrap, `Home`/`End`, automatic vs manual activation, `aria-selected`, `aria-controls`/`aria-labelledby`, and only the active panel mounted or hidden.
Key details: keyboard handling on the tablist (event delegation), tabindex managed on triggers, panels with `tabIndex={0}` so they can be scrolled, ids generated with `useId`.
*Rubric:* keyboard model 2, ARIA correctness 2, state/API design 2, focus management 1, styling/edge cases 3.

### Problem 5 — Multi-step form wizard
Requirements: 4 steps, per-step validation, back/next with confirmation, progress indicator, resumable state, and a summary step; must handle a browser refresh.
Key design: a reducer holding `{step, values, errors, status}`; validation with a shared Zod schema per step; persist to `sessionStorage` with a version key; validate on blur and on submit, not per keystroke; block navigation forward when invalid and show a focus-managed error summary.
*Rubric:* state model 3, validation 2, persistence/hydration 2, accessibility (error summary + focus) 2, UX details 1.

### Problem 6 — Virtualized list with dynamic row heights
Requirements: rows of unknown height (wrapping text), no scroll jump when content above the viewport resizes, sticky header, and correct measurement with `ResizeObserver` in `useLayoutEffect`.
Key details: measure and store per-index offsets, maintain a prefix-sum array, anchor to the item currently at the top of the viewport when offsets change, and handle the first paint of unmeasured rows with an estimate.
*Rubric:* measurement 2, anchoring 3, offsets data structure 2, performance 2, edge cases 1.

### Problem 7 — Upload queue with concurrency control
Requirements: drag-and-drop plus file picker, presigned PUT uploads, progress per file, max 3 concurrent, per-file cancel and retry, validation before upload, and a total progress bar.
Key details: `XMLHttpRequest` for upload progress events (`fetch` has no upload progress), `AbortController` for cancel, a promise-based concurrency limiter (3.3.16), immutable queue state, and stable keys.
*Rubric:* concurrency limiter 2, progress/cancel 2, validation 2, state modelling 2, UX/error handling 2.

### Problem 8 — Custom `useQuery` (mini React Query)
Requirements: cache by key, dedupe in-flight requests, stale-while-revalidate, abort on unmount/key change, retry with exponential backoff and jitter, `enabled`, and a stable `refetch`.
Grading notes: this is the single best way to internalize why a data library exists. Expect candidates to miss dedupe, abort, or backoff.
*Rubric:* caching/staleness 2, cancellation 2, retries 2, key handling 2, API ergonomics 2.

### Problem 9 — Data table with sorting, filtering, pagination and URL state
Requirements: all filter/sort/page state in the URL so it's shareable and back-button friendly; server-side pagination and sorting; debounced search that `replace`s rather than `push`es; sticky header; and 10k rows handled with virtualization.
Key details: URL as the source of truth for these concerns, `useSearchParams`, `useDeferredValue` for the search box, stable sort with a tiebreaker, and avoiding history spam.
*Rubric:* URL state design 3, async UX 2, virtualization/perf 2, a11y 3, edge cases 2.

# 4.1.26 REAL-WORLD DEBUGGING SCENARIOS (12)
Each: **Symptoms → Likely causes → Investigation → Tools → Fix → Prevention.**

1. **"The page re-renders 20 times when I open it."** Likely: state mirroring props in an effect, unstable context value, a store selector returning a new object, an effect setting state unconditionally. Investigate: Profiler "why did this render", count commits. Fix: derive instead of sync, memoize/split context, memoize the selector output. Prevention: a render-count test for critical components, lint rule.
2. **"Infinite loop of renders."** Likely: `setState` in render, effect without a guard, dep identity changing every render. Investigate: the error boundary stack, React's "Maximum update depth" message. Fix: functional update + previous-value ref, or derive. Prevention: exhaustive-deps lint, review rule against setState in render.
3. **"Input loses focus on every keystroke."** Likely: the input is being remounted — a changing `key`, a conditionally-rendered parent, or an inline component type (`const Input = () => ...` inside another component). Investigate: React DevTools highlight update to see if the node is replaced. Fix: hoist the component type, use a stable key. Prevention: ESLint rules that flag components defined inside components.
4. **"The modal closes when I click inside it."** Likely: a backdrop `onClick` firing for inner clicks (check `e.target === e.currentTarget`), or a portal bubbling click to an outside-click handler in the React tree. Fix: target check or `stopPropagation` inside the portal. Prevention: shared `useOnClickOutside` hook with documented portal caveat.
5. **"Effect runs on every keystroke and re-subscribes."** Likely: an unstable dep (object/array/function literal). Fix: primitives as deps, `useMemo` the object, `useEventCallback` for handlers. Prevention: exhaustive-deps lint, review checklist.
6. **"Data fetched twice for the same query."** Likely: missing dedupe in a custom hook, two effects (StrictMode double-invoke revealing no abort), or two components each fetching the same thing. Fix: a query layer with in-flight dedupe, or lift the fetch. Prevention: contract test asserting one network request.
7. **"The app is janky while typing in a large list."** Likely: filtering a 10k array per keystroke, unmemoized rows, layout thrashing from measurement inside a render-time loop. Fix: `useDeferredValue` or debounce, memoize the row and the filter, virtualize, batch reads/writes. Prevention: INP budget and a perf test in CI.
8. **"State resets when navigating between two users' detail pages."** Likely: no `key` on the component that should reset when the route param changes. Fix: `key={id}`. Prevention: a test asserting state resets on id change.
9. **"LocalStorage-persisted state flashes the wrong theme."** Likely: reading storage during render causes hydration mismatch; fix: read in an effect, or initialize from an inline script in the document head to avoid flash. Prevention: SSR-safe persistence hooks.
10. **"Memory grows every time I open a route with a chart."** Likely: a subscription without cleanup, an event listener not removed, a library instance not destroyed, or a growing cache keyed by request. Fix: return cleanups, dispose instances, bound caches. Prevention: heap snapshot in a regression test.
11. **"Context consumers re-render even though nothing changed."** Likely: new value object each render, or one provider holding high-churn state. Fix: split contexts, memoize value, move state to a store with selectors. Prevention: render-count assertions.
12. **"Optimistic UI shows the wrong thing after a failed save."** Likely: rollback isn't restoring the previous snapshot, or the cache key doesn't match, or the error path forgets to invalidate. Fix: use the data library's mutation lifecycle (`onMutate` snapshot, `onError` rollback, `onSettled` invalidate). Prevention: test the failure path explicitly.

# 4.1.27 REACT RAPID-FIRE (50)

| # | Question | Key points |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | What is a re-render vs a re-mount? | Re-render re-invokes the function and keeps state; re-mount destroys state and refires effects |
| 2 | What are the phases? | Render (interruptible) then commit (DOM + layout effects) then passive effects |
| 3 | Can render be interrupted? | Yes, concurrent mode, between fibers, only in the render phase |
| 4 | Can a render be discarded? | Yes, that's how transitions avoid wasted work |
| 5 | What makes render impure? | Side effects, mutation, randomness, time, network in render |
| 6 | What does StrictMode double-invoke? | Render, effects, and reducers in development only |
| 7 | Why did my effect run twice? | StrictMode dev remount, or a changing dependency |
| 8 | Does `[]` mean "mount only"? | It means no deps change, so run after the first commit; cleanup on unmount |
| 9 | What's a cleanup for? | Undo, reset, and race protection |
| 10 | Can cleanup be async? | It can't be awaited; use an `AbortController` or explicit async cleanup pattern |
| 11 | When is `useLayoutEffect` right? | Measure-then-correct before paint; never for async work |
| 12 | Does `useLayoutEffect` work in SSR? | It warns; use `useIsomorphicLayoutEffect` or gate it |
| 13 | What is the batching rule in React 18? | All updates batch, including in promises, timeouts and native handlers |
| 14 | How do you opt out of batching? | `flushSync` (rarely) |
| 15 | Why `useState(() => fn())`? | Lazy init runs once; `useState(fn())` runs every render |
| 16 | What equality does state use? | `Object.is` |
| 17 | Can I mutate state? | It breaks the bail-out and change detection; use immutability |
| 18 | What's the "adjust state during render" pattern? | Compare with a previous-value state and conditionally `setState`; React re-runs immediately |
| 19 | Keys must be… | Stable, unique among siblings, consistent type |
| 20 | Why index keys break | They match by position, so reorders reuse the wrong fibers |
| 21 | Do keys need to be unique globally? | No, only among siblings |
| 22 | `key` vs `id`? | Reconciliation identity vs DOM/label attribute |
| 23 | What does `memo` compare? | Props shallowly with `Object.is`, plus type and context |
| 24 | Does `memo` stop a parent's render? | No; the parent still renders and creates an element; `memo` skips the child's render |
| 25 | Does `memo` protect from context? | No; context propagation bypasses it |
| 26 | `useMemo` vs `useCallback`? | Value vs function identity; the latter is sugar for the former |
| 27 | Does `useMemo` guarantee stability? | No, it's a performance hint; React may discard the cache |
| 28 | Cost of memoization? | Deps array, comparisons, retained memory, correctness risk |
| 29 | When should you memoize? | After measuring, on expensive compute or identity contracts |
| 30 | What does a context change propagate? | Every consumer below the provider, ignoring `memo` |
| 31 | How do you fix a context perf problem? | Split contexts, memoize value, separate dispatch, or move to a store |
| 32 | What is `useReducer`'s `dispatch`? | Stable for the component's lifetime |
| 33 | Why must reducers be pure? | Render can re-run; impure reducers break replayability |
| 34 | What is `useSyncExternalStore` for? | Subscribing to mutable external stores without tearing |
| 35 | What does `useImperativeHandle` do? | Shapes what a parent receives from a ref |
| 36 | Why expose commands not data through refs? | A ref handle must not become a second source of truth |
| 37 | What are portals for? | Rendering outside the parent's DOM box (modals, tooltips) |
| 38 | Portal event gotcha? | Events bubble through the React tree, not the DOM tree |
| 39 | Error boundaries catch… | Render, lifecycle, and constructor errors in descendants |
| 40 | They don't catch… | Event handlers, async callbacks, themselves, SSR |
| 41 | Suspense needs… | A nearest boundary, and a transition to avoid a fallback flash |
| 42 | Suspense is for… | Slow work that must not block interaction, not every fetch |
| 43 | `useTransition` gives you… | Non-urgent updates plus a reliable `isPending` |
| 44 | `useDeferredValue` is for… | Values you don't own whose derived render should be deferrable |
| 45 | `useId` is for… | Hydration-safe DOM ids; never for data keys |
| 46 | `useRef` doesn't trigger… | A render |
| 47 | Where can you write `ref.current`? | After commit (layout or passive effect), never during render |
| 48 | What is a stale closure? | A callback reading an older render's values |
| 49 | Three stale-closure fixes? | Functional update, latest-value ref, re-subscribe on dep change |
| 50 | React 19's headline features? | Actions, `use`, `useOptimistic`, `useFormStatus`, ref as prop, metadata hoisting, Compiler |

# 4.1.28 REACT FINAL READINESS CHECKLIST

**Rendering model**
- [ ] I can describe render phase vs commit phase and what can be interrupted or discarded
- [ ] I can explain reconciliation rules and why type changes force a remount
- [ ] I can describe what a Fiber is and what it enables
- [ ] I can explain why "the virtual DOM" isn't the reason React is fast

**Re-render triggers**
- [ ] I can list every cause of a re-render and every non-cause
- [ ] I can explain `Object.is` bail-out and why one extra render can still happen
- [ ] I can use the Profiler's "why did this render" and read a commit
- [ ] I can explain why unmemoized children re-render with the parent

**State**
- [ ] I can explain state as a snapshot, batching, and functional updates
- [ ] I can explain lazy initial state and why `useState(fn())` is a bug
- [ ] I can describe the update queue and how an early bail-out works
- [ ] I can model async state as a discriminated union rather than booleans

**Effects**
- [ ] I can explain the "sync with external systems" mental model
- [ ] I can write cleanup for subscriptions, timers, observers and requests
- [ ] I can explain `[]` vs omitted vs `[a,b]` precisely
- [ ] I can name four "you might not need an effect" refactors and their fixes
- [ ] I can explain the three most common infinite-effect causes
- [ ] I know when `useLayoutEffect` is required and why it warns in SSR

**Refs and closures**
- [ ] I can list the legitimate uses of `useRef` and the illegitimate ones
- [ ] I can explain why writing `ref.current` during render is a bug
- [ ] I can fix a stale closure three different ways and justify the choice

**Memoization**
- [ ] I can explain `memo`, `useMemo`, `useCallback` and their separate costs
- [ ] I can list every reason a `memo` component still re-renders
- [ ] I can measure before and after memoizing and report numbers
- [ ] I can explain the "memo is a performance hint, not a guarantee" nuance

**Context and state management**
- [ ] I can explain how a context value change propagates and why `memo` doesn't help
- [ ] I can split contexts by update frequency and by read/write
- [ ] I can justify Context vs a store for a given requirement

**Concurrent React**
- [ ] I can explain lanes, priority, and why an input must stay urgent
- [ ] I can use `useTransition` and `useDeferredValue` and explain the difference
- [ ] I can explain why Suspense + transitions avoid spinners

**Patterns**
- [ ] I can pick controlled vs uncontrolled and justify with form size
- [ ] I can build a compound component with a throwing context hook
- [ ] I can build an accessible modal with focus trap and restore
- [ ] I can virtualize a list and name its accessibility trade-offs

**React 19 and beyond**
- [ ] I can write a form Action with `useActionState` and `useFormStatus`
- [ ] I can use `use` with a stable promise and with context
- [ ] I can implement an optimistic update with `useOptimistic`
- [ ] I can explain what React Compiler automates and what it does not fix

**Debugging**
- [ ] I can diagnose an infinite render loop from the error stack
- [ ] I can diagnose a remount loop (unstable key or component type)
- [ ] I can find a memory leak with a heap snapshot and name the retained objects
- [ ] I can fix a hydration mismatch properly rather than suppressing it

# PHASE 4, PART 2: Next.js and State Management

Everything in this file assumes you have finished 4.1 (React deep dive: rendering, hooks, Context, Suspense, `useTransition`). Cross-reference rather than re-learn: `canonical entry: 4.1.5 Reconciliation and Re-render Cost`, `canonical entry: 4.1.14 Context`, `canonical entry: PHASE 6 → Caching Layers → Rate Limiting`.

# 4.2 Next.js and Meta-Frameworks (Advanced)

Intermediate to Advanced · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.1

**What a meta-framework actually is.** React is a UI library; it ships no router, no server, no data cache, no build-time code splitting strategy. A meta-framework fills those in with *file-system conventions* so that the framework can make decisions at build time and request time (what to render on the server, what to prerender, what to cache, where the routes are). React Router 7 framework mode, Remix, Astro, Nuxt, SvelteKit, and Next.js all do this with different trade-offs. Interviewers use "meta-framework" to test whether you understand the category, not whether you can recite one vendor's docs.

**Why interviewers ask this section.** Rendering strategy decisions (static vs dynamic vs ISR), the server/client boundary, mutation security, and cache invalidation are the four highest-signal questions in any full-stack frontend interview, because they are the decisions that produce real user-facing bugs: stale pages, flash-of-wrong-content, leaked data, double charges.

**Version note.** This section describes the App Router and the caching model of Next.js 15+ where `fetch` is not cached by default, plus the newer `'use cache'` / Cache Components direction. Defaults have changed more than once. Treat the official Next.js docs (App Router pages, `fetch` API page, `use cache` directive page, upgrading guides) as the source of truth before you assert a default in an interview.

---

## 4.2.1 App Router vs Pages Router

**Definition:** The App Router (introduced in Next 13, default since 14) is a server-first, nested router built on React Server Components; the Pages Router is the legacy `getServerSideProps` / `getStaticProps` router.

**Why interviewers ask:** Almost every React developer has shipped on one of them, but few can articulate *why* the App Router changed data fetching, caching, and layout nesting. It also tests whether you can migrate a codebase rather than just scaffold one.

### Differences that matter

| Concern | Pages Router (`pages/`) | App Router (`app/`) |
|---|---|---|
| Component default | Client component; `ReactDOM.hydrateRoot` on the whole page | Server component by default; opt into client with `'use client'` |
| Rendering model | Page-level only: one page decides the whole route's render mode | Route segment level; each segment decides static/dynamic and streams independently |
| Data fetching | `getServerSideProps` / `getStaticProps` / `getStaticPaths` — framework-run functions | `async` Server Components read data directly; `fetch` and `cache()` are cached by rules, not by function name |
| Caching control | Return `revalidate` from `getStaticProps`; use `revalidate: 0` | `export const revalidate`, `dynamic = 'force-dynamic'`, `revalidatePath`, `revalidateTag`, `unstable_cache`, `'use cache'` |
| Mutations | API routes + your own client fetch | Server Actions (function-level POST) usable directly from forms |
| Layout nesting | One `_app` + one `_document` per app; per-page layout via the page component | `layout.tsx` persists per segment; nested layouts compose freely; `template.tsx` remounts |
| Streaming | Opt-in, coarse (`defer` in React 18 for `_app`) | Built in via `<Suspense>` and `loading.tsx`, with out-of-order completion |
| Error handling | `_error.js` per page subtree | `error.tsx` + `global-error.tsx` per segment, plus `not-found.tsx` |
| Middleware | `middleware.ts` supported | `middleware.ts` supported (single file, broader matcher semantics) |
| Server-only code | Needs `getServerSideProps` | Any module only imported by Server Components is never shipped to the browser |
| Metadata | `next/head` imperative | `export const metadata` / `generateMetadata`, streaming metadata |
| Runtime selection | Node only | `runtime = 'edge'` per route or segment |
| Conventions | `_app`, `_document`, `_error`, `api/` | `layout`, `page`, `loading`, `error`, `template`, `not-found`, `route`, `default`, middleware, `global-error` |

### Layout nesting: the architectural difference

Pages Router nests only one level practically: you replicate per-page wrappers by hand.

```tsx
// pages/dashboard/settings/_layout.tsx (manual, leaks into the URL scheme)
import type { ReactNode } from 'react';
export default function SettingsLayout({ children }: { children: ReactNode }) {
  return <div className="grid"><Sidebar /><main>{children}</main></div>;
}
```

App Router nests arbitrarily, and each level is a *persistence boundary*: a layout does not re-render when a child page navigates.

```
app/
  layout.tsx                 // <html><body> + global providers        (persists forever)
  template.tsx               // re-mounts on every navigation
  providers.tsx              // 'use client' — theme, query client
  (marketing)/layout.tsx     // public shell, no nav
    page.tsx
  dashboard/
    layout.tsx               // authed shell with <Sidebar/>              (persists across /dashboard/*)
    loading.tsx              // skeleton for this segment and below
    error.tsx                // catches errors from children only
    template.tsx             // fresh mount per route change (e.g. for animations)
    page.tsx                 // /dashboard
    settings/
      layout.tsx             // tabs sub-shell
      page.tsx               // /dashboard/settings
      [[...slug]]/page.tsx   // catch-all /dashboard/settings/anything
    @analytics/              // parallel route slot (renders beside children)
      default.tsx            // required fallback for the slot
    (modal)/photo/[id]/page.tsx
```

**Parallel routes and intercepting routes** are how you get "open a modal over the list without losing the list": `@modal` renders in parallel with `children`, and `(.)` intercepting conventions match the same segment on a soft navigation. Nobody asks for the full syntax; ask *why* it exists and explain that the background route keeps its state.

### Migration path that works

1. **Do not migrate by hand.** Use the official codemods (`npx @next/codemod@latest next-async-request-api .`, plus `app-dir` codemod). They handle `getInitialProps`, `_app`, `_document`, and the async request-API changes (`getServerSideProps`/`getStaticProps` params are now promises).
2. **Migrate leaf routes first.** No parents, no nested layouts, low traffic.
3. **Keep a single `app/` route group per conversion** so both routers can coexist. Next supports having `pages/` and `app/` simultaneously; a conflict on the same URL is a build error.
4. **Replace data functions with fetches.** `getServerSideProps` → `await fetch` in the page (add `cache: 'no-store'` for the old dynamic behaviour, or set `export const dynamic = 'force-dynamic'`). `getStaticProps` → static by default plus `generateStaticParams` for dynamic segments and `revalidate` for the old ISR number.
5. **Replace `_app` with a client `providers.tsx`.** Anything that used `useRouter().query` / `router.pathname` / `router.asPath` needs the `'use client'` `usePathname`, `useSearchParams`, `useParams` hooks. This is where migrations stall: `router.query` was empty on statically rendered pages, and the replacements are not equivalent.
6. **Replace API-route mutations with Server Actions** where the caller is your own UI.
7. **Audit caching.** A page that was always dynamic under Pages Router is now static by default. That is not a refactor bug, it is a behaviour change you must find with the Cache Debugger or the response headers.

```tsx
// app/posts/[id]/page.tsx
export default async function Page({ params }: { params: Promise<{ id: string }> }) {
  const { id } = await params;          // Next 15: params/searchParams are async
  const post = await db.post.findUnique({ where: { id } });
  if (!post) notFound();                 // renders not-found.tsx, 404 status
  return <PostView post={post} />;       // PostView is a Server Component: no 'use client'
}
```

**Which to use now.** For a new application, the App Router. For an existing Pages Router codebase that works, do not migrate for its own sake: it is a multi-week behavioural-risk project with no user-visible gain, and the only strong reasons are needing Server Components, React 19 Actions, or PPR. Add new features in `app/` when you can, and treat the migration as an explicitly scoped epic.

**Questions**
1. What are the real differences between App Router and Pages Router?
2. Why does `router.query` behave differently from `useSearchParams`?
3. When is a layout re-rendered, and what is a `template.tsx` for?
4. What are parallel routes and intercepting routes used for?
5. What does `notFound()` do that `return null` doesn't?
6. Why did `params` become async? What breaks if you forget to await it?
7. How do you convert `getServerSideProps` into an App Router page and keep the same caching behaviour?
8. Can `app/` and `pages/` coexist?
9. Why might migrating an existing app be a bad business decision?
10. What is `_app`'s App Router equivalent, and why does it need `'use client'`?
11. How do you find which routes are still dynamic after a migration?
12. What's the difference between a route segment and a route group?

**Model answer, Q1:** "The App Router is server-first: components are Server Components by default and are rendered ahead of time, at build time, or per request on the server, and they can read data directly with `await` instead of framework-invoked data functions. Data is cached by rules — `fetch` is uncached by default in Next 15, caching is opted into — and mutations are functions marked `'use server'` rather than hand-written fetch calls in route handlers. The unit of rendering is the *segment*: nested `layout.tsx` files persist across navigation, `loading.tsx` streams a fallback, `error.tsx` catches errors from children only. The Pages Router had one `getServerSideProps` per page deciding the whole route's mode, one `_app` for providers, and `next/head` for metadata. The tradeoff is that the App Router is more powerful and more confusing: caching defaults have changed across versions, most migrations break at `router.query`, and there is no `getServerSideProps` equivalent you can grep for to audit behaviour."

**Model answer, Q2 (senior/staff framing):** *Context.* `router.query` was a property on a singleton client router; on a statically generated page it was hydrated as an empty object because the query string was never known at build time. *Decision.* Use `useSearchParams` for query params, `usePathname` for the path, `useParams` for dynamic segments. *Reasoning.* These hooks read from the current navigation state and work identically on static and dynamic routes. *Trade-offs.* All three are client-only hooks, so calling them forces the component to be a Client Component, and in a statically rendered route the values are only known on the client, which means a static page that renders different content per query parameter is now dynamic in practice — you should make it explicit with `dynamic = 'force-dynamic'` rather than rely on the emergent behaviour. *Failure scenario.* A migrated page uses `useSearchParams()` for a tab selection; the component becomes a client component, the tab state is lost on hard refresh, and SEO sees one canonical tab. *Alternative.* Put tab choice in a path segment (`/settings/billing`) when it is indexable and shareable, and keep truly transient state out of the URL.

**Mistakes:** migrating without a caching audit; assuming `getServerSideProps` and a page-level `fetch` have the same cache lifetime; putting `'use client'` at the top of `layout.tsx` and silently making the whole tree client-side; using `router.query` in the App Router; assuming a layout re-renders on navigation (it does not) and using it for per-route state.

**Mini task:** take a three-route Pages Router app (list, detail, settings) and migrate it. Convert to `app/` with a route group, a persistent `dashboard/layout.tsx`, `loading.tsx` and `error.tsx`, one Server Action for the update form, and `generateMetadata` on the detail page. Then run `next build` and read the route table: which routes are static (`○`), dynamic (`ƒ`), or revalidated (`●` with a time)? Write down what changed versus the Pages Router behaviour and explain each line.

---

## 4.2.2 SSR vs SSG vs ISR vs CSR

**Definition:** Four strategies for *when* and *where* HTML and data are produced, and therefore how fresh, how cacheable, and how cheap the response is.

**Definition — the four modes.**
- **SSR (server-side rendering):** HTML produced per request on the server. Always fresh, always pays render cost, needs a server for every hit.
- **SSG (static site generation):** HTML produced at build time. Cheapest and most cacheable at the edge; content is frozen until a rebuild.
- **ISR (incremental static regeneration):** HTML produced at build time for the first request, then re-rendered in the background after a time interval or on-demand invalidation. Edge-cacheable and fresh; the cache is a data structure you now own.
- **CSR (client-side rendering):** a shell HTML is served, and data plus markup are produced in the browser. The server is nearly stateless, but the user waits on a network round trip and crawlers see less.

### The decision table

| | **SSR** | **SSG** | **ISR** | **CSR** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **What is generated** | Full HTML per request | Full HTML at build time | Full HTML at build/first request, then regenerated in background | Nothing meaningful; JS renders it |
| **How in App Router** | Default *when a request-time API is used*: `cookies()`, `headers()`, `searchParams`, uncached `fetch`, or `export const dynamic = 'force-dynamic'` | Route with no request-time APIs; `generateStaticParams` for known dynamic params; `dynamicParams = false` to 404 unknown params | `export const revalidate = 60`, or `fetch(url, { next: { revalidate: 60 } })`, or `revalidatePath`/`revalidateTag` from a mutation | Client Component fetching with `useEffect`/React Query, or a route that is static plus a `'use client'` subtree that fetches |
| **TTFB** | Highest (render happens first) | Lowest (CDN hit) | Low (stale hit served immediately, refresh in background) | Lowest server cost, but poor perceived speed (blank until JS runs) |
| **Freshness** | Always fresh at request time | Frozen at build | Bounded by the revalidation window, or immediately after invalidation | Fresh per visit, but only after JS loads |
| **Caching at CDN** | Usually none (per-user) | Aggressive; immutable hashed assets | Aggressive for the HTML, unless `no-store` | Only the shell |
| **Server load** | Highest | Near zero after build | One render per revalidation interval, amortized | Near zero (data API only) |
| **SEO** | Full HTML, good | Full HTML, best | Full HTML, good | Weak; needs SSR/prerender for public content |
| **Personalization** | Trivial (cookies available on server) | Hard (must be client-side or a nested dynamic segment) | Hard, same as SSG, and the CDN will serve one variant to everyone | Trivial |
| **Best for** | Account dashboards, checkout, anything per-user | Marketing pages, docs, blogs, changelogs | Product listings, article pages with "eventually fresh" content | Highly interactive, authenticated, cache-hostile apps |
| **Failure mode** | Slow TTFB under load, latency spikes | Content goes stale and nobody notices | **Stale content**: user sees old price or old inventory | Layout shift, spinner, flash of empty UI, crawlers see nothing |

### Hybrid reality: nest a dynamic island in a static page

The interesting answers in interviews are hybrids, not one mode per site.

```tsx
// app/pricing/page.tsx  — static shell, fast and CDN-cacheable
export const revalidate = 3600;

export default async function PricingPage() {
  const plans = await getPlans();               // cached with this route
  return (
    <>
      <PlanTable plans={plans} />                {/* Server Component, pure */}
      <CheckoutButton priceId={plans[0].id} />  {/* Client Component, no props of interest */}
    </>
  );
}
```
```tsx
// app/account/layout.tsx — a dynamic segment nested under a static one
// Reading cookies() makes ONLY this subtree per-request; siblings stay static.
import { cookies } from 'next/headers';
export default async function AccountLayout({ children }: { children: React.ReactNode }) {
  const token = (await cookies()).get('session');
  const user = await getUser(token);             // 'use cache' would be WRONG here
  return <AccountShell user={user}>{children}</AccountShell>;
}
```

The rule to state out loud: **dynamicness propagates upward from a segment, not downward.** A dynamic page makes its layouts dynamic because they render around it. It does not make its siblings or children dynamic. This is why an app with a fully static marketing site and a fully dynamic app area is normal and cheap.

### The caching pitfalls (the ones that actually page you at 3am)

1. **Everything is static until proven dynamic.** Under Next 15, a route with only `fetch` calls is prerendered at build time. Add `searchParams` or `cookies()` to a page and the route silently becomes per-request. This is usually the desired behaviour, but it means *data freshness is now a cache decision you made by accident*.
2. **`fetch` default changed.** Next 15 removed the implicit cache from `fetch`. Code that relied on "fetch is cached forever" now re-fetches per request; code that relied on "uncached by default" is unaffected. Any tutorial or AI-generated code you find without a stated Next version is suspect.
3. **`{ cache: 'no-store' }` disables the Data Cache but the request still happens on the server.** It does not make the response non-cacheable at the CDN, and it does not stop a route from being prerendered if nothing else touches request-time APIs.
4. **Personalized data in a static route leaks.** The classic: `const user = await getUser()` reading a header via an uncached fetch, inside a route that `generateStaticParams` prerenders. The build-time HTML contains *the build user's* data, baked into the CDN. Defensive checks: any data read used in markup must be per-request, and if you cannot prove it, use `cookies()`/`headers()` to force dynamic.
5. **`generateStaticParams` returning `[]` with `dynamicParams = true` (the default).** Unknown params are then server-rendered on demand and *cached*. Set `dynamicParams = false` if unknown ids should 404 instead of becoming a cache-growth attack surface.
6. **ISR with unbounded keys** is a memory/disk bill and a cheap way to get your origin bypassed by search bots.
7. **`revalidate` in a `layout.tsx` applies to the whole subtree**, which surprises people who meant one page. Prefer the narrowest scope: route-level `revalidate`, then tag-based, then path-based.

**Questions**
1. When would you choose SSR over SSG?
2. How do you implement ISR in the App Router?
3. What does `generateStaticParams` do, and what happens if a param is not in its list?
4. When is CSR the right answer?
5. What is the difference between `revalidate` and `dynamic = 'force-static'`?
6. Explain how a static page can contain per-user content.
7. What is the worst caching mistake you can make in a public product?
8. Why can a static route suddenly become dynamic after a deploy?
9. How do you debug "the price shows the old value"?
10. When would you use `dynamic = 'force-dynamic'` deliberately?
11. What's the CDN story for each mode?
12. How does streaming SSR change the SSR vs SSG tradeoff?

**Model answer, Q7 (senior/staff framing):** *Context.* A public product page renders from cached data at build or revalidation time; the build environment has no user session, but the code reads a "current user" to personalize a banner. *Decision.* Every input to the rendered HTML must be known at render time and safe to share. If the input is per-user, the render must be per-request. *Reasoning.* A prerendered route is a single artifact served from many edges. If any part of the render reads a per-request value, the artifact is wrong for every user except the one who produced it, and it is cached, so the error is silent, global, and long-lived. The safe patterns are: read `cookies()`/`headers()` to force the subtree dynamic, split the personalized part into a separate dynamic segment, or fetch the personalized fragment client-side after hydration. *Trade-offs.* Making the whole page dynamic raises TTFB and origin load; splitting adds a layout boundary and a client-side fetch for the banner, which costs a little CLS and one round trip; client fetching makes the personalized content absent from the HTML, which is fine for a "welcome back" chip and wrong for anything indexable. *Failure scenario.* A "you qualify for a 20% discount" banner is baked into the CDN artifact and served to everyone, including users who do not qualify, and support cannot reproduce it because it does not reproduce in dev. *Prevention.* Treat the build as an adversarial environment: no session, empty headers, untrusted input. Add an automated check that diffs the build-time HTML for known tenant/user markers, and prefer a dynamic segment for anything with a per-user branch.

**Model answer, Q4:** "CSR is right when the page is behind authentication and per-user anyway, when the content depends on client-only signals (viewport, `localStorage`, permissions), when the data lives behind a highly personalized API that a server render would only proxy, and when interactivity dominates and you have real data-fetching tooling (React Query or SWR) with caching, retries, and deduplication. The cost is real though: a blank frame, an extra round trip before first paint, worse perceived latency on slow connections, and weaker SEO. If the content is public or indexable, render it on the server and hydrate only the interactive parts — that is the whole point of the App Router."

**Mistakes:** treating SSR/SSG/ISR/CSR as a per-site decision instead of per-route and per-segment; assuming `fetch` is cached without saying which Next version; putting user data in a prerendered route; forgetting that a dynamic child forces dynamic parents; using `dynamicParams = false` on a legitimately unbounded catalogue; using `revalidate` on a `layout.tsx` when you meant one page.

**Mini task:** build the same product page four ways in four routes and diff them: `/p-ssr` (force-dynamic), `/p-ssg` (prerendered), `/p-isr` (`revalidate = 10`), `/p-csr` (client fetch). Add a "last updated" line to each. Then, with the server running, curl each route twice with `x-vercel-cache`-equivalent headers visible and record: TTFB, whether HTML contains data, and how long until data changes. Finally, add an admin route that calls `revalidateTag('product')` and show the ISR page updating instantly.

---

## 4.2.3 React Server Components vs Client Components

**Definition:** A Server Component renders to HTML on the server (or at build time) and its code never reaches the browser. A Client Component is normal React: it ships JavaScript, runs in the browser, and can use state, effects, and event handlers.

**Definition — the rule.** `"use client"` is a *module directive*, not a component decorator. It marks the file and **every module it imports** as part of the client bundle. Everything a module transitively imports becomes client code. This single fact explains most "why did my bundle explode" incidents.

### What a Server Component may and may not do

| Allowed on the server | Not allowed on the server |
|---|---|
| `async`/`await`, direct DB and filesystem access | `useState`, `useReducer`, `useEffect`, `useRef`-as-instance |
| Read `cookies()`, `headers()`, `searchParams` | Event handlers (`onClick`), `onChange`, any DOM callback |
| Import heavy Node libraries (`fs`, `node:fs`, ORM clients, SDKs) | Browser APIs: `window`, `document`, `localStorage`, `navigator`, `matchMedia` |
| Throw `notFound()` / `redirect()` | `useContext` for client-only contexts; class components with state |
| Render HTML and pass serializable props to children | Import a client-only package that touches `window` at module scope |
| `next/image`, `next/link`, `next/font` (all server-compatible) | Be invoked as a function from another component (components are not callable) |

### What crosses the boundary, and why props must be serializable

A Client Component passed from a Server Component arrives as a *deserialized reference in the Flight payload*. Everything in its props travels through `structuredClone`-like rules: primitives, plain objects, arrays, `Date`, `Map`, `Set`, `TypedArray`, `ArrayBuffer`, promises, and references to other client components or server-rendered *server component output* (as opaque children). Not serializable: functions (except server actions, which become references), class instances with methods, `Symbol`s, DOM nodes, `WeakMap`, getters.

```tsx
'use client';
// app/checkout/PayButton.tsx
import { useState } from 'react';

export function PayButton({ amountCents, cartId }: { amountCents: number; cartId: string }) {
  const [state, setState] = useState<'idle' | 'busy'>('idle');
  return (
    <button
      disabled={state === 'busy'}
      onClick={async () => {
        setState('busy');
        // plain fetch works: the handler lives in the browser
        await fetch('/api/checkout', {
          method: 'POST',
          headers: { 'content-type': 'application/json' },
          body: JSON.stringify({ cartId, amountCents }),
        });
        setState('idle');
      }}
    >
      {state === 'busy' ? 'Processing…' : 'Pay'}
    </button>
  );
}
```
```tsx
// app/checkout/page.tsx — stays a Server Component
import { PayButton } from './PayButton';
import { getCart } from '@/lib/cart';
import { db } from '@/lib/db';

export default async function CheckoutPage({ searchParams }: { searchParams: Promise<{ step?: string }> }) {
  const { step } = await searchParams;
  const cart = await db.cart.findFirst({ where: { userId: await requireUserId() } });  // server-only DB
  if (!cart || cart.lines.length === 0) redirect('/cart');

  return (
    <main>
      <h1>Checkout</h1>
      {/* Serializable props only. Passing `cart` with Dates is fine; passing the
          Prisma client or a class instance would throw a serialization error. */}
      <PayButton amountCents={cart.totalCents} cartId={cart.id} />
      {step === 'review' && <ReviewStep cart={cart} />}
    </main>
  );
}
```

### Server/client interleaving

The tree is not "server part, then client part". Each `'use client'` file is a *client island*, and its children may be passed in from the server as `children`:

```tsx
'use client';
// app/products/ProductShell.tsx
import { useState } from 'react';

export function ProductShell({ children, buyButton }: {
  children: React.ReactNode;      // rendered on the server, mounted inside client
  buyButton: React.ReactNode;     // a server-rendered element passed as a prop
}) {
  const [open, setOpen] = useState(false);
  return (
    <div>
      <button onClick={() => setOpen(v => !v)} aria-expanded={open}>
        {open ? 'Hide details' : 'Show details'}
      </button>
      <div hidden={!open}>{children}</div>   {/* server HTML, no client JS of its own */}
      {buyButton}
    </div>
  );
}
```
```tsx
// app/products/[id]/page.tsx
import { ProductShell } from './ProductShell';
import { BuyButton } from './BuyButton';

export default async function Page({ params }: { params: Promise<{ id: string }> }) {
  const { id } = await params;
  const product = await getProduct(id);
  return (
    <ProductShell buyButton={<BuyButton productId={id} price={product.priceCents} />}>
      <SpecTable specs={product.specs} />   {/* Server Component inside a client island */}
      <Reviews productId={id} />             {/* 'use client' island nested inside */}
    </ProductShell>
  );
}
```

**Keep the boundary low.** Push `'use client'` to the leaf: the interactive button, the input, the dropdown. Keep layouts, data loading, formatting, and static markup on the server. Three rules that pay off immediately: no `'use client'` in `layout.tsx` or `page.tsx` unless you truly need it; no context providers in a server layout (put them in a `providers.tsx` client component); and never import a whole UI library barrel into a client file, because one `'use client'` at the top of a library's index pulls the entire library in.

**Common mistakes**
- Marking the whole page client to get one `useState`.
- Passing a function prop that is not a server action (serialization error: "Functions cannot be passed directly to Client Components").
- Reading `window` in a module-scope initializer of a file imported by a server component.
- Using `useState` to mirror data that already came from the server — the server should own it.
- Expecting `useEffect` to run on the server.
- Server/client mismatch from `Date.now()`, `Math.random()`, locale formatting, or `localStorage` during render.

**Questions**
1. What exactly does `"use client"` do?
2. Why must props passed to a Client Component be serializable?
3. Can a Server Component use `useState`? Why not?
4. What is a client island, and how does server/client interleaving work?
5. A Server Component can pass a Server Component as `children` to a Client Component — how?
6. How do you share a Client Context provider across a server layout?
7. Why does one `'use client'` at the top of a UI library entry file hurt?
8. What causes "Functions cannot be passed directly to Client Components"?
9. How do you use a browser API from a server-rendered page?
10. How do you keep a client boundary low in practice?
11. When is a Client Component *not* hydrated but still interactive?
12. What are the serialization rules in practice?
13. How do you debug an unexpected client bundle?
14. What breaks if a Client Component imports a Node-only module?

**Model answer, Q7 (senior/staff framing):** *Context.* A team imports `import { Button } from '@acme/ui'` into a single client component, and the client bundle grows by 400 kB. *Decision.* Ensure the library's entry point is either framework-agnostic with per-component entry points (`@acme/ui/button`) or marked with `"use client"` at the leaf component files, not at `index.ts`. *Reasoning.* The directive is transitive per module graph: anything reachable from a client module goes into the client bundle. A barrel `index.ts` that re-exports 80 components makes all 80 reachable from any single import. Deep imports break the reachability edge. *Trade-offs.* Deep imports are less ergonomic and break tree-shaking assumptions in some bundler setups; per-component packages with explicit `exports` maps are the robust answer. *Failure scenario.* One developer imports a date-picker for a single page; because of a barrel file, every user of any component downloads date-fns, a locale bundle, and a popper dependency. Nobody notices because the import looks harmless. *Alternative / prevention.* Enforce a bundle budget in CI with a real analyzer, add an ESLint rule against barrel imports from client components, and require that any new client-side dependency ships a leaf entry point or be wrapped in one `'use client'` shim of your own.

**Model answer, Q5:** "Yes, because JSX children are evaluated as React elements before they are passed. The parent Server Component constructs `<SpecTable />` as a server-rendered element — the server renders it to HTML immediately, and the client component receives an opaque reference to that rendered output in the Flight payload. So the client component controls *placement and visibility* of server-rendered content without shipping that content's data-fetching code or its component code to the browser. This is why a client shell with server-rendered children is the idiomatic way to build a disclosure, a tab panel, or a modal that keeps server-rendered content inside."

**Mistakes:** `'use client'` too high in the tree; functions as props unless they are server actions; using `useEffect` to fetch data the server could have fetched; expecting `useState` to persist across a server re-render; mutating props in a "server" component; importing a Node-only SDK into a client component and getting a build error at best, a polyfilled stub at worst.

**Mini task:** take an existing interactive page and push `'use client'` down. Start with `'use client'` at the page, then move it to the smallest interactive subtree. After each move, run the build and record the First Load JS size for that route. Then write a component that takes a server action as a prop and one that tries to pass a plain callback, and read the exact error the second one produces. Finally, add a `<details>`-style disclosure whose panel content is a Server Component and confirm with `view-source` that the panel HTML is in the initial response.

## 4.2.4 Server Actions

**Definition:** A Server Action is an async function marked `"use server"` that runs on the server, invoked directly from client code or from a form, with the framework handling the network protocol.

**Definition — the mechanism.** The directive at file scope makes every exported async function an action endpoint. Next builds a reference for it, embeds an encrypted action ID in the client bundle, and sends the ID plus the serialized arguments over POST to the same route. On the server, Next validates the origin, deserializes arguments, invokes the function, and returns the serialized return value. This is why actions work without any fetch code and why they also work with JavaScript disabled.

```ts
// app/profile/actions.ts
'use server';

import { revalidatePath, revalidateTag } from 'next/cache';
import { z } from 'zod';
import { db } from '@/lib/db';
import { requireUser } from '@/lib/auth';

const UpdateProfile = z.object({
  name: z.string().trim().min(1).max(80),
  bio: z.string().max(500).optional(),
  website: z.string().url().optional().or(z.literal('')),
});

export type UpdateProfileState =
  | { status: 'idle' }
  | { status: 'pending' }
  | { status: 'success'; message: string }
  | { status: 'error'; fieldErrors: Record<string, string[]> };

export async function updateProfile(
  _prev: UpdateProfileState,
  formData: FormData,
): Promise<UpdateProfileState> {
  // 1. AUTHENTICATE AND AUTHORIZE INSIDE THE ACTION — always. Never trust the caller's UI.
  const user = await requireUser();                       // throws + redirects if not signed in

  // 2. VALIDATE — the client is an attacker with a nice UI.
  const parsed = UpdateProfile.safeParse(Object.fromEntries(formData));
  if (!parsed.success) {
    return { status: 'error', fieldErrors: parsed.error.flatten().fieldErrors };
  }
  const { name, bio, website } = parsed.data;

  // 3. AUTHORIZE THE SPECIFIC OPERATION on this resource.
  // (requireUser() gave identity; ownership/role checks happen here.)
  await db.user.update({ where: { id: user.id }, data: { name, bio: bio ?? null, website: website || null } });

  // 4. AUDIT + INVALIDATE.
  await db.auditLog.create({ data: { actorId: user.id, action: 'profile.update', at: new Date() } });
  revalidateTag('profile');
  revalidatePath('/profile');

  // 5. RETURN structured data, not a redirect, when the caller uses useActionState.
  return { status: 'success', message: 'Saved' };
}
```

```tsx
'use client';
// app/profile/EditForm.tsx
import { useActionState, useFormStatus } from 'react';
import { updateProfile, type UpdateProfileState } from './actions';

const initial: UpdateProfileState = { status: 'idle' };

export function EditForm() {
  const [state, formAction] = useActionState(updateProfile, initial);
  return (
    <form action={formAction}>
      <label htmlFor="name">Name</label>
      <input id="name" name="name" defaultValue="" aria-describedby="name-error" />
      {state.status === 'error' && state.fieldErrors.name && (
        <p id="name-error" role="alert">{state.fieldErrors.name[0]}</p>
      )}

      <label htmlFor="bio">Bio</label>
      <textarea id="bio" name="bio" defaultValue="" />

      <SubmitButton />
      <p aria-live="polite">{state.status === 'success' ? state.message : ''}</p>
    </form>
  );
}

function SubmitButton() {
  const { pending } = useFormStatus();      // must be a CHILD of the <form>
  return <button type="submit" disabled={pending}>{pending ? 'Saving…' : 'Save'}</button>;
}
```

```tsx
// Without JS: a plain <form action={updateProfile}> posts to the server, the action runs,
// and if it returns nothing/redirects the browser navigates. Same code path.
<form action={serverAction}>
  <input name="name" />
  <button>Save</button>
</form>
```

### The security model (the highest-value part of this topic)

Server Actions are a **public HTTP endpoint with an opaque path**. Anyone can invoke any action they can obtain a reference to, and references are in the client bundle of the page that imports them. Treat every action as an untrusted, authenticated-if-you-say-so POST handler.

| Threat | Control |
|---|---|
| Unauthorized mutation | Call `requireUser()`/`requireRole()` **inside the action**, on every invocation, for every mutation. Never rely on "the button is only rendered for admins". |
| Broken object-level authorization | Re-check ownership/tenant in the query: `db.post.update({ where: { id, authorId: user.id } })`, or load then assert. IDs from hidden fields are attacker-controlled. |
| Mass assignment / hidden-field tampering | Never `Object.fromEntries(formData)` straight into a DB update. Whitelist fields with a schema (Zod/Valibot), coerce types, reject unknown keys (`.strict()`). |
| Injection | Use parameterized queries/ORM APIs, not string-built SQL. Treat stored text as untrusted on render (React escapes by default; never use `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` on user data). |
| CSRF | Next verifies the `Origin`/`Host` for action POSTs. Keep `SameSite=Lax` cookies, and do not weaken it. If you accept cross-origin action-like POSTs elsewhere, enforce origin explicitly. Never rely solely on SameSite. |
| Replay / double submission | Make mutations idempotent: accept a client-generated idempotency key column with a unique index. Also disable the submit button while `useFormStatus().pending` is true. |
| Abuse / enumeration | Rate limit per user and per IP (canonical entry: PHASE 6 → Caching Layers → Rate Limiting), and return indistinguishable errors for "not found" vs "not yours". |
| Sensitive data leakage via return value | Do not return whole rows or internal error objects. Map errors to safe messages; never return stack traces or SQL errors. |
| Auditability | Log actor, action, target, outcome, and request id for anything financially or security relevant. |
| Long-running work | Return quickly and enqueue; do not hold the action open for minutes. |
| Streaming progress | You may return a `ReadableStream`; but the client must treat a truncated stream as failure and re-check server state. |

**Never trust the client that a mutation succeeded.** After an action resolves, the server may still be propagating: an event, a downstream service, a search index, a read replica. The pattern is to re-read authoritative state rather than assume.

```ts
'use server';
export async function withdraw(amountCents: number) {
  const user = await requireUser();
  const parsed = z.object({ amountCents: z.number().int().positive().max(1_000_00) })
    .safeParse({ amountCents });
  if (!parsed.success) throw new Error('invalid amount');

  // Authoritative check INSIDE the action — never trust the balance shown in the UI.
  const account = await db.account.findUniqueOrThrow({ where: { userId: user.id } });
  if (account.balanceCents < parsed.data.amountCents) {
    return { ok: false, code: 'INSUFFICIENT_FUNDS' };
  }
  const updated = await db.account.update({
    where: { id: account.id, version: account.version },   // optimistic concurrency
    data: { balanceCents: { decrement: parsed.data.amountCents }, version: { increment: 1 } },
  }).catch(() => null);

  if (!updated) return { ok: false, code: 'CONFLICT_RETRY' };
  await queueLedgerEntry({ accountId: account.id, amountCents: parsed.data.amountCents });
  revalidateTag('account');
  return { ok: true, newBalanceCents: updated.balanceCents };  // safe, minimal
}
```

Client-side, treat the result as advisory: disable the control, show pending, and on any error or unclear outcome re-fetch the authoritative balance rather than trusting the response.

**Questions**
1. What is a Server Action, mechanically?
2. Why do Server Actions work with JavaScript disabled?
3. What must you do inside every action, without exception?
4. How do you validate form input from an action?
5. Why is `Object.fromEntries(formData)` into a DB update dangerous?
6. How do you get field-level validation errors back into the form?
7. What is `useActionState` and when do you use it vs `useFormStatus`?
8. How do you make a mutation idempotent?
9. What does `revalidatePath` vs `revalidateTag` do, and which is safer?
10. How do you prevent an admin-only action from being called by a normal user?
11. Why must you never trust the client that a mutation succeeded?
12. How do you rate limit a Server Action?
13. What happens if an action is called directly via curl?
14. How do you stream progress out of an action, and what do you do if the stream breaks?
15. How do Server Actions interact with React 19 `useActionState` / form actions?

**Model answer, Q3 (senior/staff framing):** *Context.* A team has a Server Action that updates a record and was written assuming only the dashboard renders it. *Decision.* The action's first statement is authorization derived from the request, not from anything passed in. *Reasoning.* A Server Action is a public POST endpoint. Its reference ships in the client bundle to anyone who loads the page, so "the UI hides the button" is a usability measure, not a security control. Every action must independently authenticate (who is calling) and authorize (may they do this to *this* resource), and must treat every form field as attacker-controlled. *Trade-offs.* Repeating `requireUser()` and ownership checks in every action is boilerplate; wrap it, but keep the check visible at the action's top so reviewers see it, and add a lint or test that fails when a mutating action lacks it. Re-reading authorization data on every call costs a query — that is the price of correctness. *Failure scenario.* The action reads `formData.get('accountId')` and updates that account. A user edits the hidden field to another account id and the record is modified; no error appears because the code was "working". *Prevention.* Whitelist fields with a strict schema, scope the DB write by the authorized owner in the `where` clause, rate limit, audit, and add an integration test that calls the action as user A with user B's id and asserts failure.

**Mistakes:** authorizing in the page component instead of the action; trusting hidden fields and route params; returning whole entities from actions; forgetting that `useFormStatus` only works in a child of the form; double-submitting because the button was never disabled; treating a thrown error as a user-facing message (thrown errors in actions can surface as error boundaries); skipping cache invalidation after a mutation and calling it "a caching bug" later.

**Mini task:** build a rename-and-tag form for a document: schema validation, field-level errors via `useActionState`, `useFormStatus` pending state, `revalidateTag` on save, and an audit log row. Then attack it: call the action with `curl` using the action ID from the page HTML, with a tampered `documentId` belonging to another user, with an extra `role: 'admin'` field, and twice in parallel. Write down what happened for each and which control stopped it. Finally, add an idempotency key and prove the double-charge is gone.

## 4.2.5 Data Fetching and Caching in Depth

**Definition:** The set of distinct caches between your server and your users, and the rules that decide which one a given read hits.

**The four caches (memorize this list; almost every staleness question reduces to naming the layer)**

| Layer | Scope | Key | Invalidated by | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Request Memoization** | one request/render pass | method + URL + options, in-flight only | nothing, expires when request ends | dedupes identical `fetch` calls made during one render |
| **Data Cache** (framework cache) | across requests, server-side | URL + `cache`/`next` options | `revalidate` expiry, `revalidateTag`, `revalidatePath`, deploy | persistent across requests and deploys on a persistent store |
| **Full Route Cache** (Router Cache) | client-side, in memory | pathname + search params (RSC payload) | hard navigation, `router.refresh()`, `staleTime` expiry, deploy | what the browser Router holds for instant back/forward |
| **CDN / HTTP cache** | global edge | URL + headers (`Vary`) | `Cache-Control`/`s-maxage`, purge | only caches responses it is allowed to; per-user responses are skipped |

If you cannot name which of the four holds a stale value, you cannot fix it.

```ts
// Layer 2: per-fetch control (Next 15: uncached by default)
const a = await fetch('https://api.example.com/products');                     // uncached in 15+
const b = await fetch('https://api.example.com/products', {
  next: { revalidate: 60, tags: ['products'] },
});
const c = await fetch('https://api.example.com/me', { cache: 'no-store' });   // never cached
const d = await fetch(url, { next: { tags: ['user:42'] } });                  // cached forever until tag invalidated

// Cross-request memoization independent of fetch: dedupe + cache by key.
import { cache } from 'react';

export const getUser = cache(async (id: string) => {
  const res = await fetch(`${process.env.API}/users/${id}`, { cache: 'no-store' });
  if (!res.ok) throw new Error(`user ${id}: ${res.status}`);
  return res.json();
});
// Two components awaiting getUser('42') in the same render => one network call.
// getUser('43') is a separate cache entry. cache() is per-request, not per-deploy.

// React cache() and the newer directive-based cache (verify names against the docs):
// 'use cache' marks a function's result as cacheable, with a cacheKey composed of
// the function identity + arguments, and supports a cacheLife profile:
//   'use cache';
//   async function getProduct(id: string) { ... }
//   // plus <CacheBoundary> / cacheComponents for partial prerendering (see 4.2.13)
```

### Practical rules

1. **Cache reads, not decisions.** A cached `fetch` returns bytes. Authorization must happen outside the cached function, per request, or you will serve one user's data to another.
2. **Tag invalidation is coarse; path invalidation is precise; route `revalidate` is a time window.** Prefer tags for broad domains (`products`), paths for surgical page updates, and `revalidate` for bounded staleness.
3. **Never cache per-user data in a shared key.** Use `'user:42'`-style tags or per-request fetches.
4. **Stable cache keys.** `fetch` keys include the URL and (for `GET`) the body/headers you pass. Random query strings and cache-busting `?t=${Date.now()}` create unbounded cache entries and defeat caching entirely.
5. **Errors are not cached.** A `fetch` that throws is not stored, but a `404` response may be. Check `res.ok` explicitly.

### Debugging playbook: "why is my data stale?"

Work outward through the layers. This exact order is worth memorizing for the interview.

1. **Is it stale in the HTML or only after interaction?** `curl` the page and inspect. If the HTML is fresh, the problem is client state (Router Cache or a client cache library), not the server.
2. **Which cache?** In dev, run with `next dev` (dev uses a different caching behaviour than build/start — this mismatch is itself a common bug). In production, inspect response headers: `x-nextjs-cache` (HIT/STALE/MISS on Vercel), `Age`, `Cache-Control`, `ETag`. HIT means the CDN/Router cache is serving it; STALE means background regeneration is in flight; MISS means a fresh render happened.
3. **Router Cache.** In the browser, run a client-side navigation and watch Network: is the RSC payload requested at all? If not, the Router Cache is serving it. `router.refresh()` bypasses it.
4. **Did you invalidate the right tag/path?** Log inside the action. `revalidateTag('products')` does nothing if the read used `tags: ['product-list']`.
5. **Is the read inside a cached boundary?** A cached function whose inputs did not change keeps returning the old value even after you edit the database. Bump a version argument, or use a tag that you do invalidate.
6. **Is there a client cache?** React Query / SWR / RTK Query with a long `staleTime` and no `refetchOnWindowFocus` will show stale data with a healthy server. Check the Network tab for whether a request is even being made.
7. **Is a CDN in front of a per-user route?** If the response has no `Cache-Control: private, no-store` and the CDN is caching it, you have a leak and a staleness bug at the same time.
8. **Do you have clock skew?** `revalidate` timing depends on the server clock; a replica serving stale reads explains "works on one instance".
9. **Two servers, two caches.** Without a shared persistent cache store, Data Cache entries live per instance; a load-balanced deployment produces random staleness. Fix with a shared cache or by moving the read behind a real API.

**Questions**
1. Name the caching layers in Next.js App Router.
2. What does request memoization do and when does it apply?
3. Is `fetch` cached by default? (State your Next version.)
4. What is React's `cache()` for, and what is it not for?
5. When would you use tags over paths?
6. What does `x-nextjs-cache: STALE` mean?
7. Why is caching per-user fetches with a shared key dangerous?
8. How do you force a revalidation from a webhook?
9. Why does `?t=${Date.now()}` hurt?
10. What's the difference between revalidating in the route and in the action?
11. How do you debug "the data updates in the DB but not on screen"?
12. Does the dev server behave like production? Why does it matter?
13. What is `dynamicIO` / Cache Components in spirit?
14. When does a shared external cache beat the framework cache?

**Model answer, Q11 (senior/staff framing):** *Context.* Support reports the pricing page shows an old price after an update, and the update is visible in the admin tool. *Decision.* Bisect from the outside in: HTML, then CDN/router cache, then Data Cache, then client cache. *Reasoning.* Staleness is a layering problem, and each layer has a different observable signal. `curl` the page: if the HTML has the new price, the server rendered correctly and the stale value lives in the browser (Router Cache or a client cache library with a long stale time). If the HTML is stale, look at `x-nextjs-cache`/`Age`: HIT or STALE points at the CDN or Router Cache; a fresh MISS with stale HTML points at the Data Cache or at a cached function that never got its tag invalidated. *Trade-offs.* Each debugging step costs a request or a rebuild; the temptation is to "just add `no-store` everywhere". That trades a correctness fix for TTFB, origin load, and lost ISR benefits, and it hides the real bug until it reappears in a different layer. *Failure scenario.* A developer fixes it by adding `no-store` to the whole route, the page becomes slow and uncacheable, and three weeks later a webhook-driven update is missed because the underlying tag mismatch was never found. *Alternative / prevention.* Log the cache key, tag, and mode in development; assert in CI that documented-cached reads carry expected tags; prefer tag-scoped invalidation tested end-to-end, and keep a single small `revalidateTag` call at the mutation site rather than sprinkling `no-store`.

**Mistakes:** assuming a cached `fetch` and a `cache()`d function invalidate together; `no-store` as a first resort; caching a response that includes user data; relying on dev-mode caching behaviour to reason about production; invalidating a tag that no read uses; putting `revalidate` on a layout and not realizing it applies to the subtree.

**Mini task:** instrument a route with all four layers observable: a `fetch` with a tag, a `cache()`d function, a client `useQuery` with `staleTime: 60000`, and a CDN-cached page. Produce a table showing which layer must be touched to make a change visible within 1 second, and how long the fix survives a hard refresh, a client-side navigation, and a redeploy.

## 4.2.6 Streaming and Suspense

**Definition:** Streaming SSR sends the shell of the page as soon as it is ready and flushes subsequent HTML as Suspense boundaries resolve, so the browser can paint and become interactive before the slowest data has arrived.

```tsx
// app/dashboard/page.tsx — one shell, three independently timed regions
import { Suspense } from 'react';

export default function DashboardPage() {
  return (
    <Shell>
      <Suspense fallback={<StatsSkeleton />}>
        <Stats />            {/* awaits slow aggregate query */}
      </Suspense>
      <Suspense fallback={<ListSkeleton />}>
        <RecentOrders />     {/* awaits paginated query */}
      </Suspense>
      <Suspense fallback={<RecommendationsSkeleton />}>
        <Recommendations /> {/* third-party, slowest */}
      </Suspense>
    </Shell>
  );
}
```
`loading.tsx` in a segment is sugar for wrapping that segment's children in `<Suspense fallback={<Loading/>}>`. You need explicit `<Suspense>` when several async siblings are on the same page and you want them to stream independently.

**What it buys you**
- **TTFB** drops to roughly the time to render the shell, not the slowest query.
- **Out-of-order completion:** boundary 3 can finish and flush before boundary 1, so a fast widget is not held hostage by a slow one.
- **Progressive rendering and fewer spinners in the user's perception**, but a caveat: `<Suspense>` shows a fallback for boundaries that are *not yet resolved in that navigation*. Already-resolved boundaries (for example, cached RSC payloads from the Router Cache) do not flash a fallback on client-side navigation.
- **Fewer data waterfalls** when you hoist fetches into sibling Suspense boundaries that run in parallel, since React starts them all as it renders.

**Cost and gotchas**
- Streaming requires a server (it is the opposite of static export). With `output: 'export'` there is no runtime to stream from.
- A slow boundary without a fallback is just a blank region; put fallbacks where layout matters.
- Streaming interacts with error boundaries: an error thrown inside a streamed boundary arrives *after* the shell, so `error.tsx` must be present or the user sees a half-rendered page with a broken region. Use `error.tsx` per segment.
- Streaming does not help SEO negatively (crawlers read the completed HTML), but it does complicate "wait for full load" tooling.
- With `Suspense`, React can commit a fallback and later swap in content — a form of state you must design for, especially inputs (unmounting a focused input loses focus and value; use `key` carefully and keep state lifted).
- `useTransition` (see 4.1) plus streaming gives you non-blocking navigation: set a pending state, stream the new route, keep the old UI visible.

```tsx
// app/feed/loading.tsx
export default function Loading() { return <FeedSkeleton />; }

// app/feed/error.tsx — 'use client' required; receives error + reset
'use client';
export default function Error({ error, reset }: { error: Error; reset: () => void }) {
  useEffect(() => { reportError(error); }, [error]);
  return (
    <div role="alert">
      <p>Could not load the feed.</p>
      <button onClick={reset}>Try again</button>
    </div>
  );
}

// app/feed/not-found.tsx
export default function NotFound() { return <EmptyState title="No posts" />; }
```

**Questions**
1. What does streaming SSR change about perceived performance?
2. What is out-of-order completion?
3. What does `loading.tsx` do under the hood?
4. Why can a streamed error appear after a good-looking page?
5. How does Suspense affect data waterfalls?
6. When does streaming hurt rather than help?
7. Does streaming work with static export?
8. How do you avoid losing input state when a Suspense boundary re-renders?
9. What's the difference between `loading.tsx` and `<Suspense>`?
10. How do you debug "the spinner flashes on every navigation"?
11. How does streaming interact with `error.tsx` vs `global-error.tsx`?
12. When do you use `useTransition` vs `loading.tsx`?

**Model answer, Q4 (senior/staff framing):** *Context.* A page streams its shell and three data regions; one region's query rejects after 2 seconds. *Decision.* Every data-dependent region needs its own boundary pair: a `<Suspense>` for the pending state and an `error.tsx` (or a nested `<ErrorBoundary>`) for the rejection. *Reasoning.* With streaming, the response is committed to the client in pieces. The shell is already sent when the failing region rejects, so the browser has a complete, valid document with a hole in it. The error is not a page-level failure; it is a *region* failure, and without a boundary at that region React cannot attribute it and will escalate to the nearest enclosing boundary — ultimately `global-error.tsx`, which replaces the whole page including the shell the user was already reading. *Trade-offs.* Fine-grained boundaries mean more files and more boilerplate, and each boundary's fallback is a design decision (skeleton vs spinner vs "keep old data"). A single top-level boundary is simpler but sacrifices the streaming benefit, because one slow query then blocks everything. *Failure scenario.* A flaky third-party feed fails; the app has no `error.tsx` under that segment, so the user's entire dashboard is replaced by a generic error page, discarding the stats they were reading, and the incident looks like "the dashboard is down" when only one widget was. *Prevention.* One `<Suspense>` + one error boundary per independent data region by default; a design-system skeleton per fallback; log boundary name and request id with the error; test the failure path by making the query reject.

**Mistakes:** one giant top-level `<Suspense>` that defeats streaming; no `error.tsx` for streamed regions; using `loading.tsx` for a region that should keep showing stale content; assuming `loading.tsx` is required for client-side navigation (it is for dynamic routes; for static routes the prefetched payload may make it flash or not appear); measuring TTFB only and missing that FCP/LCP depend on the *first* region completing.

**Mini task:** build a page with three suspense-wrapped regions backed by routes with 50ms, 400ms, and 1500ms artificial delays. Measure TTFB, FCP, and LCP with the browser's performance panel before and after adding `<Suspense>`; note the difference. Then make the slow region fail and observe the escalation behaviour with and without a nested `error.tsx`.
