## 4.2.7 Route Handlers

**Definition:** `app/**/route.ts` files define HTTP endpoints using exported functions named after methods, replacing `pages/api/*`.

```ts
// app/api/products/route.ts
import { NextResponse } from 'next/server';

export const runtime = 'nodejs';                 // 'nodejs' | 'edge'
export const dynamic = 'force-dynamic';          // or 'force-static' for a cacheable GET
export const revalidate = 300;                   // cache lifetime when static
export const preferredRegion = 'home';           // Vercel hint; verify against docs

export async function GET(request: Request) {
  const url = new URL(request.url);
  const cursor = url.searchParams.get('cursor');
  const limit = Math.min(Number(url.searchParams.get('limit') ?? 20), 100);

  const page = await listProducts({ cursor, limit });
  return NextResponse.json(
    { items: page.items, nextCursor: page.nextCursor },
    {
      headers: {
        // keyset pagination + explicit caching is better than ETag guessing here
        'cache-control': 'private, max-age=0, must-revalidate',
        vary: 'authorization',
      },
    },
  );
}

export async function POST(request: Request) {
  const user = await requireUser();                       // auth inside the handler
  const json = await request.json().catch(() => null);    // never assume a body
  const parsed = CreateProduct.safeParse(json);
  if (!parsed.success) {
    return NextResponse.json(
      { error: { code: 'INVALID_INPUT', fields: parsed.error.flatten().fieldErrors } },
      { status: 422 },
    );
  }
  const created = await db.product.create({ data: { ...parsed.data, ownerId: user.id } });
  revalidateTag('products');
  return NextResponse.json({ id: created.id }, { status: 201, headers: { location: `/api/products/${created.id}` } });
}
```

**All methods:** `GET`, `POST`, `PUT`, `PATCH`, `DELETE`, `HEAD`, and `OPTIONS`. `HEAD` and `OPTIONS` are auto-implemented from `GET`/middleware if not exported. A `route.ts` may **not** co-exist with a `page.tsx` at the same route path — one URL, one handler. Anything not matching a method returns 405.

**Streaming responses** work directly and are the right tool for progress and large exports.

```ts
// app/api/export/route.ts
export async function GET() {
  const encoder = new TextEncoder();
  const stream = new ReadableStream({
    async start(controller) {
      const rows = await db.report.findMany();
      controller.enqueue(encoder.encode('id,total\n'));
      for (const r of rows) controller.enqueue(encoder.encode(`${r.id},${r.total}\n`));
      controller.close();
    },
  });
  return new Response(stream, {
    headers: {
      'content-type': 'text/csv; charset=utf-8',
      'content-disposition': 'attachment; filename="report.csv"',
      'cache-control': 'no-store',
    },
  });
}
```

**Static vs dynamic route handlers.** A `GET` handler with no request-time API usage can be built as a static route (`export const dynamic = 'force-static'`), served and cached like a page; anything using `request`, `cookies()`, or POST becomes dynamic. Note the caching gotcha: in some versions GET handlers are not cached by default; verify against the docs and set `dynamic` explicitly rather than relying on inference.

**When a route handler is the wrong tool**
- **It's an internal mutation called only by your own UI** → Server Action (less boilerplate, typed args, progressive enhancement).
- **It's a webhook from a third party** → route handler is right, but disable CSRF/origin expectations for it, verify the signature, allow POST from any origin, and reply fast with the raw body before doing async work.
- **It's a file upload or long job** → presigned upload to object storage + queue; the handler should not proxy bytes.
- **It's an HTML page** → `page.tsx` with RSC, not a string of markup.
- **It's a static asset** → put it in `public/` or serve from a CDN, not a handler.
- **It's a cross-origin API consumed by a separate SPA** → route handler, and be deliberate about CORS (`OPTIONS` preflight, `Access-Control-Allow-Origin`, never `*` with credentials).

**Questions**
1. How do you define a route handler and which methods exist?
2. How do you return a stream from a handler?
3. How do you choose runtime per route?
4. Are GET handlers cached? (State your version and how you control it.)
5. What happens if a route.ts and page.tsx share a path?
6. Why can't you return per-user data from a static GET handler?
7. When is a Server Action better than a route handler?
8. How do you handle a webhook endpoint securely?
9. Why use keyset pagination instead of offset?
10. How do you do file uploads through Next?
11. What's the `Vary` header for here and why?
12. How do you add rate limiting and CORS to a handler?
13. When do you return 405, 422, 409, 429?
14. How do you version an API in the App Router?

**Model answer, Q7:** "Route handlers are the right tool when the caller is not my own UI — a mobile app, a webhook, a third-party integration, or anything that needs the HTTP contract to be explicit and stable. Server Actions are better when the caller *is* my own UI: the arguments are typed, I skip the fetch boilerplate and the serialization layer, I get validation-friendly form integration and progressive enhancement, and I get a reference-based call instead of a URL I have to keep in sync. The rule of thumb: if the only consumer is a component in this repo, use an action; if the endpoint has an external contract, use a handler."

**Mistakes:** trusting `request.json()` to succeed; returning 200 with an error body; missing `Vary: authorization` on a per-user response; forgetting `runtime` and shipping a Node-only API to the edge; proxying uploads through the server; duplicating auth logic instead of sharing it with actions; relying on implicit GET caching.

**Mini task:** build `/api/products` with `GET` (cursor pagination, `Vary`), `POST` (schema validation, 201 + `location`), `PATCH` (partial update, 404 vs 409 semantics), `DELETE` (idempotent, 204), and an `OPTIONS` handler for CORS. Then add a `ReadableStream` CSV export endpoint, a per-IP rate limit using a token bucket, and a webhook endpoint that verifies an HMAC signature with a timing-safe compare and enqueues work after responding.

## 4.2.8 Middleware

**Definition:** `middleware.ts` (or `src/middleware.ts`) runs before a matched request is handled — before pages, route handlers, and rewrites — on the Edge runtime by default.

```ts
// middleware.ts
import { NextResponse, type NextRequest } from 'next/server';

export const config = {
  // Matchers are evaluated in order and first-match-wins within a file.
  matcher: [
    // Exclude API routes, Next internals, and static files. Prefer this over
    // checking pathname inside the function: matchers run before any extra work.
    '/((?!api|_next/static|_next/image|favicon.ico|robots.txt|sitemap.xml|.*\\.png$).*)',
    {
      source: '/dashboard/:path*',
      missing: [{ type: 'cookie', key: 'session' }],
    },
  ],
};

export function middleware(req: NextRequest) {
  const token = req.cookies.get('session')?.value;
  if (!token) {
    const url = req.nextUrl.clone();      // clone, never mutate req.nextUrl
    url.pathname = '/login';
    url.searchParams.set('next', req.nextUrl.pathname + req.nextUrl.search);
    return NextResponse.redirect(url);
  }
  return NextResponse.next();
}
```

**Ordering.** Matchers and middleware run in a defined order: `middleware.ts` runs before route handlers and before the `headers`/`redirects`/`beforeFiles` rewrites; multiple middleware files are not supported in one project (middleware is not additive across the app — you get one entry point per project), so chain concerns inside it. Note also that middleware does **not** run for statically served files in `/_next/static`, and — a subtle but important one — a matcher that is too broad will run middleware on every request including images and RSC payloads, costing latency and (on some hosts) function invocations.

**What middleware can and cannot do**

| Can | Cannot |
|---|---|
| Read/rewrite request headers, cookies, and the URL | Access the database reliably (Edge runtime, latency, no connection pooling) |
| Redirect, rewrite, or return a JSON response directly | Rely on Node APIs unless you opt into `runtime: 'nodejs'` (supported in newer versions; verify) |
| Set request headers visible to Server Components via `headers()` | Read the response body to make decisions |
| Gate by cookie/header/geo cheaply, before rendering | Do your real authorization — it is a cheap gate, not a security boundary |
| A/B or geo-routing, feature flags, bot detection, canonical host rewrites | Persist state across requests reliably (Edge isolates are ephemeral; use a KV/edge store) |

**Auth in middleware: the pattern and its limits.** The right architecture is *cookie-presence check in middleware (fast rejection), full session verification in the data layer*. Middleware verifies a cookie exists and maybe that it is well-formed and not expired; the actual signature/JWT verification, session lookup, and object-level authorization happen where the data is read (Server Component, route handler, action).

```ts
// lib/auth.ts — the real check, called from every data access site
import { cookies } from 'next/headers';
import { db } from '@/lib/db';

export async function requireUser() {
  const token = (await cookies()).get('session')?.value;
  if (!token) redirect('/login');                    // or throw for API contexts
  const session = await cache(getSession)(token);    // cheap, cached lookup
  if (!session || session.expiresAt < Date.now()) {
    (await cookies()).delete('session');
    redirect('/login');
  }
  return session.user;
}
```

**The classic redirect loop.** Symptom: `GET /dashboard` returns 302 to `/login`, and `/login` also redirects (or middleware matches `/login` too). Causes and fixes:
1. **Broad matcher including `/login`.** Fix: exclude auth routes explicitly (matcher regex or `next.config`).
2. **Cookie present but invalid.** Middleware checks presence only; the page's `requireUser()` sees an invalid session and redirects to `/login`; middleware sees the cookie still present and redirects to `/dashboard`; infinite loop. Fix: middleware must delete the cookie when it rejects, and the deep check must `cookies().delete()` on failure.
3. **Param-aware routes.** Middleware uses `pathname` only, so `/login?next=/login` or an i18n prefix mismatch loops. Fix: normalize with the same prefixes your routes use and validate `next` to prevent open redirects.
4. **Trailing slash / case mismatch** producing `/Login` which doesn't match your exclusion.
5. **Rewrite loops:** rewriting to a path that matches your own matcher again.

```
/dashboard → 307 /login?next=/dashboard
/login → 307 /login            ← loop
```
Debug: log `pathname` and the decision at the top of middleware (log once, not per request, or you create the loop's worst case: log amplification), inspect the cookie attributes (`Path`, `Domain`, `Secure`, `SameSite`, expiry), and check whether a rewrite is re-entering middleware.

**Questions**
1. When does middleware run in the request lifecycle?
2. What does the `config.matcher` do and why prefer it over pathname checks?
3. Can middleware do authorization? Why not?
4. What is the redirect loop bug and what causes it?
5. What runtime does middleware use, and what are the limits?
6. Middleware ordering with rewrites in `next.config`?
7. How do you do A/B testing in middleware?
8. How do you add a header that a Server Component can read?
9. Why might a broad matcher hurt performance?
10. How do you debug a loop — what do you log?
11. Can you have multiple middleware files?
12. When is middleware the wrong tool?

**Model answer, Q4 (senior/staff framing):** *Context.* A `/dashboard` route 307s to `/login` and the browser reports "too many redirects". *Decision.* Trace the two redirect sources as separate actors: the middleware gate and the data-layer session check, and find out which pair is disagreeing. *Reasoning.* The canonical cause is a split-brain: middleware validates *presence* of the `session` cookie while the page calls `requireUser()`, which validates the session *value*. When the session is expired, revoked, or signed with a rotated key, the cookie still exists. Middleware therefore admits the request, `requireUser()` rejects it and redirects to `/login`, middleware sees the cookie still present and redirects back to `/dashboard`. Neither component is wrong in isolation; the missing invariant is that the cookie's presence must imply its validity, or that rejection must clear the cookie. *Trade-offs.* Full verification in middleware (JWT signature check, session lookup) is cheap for stateless JWTs and expensive for database sessions on the Edge; verifying fully makes every request pay that cost and still leaves object-level authorization undone. *Failure scenario.* A key rotation invalidates all sessions; 100% of authenticated traffic 307-loops, error monitors fill with 3xx chains, and login itself appears broken. *Prevention.* Clear the cookie on rejection at every layer; make the matcher exclude `/login` and static assets; keep one `requireUser()` used by pages, handlers, and actions; add a synthetic check that requests a protected route with an invalid session cookie and asserts a single redirect to `/login`, plus a loop detector alert on repeated redirect chains.

**Mistakes:** doing DB authorization in Edge middleware; mutating `req.nextUrl` in place instead of cloning; matching everything and slowing every request; treating middleware as the only auth check; forgetting that middleware can run on RSC/prefetch requests and must be cheap; using `redirect()` in middleware when you meant an internal rewrite.

**Mini task:** implement middleware with a narrow matcher, a presence-only session gate, a safe `next` redirect parameter (validate it is a same-origin relative path), and a debug header `x-mw: allowed|redirected`. Then deliberately create each of the five loop causes above, observe the redirect chain with `curl -I -L`, and fix each by editing only middleware or `requireUser()`. Add a test asserting exactly one redirect for an invalid cookie.

## 4.2.9 Image and Font Optimization

**Definition:** `next/image` and `next/font` are build/serve-time optimizers that change the *default output* of images and fonts — formats, dimensions, responsive `srcset`, preload hints, self-hosting, and layout-shift prevention — without changing your markup API much.

**Why `next/image` exists.** Browsers ship three problems you can solve yourself or pay a service to solve:
1. **Bytes:** modern formats (AVIF/WebP) cut image weight substantially at similar quality.
2. **Layout shift:** an image with unknown dimensions shifts content when it loads, which directly hurts CLS. The optimizer reserves space using `width`/`height` (and `fill` + aspect ratio) and emits the right `srcset`/`sizes`.
3. **Responsiveness and laziness:** one `<img>` cannot serve the right resolution per device; `next/image` generates a `srcset` and can defer non-critical images.

```tsx
// next.config.ts — remote images must be explicitly allowed
import type { NextConfig } from 'next';
const nextConfig: NextConfig = {
  images: {
    remotePatterns: [{ protocol: 'https', hostname: 'cdn.example.com', pathname: '/products/**' }],
    formats: ['image/avif', 'image/webp'],
    deviceSizes: [640, 750, 828, 1080, 1200, 1920],
  },
};
export default nextConfig;
```
```tsx
import Image from 'next/image';

export function ProductImage({ src, alt, priority }: { src: string; alt: string; priority?: boolean }) {
  return (
    <Image
      src={src}
      alt={alt}                 // "" for decorative
      width={800}               // or height, or fill + sizes
      height={600}
      sizes="(max-width: 640px) 100vw, 50vw"   // required with fill + responsive sizes
      priority={priority}       // ONLY for the LCP image; it disables lazy-loading
      quality={75}              // per-image override of the quality allowlist
      placeholder="blur"        // requires blurDataURL or automatic detection
      blurDataURL="data:image/..."             // tiny inline base64 preview
    />
  );
}
```

**Trade-offs, honestly.**
- `next/image` costs CPU and memory per transform, and on self-hosted Node it adds a caching layer you must operate (`/\_next/image` responses are cacheable but the cache is local to your instance).
- `priority` (formerly `priority`/`loading="eager"`) should be used for the LCP image and nothing else — it disables lazy loading and preloads, and misusing it on many images hurts.
- `<img>` is fine when: you already have a CDN doing AVIF/WebP with `srcset` (Cloudinary, imgix, Fastly), the image is user-uploaded and served from a signed URL on your own CDN, you need `<picture>`/art direction you don't want to fight the API for, the file is an inline SVG or a tiny icon (use SVG sprite or CSS), or the content is truly static and tiny. In those cases a plain `<img>` with explicit `width`/`height` and `loading="lazy"` is simpler and just as good.
- Do not lazy-load the LCP image. Do not forget `alt`; use `alt=""` for decorative images (accessibility: canonical entry: PHASE 3 Part 2 → Accessibility).
- SVG needs `dangerouslyAllowSVG` for remote SVGs, which is an XSS risk; prefer serving SVGs yourself or sanitizing. Treat any remote SVG as untrusted input.

```ts
// next/font — self-hosted, subsetted, preloaded, with zero layout shift (font metrics
// adjust the fallback so the swap does not reflow), and font-display: swap by default.
import { Inter, Noto_Sans_SC } from 'next/font/google';

const inter = Inter({ subsets: ['latin'], display: 'swap', variable: '--font-inter', preload: true });
const cjk = Noto_Sans_SC({ weight: ['400', '700'], display: 'swap', variable: '--font-cjk' });

export default function RootLayout({ children }: { children: React.ReactNode }) {
  return (
    <html lang="en" className={`${inter.variable} ${cjk.variable}`}>
      <body>{children}</body>
    </html>
  );
}
// A CJK/emoji subset can be megabytes: subset aggressively, preload only the primary
// face, and prefer a variable font or a self-hosted subset over downloading all weights.
```

**When optimization is worth it:** large photographic catalogues (bytes), any page whose LCP is an image (LCP time), user-facing pages with CLS problems, and font-heavy editorial sites (FOUT/FOIT and layout shift). **When it is not:** internal dashboards with tiny thumbnails already served by your CDN, or a project where you would spend more time fighting the optimizer than the budget it saves. Measure first: RUM/field data (LCP, CLS) beats theory.

**Questions**
1. Why does `next/image` exist? What are its three main benefits?
2. What prevents layout shift for images?
3. What does `priority` do and when should you use it?
4. What are `remotePatterns` and why are they required?
5. When is a plain `<img>` acceptable?
6. What does `next/font` do better than a Google Fonts `<link>`?
7. What is `font-display: swap` and what is the trade-off?
8. How do you handle CJK fonts?
9. What is the risk of `dangerouslyAllowSVG`?
10. Self-hosting: what's the caching gotcha with the image optimizer?
11. What does `sizes` do and when do you need it?
12. How do you prove image optimization helped?

**Model answer, Q10 (senior/staff framing):** *Context.* We self-host Next.js behind nginx on two instances, and users intermittently see placeholder-quality images. *Decision.* Stop relying on the default in-memory image cache; run the optimizer with a shared cache (or an external image CDN) and set explicit `Cache-Control` on `/_next/image`. *Reasoning.* The image optimizer caches transformed variants in the Node process by default. With multiple instances behind a round-robin load balancer, each instance has a different cache, so the same URL is transformed repeatedly, and cold instances are slow — which is exactly what "sometimes blurry/slow" looks like. The fix is not to disable optimization but to make the cache shared and the headers correct: immutable, long-lived caching for content-addressed URLs, and a stable cache key. *Trade-offs.* A shared cache adds latency (one hop) and operational cost; disabling optimization and using `<img>` loses AVIF/WebP and responsive `srcset` and reintroduces CLS risk on any image you forget to size. *Failure scenario* to avoid: someone "fixes" flakiness by setting `unoptimized: true`, which removes the flakiness and the CLS regression returns quietly a month later, now attributed to the redesign. *Prevention.* Monitor optimizer cache hit rate and p95 transform latency, budget image bytes per page in CI, and keep CLS in the release gate.

**Mistakes:** `priority` on many images; missing `sizes` with `fill`; serving a 4 MB original because `remotePatterns` wasn't configured so it silently fell back; loading a full CJK font unsubsetted; `dangerouslyAllowSVG` for user-uploaded SVGs; ignoring `alt`.

**Mini task:** build a gallery page with 24 remote images. Measure total transferred bytes and CLS. Convert to `next/image` with `sizes`, `priority` on exactly one image, and `placeholder="blur"`. Re-measure. Then swap `next/image` for a plain `<img>` with a CDN `srcset` and confirm the numbers converge — then write the argument for which you'd keep.

## 4.2.10 Edge vs Node Runtime

**Definition:** Every route, page, layout, and middleware in Next.js runs on a runtime. `nodejs` (the default) gives you the full Node API surface; `edge` runs on a lightweight V8-based isolate with Web APIs and much smaller APIs.

```ts
export const runtime = 'edge';   // per route/layout; middleware is edge by default
```

| Concern | Node runtime | Edge runtime |
|---|---|---|
| Startup / cold start | Slower (process boot, module init) | Very fast (isolate) |
| CPU | Full cores, unbounded-ish | Limited, billed per ms, hard caps |
| Memory | GB-scale heap | ~128 MB–2 GB depending on platform, hard cap |
| Network | Regional, but same region | Deploys to dozens/hundreds of PoPs |
| Node APIs | Everything (`fs`, `node:crypto`, `net`, streams, native modules, TCP servers) | Mostly absent or shimmed: no native addons, no raw TCP, limited `node:crypto`, many `node:*` modules unsupported |
| Web APIs | Available via `undici`/globals | First-class (`fetch`, `Request`, `Response`, `crypto.subtle`, `ReadableStream`, `WebSocket`) |
| DB drivers | Native/pooled drivers (pg, mysql2) | HTTP-based drivers (Prisma accelerate, PlanetScale, Neon over HTTP, Drizzle HTTP) |
| Long CPU work | Fine | Badly penalized and time-limited |
| Streaming responses | Yes | Yes (edge has good streaming support) |
| Cost model | Per-instance time | Per-invocation + duration, usually cheaper for cold, spiky traffic |
| Cold-cache DB calls | Fast (same region, pooled) | Slow (DB is in one region) — the classic edge latency trap |

**How to choose.**
- **Edge when** the work is: a redirect/rewrite, a cookie or geo check, a JWT verify, a small fetch to a globally distributed API, a response transformation, or an image/asset decision. All of it is latency-bound and stateless.
- **Node when** the work touches: a database with a driver, `fs`, crypto (hashing, bcrypt), long CPU, big payloads, WebSocket servers, native modules, or anything needing connection pooling.
- **The hybrid is normal:** middleware (edge) for cheap gating + a Node route handler for the real work. Or an edge route handler that calls a Node API.
- **Watch out for:** middleware calling a regional database from an edge PoP (adds 100–300 ms and burns invocations); `next/image` optimization running on the edge (supported, but capacity-limited); heavy npm packages pulled into an edge bundle (build-time failure or a runtime shim that misbehaves); streaming large responses through edge memory limits.

**Questions**
1. What's the difference between the Node and Edge runtimes in Next?
2. When would you choose Edge for a route handler?
3. Why is an edge middleware calling a database a problem?
4. What's missing in the Edge runtime?
5. How does the cost model differ?
6. Middleware: which runtime, and can you change it?
7. When does Edge hurt more than help?
8. How do you check which runtime a route uses?
9. Streaming on the Edge: supported?
10. Where does `next/image` optimization run?

**Model answer, Q3:** "An edge function runs in a PoP near the user but your database lives in one region. Every edge invocation that queries the DB crosses an ocean and opens a connection that a pool cannot keep warm across PoPs, so you pay 100–300 ms of network latency on *every* request plus connection exhaustion risk and per-invocation billing. The rule is: edge for stateless, latency-critical decisions (redirects, JWT verification, cookie gating, a global CDN API call); Node for anything that touches a regional resource. If you need both, gate in edge middleware and do the data work in a Node route handler or a separate API."

**Mistakes:** putting a DB query in middleware; using the Edge runtime for CPU-heavy work; pulling native modules into an edge bundle; assuming streaming behaves identically on both; not noticing that the same route can have different runtimes in different environments.

**Mini task:** create the same `/api/report` route twice — once `runtime: 'edge'` with an HTTP-based DB call, once `runtime: 'nodejs'` with a pooled driver. Measure cold and warm latency for both from a client far from the DB region, and compute cost per 1000 requests. Write the recommendation with numbers.

## 4.2.11 SEO and Metadata

**Definition:** Metadata API — `export const metadata` / `generateMetadata` — plus Open Graph/Twitter cards, JSON-LD structured data, `sitemap.ts`, `robots.ts`, and canonical URLs.

```tsx
// app/products/[id]/page.tsx
import type { Metadata } from 'next';

export async function generateMetadata({ params }: { params: Promise<{ id: string }> }): Promise<Metadata> {
  const { id } = await params;
  const product = await getProduct(id);          // dedupe: may share a cache() with the page
  if (!product) return {};
  return {
    title: { default: 'Acme Store', template: '%s · Acme Store' },
    description: product.summary,
    alternates: { canonical: `/products/${product.slug}` },
    openGraph: {
      type: 'website',
      title: product.name,
      description: product.summary,
      url: `/products/${product.slug}`,
      images: [{ url: product.ogImage, width: 1200, height: 630, alt: product.name }],
    },
    twitter: { card: 'summary_large_image', title: product.name, images: [product.ogImage] },
    robots: product.discontinued ? { index: false, follow: true } : undefined,
  };
}
```
```ts
// app/sitemap.ts — returns URLs; revalidate with export const revalidate = 3600
import type { MetadataRoute } from 'next';
export default async function sitemap(): Promise<MetadataRoute.Sitemap> {
  const products = await db.product.findMany({ select: { slug: true, updatedAt: true } });
  return [
    { url: 'https://acme.example', lastModified: new Date(), changeFrequency: 'daily', priority: 1 },
    ...products.map(p => ({ url: `https://acme.example/products/${p.slug}`, lastModified: p.updatedAt })),
  ];
}
```
```ts
// app/robots.ts
import type { MetadataRoute } from 'next';
export default function robots(): MetadataRoute.Robots {
  return {
    rules: [{ userAgent: '*', allow: '/', disallow: ['/api/', '/admin/'] }],
    sitemap: 'https://acme.example/sitemap.xml',
    host: 'https://acme.example',
  };
}
```
```tsx
// JSON-LD: a <script type="application/ld+json"> rendered by a Server Component.
// Values must be JSON-escaped: JSON.stringify handles </script>; never interpolate raw text.
export function ProductJsonLd({ p }: { p: Product }) {
  const data = {
    '@context': 'https://schema.org',
    '@type': 'Product',
    name: p.name,
    image: [p.imageUrl],
    description: p.summary,
    sku: p.sku,
    offers: { '@type': 'Offer', price: (p.priceCents / 100).toFixed(2), priceCurrency: 'USD', availability: 'https://schema.org/InStock' },
    aggregateRating: p.rating ? { '@type': 'AggregateRating', ratingValue: p.rating, reviewCount: p.reviewCount } : undefined,
  };
  return <script type="application/ld+json" dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: JSON.stringify(data) }} />;
}
```

**Why CSR-only content is a problem for crawlers.** Some crawlers execute JavaScript; many social scrapers and preview bots do not. Content that only exists after hydration is invisible to them: link previews are blank, and content discovery depends on rendering budget. Even for JS-capable crawlers, client-rendered content is later and less reliably indexed, and hydration failures turn your page into an empty shell for that crawler. Therefore: server-render anything indexable, keep metadata in the head server-side (streaming metadata does this progressively), and give crawlers real URLs via `sitemap.ts`. Also handle `hreflang` for locales and canonical URLs to prevent duplicate-content dilution (canonical entry: PHASE 7 → Networking → CORS & caching; canonical entry: PHASE 6 → Indexing for the database-side analogue).

**Questions**
1. How do you set page metadata dynamically?
2. What are Open Graph and Twitter cards for?
3. How do you generate a sitemap without hand-writing it?
4. Why does CSR-only content hurt SEO?
5. What is JSON-LD and what is it used for?
6. When do you need `robots.ts` vs a static file?
7. What is a canonical URL for, and what goes wrong without it?
8. How do you handle per-locale metadata?
9. What is streaming metadata?
10. Where should SEO-critical content live?

**Model answer, Q4 (senior/staff framing):** *Context.* A marketing page renders its content only after a client-side fetch; traffic is fine but organic traffic is flat and link previews are blank. *Decision.* Server-render or prerender anything indexable, and treat client fetching as an enhancement. *Reasoning.* Crawlers vary in whether and how well they execute JavaScript; social scrapers generally do not execute it at all; and even JS-capable crawlers allocate limited rendering budget and penalize failures or errors during hydration. Content that depends on hydration is therefore discovered later, indexed less reliably, and never seen at all by non-executing scrapers. The specific failure here — blank link previews — is a direct consequence: the OG tags that would produce the preview are either absent from the initial HTML or the preview scraper sees an empty body. *Trade-offs.* Server-rendering the content costs TTFB and server compute versus a static shell; prerendering (SSG/ISR) gets most of the SEO benefit at near-zero cost but requires build-time or revalidation-time data access, which means a build can fail on stale or unavailable data and dynamic segments need `generateStaticParams`. *Failure scenario.* A redesign converts every page to CSR with `useEffect` fetching; rankings drop 30% over two months, and the diagnosis is confusing because "it works in the browser" and Core Web Vitals in the field are fine. *Prevention.* Put indexable content in the server/prerendered path, assert in CI that key pages contain the expected text in the raw HTML response, keep `generateMetadata` server-side, and monitor crawler logs for 5xx/JS errors.

**Mistakes:** relying on `<title>` in a client component; forgetting canonical URLs with query-param variants; `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` with interpolated user text in JSON-LD (XSS); indexing `?page=2` duplicates; assuming crawlers render.

**Mini task:** build `/products/[slug]` with `generateMetadata` (title template, canonical, OG image, `robots: noindex` for discontinued items), JSON-LD `Product` with `offers`, `sitemap.ts` generated from the DB, and `robots.ts` disallowing `/admin`. Then `curl` the HTML and confirm every tag is in the *initial* response, not added by JS. Finally, run a Google rich-results-style validator or a structured-data linter and fix what it flags.

---

## 4.2.12 Deployment

**Definition:** Deployment is the set of decisions about where the Next.js server runs, how it is built and started, and how caching/CDN layers sit in front of it.

### Vercel vs self-hosted

| Concern | Vercel (or a similar managed platform) | Self-hosted Node / Docker |
|---|---|---|
| Setup | Zero config, preview deploys per PR | You own Dockerfile, build, process manager, autoscaling |
| ISR / on-demand revalidation | Built in; `revalidateTag` invalidates the global cache instantly | You must run a cache store you control and wire invalidation to it |
| Image optimization | Built-in service, global | Local `/_next/image` service; needs memory/CPU and a shared cache |
| Middleware / Edge | Managed edge network | You self-host or use a CDN edge function |
| Caching | Managed CDN with `x-nextjs-cache` observability | Your CDN (Cloudflare, Fastly, CloudFront) plus correct `Cache-Control` |
| Cost | Simple at low volume, can surprise at high volume | Cheaper at scale, but you pay ops |
| Lock-in | High | Low |
| Control | Limited (config file, no custom server runtime) | Total |
| Best for | Fast iteration, marketing sites, small teams, preview-per-PR | Cost at scale, compliance, custom networking, existing k8s |

**Static export.** `output: 'export'` in `next.config.ts` produces a pure static site in `out/`: no Node server, so no SSR, no ISR, no middleware, no Server Actions, no route handlers, no image optimization (or images must be pre-optimized yourself). Use it for docs sites and blogs where everything can be built. Everything dynamic must move to a client fetch or a separate backend.

**Self-hosting gotchas**
1. **`output: 'standalone'`** bundles a minimal server (`node server.js`) with only the traced dependencies — smaller images. Set `HOSTNAME`, `PORT`, and copy `public/` and `.next/static` alongside.
2. **Static assets must be served by your CDN/reverse proxy**, not the Node process: `/_next/static/*` are content-hashed and immutable; cache them for a year. Serve HTML with `no-cache` so the CDN revalidates.
3. **The image optimizer needs a shared cache** across replicas (see 4.2.9) and enough memory (`sharp`/libvips). If you don't want to run it, disable it and use a CDN image service.
4. **ISR/on-demand revalidation needs a persistent cache store** shared across instances, otherwise invalidation is per-instance and your behaviour is nondeterministic under load.
5. **Don't cache per-user HTML.** Any route reading cookies must send `Cache-Control: private, no-store` and be excluded from CDN caching.
6. **Streaming requires disabling response buffering** in nginx (`proxy_buffering off;`) or your Suspense benefits vanish.
7. **Timeouts and long actions:** platforms kill long requests; enqueue instead.
8. **Env vars:** build-time (`NEXT_PUBLIC_*`) are inlined into the bundle and are public; runtime secrets must be read server-side only.

### Preview deployments
A good CI setup builds the app, runs lint/typecheck/unit/e2e, deploys a preview with a real database branch or fixture data, runs Playwright against it, then merges. The interview-relevant point: preview environments are how you catch "works locally, breaks in production" caching and env problems before users do.

**Questions**
1. Vercel vs self-hosted: what decides it?
2. What does `output: 'standalone'` do?
3. What breaks with `output: 'export'`?
4. How should you set cache headers for `/_next/static` vs HTML?
5. Why does self-hosting ISR get flaky?
6. What nginx setting matters for streaming?
7. How do you prevent a CDN from caching per-user pages?
8. What are preview deployments for?
9. How do you handle secrets with `NEXT_PUBLIC_`?
10. What is the image-optimizer memory story?

**Model answer, Q1:** "It is a cost/complexity/lock-in tradeoff. Managed platforms give you global CDN, integrated ISR, the image optimizer, edge middleware, and PR previews with near-zero ops, at the price of per-invocation pricing above free tiers and framework lock-in. Self-hosting gives you full control, predictable cost at scale, and the ability to sit inside existing compliance and networking constraints, but you now own the CDN cache headers, the image optimizer's memory and shared cache, the ISR cache store, and streaming buffering. My default for a new product is managed; my default for a large existing platform with k8s and traffic is self-hosted."

**Mistakes:** caching HTML at the CDN without `private` markers; buffering streaming responses; running the image optimizer on every replica with no shared cache; expecting on-demand revalidation to be global on a multi-replica self-hosted setup; putting secrets in `NEXT_PUBLIC_*`; forgetting `public/` and `.next/static` in the Docker image.

**Mini task:** containerize with `output: 'standalone'`, put nginx in front, and configure: immutable 1-year caching for `/_next/static`, `no-cache` for HTML, `proxy_buffering off`, and a `/health` route on the Node process. Then verify a deploy: (1) assets are cached, (2) HTML revalidates, (3) a Suspense page streams (watch `curl -N`), (4) a per-user page is never CDN-cached, (5) memory stays flat under image load. Write the cache header table you settled on.

---

## 4.2.13 Meta-Frameworks, React 19 Interplay, and Partial Prerendering

**What "meta-framework" means.** A framework that owns routing, rendering strategy, data fetching, and build-time optimization, driven by conventions. The category's real split:

| Framework | Model | Rendering strengths | Choose it when |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Next.js** | React, RSC, server-first | Hybrid rendering (static/ISR/SSR/CSR) in one app; huge ecosystem; strong SEO defaults | You want maximum flexibility and are on React/TypeScript |
| **React Router 7 (framework mode)** | React, loaders/actions, based on Remix | Simple mental model: `loader` per route, nested, forms as first-class; less framework magic | You want the App Router's ergonomics without the caching-opacity; you know Remix; you like explicit data flow |
| **Remix** | Predecessor of RR7 framework mode | Same loader/action model; mature; the original "web-first" framework | Existing Remix apps; you value the explicit loader/action split |
| **Astro** | Islands architecture; multi-framework | Ships zero JS by default; islands; content-focused; great Markdown/MDX | Content sites, marketing, docs, blogs; you want minimal JS and don't need a big app |
| **Nuxt** | Vue | Analogous hybrid rendering, SSR/SSG/SPA modes | You are on Vue |
| **SvelteKit** | Svelte | Compiles away; very small runtime; excellent DX | You are on Svelte, or you want the smallest client runtime |
| **SolidStart** | Solid | Fine-grained reactivity, no virtual DOM | You want fine-grained perf and like Solid |

**The honest comparison.** No one of these is "better". They differ in where they place the abstraction boundary: React Router 7 makes data flow explicit (`loader` returns data, `action` handles mutations — you can read the whole request lifecycle), Next.js makes data flow implicit (data is fetched wherever it is rendered, cached by rules you must learn). Next wins on ecosystem, image/edge/CDN integration, and hiring pool; React Router wins on explicitness and a smaller conceptual surface; Astro wins on shipping zero JS; SvelteKit wins on client runtime size. A senior answer names the tradeoff, not a winner.

**React 19 interplay.** React 19 formalizes what Next's Server Actions were built on:
- **`useActionState`** replaces `useState` + manual pending for form actions: you get a state object that includes `pending`, the returned state, and the error.
- **`useFormStatus`** gives `pending` inside a child of the form (also `data`, `method`, `action`), so you can build a submit button that knows it is submitting.
- **Form `action` prop**: `<form action={fn}>` where `fn` can be a Server Action or a client function. Server Actions *are* React 19 form actions, which is why progressive enhancement works: without JS the browser posts to the action endpoint.
- **`useOptimistic`** for optimistic UI driven by an action's pending state — the framework gives you a temporary optimistic value that reverts if the action fails, which is a better default than hand-rolled rollback.
- **`use()`** reads a promise (including one created during render) and suspends — the basis for reading data without `async` in the component, useful in client components.
- **Streaming and transitions** are more capable in React 19: `<Suspense>` boundaries can be revealed out of order, and transitions are more interruptible, which is what makes Next's navigation feel instant.

```tsx
'use client';
import { useActionState, useOptimistic } from 'react';

type Msg = { text: string; pending?: boolean; error?: string };

export function Chat({ roomId, send }: { roomId: string; send: (fd: FormData) => Promise<Msg[]> }) {
  const [messages, formAction, isPending] = useActionState<Msg[], FormData>(send, []);
  const [optimistic, addOptimistic] = useOptimistic(
    messages,
    (state, newMsg: Msg) => [...state, { ...newMsg, pending: true }],
  );

  return (
    <ul aria-live="polite">
      {optimistic.map((m, i) => (
        <li key={i} style={{ opacity: m.pending ? 0.6 : 1 }}>
          {m.text}{m.error ? ` — ${m.error}` : ''}
        </li>
      ))}
      <form action={formAction}>
        <input name="text" />
        <button disabled={isPending}>Send</button>
      </form>
    </ul>
  );
}
```

**Partial Prerendering (PPR) — awareness.** The idea: split a route's render into a *static shell* (prerendered at build time, cacheable globally) and *dynamic holes* (streamed per request inside `<Suspense>`). A product page's shell — layout, nav, product title, image — is prerendered and served from the edge; the personalized "your cart" widget and live price are holes streamed per request. Result: static-cacheable latency with per-request freshness. It requires explicit opt-in via cache directives/`<Suspense>` boundaries (in newer Next versions, Cache Components / `dynamicIO` and `'use cache'`), and it is still evolving — verify the current status in the Next.js docs (Cache Components, PPR preview/blog) rather than asserting specifics. The interview-worthy insight: PPR makes the caching boundary a *developer-marked region* rather than a per-route setting, which is the same philosophical shift as RSC: explicit boundaries beat implicit global defaults.

**Questions**
1. What is a meta-framework?
2. How does React Router 7 framework mode differ from Next's data model?
3. When is Astro the right choice?
4. What does React 19 add for forms and actions?
5. What is `useActionState` and how does it differ from `useFormStatus`?
6. What does `useOptimistic` do, and is it safer than manual rollback?
7. What is `use()` for?
8. What is Partial Prerendering and what problem does it solve?
9. Why is "which framework is best" a bad interview answer?
10. What would make you leave Next.js for another meta-framework?
11. How does the App Router's implicit caching compare with React Router's explicit loaders?

**Model answer, Q10 (senior/staff framing):** *Context.* I'm the frontend lead on a large React/TypeScript product with heavy e-commerce traffic, a small team, and an existing Next.js codebase. *Decision.* I would not migrate on preference; I would migrate only if a specific, measurable blocker appears. *Reasoning.* The migration cost is not the framework — it is re-deriving caching semantics, auditing every data access, retraining the team, and re-tuning SEO and Core Web Vitals with a real regression budget. That cost is justified only when a capability we need is unavailable or a specific defect is unfixable: for example, if we need a data model we can fully understand and audit (React Router's `loader`/`action` split makes every request's data flow explicit and greppable, whereas Next's caching is rule-based and has changed defaults across versions), or if the App Router's implicit caching has repeatedly produced production staleness incidents we cannot prevent. *Trade-offs.* On the other side: React Router gives up the managed edge/CDN/image/ISR integration and the hiring pool; keeping Next means living with its abstractions and version churn. *Failure scenario.* A rewrite "because the framework is wrong" ships three months late, misses the holiday traffic window, and the new app has worse LCP because nobody carried over image and streaming work. *Alternative.* Incremental mitigation inside Next: add explicit caching documentation, tag discipline, and staleness alerts; adopt new features route by route; evaluate a *carve-out* (a docs or marketing site in Astro, a widget in React Router) rather than a whole-app rewrite. *Signals I would actually act on:* repeated data-leak or staleness incidents, a framework limitation blocking a roadmap item with real revenue behind it, or hiring/velocity data showing the framework is the constraint.

**Mistakes:** declaring one framework universally best; ignoring that migrations cost behavior, not just syntax; over-claiming PPR as stable; treating `useOptimistic` as a substitute for a server-authoritative re-read.

**Mini task:** build the same CRUD "notes" app twice: once in Next.js App Router with Server Actions and ISR, once in React Router 7 framework mode with a `loader` and an `action`. Diff them on: lines of data-fetch code, where caching lives, how a mutation invalidates a read, and how hard it is to reason about staleness. Then write one paragraph justifying your choice for a specific product, citing evidence from the exercise.

---

---

# PHASE 4, PART 2 (CONTINUED): State Management, Data Fetching, and Routing

Part 2 opened with 4.2 (meta-frameworks). This file covers the three sections that turn a working React app into an app that survives contact with a real backend, a real URL bar, and a second engineer: **4.3 State Management**, **4.4 Data Fetching and Forms**, **4.5 Routing Strategies**. Everything assumes 4.1 (rendering model, hooks, Context, `useSyncExternalStore`, Suspense) and 4.2 (RSC, caching on the server, loaders). Where the canonical entry lives elsewhere, I say so instead of repeating it: `canonical entry: 4.1.14 Context`, `canonical entry: 4.2.5 Data Fetching and Caching`, `canonical entry: 4.6.5 Folder structure`.

The through-line of these three sections: **every piece of state has an owner, a lifetime, and an invalidation story.** Most frontend state bugs are not rendering bugs — they are ownership bugs: two sources of truth for the same fact, a cache with no invalidation policy, a URL that does not describe what the screen shows. Learn to ask "who owns this, who may write it, and what makes it stale?" and most of the library-choice debate dissolves.

Two framings to keep in your head while reading:

1. **Server state is not state.** It is a *cache of a remote truth* owned by the server. Treat it with cache semantics (staleness, invalidation, dedupe, GC), not with `useState` semantics. This one distinction answers most "which library" questions.
2. **The URL is state.** Anything a user can share, bookmark, or survive a refresh with belongs in the URL. Anything that should reset on navigation must *not* be in the URL.

---

# 4.3 State Management

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.1.14 (Context), 4.1.10 (memoisation)

**What state management actually is.** Not "a place to put data". It is the set of decisions that answer four questions for each piece of data in the application:

1. **Where does it live?** Component, lifted parent, context, a global store, the URL, or the server.
2. **Who may write it?** One component, or many (and does order matter)?
3. **How does it change?** Immutably replaced, mutated in place, pushed by an event, or pulled by a render.
4. **What invalidates it?** A refetch, a full reload, a sign-out, a TTL, or never.

A library is just a default answer to (1) and (4). Choosing well means choosing the *right default*, or choosing no default at all.

**The three-layer model used throughout this section**

```
Layer 3  Remote truth        the database                owned by the server
Layer 2  Cache of remote     TanStack Query / RTK Query   invalidated, TTL'd, deduped
Layer 1  Client state        route params, form input,    owned by the browser,
                             UI flags, wizard step        dies with the tab (or not)
Layer 0  Derived             totals, filtered lists,      recomputed, never stored,
                             canSubmit, breadcrumbs       never a source of truth
```

Errors are almost always a violation of this model: storing layer 3 in layer 1, storing layer 0 in layer 1, or letting two layers write the same fact.

---

## 4.3.1 The state taxonomy: local UI state, derived state, server state, URL state, global client state

🟢 Beginner · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.1.4 (`useState`), 4.1.7 (effects vs events)

**Definition:** State taxonomy is the practice of *classifying every piece of state in the app* into exactly one of five kinds, each with a different owner, lifetime, persistence rule, and tool. Getting the classification right removes most state-management debate, because each kind has a defensible home and the home is not negotiable by preference.

| Kind | Definition | Owner | Survives refresh? | Survives share/back? | Tool | Canonical example |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Local UI state** | Transient interaction state of one component subtree | The component | No | No | `useState`, `useReducer` | Is the popover open? Which tab? Draft text in one field? |
| **Derived state** | A pure function of other state | Nobody — it is *computed* | n/a | n/a | computed during render, `useMemo` if expensive | `items.filter(...).length`, `canSubmit`, `total`, `isEmailValid` |
| **Server state** | A cache of data owned by a remote server | The server; client holds a cache | Yes (re-fetched) | Yes | TanStack Query / SWR / RTK Query / RSC loaders | The user list, a product, an order |
| **URL state** | Anything that describes *which view* the user is looking at | The URL bar | Yes | Yes — this is the point | `useSearchParams`, route params, hash | `?tab=billing&page=2&q=shoes&sort=asc` |
| **Global client state** | Client-only state that genuinely needs to cross subtree boundaries | A store (or a carefully split context) | Usually yes (if persisted) | Only if encoded in the URL | Zustand / Redux Toolkit / Jotai / MobX | Current user preferences, theme, a multi-step wizard's progress, an unsaved-changes flag, feature entitlements |

**The mechanics of why the classification matters.** Each kind has different failure modes, and only the taxonomy tells you which failure to expect:

- **Local UI state** fails when you *lift it too far*. The classic symptom is prop drilling six levels to set a boolean. Fix: composition, a custom hook with its own state, or colocate the state.
- **Derived state** fails when you *store* it in `useState` and keep it in sync with an effect. This creates a second source of truth that is wrong for exactly as long as the effect takes to run — and wrong forever if the dependency is missed. Fix: delete the state and compute it.
- **Server state** fails when you store it in `useState`. Then you own caching, deduplication, staleness, garbage collection, retries, and cross-component sharing, badly. Fix: use a query cache.
- **URL state** fails when you keep it in a store. Then the back button breaks, the page cannot be shared, refresh loses it, and SEO/analytics cannot see it. Fix: put it in the URL.
- **Global client state** fails when it becomes a dumping ground for layer 2 and 3. Fix: enforce the boundary — a store must not hold server data.

```tsx
// The same feature, correctly classified. Note what is NOT in the store.
import { useState, useMemo } from 'react';
import { useSearchParams } from 'react-router-dom';
import { useUsers } from './api/users';        // TanStack Query: layer 2

type Filters = { q: string; role: 'all' | 'admin' | 'member'; page: number };

// Layer 1 — local, dies with the component
const [selectedId, setSelectedId] = useState<string | null>(null);
const [isInspectorOpen, setInspectorOpen] = useState(false);

// Layer 4 — URL: shareable, back-button-correct, SSR-visible
const [params, setParams] = useSearchParams();
const filters: Filters = useMemo(
  () => ({
    q: params.get('q') ?? '',
    role: (params.get('role') as Filters['role']) ?? 'all',
    page: Math.max(1, Number(params.get('page') ?? 1)),
  }),
  [params],
);
function setFilters(next: Partial<Filters>) {
  const merged = { ...filters, ...next, page: next.page ?? 1 };
  setParams(
    { q: merged.q || null, role: merged.role === 'all' ? null : merged.role, page: merged.page > 1 ? String(merged.page) : null },
    { replace: true },   // replace, so typing in a filter box doesn't fill history
  );
}

// Layer 2 — server: cached, deduped, invalidated
const usersQuery = useUsers({ q: filters.q, role: filters.role, page: filters.page });

// Layer 0 — derived: computed, never stored
const visible = useMemo(
  () => (usersQuery.data?.users ?? []).filter((u) => (filters.role === 'all' ? true : u.role === filters.role)),
  [usersQuery.data, filters.role],
);
const isEmpty = usersQuery.isSuccess && visible.length === 0;
const canDelete = selectedId !== null && !usersQuery.isFetching;
```

**When to use each, and when not to.**

- Local UI state: default for anything one subtree cares about. Do **not** use for: anything two distant components need (that is lifted, or a store, or the URL), anything derivable, or anything another engineer will need to find (put it in a feature folder, not scattered `useState`s).
- Derived state: always prefer computing. The only reason to `useMemo` is cost; the only reason to keep it in state is that it is *not* actually derived (it was user input).
- Server state: always via a query library or a framework loader. "Always" except in throwaway demos and tests.
- URL state: anything shareable, bookmarkable, or that should survive refresh; also anything that changes what the *server* renders (SEO). Not: secrets, and not transient hover/animation flags.
- Global client state: last resort after lifting and Context fail. Justify it with a concrete requirement ("five routes deep, changes at 60 Hz, and never persisted").

**Trade-offs.** A taxonomy with five buckets costs a little classification discipline up front and buys: no stale-copy bugs, back-button correctness for free, shareable URLs for free, cache hits for free, and a codebase where a reader can *guess* where any value lives. The cost is that you sometimes route trivial values through the URL (verbose URLs) or keep tiny state in a store (global mutable surface). Both are acceptable; debugging two sources of truth for one fact is not.

**Production and operational.** Persisted client state (theme, sidebar collapsed, table column widths) should be versioned in its storage key (`colwidths:v2`) so a schema change does not crash old clients — see 4.3.8. Any client-side entitlement or flag read by a global store must be treated as *untrusted*: it gates UI, never authorisation (cross-ref 4.6.9). Instrument state migrations: log how many sessions hit the migration path.

**Common mistakes.**
- Storing a *filtered list* in state next to the unfiltered list ("keep the same reference when unchanged" is a symptom of a duplication bug).
- Storing `isLoading` / `error` next to the data instead of using the query's own status object — you now have three places that can disagree.
- Keeping page/filter state in a store "just for the session", so it survives navigation into a route that should have been fresh.
- Using `useState` + `useEffect` to mirror a prop into state instead of using a `key` or the prop.
- Classifying a WebSocket buffer as local UI state.

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "Where does each of these five values live in your app, and why?" (they want the taxonomy, not "in Redux")
- "What is derived state and what goes wrong when you store it?"
- "How do you decide something is server state?"
- "Your filter resets when the user clicks a row and comes back — whose bug is that?"
- "Why is it a mistake to store server data in a global store?"

**Mistakes:** naming five kinds and then putting all five in one store; calling derived state "state"; using a store as a cache without a staleness policy; URL state that includes anything the user should not see.

---

## 4.3.2 Context: the right use, the wrong use, and the performance cliff

🟢 Beginner · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.1.14 (Context propagation)

**Definition:** Context is a *push-based* dependency-injection and read-through-broadcast channel: a provider publishes one value; every descendant that calls `useContext` for that context is notified when the published value changes identity. It solves "get a value to a distant descendant without prop drilling". It is not a state manager, and using it as one is the single most common React performance incident.

**How it works mechanically.** Three facts explain everything about Context:

1. **Subscription is by context, not by prop.** When a Provider's `value` prop changes (`Object.is` fails against the previous render's value), React walks its subtree and marks *every fiber that reads that context* for re-render. `React.memo` is irrelevant: memo compares props, and a context broadcast is not a props change.
2. **Propagation is a tree walk, not a subscription registry.** With many providers/consumers this is why React can start tearing across concurrent rendering — hence `useSyncExternalStore` (see below) as the safe way to expose an *external* mutable store.
3. **`value` identity is the change signal.** `value={{ user }}` creates a new object every render, so *every* consumer re-renders on *every* provider render, regardless of whether `user` changed. This is the performance cliff.

```tsx
// The three anti-patterns, then the three fixes.

// ANTI-PATTERN 1 — new object identity every render: every consumer re-renders, always.
function App({ children }: { children: ReactNode }) {
  const [user, setUser] = useState<User | null>(null);
  const [theme, setTheme] = useState<'light' | 'dark'>('light');
  return <Ctx.Provider value={{ user, setUser, theme, setTheme }}>{children}</Ctx.Provider>;
}

// FIX 1 — memoize the value, and split by update frequency (two contexts, not one).
const UserCtx = createContext<User | null>(null);
const UserDispatchCtx = createContext<Dispatch<SetStateAction<User | null>>>(() => {});
const ThemeCtx = createContext<'light' | 'dark'>('light');

function App({ children }: { children: ReactNode }) {
  const [user, setUser] = useState<User | null>(null);
  const [theme, setTheme] = useState<'light' | 'dark'>('light');
  return (
    <UserCtx.Provider value={user}>
      {/* dispatch identity is stable for the component's lifetime, so this NEVER invalidates consumers */}
      <UserDispatchCtx.Provider value={setUser}>
        <ThemeCtx.Provider value={theme}>{children}</ThemeCtx.Provider>
      </UserDispatchCtx.Provider>
    </UserCtx.Provider>
  );
}

// ANTI-PATTERN 2 — one high-churn context above a large tree.
// A cart-badge counter that updates on every mouse-move of a live region sits above the whole app,
// so every keystroke anywhere re-renders every component below it.
//
// FIX 2 — push the churn down to the leaf that needs it (context by slice, not by app), or
// move the hot value into a store with selectors so only subscribed components update.
const CartCountCtx = createContext<number>(0);

// ANTI-PATTERN 3 — value + everything derived from it in one provider.
const { total, tax, grandTotal } = useMemo(() => computeTotals(lines), [lines]);
<CheckoutCtx.Provider value={{ lines, total, tax, grandTotal }}>   // typing in an address field re-renders Totals
```

**The escape hatch: context over an external store.** When you need Context's ergonomics (a hook, no prop threading) but not its broadcast behaviour, publish a `useSyncExternalStore` subscription through a context whose value is *the store object itself* — that object is stable for the app's lifetime, so nothing ever invalidates. This is the pattern React Router, Jotai, Zustand, and Redux's `useSelector` all use internally.

```tsx
// The canonical "context as a typed global store" you should be able to write from memory.
import { createContext, useCallback, useContext, useMemo, useRef, useSyncExternalStore } from 'react';

export type Listener = () => void;
export type Store<T> = { getState: () => T; setState: (updater: T | ((prev: T) => T)) => void; subscribe: (l: Listener) => () => void };

export function createStore<T>(initial: T): Store<T> {
  let state = initial;
  const listeners = new Set<Listener>();
  return {
    getState: () => state,
    setState(updater) {
      const next = typeof updater === 'function' ? (updater as (p: T) => T)(state) : updater;
      if (Object.is(next, state)) return;               // bail out: identity is the change signal
      state = next;
      listeners.forEach((l) => l());                     // synchronous notify; React batches the renders
    },
    subscribe(l) { listeners.add(l); return () => { listeners.delete(l); }; },
  };
}

const StoreCtx = createContext<Store<AppState> | null>(null);

export function StoreProvider({ store, children }: { store: Store<AppState>; children: ReactNode }) {
  return <StoreCtx.Provider value={store}>{children}</StoreCtx.Provider>;  // value === store: stable
}

export function useStore<T>(selector: (s: AppState) => T): T {
  const store = useContext(StoreCtx);
  if (!store) throw new Error('useStore must be used inside <StoreProvider>');
  // getSnapshot must return a stable value for unchanged state, or React loops.
  // `selector` must therefore be cheap and pure, and must not build new objects unless memoised.
  return useSyncExternalStore(store.subscribe, () => selector(store.getState()), () => selector(store.getState()));
}
```

Note the third argument to `useSyncExternalStore`: the server snapshot. In an SSR app the client must render the same first output as the server sent, so the server snapshot is whatever was serialised into the page. For *derived* state you usually return a constant and then re-render after hydration; for *persisted* state you return the value from the server payload (see 4.3.8).

**When to use Context and when not to.**

| Use Context for | Do not use Context for |
|---|---|
| Theme, locale, i18n, routing prefix — values that change rarely | Anything that changes at interaction frequency (scroll, drag, keystrokes) |
| A dependency you inject (an `api` client, an `analytics` function) | Prop drilling for one value that two siblings need — lift it instead |
| A stable service object (store, `fetch` wrapper, router instance) | Replacing a data-fetching layer (no caching, no dedupe, no staleness) |
| Splitting a value that must be readable deep without threading props | Global mutable state with many writers and order-dependent transitions |

**Trade-offs.** Context is ~zero-dependency, type-safe, built in, and the right answer for roughly 30% of "shared state" needs. Its costs: broadcast invalidation (a cliff, not a slope), no devtools, no middleware, no time travel, no persistence, and no way to subscribe to a *slice*. The upside of a store is the mirror image: precise subscriptions and tooling, paid for with a dependency and a ceremony that small apps do not need.

**Production and operational.** Count consumers. `grep -rn "useContext(" src | wc -l` and if one context has 200 consumers, it will eventually be the cause of an INP regression. Split providers by route subtree so a provider mounted at `/settings` cannot invalidate the dashboard. Never pass a *store instance* you mutate during render. In SSR, serialise the initial store state once and hydrate from it (`window.__PRELOADED_STATE__`) rather than re-running effects to rebuild it, or you get a hydration mismatch or a flash of empty state.

**Common mistakes.**
- `value={{ ... }}` without `useMemo` — the cliff, in its purest form.
- One context holding both `items` and `dispatch`, so dispatch-only consumers re-render on every item change.
- Context for server data: no dedupe, so ten components using the same context fire ten requests, or one request whose result nobody can refresh.
- Setting state on the context *value object* (`ctx.items.push(...)`) — mutating a shared reference that `Object.is` considers unchanged.
- Reading a context in a component you do not control just to get a theme, when CSS custom properties would do it with no re-render at all.

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "Explain exactly why `memo` does not protect a context consumer."
- "Your theme provider wraps the whole app; the app feels sluggish on the settings page. Diagnose."
- "When would you *not* reach for Context even though you could?"
- "How does `useSyncExternalStore` change Context's behaviour, and what does `getServerSnapshot` do?"
- "What is the difference between a stable context value and a memoised one?"

**Mistakes:** assuming Context is scoped to a "component boundary" (it is not — it is subtree-wide); forgetting that reading a context makes the component a consumer even if the read is conditional; using Context as a cache.

---

## 4.3.3 Redux Toolkit: store shape, slices, createSlice, createAsyncThunk, RTK Query

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.1.14 (Context), immutability and identity from 4.1.10

**Definition:** Redux Toolkit (RTK) is an opinionated layer over Redux that (a) removes the boilerplate that made Redux unpopular, (b) makes the store a *single immutable state tree* changed only through serialisable actions, and (c) ships two libraries on top: `createAsyncThunk` for imperative async workflows and RTK Query for declarative server-state fetching with caching, deduplication, invalidation, and normalised caching. RTK 2.x targets Redux 5 and React 18+.

**Store shape — the contract you are signing.** Redux's actual contract has four parts, and every Redux decision follows from them:

```
1. State is a single immutable tree.      store.getState(): RootState
2. Changes are described by plain actions. { type: 'cart/itemAdded', payload: Item }
3. Reducers are pure functions.           (state, action) => newState   — no fetch, no Date.now(), no Math.random()
4. Reading is done through subscriptions  store.subscribe(cb) — which is why useSelector must narrow
```

```ts
// store/index.ts — the shape, wired with the recommended API
import { configureStore, combineReducers } from '@reduxjs/toolkit';
import { setupListeners } from '@reduxjs/toolkit/query';
import { authApi, usersApi } from './api';
import cartReducer from './features/cart/cartSlice';
import uiReducer from './features/ui/uiSlice';

export const rootReducer = combineReducers({
  cart: cartReducer,
  ui: uiReducer,
  // RTK Query mounts its own reducers: `api` holds the query cache, `authApi`/`usersApi` the endpoint registries.
  [usersApi.reducerPath]: usersApi.reducer,
  [authApi.reducerPath]: authApi.reducer,
});

export const store = configureStore({
  reducer: rootReducer,
  middleware: (getDefault) =>
    getDefault({
      serializableCheck: { ignoredActions: [FLUSH, REHYDRATE, PAUSE, PERSIST, PURGE, REGISTER, 'usersApi/executeMutation/pending'] },
      immutableCheck: true,
    }).concat(usersApi.middleware, authApi.middleware, myListenerMiddleware),   // order matters: API middleware first
});

// Refetch on reconnect / focus — one line, and the reason RTK Query feels "correct"
setupListeners(store.dispatch);

export type RootState = ReturnType<typeof store.getState>;
export type AppDispatch = typeof store.dispatch;
export type AppThunk<R = void> = ThunkAction<R, RootState, unknown, UnknownAction>;
```

**Typed hooks — non-negotiable.** `useSelector(state => state.foo)` with an untyped selector is how `any` leaks into a "strictly typed" Redux codebase.

```ts
// store/hooks.ts
import { useDispatch, useSelector, useStore } from 'react-redux';
import type { AppDispatch, RootState } from './index';

export const useAppDispatch = useDispatch.withTypes<AppDispatch>();   // RTK 2 / Redux 5
export const useAppSelector = useSelector.withTypes<RootState>();
export const useAppStore = useStore.withTypes<RootState>();
```

**`createSlice` — one file per feature.** A slice generates action creators, a reducer, and *types for both* from one reducer body, which is why the redux-actions-pattern boilerplate is gone.

```ts
// features/cart/cartSlice.ts
import { createSlice, type PayloadAction } from '@reduxjs/toolkit';
import type { RootState } from '../../store';

export type CartLine = { sku: string; qty: number; unitPrice: number };

export type CartState = {
  linesBySku: Record<string, CartLine>;   // normalized (see 4.3.4)
  couponCode: string | null;
  status: 'idle' | 'loading' | 'succeeded' | 'failed';
  error: string | null;
  _v: number;                            // schema version, for the persistence in 4.3.8
};

const initialState: CartState = {
  linesBySku: {}, couponCode: null, status: 'idle', error: null, _v: 1,
};

const cartSlice = createSlice({
  name: 'cart',
  initialState,
  reducers: {
    lineAdded: {
      reducer(state, action: PayloadAction<{ sku: string; qty: number; unitPrice: number }>) {
        const { sku, qty, unitPrice } = action.payload;
        const prev = state.linesBySku[sku];
        state.linesBySku[sku] = { sku, qty: (prev?.qty ?? 0) + qty, unitPrice };
      },
      // prepare lets the component pass two arguments and keep the action serialisable
      prepare(sku: string, qty = 1, unitPrice = 0) {
        return { payload: { sku, qty, unitPrice } };
      },
    },
    qtyChanged(state, action: PayloadAction<{ sku: string; qty: number }>) {
      const line = state.linesBySku[action.payload.sku];
      if (!line) return;
      if (action.payload.qty <= 0) delete state.linesBySku[action.payload.sku];   // Immer makes this legal
      else line.qty = action.payload.qty;
    },
    couponApplied(state, action: PayloadAction<string | null>) { state.couponCode = action.payload; },
  },
  selectors: {
    selectLines: (s: CartState) => Object.values(s.linesBySku),            // slice-local selectors (4.3.4)
    selectQtyBySku: (sku: string) => (s: CartState) => s.linesBySku[sku]?.qty ?? 0,
  },
  extraReducers: (builder) => {
    // Extra reducers for thunk lifecycle actions. A discriminator-free builder is the recommended style.
    builder
      .addCase(checkoutThunk.pending, (s) => { s.status = 'loading'; s.error = null; })
      .addCase(checkoutThunk.fulfilled, (s, a) => { s.status = 'succeeded'; s.lastOrderId = a.payload.id; })
      .addCase(checkoutThunk.rejected, (s, a) => {
        s.status = 'failed';
        s.error = a.error.message ?? 'Checkout failed';   // rejectedWithValue carries your own message
      });
  },
});

export const { lineAdded, qtyChanged, couponApplied } = cartSlice.actions;
export const { selectLines, selectQtyBySku } = cartSlice.selectors;
export default cartSlice.reducer;
```

Two mechanics worth saying out loud in an interview. **Immer:** `createSlice` wraps your reducer in Immer's `produce`, so "mutating" `state.linesBySku[sku]` is safe — it produces a new tree with structural sharing. That is *why* you can delete keys and why `delete` works at all. **Serialisability:** the default middleware throws in development if an action or state chunk contains a non-serialisable value (a `Date`, a `Map`, a class instance, a function). That check is a feature: it is what makes DevTools time travel, persisted-state migration, and SSR serialisation work at all.

**`createAsyncThunk` — the thunk middleware in a typed wrapper.** Thunk middleware exists because reducers must be pure and cannot perform I/O. A thunk is a function `(dispatch, getState, extra) => Promise` that reducers cannot be, so it can dispatch plain actions around the async work.

```ts
// features/checkout/thunks.ts
import { createAsyncThunk, type AsyncThunkConfig } from '@reduxjs/toolkit';
import type { RootState, AppDispatch } from '../../store';

type ThunkConfig = { state: RootState; dispatch: AppDispatch; extra: { api: ApiClient }; rejectValue: string };

// withTypes() replaces the old `createAsyncThunk<Return, Arg, { state: RootState }>` triple-generic
export const checkoutThunk = createAsyncThunk.withTypes<ThunkConfig>()(
  'checkout/submit',
  async (payload: CheckoutPayload, { rejectWithValue, dispatch, extra, signal, requestId }) => {
    try {
      const order = await extra.api.post<Order>('/orders', payload, { signal, idempotencyKey: requestId });
      dispatch(cartSlice.actions.cleared());
      return order;
    } catch (err) {
      // rejectWithValue surfaces your own message into `action.payload`; anything else lands in `action.error`
      return rejectWithValue(err instanceof ApiError ? err.userMessage : 'Something went wrong');
    }
  },
  {
    condition: (_payload, { getState }) => {
      const cart = getState().cart;
      if (cart.status === 'loading') return false;                 // dedupe: no concurrent checkout
      return Object.keys(cart.linesBySku).length > 0;
    },
    getPendingMeta: () => ({ startedAt: Date.now() }),
    // dispatchConditionRejects defaults to true: a `condition` returning false dispatches `rejected` too
  },
);
```

Key mechanics: the thunk **always** dispatches `pending`, then exactly one of `fulfilled` / `rejected`, and always dispatches `fulfilled` / `rejected` with `requestId` and arg, so late answers can be ignored. `condition` is where dedupe/precondition logic belongs (it does not run for already-rejected duplicates). `extraArgument` is the clean place to inject `fetch`, an API client, or an Sentry client instead of importing them — that is what makes thunks testable.

```ts
// Thunk with no extraArgument: reach for RTK Query instead. This is the important interview point.
export const refetchUser = () => async (dispatch: AppDispatch) => {
  const { data } = await dispatch(usersApi.endpoints.getUser.initiate(userId));
  return data;
};
```

**RTK Query — the modern answer for "Redux + server state".** If your Redux store talks to an API, RTK Query should own that. Hand-written thunks for fetching mean you have hand-written caching, deduplication, polling, invalidation, and loading state — and you will get them wrong.

```ts
// api/usersApi.ts
import { createApi, fetchBaseQuery } from '@reduxjs/toolkit/query/react';

export const usersApi = createApi({
  reducerPath: 'usersApi',
  baseQuery: fetchBaseQuery({
    baseUrl: '/api',
    prepareHeaders: (h) => { const t = localStorage.getItem('token'); return t ? { ...h, authorization: `Bearer ${t}` } : h; },
    credentials: 'include',
  }),
  tagTypes: ['User', 'Invoice'],                       // tags are the invalidation currency
  endpoints: (b) => ({
    getUsers: b.query<UserPage, { q?: string; page?: number }>({
      query: (params) => ({ url: '/users', params }),
      // result: cache the whole page under ONE key; no other hook can read it -> avoids the "two hooks, two requests" bug
      transformResponse: (r: UserPage) => r,
      providesTags: (result, _e, arg) => [
        ...result.users.map(({ id }) => ({ type: 'User' as const, id })),
        { type: 'User', id: 'LIST' },
      ],
      keepUnusedDataFor: 60,                          // seconds a subscriber-less cache entry survives
    }),
    updateUser: b.mutation<User, { id: string; patch: Partial<User> }>({
      query: ({ id, patch }) => ({ url: `/users/${id}`, method: 'PATCH', body: patch }),
      // Optimistic update: the UI updates before the response, and reverts on failure.
      async onQueryStarted({ id, patch }, { dispatch, queryFulfilled, getState }) {
        const patchResult = dispatch(usersApi.util.partialUpdateCache(dispatch, usersApi.endpoints.updateUser.initiate, { id, patch }));
        try {
          await queryFulfilled;
          patchResult.undo();                          // commit the server's authoritative copy
        } catch {
          patchResult.undo();
          dispatch(usersApi.util.invalidateTags([{ type: 'User', id }, 'User']));
        }
      },
      invalidatesTags: (_r, _e, { id }) => [{ type: 'User', id }, 'User'],  // broad-list reads must refresh
    }),
  }),
});

export const { useGetUsersQuery, useUpdateUserMutation } = usersApi;

// Policy: any component can `useGetUsersQuery(sameArgs)` and share one request + one cache entry.
```

RTK Query mechanics you must be able to state:
- **`queryKey` = `(endpointName, serialisedArgs)`.** Two hooks with equal args = one request, one cache entry. Different but overlapping args = two cache entries (the classic "list and detail drift" bug).
- **`providesTags` describes what the entry depends on; `invalidatesTags` invalidates.** This is a *dependency graph*, coarse-to-fine, and it is the whole invalidation story.
- **Cached data is returned for a subscription and re-fetched when stale** (`keepUnusedDataFor` governs eviction of *unsubscribed* entries; `refetchOnMount`/`refetchOnFocus`/`refetchOnReconnect` govern revalidation of used ones).
- **`transformResponse` is for shaping one shape into another.** Never use it to merge two caches; use `providesTags`.
- **`onQueryStarted` is the optimistic-update hook.** It gets `{ dispatch, queryFulfilled, getState }` and its returned promise resolves when the query settles.
- **`.selectFromResult` narrows what a component subscribes to**, which is the single most effective RTK Query performance lever (`selectFromResult: ({ data, isLoading }) => ({ users: data?.users, isLoading })`).

**When to use Redux Toolkit, and when not to.**

Use RTK when: many components mutate the same client state; state transitions are order-dependent or non-trivial; you want every action logged and replayable; multiple teams need to extend one store; you want a place to put *client* cross-cutting workflows (featurisation, entitlement, i18n) without a forest of hooks. Do not use it when: your state fits in two components (a store is scaffolding you will maintain for nothing); you have no order-dependent logic; you are only fetching server data (use RTK Query *or* TanStack Query, not both); you need to ship this week and nobody on the team can debug a Redux trace.

**Trade-offs.** Redux's costs are real: ceremony (file per slice), an indirection between a click and a render, dispatch typing overhead, and a bundle cost you do not pay with Zustand. Its benefits are also real: one mutation path, one DevTools timeline, deterministic replay, serialisability as an architectural constraint, and RTK Query's cache and invalidation graph. Net verdict that a senior engineer would give: *Redux pays for itself in codebases with many writers of shared state and a real DevTools dependency; it is overkill for a five-screen app with two forms.*

**Production and operational.**
- Turn `serializableCheck`/`immutableCheck` off in production (they are dev-only and already are, but be explicit), and add them to CI as *tests*: dispatch every action from every slice in a test and assert no non-serialisable payload.
- DevTools in production behind a flag (`window.__REDUX_DEVTOOLS_EXTENSION__`), never shipped to all users.
- Never store access tokens in the store — it makes every action log a credential leak (cross-ref 4.6.9). Read them from a cookie-scoped `api` instance.
- Persist a *whitelist* of slices with a version and a migration (4.3.8); do not persist the whole store blindly, because RTK Query's cache already has its own GC and double-persisting it doubles the staleness surface.
