- Add `store.subscribe` monitoring: alert on the number of dispatches per interaction (a render loop in a reducer shows up here first).

**Common mistakes.**
- Hand-rolled `fetch` in a thunk *and* RTK Query in the same app — two caches, two staleness policies.
- Passing a class instance or `Date` in an action payload and disabling the serialisable check instead of fixing it.
- Mutating outside a reducer (a reducer that mutates the action payload, or `store.getState().x = 1` anywhere).
- `useSelector(s => s.users.items)` where `items` is an array rebuilt on every state change — the component re-renders forever (see 4.3.4).
- `return`ing a fresh object from a selector: `useSelector` will loop under `useSyncExternalStore`.
- Using `combineReducers` with a top-level array of "panes" instead of keyed slices — the array becomes the bottleneck and the reducer non-deterministic.
- Assuming `store.getState()` is fresh inside a reducer (it is not — use the `state` argument).

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "Why must reducers be pure, and what breaks if they are not?" (time travel, SSR serialisation, StrictMode double-invocation, and correctness under concurrent rendering.)
- "Explain `createAsyncThunk`'s lifecycle and where you would put deduplication." (`condition`.)
- "What problem does RTK Query solve that `createAsyncThunk` does not?" (caching, dedupe by key, invalidation graph, loading/error state, normalised results.)
- "How do you share one cache entry between two components that want different fields?" (same `args`, then `selectFromResult`.)
- "Why does my component re-render on every dispatch?" (selector returns a new reference, or no memoisation.)
- "What is the difference between `keepUnusedDataFor` and `refetchOnFocus`?" (eviction of unsubscribed entries vs revalidation of subscribed ones.)

**Mistakes:** using Redux for everything "because it scales"; writing fetch logic by hand when RTK Query exists; believing `createSlice` makes mutation impossible (Immer handles it, but only *inside* reducers); storing secrets in the store.

---

## 4.3.4 Selectors, reselect, memoisation, and normalized state (normalizr / EntityAdapter)

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.3.3, 4.1.10 (`useMemo`, referential identity)

**Definition:** A *selector* is a pure function from state to a value. *Reselect* is a memoisation library that makes selectors cheap by caching on their inputs. *Normalized state* is the shape where entities live in a keyed map (`byId: Record<Id, T>`) plus an array of ids, instead of being duplicated inside every parent object.

**The problem selectors solve.** Reading state is fine; *re-rendering* because of how you read it is the problem. `useSelector` subscribes to the store and compares the selected value with `Object.is`. So the rule is: **a selector must return a referentially stable value for unchanged inputs, and must return the *narrowest* thing the component needs.**

```ts
// BROKEN — three different bugs in four lines
const a = useAppSelector(s => s.cart.items);              // fine only if items is a stable reference
const b = useAppSelector(s => ({ total: sum(s.cart.items) }));   // ALWAYS a new object → re-render every dispatch
const c = useAppSelector(s => s.users.filter(u => u.active));   // new array every dispatch → infinite re-renders
const d = useAppSelector(s => s.cart.lines.filter(l => l.qty > 0).map(l => l.sku));  // worse
```

```ts
// FIXED — memoised, narrow, and composable. Reselect 5 with lruMemoize.
import { createSelector, createSelectorCreator, lruMemoize } from 'reselect';

const selectCartState  = (s: RootState) => s.cart;
const selectItemMap    = (s: RootState) => s.cart.itemsById;
const selectItemIds    = (s: RootState) => s.cart.itemIds;

export const selectItemList = createSelector([selectItemIds, selectItemMap], (ids, byId) =>
  ids.map((id) => byId[id]),
);

export const selectActiveLines = createSelector(
  [selectItemList],
  (items) => items.filter((i) => i.active),
);

// Cheap selectors used by many others: cache them too, or every dependant recomputes.
const selectCartCount = createSelector([selectItemIds], (ids) => ids.length);

// Composite: takes its own parameter as an input selector factory.
export const makeSelectQtyBySku = () =>
  createSelector(
    [(sku: string) => sku, selectItemMap],
    (sku, byId) => byId[sku]?.qty ?? 0,
  );

// For object/array results, memoize *on both* inputs — cache size 1 is the default and is correct.
const selectCartTotals = createSelectorCreator({ memoizeOptions: { maxSize: 10, resultEqualityCheck: shallowEqual } })(
  [selectItemList, (s: RootState) => s.cart.coupon],
  (items, coupon) => ({ subtotal: sum(items, 'price'), discount: coupon ? 0.1 : 0 }),
);

// In the component: narrow the subscription
const count = useAppSelector(selectCartCount);   // component re-renders ONLY when the count changes
```

**Why "cache size 1" is usually right.** The last-value cache is correct for the common case where the same selector is used with the same state across the tree, and one selector per output shape keeps the graph shallow. `maxSize > 1` is for selectors whose *parameter* varies (a `makeSelectUser(id)` factory with a small `maxSize`). `resultEqualityCheck` is for selectors returning objects/arrays where you want deep-ish comparison — but understand that it makes each call more expensive, so it is a bug-swapper, not a free lunch.

**Parameterised selectors in components — the React version.** For "select one thing by an id" you want a hook, not a selector instance per render:

```ts
import { useMemo } from 'react';
export const useSelectUser = (id: string) =>
  useAppSelector((s) => (s.usersById[id] ?? null));   // primitive-ish value, no new reference
// and the memoised-object case:
export const useSelectUserStats = (id: string) =>
  useAppSelector(useMemo(() => createSelector([selectUserMap, (_s: RootState, i: string) => i], (m, i) => m[i]?.stats ?? EMPTY), [id]));
```

**Normalized state — the shape that makes everything else possible.**

```
DENORMALIZED (the bug)                        NORMALIZED (the fix)
{                                            {
  posts: [{ id:'p1', title, author: {         posts: { byId: { p1: { id:'p1', title, authorId:'u1' } },
          id:'u1', name } }],                   u1: {...} }, order: ['p1'] },
  comments: [{ id:'c1', postId:'p1',            comments: { byId: { c1: {...} }, order: ['c1'] },
          author: { id:'u1', name } }],         users: { byId: { u1: {...} }, order: ['u1'] }
}
```

The denormalized version stores the author three times. Updating the user's name requires three writes; missing one produces "the old name on the comments page". Worse, the copies are *different objects*, so a selector comparing two of them never matches, and every list re-renders. Normalization makes each entity exist once, so a write is a write and identity is identity.

```ts
// Option A — RTK EntityAdapter: the default for Redux. It gives you the shape and the reducers.
import { createEntityAdapter, createSlice, type PayloadAction } from '@reduxjs/toolkit';

export const usersAdapter = createEntityAdapter<User>({
  sortComparer: (a, b) => a.name.localeCompare(b.name),
  selectId: (u) => u.id,
});

const usersSlice = createSlice({
  name: 'users',
  initialState: usersAdapter.getInitialState({ lastFetchedAt: 0 }),
  reducers: {
    userReceived: usersAdapter.addOne,                                  // also: addMany, setOne, setAll
    userUpdated: usersAdapter.updateOne,                                // also: updateMany, upsertOne
    usersLoaded(usersAdapter.setAll),
    userRemoved: usersAdapter.removeOne,                                // also: removeMany, removeAll
    userFollowed(state, action: PayloadAction<{ userId: string; followerId: string }>) {
      // your own extra reducers coexist with the adapter's
      const u = state.entities[action.payload.userId];
      if (u) u.followerIds.push(action.payload.followerId);
    },
  },
});

export const usersSelectors = usersAdapter.getSelectors<RootState>((s) => s.users);
export const { userReceived, userUpdated, usersLoaded, userRemoved, userFollowed } = usersSlice.actions;
export default usersSlice.reducer;

// Adapter surface worth memorising: getInitialState, addOne, addMany, addManyNoUpdate, updateOne,
// updateMany, upsertOne(s), setOne, setAll, setMany, removeOne, removeMany, removeAll,
// sortComparer, selectId, getSelectors(state) -> { selectIds, selectEntities, selectAll, selectTotal, selectById }.
// With sortComparer, selectIds/selectAll come back SORTED, so your component never sorts.

// Option B — normalizr, when the payload arrives from an arbitrary API (the classic REST shape).
import { normalize, schema } from 'normalizr';
const user = new schema.Entity('users');
const comment = new schema.Entity('comments', { commenter: user });
const post = new schema.Entity('posts', { author: user, comments: [comment] });
const result = normalize(apiResponse, { posts: [post], comments: [comment] });
// -> { result: [{ id:'p1', author:'u1', comments:['c1'] }, ...], entities: { posts: {...}, users: {...} } }
// denormalize(result.result, { posts: post, comments: comment }) reads it back for a component that wants trees.
```

**Which one.** `EntityAdapter` when you control the store shape (Redux): it gives typed reducers and memoised selectors for free, and writes normalised state for you. `normalizr` when you do *not* control the store (an API returns nested JSON and you want to flatten it on the way in) — its `defineSchema`/`merge` also help when the same entity arrives from several endpoints with different shapes.

**When to normalise and when not to.** Normalise anything that appears in more than one place, is updated independently, or is large enough that duplication costs (users, products, orders, comments, tags, invoices). Do not normalise a genuinely single-use response (a search results page you will never revisit), and do not normalise lists where order *is* the data and there is no random access (a checklist); for those, an ordered array plus `orderById` is enough.

**Trade-offs.** Normalisation costs indirection: every read is `byId[id]`, every write is one function, and a component that wants a tree needs a `denormalize`/join step (which must itself be memoised). The payoff is that a single write updates every view, entity updates can be subscribed to per-id (`useAppSelector(s => s.users.entities[id])` — one user's change does not re-render a list of a thousand), and list operations stop being `O(n²)` `find`/`includes` calls. Selectors trade a memory cost and a dependency list for "recompute only when inputs changed"; the failure mode is a wrong dependency, which returns stale data — so keep input selectors trivially correct (one property access each) rather than clever.

**Production and operational.**
- RTK Query already returns normalised results internally (`result.ids` / `result.entities`) — do not duplicate that work by normalising again. If you need a different shape, use `transformResponse` and keep the adapter as the only writer.
- Enforce the shape in review or lint: a `createEntityAdapter` slice plus a `no-restricted-syntax` rule banning `.find(` on entity arrays.
- Log selector recomputation counts in dev (a `console.count` in a development-only branch) to find accidental `createSelector` calls inside components — creating a selector per render defeats all memoisation.
- For very large normalised collections, `Object.freeze` in dev (RTK does this in development) so accidental mutation fails loudly.

**Common mistakes.**
- Calling `createSelector` *inside* a component (new cache every render).
- A selector that takes `state` and returns `state.a.b.filter(...)` — a new array per call.
- Mixing shapes: `usersById` plus a `users` array that can disagree.
- Deep-cloning in a selector to "fix" mutation (`structuredClone` in a selector destroys identity and perf).
- Using `useSelector(shallowEqual)` from React Redux as a universal fix instead of memoising — it is correct but costs a full shallow comparison per store update for every subscriber; a memoised selector is cheaper and narrower.
- Using `normalizr` where you only need one level (a plain `Map` and two reducers are enough).

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "Why does `useSelector` re-render constantly here, and what are the two possible fixes?"
- "Explain what reselect memoises and what happens when a dependency is wrong."
- "Why normalised state? Give a concrete bug it prevents."
- "When would you use `normalizr` over `EntityAdapter`?"
- "How do you avoid rebuilding an object in a selector?"
- "What is the complexity difference and where does it show up?"

**Mistakes:** treating reselect as a correctness mechanism rather than a performance one; normalising lists where order is the data; writing selectors in components; returning object literals from `useSelector`.

---

## 4.3.5 Middleware: thunk vs saga vs listener middleware, and when you need any

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.3.3 (`createAsyncThunk`), 4.2.4 (Server Actions)

**Definition:** Redux middleware is a function that wraps `dispatch` and can do work before, after, or instead of passing the action along:

```ts
type Middleware = (api: { getState: () => RootState; dispatch: Dispatch }) => (next: Dispatch) => (action: unknown) => unknown;
// api.getState  — read state without importing the store (the single biggest win)
// next(action)   — continue the chain; the return value is what dispatch() returns
// The order of concat() is the order actions travel.
```

Middleware is the *right* place for cross-cutting, non-component logic: analytics on every action, persistence, logging, an audit trail, feature-flag evaluation, and any workflow that spans slices.

**The three flavours.**

| | Mechanism | Owns async? | Cancellation | Great for | Costs |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Thunk** (via `createAsyncThunk`) | `dispatch(plainFn)` where `plainFn` returns a promise and dispatches plain actions | Yes, inside the function | `AbortController` via `signal`, plus `condition` for dedupe | One request → one success/failure; 90% of cases | Little; you write the sequencing by hand |
| **Saga** (`redux-saga`) | Generators that `yield` effects, driven by a saga middleware | Yes, via `call`/`fork` | `takeLatest` cancels, `spawn` detaches | Long-running, multi-step, event-driven flows: polling loops, websockets, retries with backoff, orchestrating several APIs | A second programming model; debugging is a stack trace of effects, not of actions |
| **Listener middleware** (RTK 2, built in) | `createListenerMiddleware()` + `startListening({ predicate, effect })` | Yes, inside `effect` | `cancelActiveListeners()`, `signal` for aborts | Reacting to *actions*: analytics, invalidation, toast, cross-slice side effects | Effects run after the reducer; no generator |

**Listener middleware — the modern default for "when X happens, do Y".**

```ts
// store/listenerMiddleware.ts
import { createListenerMiddleware, isAnyOf, isRejected, isFulfilled } from '@reduxjs/toolkit';
import { usersApi } from '../api/usersApi';
import { checkoutThunk } from '../features/checkout/thunks';
import { cartLineAdded } from '../features/cart/cartSlice';

export const listenerMiddleware = createListenerMiddleware();

// 1. Analytics + toast on cart activity. Fires AFTER the reducer ran.
listenerMiddleware.startListening({
  actionCreator: cartLineAdded,
  effect: async (action, api) => {
    const cart = api.getState().cart;                       // read state, never import the store
    track('cart_line_added', { sku: action.payload.sku, total: cart.itemIds.length });
    if (cart.itemIds.length === 5) api.dispatch(showToast({ kind: 'info', text: 'Free shipping unlocked' }));
  },
});

// 2. Cache invalidation on a mutation failure: react to any rejected async thunk.
listenerMiddleware.startListening({
  matcher: isRejected,
  effect: (action, api) => {
    api.dispatch(notificationAdded({ level: 'error', text: action.error.message ?? action.payload }));
  },
});

// 3. Cross-slice workflow with cancellation: restart the sync when the cart changes.
const startCartSync = listenerMiddleware.startListening.withTypes<{ state: RootState; dispatch: AppDispatch }>()({
  matcher: isAnyOf(cartLineAdded, qtyChanged, couponApplied),
  effect: async (_action, api) => {
    api.cancelActiveListeners();               // cancel any in-flight sync
    await api.delay(300);                      // debounce
    const cart = api.getState().cart;
    await api.dispatch(syncCartToServer({ lines: cart.linesBySku }));   // undo if the state changed while we waited
  },
});
```

The `api` argument is the reason this exists: `getState`, `dispatch`, `delay`, `signal`, `cancelActiveListeners`, `fork`/`unwrap`. It means listener code is testable with a fake store and has no import cycle. Note the ordering guarantee: listener `effect`s run *after* the reducer, so `api.getState()` is post-update.

**Sagas — when the flow genuinely is a sequence of events.** The honest rule: if you find yourself needing `takeLatest` semantics, debouncing across actions, or a loop that runs until a condition, a saga is a legitimate choice; if you are wrapping a single `fetch` in a generator to feel modern, it is not.

```ts
// store/sagas.ts
import { all, call, put, select, take, takeLatest, fork, cancel, cancelled, delay, race, take as takeAction } from 'redux-saga/effects';
import type { SagaIterator } from 'redux-saga';
import { checkoutThunk, syncStarted } from '../features/checkout/thunks';
import { checkoutConfirmed, checkoutFailed } from '../features/checkout/cartSlice';

// take = passive: wait for an action. put = dispatch an action. call = invoke fn, await promise. fork = start, don't wait.
function* checkoutSaga(): SagaIterator {
  while (true) {
    // take on an action creator (RTK action creators carry a .type, so they work directly)
    const action = yield take(checkoutThunk.pending);
    try {
      // race: whichever settles first wins; the loser is cancelled
      const { ok, order } = yield race({
        ok: call(api.createOrder, action.meta.arg),
        timeout: delay(15_000),
      });
      if (!ok) { yield put(checkoutFailed({ reason: 'timeout' })); continue; }

      // fork parallel work that must not block the flow; join when you must wait for all
      const tasks = yield all([fork(reserveInventory, order), fork(sendConfirmation, order)]);
      yield put(checkoutConfirmed(order));
      void tasks;
    } catch (err) {
      yield put(checkoutFailed({ reason: (err as Error).message }));
      // cleanup when the task was cancelled, e.g. by takeLatest
      if (yield cancelled()) yield put(inventoryReleased());
    }
  }
}

// takeLatest: cancels the *previous* run of the task when a new one starts. The classic for search & autosave.
export function* watchCheckout(): SagaIterator { yield takeLatest(checkoutThunk.pending, checkoutSagaInner); }
// takeEvery: run to completion, concurrently. takeLeading: ignore new ones while one runs (button debounce).
// fork is detached (errors do not bubble, runs in parallel); spawn is detached *and* isolated (errors do not crash the root).
// All actions flow through the same middleware — no direct component -> saga wiring needed.
```

Sagas' one genuinely superior property: a saga is a *paused program*. You can unit-test it by asserting the sequence of yielded effects without rendering anything, and you can `cancel` mid-flight. Everything else (error handling, cancellation of fetches) is also achievable with thunks and listener middleware, with less indirection.

**Which one to pick.** Default to `createAsyncThunk` for request/response. Default to listener middleware for "action happened → side effect". Reach for a saga only for flows with ≥2 long-lived concurrent processes, cross-flow cancellation, or a WebSocket/polling loop you want written as a state machine. Reach for none if you only need to fetch data — that is RTK Query's job (4.3.3).

**Trade-offs.** Middleware is invisible to junior developers: an action that triggers a fetch that triggers three dispatches that trigger a listener is a hard trace. It is also the *best* place for cross-cutting concerns because it is applied uniformly and testable outside React. The risk of middleware is a hidden cascade: three layers of middleware that each dispatch two actions is unmaintainable. Keep middleware shallow: at most one listener per action domain, and log dispatches in development so cascades are visible.

**Production and operational.** Wrap every middleware side effect in try/catch — a throwing middleware breaks dispatch for every action after it in the chain. Put analytics in middleware rather than in components (one place to consent-gate and batch; cross-ref 4.6.8). For a saga: monitor `sagaMonitor` in development, and never let a saga run forever without a `take` that can end it. For a listener: remember that effects run *after* the reducer, so if you need pre-reducer behaviour, that's not middleware, that's a custom reducer or a thunk.

**Common mistakes.**
- Using middleware to do things during the render cycle (it runs on dispatch, not on render).
- Writing the same analytics event in a component and in middleware — double counting.
- `takeEvery` for a submit button (allows 20 double-charges) — you want `takeLeading` or a thunk `condition`.
- Assuming middleware can short-circuit a reducer; it can *intercept* the action (don't call `next`) but then the reducer never sees it.
- Writing saga effects without `try/finally`, leaking `call`s on cancel.
- Middleware ordering forgotten: RTK Query's middleware must be added; a custom middleware that reads a slice which RTK Query's middleware has not yet hydrated reads `undefined`.

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "What is the middleware signature and what can it do?" (read state, delay, abort, swallow or intercept, and return a value from dispatch)
- "Difference between thunk, saga and listener middleware — when each?"
- "How do you debounce an action with cancellation?" (listener middleware `cancelActiveListeners` + `delay`, or saga `takeLatest`.)
- "Why not do all async in components?"
- "How do you test middleware?"
- "What happens if a middleware throws?"

**Mistakes:** putting data fetching in middleware *and* in RTK Query; deep chains of dispatching middleware; assuming `getState()` sees pre-reducer state in a listener effect.

---

## 4.3.6 Zustand, Jotai, MobX, Recoil: comparison and selection criteria

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.3.2, 4.3.6 note on Recoil's status

**Definition:** Four "minimal global state" libraries that all try to beat Redux's ceremony while keeping a global store. They differ in the *unit of state*: a store with selectors (Zustand), an atom graph (Jotai), observable objects with a reaction system (MobX), and atom graphs with dependency tracking + selectors (Recoil).

> **Status note (matters in interviews):** Recoil was **archived by Meta in 2023** and receives no new features or bug fixes; `facebook/recoil` is read-only. Recoil 0.7.x still runs, and plenty of large codebases (including internal Meta ones) still use it, so you must be able to *read* it. Do not *propose* it for new work. Jotai is the practical successor for atom-graph designs (the same author, ` dai-shi`), and Zustand is the most common choice today. Saying "Recoil is archived, here's why, and here's what I'd use instead" is a strong senior signal.

**Mechanical comparison.**

| | Unit of state | Subscription | Derived state | Mutations | Devtools | Bundle (gz, approx.) | Typed hooks |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Zustand v5** | One store = object; `useStore(selector)` | Per-component selector with `useSyncExternalStore`; equality fn configurable (`useShallow`) | Write selectors that recompute on change; no automatic dependency graph | `set` anywhere, no Immer by default (it is opt-in via `immer` middleware) | Official middleware | ~1.5 kB | Free (generic store) |
| **Jotai v2** | `atom(primitive)`; `atom(obj)`; derived atoms | Atom subscriptions; only dependents of a changed atom re-render | `atom(get => ...)` — lazy, memoised, and dependency-tracked automatically | Atoms are read-only from outside; you `set` them (`write` atoms for actions) | Devtools | ~3 kB | Free, excellent |
| **MobX 6** | Observable objects (`makeAutoObservable`) | Tracks which properties a *reaction* (component render, `autorun`, `computed`) actually read | `computed` values, cached and invalidated automatically — the most powerful model | Direct: `store.items.push(x)`; enforcement via `enforceActions` | Excellent, best-in-class time travel | ~15 kB (+ `mobx-react-lite`) | `useStores` typed helper |
| **Recoil** (archived) | Atoms + selectors + atom families | Recoil subscriptions + `useRecoilValue` | `selector({ get, set })` with a dependency graph | `useSetRecoilState` / `useRecoilCallback` | Official | ~25 kB | Awkward (`RecoilState` generic per atom) |

```ts
// ZUSTAND — one store, slice-by-slice with selectors. The workhorse.
import { create } from 'zustand';
import { useShallow } from 'zustand/react/shallow';
import { persist, createJSONStorage, devtools } from 'zustand/middleware';
import { immer } from 'zustand/middleware/immer';

type CartState = {
  linesBySku: Record<string, { sku: string; qty: number }>;
  add: (sku: string, qty?: number) => void;
  remove: (sku: string) => void;
  clear: () => void;
};

export const useCart = create<CartState>()(
  devtools(
    persist(
      immer((set) => ({
        linesBySku: {},
        add: (sku, qty = 1) =>
          set((s) => {
            const prev = s.linesBySku[sku];
            s.linesBySku[sku] = { sku, qty: (prev?.qty ?? 0) + qty };
          }),
        remove: (sku) => set((s) => { delete s.linesBySku[sku]; }),
        clear: () => set({ linesBySku: {} }),
      }),
      {
        name: 'cart:v2',                                       // version in the key, not in the payload
        storage: createJSONStorage(() => localStorage),
        partialize: (s) => ({ linesBySku: s.linesBySku }),    // NEVER persist functions or transient flags
        version: 2,
        migrate: (persisted, fromVersion) => fromVersion < 2 ? migrateV1toV2(persisted) : persisted,
        skipHydration: false,
      },
    ),
    { name: 'cart', enabled: process.env.NODE_ENV !== 'production' },
  ),
);

// Reading: each component subscribes to the narrowest slice it can.
const lines = useCart((s) => s.linesBySku);                       // re-renders when the map identity changes
const count = useCart((s) => Object.keys(s.linesBySku).length);    // re-renders on every qty change (unavoidable: it is derived)
const skuSet = useCart(useShallow((s) => new Set(Object.keys(s.linesBySku))));  // shallow compare for new containers
// WRONG: useCart((s) => ({ add: s.add, count: ... }))  -> new object every render -> loop. Wrap in useShallow.
```

```ts
// JOTAI — the atom graph. Derivation is automatic and lazy.
import { atom, useAtom, useAtomValue, useSetAtom, atomWithStorage } from 'jotai';

const countAtom = atom(0);                                   // primitive
const cartAtom = atom<Record<string, number>>({});           // object
const subtotalAtom = atom((get) =>                           // derived: only re-computes when a dependency changes
  Object.entries(get(cartAtom)).reduce((n, [sku, qty]) => n + PRICES[sku] * qty, 0),
);
const isEmptyAtom = atom((get) => Object.keys(get(cartAtom)).length === 0);
const cartWithActionsAtom = atom(                             // a "store" per slice, if you prefer that shape
  (get) => ({
    items: get(cartAtom),
    subtotal: get(subtotalAtom),
    add: (sku: string) => set(addAtom, sku),
  }),
  (get, set, sku: string) => {
    const next = { ...get(cartAtom), [sku]: (get(cartAtom)[sku] ?? 0) + 1 };
    set(cartAtom, next);                                     // an async write would use set(cartAtom, async (get, set) => {...})
  },
);
export const useCount = () => useAtom(countAtom);
const themeAtom = atomWithStorage('theme', 'system');        // persisted, with localStorage (see 4.3.8)
// selectAtom gives you a subscription to a slice of an atom value: const n = useAtomValue(selectAtom(cartAtom, (c) => c.skuA));
```

```ts
// MOBX 6 — observable objects + computed. The most capable, the biggest bundle.
import { makeAutoObservable, runInAction, reaction, autorun } from 'mobx';
import { observer } from 'mobx-react-lite';

class TodoStore {
  items: Todo[] = [];
  filter: 'all' | 'open' | 'done' = 'all';
  draft = '';
  isSaving = false;

  constructor() { makeAutoObservable(this, {}, { autoBind: true }); }   // autoBind methods

  get visible(): Todo[] { return this.items.filter((t) => this.filter === 'all' || (this.filter === 'open' ? !t.done : t.done)); }
  get remainingCount(): number { return this.items.filter((t) => !t.done).length; }   // cached; invalidated by its deps

  async add(title: string) {
    this.draft = '';
    this.isSaving = true;
    try {
      const created = await api.post<Todo>('/todos', { title });
      runInAction(() => this.items.push(created));   // async boundaries need runInAction
    } finally { runInAction(() => { this.isSaving = false; }); }
  }
  setFilter = (f: TodoStore['filter']) => { this.filter = f; };   // observable actions enforce `enforceActions`
}
const store = new TodoStore();
const Todos = observer(function Todos() { return <li>{store.visible.length} of {store.remainingCount}</li>; });
// reaction(() => store.items.length, (n) => track('todo_count', { n }));   // side effect on change, not on every read
// MobX's guarantee: reactions run synchronously and *never* observe a partially-updated state (actions batch).
```

```ts
// RECOIL (archived — read this, do not propose it)
import { atom, selector, atomFamily, DefaultValue, isRecoilValue } from 'recoil';
const userAtom = atom<User | null>({ key: 'user', default: null });
const userIdAtom = atomFamily<string, User>({ key: 'userById', default: isRecoilValue ? RECOIL_DIFFERENT : null });
const unreadSelector = selector({ key: 'unread', get: ({ get }) => get(messagesAtom).filter((m) => !m.read).length });
// atomFamily gives per-id caches; selectors are memoised on their dependency graph; the key property is
// that reading an atom inside a component's render registers a *finer* subscription than reading the whole graph.
// Cost: a large internal graph (React 18 StrictMode/streaming incompatibility historically), a big bundle,
// and no further maintenance. Recognise it in codebases; do not add it.
```

**When to use which — the selection criteria that actually decide it.**

1. **Team familiarity and hiring.** The single biggest factor. A bespoke Zustand store is 40 lines of convention; a MobX codebase needs someone who can reason about observability. Optimise for the five people who will maintain it.
2. **Do you need to *derive* state reactively and often?** Jotai's derived atoms or MobX `computed` earn their keep if you have chained derivations. If derivations are one-liners in `useMemo`, plain Zustand is fine.
3. **Do you need fine-grained reactivity on large collections?** MobX tracks per-property reads inside a render and re-runs only what depends on what changed — the best model for a virtualised list or a spreadsheet-like grid. Zustand's selector is per-component, which is nearly as good with a good selector.
4. **Do you need middleware, devtools time travel, and a big team on a large app?** Redux Toolkit. It is the only one of the four with a first-party DevTools timeline, serialisability enforcement, and a documented answer to "who dispatched this".
5. **Do you need cross-cutting async workflows (4.3.5)?** Redux Toolkit, or RTK Query. Zustand can do it (`store.getState().doThing()`) but there is no equivalent of `predicate`/`effect`/`cancelActiveListeners`.
6. **Do you need the smallest possible bundle?** Zustand (~1.5 kB gz) ≲ Jotai (~3 kB) ≪ MobX (~15 kB) ≪ Recoil (~25 kB).
7. **Bundle + SSR?** Jotai and Recoil need `Provider` with an initial-state prop; Zustand's vanilla store makes SSR hydration explicit; MobX needs `enableStaticRendering(typeof window === 'undefined')` on the server.

**What *not* to pick them for.** None of them replaces server-state caching. Zustand, Jotai, and MobX all happily become a place where you `set({ users })` and then own dedupe, staleness, and invalidation by hand. That is the most common way these libraries get misused, and the answer is TanStack Query / RTK Query for layer 2 (4.4.2).

**Trade-offs summary.** Zustand: least ceremony, most examples, best default; costs: no built-in devtools time travel, patterns emerge by convention. Jotai: the cleanest derived-state story and the best TypeScript inference; costs: a second mental model (atoms vs stores) and a smaller ecosystem. MobX: the most powerful and least-buggy reactive model once you understand it; costs: bundle size, decorator-era baggage in docs, and a class-based store that some teams dislike. Redux Toolkit: the most explicit and most inspectable; costs: files and ceremony. Recoil: superseded.

**Common mistakes.**
- `useStore((s) => ({ a: s.a, b: s.b }))` without `useShallow` → infinite re-render.
- Persisting functions or `Date` objects with `zustand/persist` (JSON round-trip destroys them silently).
- Using `useAtom` (read+write) where `useAtomValue`/`useSetAtom` would do, subscribing to reads you never use.
- MobX without `runInAction` after an `await`, or with `enforceActions: 'observed'` and mutation inside a render.
- Using MobX/Jotai to cache server data.
- Writing to the same key from two stores in an app that has "one store per team" as a rule — the merge problem.

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "Compare Zustand, Jotai, MobX and Redux Toolkit; what would you pick for *this* app and why?"
- "What is Recoil's status today?"
- "How does MobX know what to re-render?"
- "Why does `useStore` with an object selector loop?"
- "Does any of these handle server-state caching?"
- "How would you debug state in each one?"

**Mistakes:** citing bundle sizes you have not verified; proposing Recoil for a new build; using any of them as a cache; evaluating on "Redux vs Zustand" tribalism instead of requirements.

---

## 4.3.7 XState and state machines: when a finite state machine beats booleans

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.1.5 (`useReducer` transitions), 4.4.9 (async work)

**Definition:** A finite state machine (FSM) models a system as a *finite set of states* plus a *finite set of transitions* between them, where transitions may be guarded and may perform effects. XState is the reference implementation for React/TS. It replaces "a pile of booleans that can contradict each other" with "one state value that cannot".

**The bug an FSM eliminates.** Booleans multiply invalid states. A video player with `isPlaying`, `isBuffering`, `isPaused`, `hasEnded`, `isFullscreen` has 2⁵ = 32 combinations, of which about 6 are real. Every new feature adds a boolean and every existing `if` becomes wrong somewhere. One state value with 6 states has exactly 6 combinations.

```
Boolean soup (32 states, ~6 valid)         FSM (6 states, all valid)
idle: playing=false, buffering=false       idle | loading | buffering | playing
      paused=false, ended=false, ...       →    playing: PAUSE → paused
playing: playing=true, buffering=false,    →    paused: PLAY → playing, SEEK → seeking
      paused=false, ended=false            seeking: SEEKED → playing | paused | idle
buffering: playing=true, buffering=true    idle:  CANCEL → idle
...                                       CANCEL allowed from loading|buffering|seeking only (see the guard)
```

**XState v5 in full — a player with guarded transitions, invoked services, and retry.**

```ts
import { setup, assign, fromPromise, fromCallback } from 'xstate';
import { useMachine, useSelector } from '@xstate/react';

export const playerMachine = setup({
  types: {
    context: {} as { videoUrl: string; bufferPercent: number; error: string | null },
    events: {} as
      | { type: 'PLAY' } | { type: 'PAUSE' } | { type: 'SEEK'; to: number }
      | { type: 'PROGRESS'; percent: number } | { type: 'BUFFERING' } | { type: 'ENDED' }
      | { type: 'RETRY' } | { type: 'ERROR'; message: string },
    input: {} as { videoUrl: string },
  },
  actors: {
    // A service is a promise/callback/observable run by the machine: the async side effect of a transition.
    loadSource: fromPromise(async ({ input }: { input: { videoUrl: string } }) => {
      const res = await fetch(input.videoUrl, { signal: AbortSignal.timeout(10_000) });
      if (!res.ok) throw new Error(`source ${res.status}`);
      return res;
    }),
  },
  guards: {
    canSeek: ({ context }) => context.videoUrl.length > 0,      // named guards beat inline lambdas: testable, reusable
    isNearEnd: ({ event }) => event.type === 'SEEK' && event.to > 0.95,
  },
  actions: {
    trackError: assign(({ event }) => ({ error: event.type === 'ERROR' ? event.message : null })),
    clearError: assign({ error: null }),
    setProgress: assign({ bufferPercent: ({ event }) => (event.type === 'PROGRESS' ? event.percent : 0) }),
  },
}).createMachine({
  id: 'player',
  initial: 'idle',
  context: ({ input }) => ({ videoUrl: input.videoUrl, bufferPercent: 0, error: null }),
  on: {                                 // global transitions: legal from every state
    ERROR: { actions: 'trackError', target: '.failed' },
  },
  states: {
    idle: { on: { PLAY: { target: 'loading', guard: 'canSeek' } } },
    loading: {
      invoke: {
        src: 'loadSource',
        input: ({ context }) => ({ videoUrl: context.videoUrl }),
        onDone: { target: 'playing', actions: 'clearError' },
        onError: { target: 'failed', actions: ({ event }) => assign({ error: String(event.error) }) },
      },
      // `initial` in a transient state means: pass through immediately, don't render it
      on: { CANCEL: 'idle', PLAY: 'loading' },
    },
    buffering: {
      after: { 5000: { target: 'failed', actions: assign({ error: 'Buffering timed out' }) } },
      on: { PROGRESS: [{ guard: ({ event }) => event.percent >= 100, target: 'playing' }, { actions: 'setProgress' }] },
    },
    playing: {
      on: {
        PAUSE: 'paused',
        BUFFERING: 'buffering',
        ENDED: { target: 'ended' },
        SEEK: [{ guard: 'isNearEnd', target: 'ended' }, 'seeking'],
        ERROR: { target: 'failed' },
      },
    },
    seeking: { after: { 250: 'playing' }, on: { PAUSE: 'paused', CANCEL: 'playing' } },
    paused:  { on: { PLAY: 'playing', SEEK: 'seeking' } },
    ended:   { on: { PLAY: { target: 'loading', actions: 'clearError' }, SEEK: 'seeking' } },
    failed:  { on: { RETRY: { target: 'loading', actions: 'clearError' } } },
  },
});

function Player({ videoUrl }: { videoUrl: string }) {
  const [state, send] = useMachine(playerMachine, { input: { videoUrl } });
  const error = useSelector(state, (s) => s.context.error);
  // exhaustive rendering: no state can be unhandled
  return (
    <div>
      <button onClick={() => send({ type: 'PLAY' })} disabled={state.matches('loading')}>Play</button>
      <button onClick={() => send({ type: 'PAUSE' })} disabled={!state.matches('playing')}>Pause</button>
      {state.matches('buffering') && <Spinner />}
      {state.matches('failed') && <Error message={error!} onRetry={() => send({ type: 'RETRY' })} />}
      <span data-testid="state">{String(state.value)}</span>   {/* assert this in tests */}
    </div>
  );
}
```

**Mechanics you must be able to state.**
- **`state.matches({ playing: {} })`** tests the state value. `state.matches('playing')` for a top-level string. There is no way to be "half in a state".
- **Events are the only input.** The machine is a pure function of (state, event). Side effects happen only in `actions` and `invoke`.
- **`assign` is the only way to change context**, and it must be written as a function of (context, event) so it stays serialisable and pure. `assign({ x: 5 })` is sugar for a constant assignment.
- **Guards are predicates** evaluated *before* a transition is taken; the first matching entry in an array of transitions wins, so order matters.
- **States can be nested** (`{ idle: { on: { FOCUS: 'editing' } }, editing: { initial: 'clean', states: { clean: {...}, dirty: {...} } } }`), giving compound states without multiplying values.
- **`invoke` ties a service's lifetime to a state**: when the machine leaves `loading`, the fetch is aborted (`onError` with an abort must be filtered, or use the signal).
- **`after` is a delayed transition**, cancelled if the state is left first — the built-in answer to "clear this timeout in cleanup".
- **`always` transitions** are evaluated on every event while in a state (guards that fire immediately); `history` states let you resume where you were; `choice` picks one of N transitions.

**When a machine beats booleans, precisely.** Use one when: (a) the number of valid states is small but the *transitions* are the interesting logic (uploads, checkout, wizards, media players, editors, network connections, feature-flag evaluation with a rollout sequence, AI streaming with tool-calls); (b) you need to reason about which events are legal in which state — e.g. "ignore a double-click while submitting" or "allow BACK only from step 2"; (c) the UI must be *provably* consistent, which matters for money, permissions, and irreversible actions; (d) you want the logic unit-testable without rendering. Do not use one when: state is a bag of independent values (a preferences object), there is one flag, the flow is a simple request/response, or you would be modelling CRUD list filtering — a `useReducer` or a store is smaller and clearer. Overuse is the real failure: an FSM for a dropdown is a maintenance cost with no benefit.

**Trade-offs.** You gain: impossible states rendered impossible, explicit and testable logic, guaranteed cleanup via state-scoped `invoke`/`after`, a single place to see every legal transition, and trivially inspectable debugging (`state.value` is a data value). You pay: a steeper learning curve, a second mental model (event/action/service), serialisation if you want to resume a machine mid-flow (persisted `State`), heavier code for simple cases, and a smaller talent pool. The judgement call is about *transition complexity*, not state count.

**Production and operational.** Keep machines in their own files with tests that walk every state (`createActor(machine).getSnapshot().value`) and, better, use `@xstate/graph` to *generate* a test that visits every reachable state and asserts no action throws. Persist state only for flows that must survive a reload (a long upload wizard): serialise `actor.getPersistedSnapshot()` and restore with `createActor(machine, { snapshot })`. Report state to analytics as a state name, not a boolean set — `{ state: 'buffering' }` is queryable, `buffering: true` is not. Never let a machine's `entry` action do unbounded work; entry actions run synchronously on entry.

**Common mistakes.**
- Modelling a machine with `on: { EVENT: ... }` transitions that don't have targets (side-effect-only transitions), which makes the state chart a lie.
- Using `guard` to mutate instead of `guard` + `assign`.
- Forgetting that `invoke`'s promise keeps running after you leave the state unless you pass the `signal` through.
- Deep nesting states so that `state.matches('editing')` is true for six visually different screens.
- Building an FSM for a resource that has no interesting transitions.
- Writing machines inside components (recreated every render); define them at module scope.

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "When is a state machine better than booleans? Give a case where it is worse."
- "How does an FSM help with 'ignore the double click'?"
- "What does `assign` do and why must it be a function?"
- "How do you guarantee an async service is cancelled?"
- "How do you test a machine exhaustively?"
- "What does `always` do, and what is the difference from `on`?"

**Mistakes:** over-applying FSMs; treating a machine as async-by-default; assuming `matches()` handles nested states without care; not persisting long flows.

---

## 4.3.8 URL as state, localStorage persistence, and hydration-safe storage

🟢 Beginner · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.5.9 (URL design), 4.1.20 (hydration)

**Definition:** Two persistence mechanisms with opposite visibility. **URL state** is state the user can see, copy, bookmark, share, and go back from — it lives in `location`, survives everything, and requires zero storage. **Client storage** (`localStorage`, `sessionStorage`, IndexedDB, a cookie) is state the user cannot see, which survives a refresh, is scoped to an origin, and is *not* part of the document.

**URL as state — the mechanics and the rules.**

```
https://app.example.com/invoices/INV-42?tab=history&sort=-created&page=3&q=overdue&layout=compact

path        → the resource identity            → router params (route config: what renders)
search      → view state for that resource      → useSearchParams (your state: filters, sort, page, tab)
hash        → fragment, rarely state            → legacy routing (#/path) or in-page anchors (#section)
cookie      → server-readable, HTTP-only state  → session, locale, feature-flag cohort
localStorage→ invisible, per-origin, ~5 MB       → preferences, drafts, non-authoritative flags
```

Rules that follow from the mechanics:

1. **Anything a user might want to send someone goes in the search params.** Not in a store, not in `localStorage`.
2. **`push` vs `replace` is a product decision, per interaction.** Typing in a search box, changing a filter, paginating: `replace`, so the back button leaves the *page* rather than walking through 40 filter states. Following a result, opening an item, submitting a form: `push`.
3. **Params must be canonical.** `/invoices?page=1` and `/invoices` should be the same screen; parse to defaults on read, serialise by omitting defaults on write. Non-canonical URLs split analytics funnels and create duplicate-content SEO issues.
4. **Server-renderable.** Query params must decide *what the server returns* (pagination, filters), otherwise SEO and share-links show page 1 while the URL says page 3.
5. **Never put a secret in a URL** — query strings appear in `Referer`, browser history, and server logs (cross-ref 4.6.9).
6. **Parse, don't spread.** Validate params into a typed object at the route boundary with a schema (Zod, 4.4.8) so `?page=abc` cannot become `NaN`.

```tsx
// Typed, defaulted, canonical URL state. This is the pattern to memorise.
import { useSearchParams } from 'react-router-dom';
import { z } from 'zod';

const filtersSchema = z.object({
  q: z.string().max(120).catch(''),
  status: z.enum(['all', 'open', 'overdue', 'paid']).catch('all'),
  sort: z.enum(['-created', 'created', '-amount']).catch('-created'),
  page: z.coerce.number().int().min(1).max(500).catch(1),      // coerce then clamp then default
});
type Filters = z.infer<typeof filtersSchema>;

export function useFilters() {
  const [params, setParams] = useSearchParams();
  const parsed = filtersSchema.parse(Object.fromEntries(params));   // invalid input degrades, never throws
  const set = (patch: Partial<Filters>, opts?: { push?: boolean }) => {
    const next = { ...parsed, ...patch };
    if (next.page < 2) next.page = 1;
    const sp = new URLSearchParams();
    if (next.q) sp.set('q', next.q);
    if (next.status !== 'all') sp.set('status', next.status);
    if (next.sort !== '-created') sp.set('sort', next.sort);
    if (next.page > 1) sp.set('page', String(next.page));           // omit defaults -> canonical
    setParams(sp, { replace: !opts?.push });                      // filters replace; navigation pushes
  };
  return [parsed, set] as const;
}

// Debounce the *write*, not the read: the input stays responsive, the URL settles.
const [filters, setFilters] = useFilters();
const [q, setQ] = useState(filters.q);
useEffect(() => {
  const t = setTimeout(() => setFilters({ q, page: 1 }), 250);
  return () => clearTimeout(t);
}, [q]);
```

**localStorage — the API and its limits.** It is a synchronous, string-only, origin-scoped, ~5 MB map that survives everything and is *shared across every tab on the origin*. It blocks the main thread (serialise + parse of a large value is real INP cost), it is fully readable by any XSS on the origin (so never store tokens — cross-ref 4.6.9), and `storage` is a quota-exceeded exception you must handle. `sessionStorage` is the same but per-tab and cleared on close. IndexedDB is async, large, structured, and is what you want for offline data.

```tsx
// Hydration-safe, versioned, validated, cross-tab-synced useLocalStorage. Build this once, reuse everywhere.
import { useCallback, useEffect, useRef, useState } from 'react';

export function useLocalStorage<T>(key: string, initial: T, opts: { validate?: (v: unknown) => v is T } = {}) {
  const { validate } = opts;
  // The server has no localStorage, so the first client render MUST match the server's output.
  const [value, setValue] = useState<T>(initial);
  const [hydrated, setHydrated] = useState(false);
  const keyRef = useRef(key);
  keyRef.current = key;

  // Read AFTER mount -> no hydration mismatch, no flash-of-wrong-theme if you gate rendering on `hydrated`.
  useEffect(() => {
    try {
      const raw = window.localStorage.getItem(keyRef.current);
      if (raw !== null) {
        const parsed: unknown = JSON.parse(raw);
        if (!validate || validate(parsed)) setValue(parsed as T);
        else window.localStorage.removeItem(keyRef.current);       // corrupt or stale-shape: drop it, don't throw
      }
    } catch (err) {
      console.warn('[useLocalStorage] read failed', err);         // Safari private mode throws on access
    }
    setHydrated(true);
  }, [validate]);

  const set = useCallback((next: T | ((prev: T) => T)) => {
    setValue((prev) => {
      const resolved = typeof next === 'function' ? (next as (p: T) => T)(prev) : next;
      try {
        window.localStorage.setItem(keyRef.current, JSON.stringify(resolved));
      } catch (err) {
        // QuotaExceededError (Safari private browsing throws on *write* too), or a cyclic object.
        console.error('[useLocalStorage] write failed', err);
      }
      return resolved;
    });
  }, []);

  // Cross-tab sync: the `storage` event fires in *other* tabs only, so this does not echo our own writes.
  useEffect(() => {
    const onStorage = (e: StorageEvent) => {
      if (e.key !== keyRef.current || e.storageArea !== window.localStorage) return;
      try {
        if (e.newValue === null) { setValue(initial); return; }
        const parsed: unknown = JSON.parse(e.newValue);
        if (!validate || validate(parsed)) setValue(parsed as T);
      } catch { /* ignore a corrupt write from another tab */ }
    };
    window.addEventListener('storage', onStorage);
    return () => window.removeEventListener('storage', onStorage);
  }, [initial, validate]);

  return [value, set, hydrated] as const;   // gate on `hydrated` for anything that must not flash
}
```

**Versioned persistence with migration.** The essential production pattern: the persisted shape *will* change, so version it in the payload, migrate on read, and never assume your own past writes are readable.

```ts
// A migration table beats an if-else chain you will forget to update.
const MIGRATIONS = [
  { to: 2, up: (s: any) => ({ ...s, theme: s.theme ?? 'system', layout: 'comfortable' }) },
  { to: 3, up: (s: any) => ({ ...s, density: 'comfortable', _v: 3 }) },
] as const;

export function migratePersisted(raw: unknown): unknown {
  let state = raw as { _v?: number } & Record<string, unknown>;
  let v = state?._v ?? 1;
  while (v < 3) {
    const step = MIGRATIONS.find((m) => m.to === v + 1)!;
    state = step.up(state);
    v = step.to;
  }
  return state;
}
```

**Cookies as the third option.** Cookies are the only client storage the *server* can read, which makes them right for sessions (ideally `HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Lax; Path=/`, scoped to the minimum path/domain) and wrong for large data and for anything per-tab. A `SameSite` cookie plus the server is the real CSRF defence — not a token in `localStorage`.

**What belongs where — a table you can defend.**

| State | URL | `localStorage` | Cookie | Store |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Current entity / route | Path segment | — | — | — |
| Filters, sort, page, tab, search | Query params | — | — | — |
| Sorting/column widths of a data grid | — (if deep-linkable) | Yes (with version) | — | — |
| Theme, density, language preference | — (optional) | Yes, or a cookie if SSR must know | Cookie for SSR-critical | Yes, but then SSR can't read it |
| Unsaved form draft | — | Yes (namespace per form + entity) | — | Yes (session-lifetime) |
| Auth session | — | **No** | `HttpOnly` cookie | No (never store tokens) |
| Feature flag cohort | — | Yes (short TTL) | Yes (so SSR agrees) | Yes |
| Server data | — | No (that is a cache — use IndexedDB offline, or nothing) | — | No (use TanStack Query) |
| Anything the user should be able to share | Yes | No | No | No |

**Trade-offs.** URL state costs verbosity (`?tab=history&sort=-created`), requires you to parse/validate/serialise, and can produce unfriendly URLs that you must design (path vs query, short keys vs readable keys). It buys shareability, back-button correctness, SSR/SEO, analytics attribution, and survival across devices — five things `localStorage` cannot buy. `localStorage` costs synchronous main-thread reads, cross-tab coupling, an untrusted-by-your-own-app JSON blob, XSS exposure, and versioning burden. It buys survival across reloads for *invisible preferences*.

**Production and operational.**
- Prefix keys with app name and version: `myapp:colwidths:v3`. Clear-on-deploy is a blunt fallback — log migration failures and count them.
- Treat anything in storage as **untrusted input** (a user can edit it in devtools). Validate with the same schema you validate the network with; never `JSON.parse` and cast.
- Theme flash: read the theme in a tiny **blocking inline script** in `<head>` before first paint (or from a cookie), then hydrate React with that value — that is the only way to avoid a white flash, and it is the one place an inline script is worth its CSP cost (4.6.9).
- Gate any render that depends on storage on the `hydrated` flag, or accept the mismatch; `suppressHydrationWarning` on `<html>` with a `color-scheme` attribute is a legitimate single use.
- Cap what you persist: drafts over 30 days old are noise; IndexedDB with an eviction policy if you need real offline data.

**Common mistakes.**
- Writing to `localStorage` during render (side effect in render; StrictMode double-writes).
- Reading `localStorage` during render on the server path → `ReferenceError` or hydration mismatch.
- Persisting the entire store (including server cache) with no version and no migration.
- `push`ing every keystroke of a search box into history (back button becomes unusable).
- Storing tokens in `localStorage` (any XSS exfiltrates them for the life of the token).
- Expecting cross-tab listeners to fire in the writing tab (they do not).
- Copying URL params straight into a query key without validation (`NaN`, `Infinity`, `__proto__`).

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "What state should go in the URL, and why?"
- "`push` or `replace` for a filter change? Defend it."
- "How do you avoid a hydration mismatch with `localStorage`?"
- "How do you version persisted state?"
- "Why are tokens in `localStorage` a problem?"
- "When would you choose a cookie over `localStorage`?"

**Mistakes:** treating the URL bar as decoration; persisting unvalidated blobs; assuming storage is per-tab; using `push` for every filter tweak.

---

## 4.3.9 Optimistic updates and rollback

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.4.4 (mutations), 4.3.4 (immutable snapshots)

**Definition:** An optimistic update applies the expected result of a write to the client cache *before* the server confirms it, so the UI responds immediately; if the server rejects the write, the client rolls the cache back to the previous snapshot and re-syncs. It trades a small correctness window for perceived latency. It is a *decision about user-visible risk*, not a performance trick.

**The mechanics — five moving parts, and all five are required.**

```
1. PREDICT  what the server will return (the "optimistic" value, including any server-generated id)
2. SNAPSHOT the exact cache entries you are about to change, deeply enough to restore
3. APPLY    write the optimistic value into the cache synchronously
4. RECONCILE on success: merge/overwrite with the server's authoritative value; on failure: RESTORE the snapshot
5. INVALIDATE on failure: mark the affected queries stale so a refetch corrects anything you got wrong
```

The part everyone forgets is 5: rollback restores *your* snapshot, which may already have been stale. After a failed optimistic mutation you must refetch, not merely revert.

**A complete TanStack Query implementation, including the failure path.**

```tsx
import { useMutation, useQueryClient, keepPreviousData } from '@tanstack/react-query';

type Todo = { id: string; title: string; done: boolean; updatedAt: string };

type NewTodo = { tempId: string; title: string };

export function useToggleTodo() {
  const qc = useQueryClient();
  return useMutation({
    mutationFn: async ({ id, done }: { id: string; done: boolean }): Promise<Todo> => {
      const res = await fetch(`/api/todos/${id}`, {
        method: 'PATCH',
        headers: { 'content-type': 'application/json' },
        body: JSON.stringify({ done }),
      });
      if (!res.ok) throw new ApiError(res.status, await res.json().catch(() => null));
      return res.json();
    },

    // 1 + 3 — applied synchronously when mutate() is called. Renders before the network round-trip.
    onMutate: async ({ id, done }) => {
      // Cancel in-flight reads of the keys we are about to touch, so a late refetch cannot
      // overwrite the optimistic value (this is the single most commonly skipped line).
      await qc.cancelQueries({ queryKey: ['todos'] });
      await qc.cancelQueries({ queryKey: ['todo', { id }] });

      // 2 — snapshot exactly what we will change. Use structuredClone, not a reference:
      // many caches hand back the same object, and "immutable" caches may hand back frozen ones.
      const prevList = qc.getQueryData<Todo[]>(['todos']);
      const prevDetail = qc.getQueryData<Todo>(['todo', { id }]);

      qc.setQueryData<Todo[]>(['todos'], (old) => old?.map((t) => (t.id === id ? { ...t, done } : t)));
      qc.setQueryData<Todo>(['todo', { id }], (old) => (old ? { ...old, done } : old));

      return { prevList, prevDetail };   // the rollback context — one snapshot for both callbacks
    },

    // 4 — success: replace with the server's authoritative copy (it may set updatedAt, version, etc.)
    onSuccess: (server, { id }) => {
      qc.setQueryData<Todo[]>(['todos'], (old) => old?.map((t) => (t.id === id ? server : t)));
      qc.setQueryData<Todo>(['todo', { id }], server);
    },

    // 4 — failure: restore the snapshot exactly as it was
    onError: (_err, _vars, ctx) => {
      if (ctx?.prevList) qc.setQueryData(['todos'], ctx.prevList);
      if (ctx?.prevDetail) qc.setQueryData(['todo', { id: _vars.id }], ctx.prevDetail);
    },

    // 5 — failure AND success: refetch the truth. Cheap (usually cached) and always correct.
    onSettled: () => {
      qc.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['todos'] });
      qc.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['todo'] });
    },
  });
}
```

**Creating entities optimistically — the case where you must invent an id.**

```tsx
const tempId = crypto.randomUUID();                  // stable across retries: it is the dedupe key
qc.setQueryData<Todo[]>(['todos'], (old) => [
  { id: tempId, title, done: false, updatedAt: new Date().toISOString(), _pending: true },
  ...(old ?? []),
]);

// Prefer a server-assigned id + a client id echoed back, so you can reconcile:
mutationFn: async (input: NewTodo) =>
  (await fetch('/api/todos', { method: 'POST', body: JSON.stringify({ clientId: input.tempId, title: input.title }) })).json(),
onSuccess: (created, vars) => {
  // replace the temp row with the server row, matching on the clientId we sent
  qc.setQueryData<Todo[]>(['todos'], (old) => old?.map((t) => (t.id === vars.tempId ? created : t)));
},
```

The reconciliation rule: **always send a client-generated id (`clientId`) with a create, and always reconcile by it.** Otherwise a retry after a network timeout creates two rows, and you must choose which one to delete — on the client, with no transaction.

**Making it robust.** Show the pending state instead of pretending: an `isPending` mutation and a `_pending: true` marker let the row render with reduced opacity and an undo affordance. Undo is genuinely different from rollback: undo is a *new* request; rollback is a cache revert. For mutations where the server can be slow and the action is reversible (archive, star, rename, reorder), expose undo and keep the optimistic value until the server confirms. For money and anything irreversible (payment, delete, send email), **do not** be optimistic: show a pending state and wait. If you must (you have an idempotency key and a reconciliation path), mark the row as "pending" rather than "done", so the user is never told something happened that has not happened.

**When to use it and when not to.**

| Optimistic is right when | Optimistic is wrong when |
|---|---|
| High-frequency, low-stakes, easily reversible (like/star, toggle, reorder, rename, star-count) | Irreversible or high-stakes: payment, transfer, "send", "publish", "delete account" |
| The predicted value is unambiguous (a toggle, a value you sent) | The server transforms the input substantially (slug generation, moderation, computed totals) |
| Failure is common enough that a visible error path is designed (offline-first) | You have no way to show a failure — a silent revert after a send is worse than a spinner |
| Latency is visible and the interaction is a drag or a tap (perceived latency *is* the UX) | Latency is not visible (background prefetch) — optimism buys nothing |
| You can reconcile with an id or version | The server returns an aggregate that many rows depend on (a recomputed balance) |

**Trade-offs.** Optimistic updates cut perceived latency to zero and make the UI feel instant; they also introduce a class of bugs that are hard to see — a wrong prediction (the server rejects with a validation error you never predicted), a partial apply (two mutations touching the same list concurrently, second snapshot taken *after* the first applied), a stale rollback (snapshot was already stale), and a flash of wrong data for other users if you broadcast optimistically to a shared cache. Mitigations: `cancelQueries` before mutating, snapshot with `structuredClone`, always `invalidateQueries` on settle, and make every optimistic mutation *narrow* — one entity, not a whole collection.

**Production and operational.** Send an idempotency key with every optimistic mutation (`crypto.randomUUID()`, echoed back by the server) so retries and double-submits cannot double-charge. Track a rollback *rate* metric per endpoint: a 5% rollback rate means your prediction model is wrong and the optimism is costing users more than it saves. For collaborative features, an optimistic local write is a *replica* — you need conflict resolution (last-write-wins per field, or server revision numbers, or a CRDT). Never let an optimistic update apply to a query that another user could change between your snapshot and your response; that is a lost-update bug by construction.

**Common mistakes.**
- Forgetting `cancelQueries` in `onMutate`, so a background refetch overwrites the optimistic value and the UI flickers back.
- Snapshotting by reference (`prev = getQueryData(...)`) without `structuredClone`, so "rollback" restores the object you already mutated.
- Rolling back and *not* invalidating, leaving the cache in its pre-mutation (possibly stale) state.
- Optimistic create without a `clientId`, so a retry duplicates.
- Optimistic update on a list while a `refetchInterval` poll is running.
- Disabling the button on submit *and* being optimistic — the user sees the change but no sign it has not been saved.
- Treating `isPending` from `useMutation` as per-row; you need per-item pending state (a `Set` of ids).

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "Walk me through the full lifecycle of an optimistic mutation."
- "Why is `cancelQueries` needed in `onMutate`?"
- "How do you roll back correctly when two mutations touch the same list?"
- "When would you *not* be optimistic, and what would you show instead?"
- "How do you handle an optimistic create when the server assigns the id?"
- "What is idempotency and where does it live?"

**Mistakes:** optimism without a failure path; rollback without refetch; optimism on irreversible actions; assuming `onMutate`'s returned context survives a component unmount (it does — the mutation owns it, which is a feature).

---

## 4.3.10 The state-management decision flowchart

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.3.1–4.3.9

**Definition:** A deterministic procedure that, given a piece of state and its requirements, names the tool — and justifies it in one sentence. Interviewers use this to test *decision-making under constraints* rather than library recall. The value is in the questions you ask before you pick.

**The flowchart.**

```
                        ┌─ Is the truth owned by a server (DB, API, another user's action)?
                        │     │
                        │   YES ──▶ LAYER 2. TanStack Query / SWR / RTK Query / framework loader.
                        │            Never a store, never useState. (4.4.2)
                        │
                        │  NO ──▶ ┌─ Can it be computed from other state, with no user input?
                        │          │     YES ──▶ LAYER 0. Compute during render. useMemo only if
                        │          │            measurably expensive or identity is a contract. (4.1.7)
                        │          │
                        │          │  NO ──▶ ┌─ Would a user expect to share/bookmark/back out of it,
                        │          │          │     or must it survive a refresh or a second device?
                        │          │          │     YES ──▶ LAYER 4. URL. Path + search params, canonical,
                        │          │          │            push vs replace per interaction. (4.5.9)
                        │          │          │
                        │          │          │  NO ──▶ ┌─ Does exactly one component subtree need it?
                        │          │          │          │     YES ──▶ LAYER 1. useState/useReducer. Colocate.
                        │          │          │          │
                        │          │          │          │  NO ──▶ ┌─ 2–5 components nearby?
                        │          │          │          │          │    YES ──▶ Lift to nearest common ancestor.
                        │          │          │          │          │            OR colocate in a custom hook.
                        │          │          │          │          │
                        │          │          │          │          │  NO ──▶ ┌─ Value changes rarely (theme, locale, i18n,
                        │          │          │          │          │          │    an injected api client, once at mount)?
                        │          │          │          │          │          │    YES ──▶ Context (memoize value; split by
                        │          │          │          │          │          │            update frequency; split dispatch). (4.3.2)
                        │          │          │          │          │
                        │          │          │          │          │  NO ──▶ ┌─ Does it need order-dependent or guarded transitions,
                        │          │          │          │          │          │    cancellation, dedupe, or middleware?
                        │          │          │          │          │          │    YES ──▶ Redux Toolkit (+ RTK Query for LAYER 2,
                        │          │          │          │          │          │            + listener middleware for cascades). (4.3.3, 4.3.5)
                        │          │          │          │          │
                        │          │          │          │          │  NO ──▶ ┌─ High-frequency updates (scroll/drag/canvas/typing),
                        │          │          │          │          │          │    many independent writers, or derived graphs?
                        │          │          │          │          │          │    YES ──▶ A store with selectors: Zustand (default),
                        │          │          │          │          │          │            Jotai (derivation-heavy), MobX (fine-grained).
                        │          │          │          │          │
                        │          │          │          │          │  NO ──▶ Context, or one slice of a small store. (4.3.6)
                        │          │          │          │
                        └─ Layer 1/4/5 crosses the boundary?

Layer 5 (global client state) hygiene rules that apply wherever you land:
  · a store must never hold layer 2 or layer 3 (server data)
  · one fact → one owner → one write path
  · if two stores can write the same key, you have designed a race
  · every persisted value is versioned and validated on read
```

**The three questions that resolve 90% of ambiguity.**

1. **Who is allowed to write it, and what is the order of writes?** One writer → `useState`. Many writers with no ordering requirement → a store. Many writers where B must not run until A resolves → `useReducer` or an FSM. Interruption/cancellation requirements → an FSM (4.3.7).
2. **What makes it stale, and who is allowed to refresh it?** Nothing (client state, never stale) → store/URL. A TTL → query cache. A server event → query cache invalidated by a subscription. A user action → invalidation with a scope.
3. **What is the blast radius of a wrong value?** Cosmetic (hover, animation) → be liberal. Visible and recoverable → be conservative. Money/permissions/irreversible → never optimistic, never client-authoritative.

**Decision table for the common interview scenario.** "A team wants one store for everything."

| Requirement | Answer | Why |
|---|---|---|
| "One place to see all client state in DevTools" | Redux Toolkit (devtools) | Time travel + serialisability checks are a real benefit at team size |
| "Minimize boilerplate; small team; 8 screens" | Zustand + TanStack Query | Lowest ceremony, cache delegated to the query layer |
| "Deeply derived state (pricing rules, permissions matrix)" | Jotai derived atoms or MobX `computed` | Automatic dependency graph, memoised, no manual invalidation |
| "A virtualised grid with 100k rows and per-cell subscriptions" | MobX, or a normalised store + virtualisation | Per-property reaction tracking; re-render granularity |
| "Complex async orchestration (uploads, retry, cancellation)" | Redux Toolkit + listener middleware, or XState | Explicit, testable, cancellable; see 4.3.5/4.3.7 |
| "We already have X and it works" | Keep X | Migration cost is the dominant term; only migrate for a named defect |

**A migration playbook** (the real answer to "we have `useState` everywhere, where do we start?"): (1) Inventory every state and classify it with 4.3.1's table — this alone surfaces the derived-state duplication bugs. (2) Move all server data to the query layer first; it is the biggest win and the least invasive because components keep the same `data`/`isLoading`/`error` shape. (3) Move shareable view state to the URL. (4) Only then introduce a store for what is left, one slice at a time, keeping the old code path behind a feature flag. (5) Delete the old store when the last consumer migrates; never maintain two sources of truth "temporarily".

**Common mistakes.** Choosing a library before classifying the state; asking "Redux or Zustand?" instead of "what is this state's writer and lifetime?"; putting derived state in the store "for performance" (which is backwards — it is the one thing that costs); a store used as a cache; refusing context because "context is slow" without checking whether the problem is an unmemoized `value`; and treating a flowchart as a decision tree that yields one answer when the right answer is often "this is layer 2, not a state-management question at all".

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "Here are 6 pieces of state in my app — classify each and justify."
- "Why is 'one store for everything' a red flag?"
- "How would you migrate this codebase to a query library incrementally?"
- "When would you *add* a store to an app that has none?"
- "What would make you choose MobX over Zustand here?"

**Mistakes:** a flowchart without the "what makes it stale" question; assuming a bigger library is a bigger win; skipping the classification step entirely.

---

# 4.4 Data Fetching and Forms

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.1.7 (effects), 4.3.1 (server state)

**Framing.** Data fetching is the largest source of user-perceived latency and the largest source of subtle frontend bugs: duplicate requests, waterfalls, stale data, race conditions, lost writes, and the loading/empty/error matrix nobody designs. The canonical entry for *server-side* rendering and caching is 4.2; this section is the *client* data layer and the form lifecycle. The single organising idea:

> **A data-fetching layer is a cache with a policy.** Policy = when to fetch, when to reuse, when to revalidate, when to throw the data away, and what happens when it fails. Libraries differ only in how expressive that policy language is.

---

## 4.4.1 The data-fetching model: loaders vs effects, and the waterfall problem

🟢 Beginner · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.1.5, 4.2.5

**Definition:** A *loader* fetches data as part of a navigation or a render's dependency graph, before the consuming UI renders. An *effect* fetches data after the component has committed, imperatively. Almost every bug in this area is a consequence of choosing effects where a loader model was needed.

**Loader vs effect — mechanics and consequences.**

```
EFFECT MODEL (useEffect + fetch + setState)
  render → commit → paint → effect → fetch (t=0) → response (t=300ms) → setState → render → paint
  · waterfall: the second effect runs only after the first commit, so dependent requests serialise
  · states are hand-written: isLoading? error? empty? race? unmount?
  · two components fetching the same thing fetch it twice
  · no cache: every mount is a request

LOADER MODEL (route loader / RSC / use() + Suspense / query cache prefetch)
  navigation → (parallel fetches) → 300ms → render with data
  · no waterfall: sibling loaders are concurrent, and independent requests can be started together
  · states come from the framework: pending (Suspense fallback) / error (boundary) / resolved
  · data is cached and shareable
  · the "fetch" has a home that is not a component, so it survives remounts
```

The waterfall is the practical difference. In an effect-based fetch, request B cannot start until request A has resolved *and* caused a render, *and* committed, *and* its effect has run. Three client round trips become three sequential round trips.

```tsx
// THE WATERFALL, and the fix. 4 sequential renders/effects == 4 round trips.
function Page({ orgId }: { orgId: string }) {
  const [org, setOrg] = useState<Org>();
  const [members, setMembers] = useState<Member[]>();
  const [permissions, setPermissions] = useState<Perms>();

  useEffect(() => { api.get(`/orgs/${orgId}`).then(setOrg); }, [orgId]);
  useEffect(() => { if (!org) return; api.get(`/orgs/${org.id}/members`).then(setMembers); }, [org]);      // waits for org!
  useEffect(() => { if (!org) return; api.get(`/orgs/${org.id}/permissions`).then(setPermissions); }, [org]); // waits for org!
  // Total: t0 → org(120ms) → t120 → members(90ms) → t210 → permissions(80ms) = 290ms of serialisation.
}
```

```tsx
// FIX 1 (no library): start independent requests together in one effect, race-proof with AbortController.
function Page({ orgId }: { orgId: string }) {
  const [data, setData] = useState<{ org: Org; members: Member[]; perms: Perms } | null>(null);
  const [error, setError] = useState<Error | null>(null);
  useEffect(() => {
    const ac = new AbortController();
    // All three start in the same tick → one round trip's latency total, not three.
    Promise.all([
      api.get(`/orgs/${orgId}`, ac.signal),
      api.get(`/orgs/${orgId}/members`, ac.signal),
      api.get(`/orgs/${orgId}/permissions`, ac.signal),
    ])
      .then(([org, members, perms]) => setData({ org, members, perms }))
      .catch((e) => { if (e.name !== 'AbortError') setError(e); });
    return () => ac.abort();          // unmount / param change: cancel, so no late write to a dead component
  }, [orgId]);
  if (error) return <ErrorState error={error} />;
  if (!data) return <Skeleton />;
  return <OrgView {...data} />;
}
```

Note what is still missing in FIX 1: caching between navigations, deduplication between components, retries, staleness, background revalidation, and a shared error type. That is why production code uses a query layer.

```tsx
// FIX 2 (TanStack Query): parallel by default, and each hook suspends independently.
function Page({ orgId }: { orgId: string }) {
  // Three hooks → three parallel requests. Suspense boundaries wrap each: each section resolves when it can.
  const org = useQuery({ queryKey: ['org', { orgId }], queryFn: () => api.get(`/orgs/${orgId}`) });
  const members = useQuery({ queryKey: ['members', { orgId }], queryFn: () => api.get(`/orgs/${orgId}/members`), staleTime: 60_000 });
  const perms = useQuery({ queryKey: ['perms', { orgId }], queryFn: () => api.get(`/orgs/${orgId}/permissions`), staleTime: 5 * 60_000 });
  // Permissions change rarely → long staleTime = fewer requests. Org changes often → 0.
  // And ONE request per key no matter how many components ask.
}

// FIX 3 (router loader / React Router): fetch before the route renders, on navigation.
export const loader = ({ params }: LoaderFunctionArgs) => {
  // Defer + return promises: the route renders immediately with whatever resolves first; slow
  // data streams in behind <Suspense>/<Await>. Parallel because there is no await in the way.
  return defer({
    org:      orgLoader.load(params.orgId),      // use plain promises for infinite streams
    members:  createDeferredPromise(),
    perms:    permsLoader.load(params.orgId),
  });
};
export default function Page() {
  const { org, members } = useLoaderData<typeof loader>();
  return (
    <OrgView org={org}>
      <Suspense fallback={<MembersSkeleton />}>
        <Await resolve={members}>{(m) => <MemberList items={m} />}</Await>
      </Suspense>
    </OrgView>
  );
}
```

**The other four bugs the loader model fixes, and the effect model causes.**

1. **Race conditions.** Two rapid param changes → the *first*, slower response can land last and win. Fix with `AbortController` (cancels the loser) or a request-id guard.
2. **Unnecessary requests on every mount.** Back-and-forth navigation re-fetches everything. Fix with a cache and a staleness policy.
