3. **Missing cleanup.** No abort means a `setState` after unmount and a wasted connection.
4. **Request cancellation on unmount** — the same abort controller.

```tsx
// The race guard, written once and reused (this is also what a query library does internally).
useEffect(() => {
  let cancelled = false;
  const id = orgId;
  api.get(`/orgs/${id}`).then((o) => { if (!cancelled) setOrg(o); });   // ignore stale answers
  return () => { cancelled = true; };
}, [orgId]);
```

**Prefetching: turning navigations warm.** The best waterfall optimisation is *not starting late*, it is *starting early*.

```tsx
// Prefetch on hover / intent / when a link enters the viewport.
<Link to={`/orgs/${o.id}`} onMouseEnter={() => qc.prefetchQuery({ queryKey: ['org', { orgId: o.id }], queryFn: () => api.get(`/orgs/${o.id}`) })}>
// Preload critical resources in the route module so the *chunk* is warm too:
<link rel="modulepreload" href="/assets/OrgView-B1c2d3.js" />
// And on intent:
function useIntentPrefetch(target: string, ms = 120) {
  const timer = useRef<number>();
  return {
    onPointerEnter: () => { timer.current = window.setTimeout(() => preloads.preloadRoute(target), ms); },
    onPointerLeave: () => clearTimeout(timer.current),       // only if it has not fired
    onClick: () => preloads.preloadRoute(target),
  };
}
```

**When to use which.** Effects: one-off reads not worth a cache, browser-only APIs (`geolocation`, `mediaDevices`), imperative subscriptions, and third-party SDKs. Loaders (router/framework): anything that must exist *before* the route renders, anything the server needs to know about, and anything that should be SSR'd or streamed. Query library: everything else in a client-rendered app. Effects-with-fetch is basically always a smell at scale, but it is a legitimate answer in an interview for a single component with an `AbortController` and a cancellation guard — say *that*, not "just use an effect".

**Trade-offs.** Loaders require a router or framework (harder to unit test in isolation, coupled to navigation), but they make data flow explicit and enable streaming, `defer`, error boundaries per route, and prefetch-on-navigation. Effects are framework-free and trivially local, and cost you every caching and cancellation property. Query libraries sit between: they own the cache, and they work inside components (so they compose with Suspense and don't force a router).

**Production and operational.** Every request should be cancellable (`AbortSignal`), retried with exponential backoff *and jitter* for idempotent reads, and carry a request id for tracing. Set a global `staleTime`/`retry` default in one `QueryClient` config rather than per-hook. Aggregate client-side analytics on waterfalls: if `TTFB` is fine but time-to-render is not, the problem is serialisation, not the server. For very large fan-outs, batch endpoints server-side (`POST /dashboard` returning many aggregates) rather than optimising the client's request graph — that is the actual fix for a 12-widget dashboard.

**Common mistakes.**
- `useEffect(() => { fetch(...); }, [])` with no abort and no error branch.
- A dependent request guarded by `if (!parent) return` inside a *separate* effect (a waterfall by construction).
- `Promise.all` where `Promise.allSettled` was needed because one panel's failure should not blank the page.
- Fetching the same resource in a parent and passing it down as a prop *and* having the child fetch it too.
- Prefetching on hover with no delay (wastes bandwidth on pass-over links).
- Assuming cancellation stops the server work — it stops the response being used; say so.

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "Draw the timeline of an effect-fetch vs a loader-fetch with three dependent requests."
- "How do you fix a race between two param changes?"
- "What is prefetching and when is it worth it?"
- "When is `Promise.all` the wrong choice?"
- "Does aborting a fetch save server work?"

**Mistakes:** conflating "parallel" with "optimised"; forgetting the unmount cleanup; treating a loading spinner as the only pending state.

---

## 4.4.2 TanStack Query: queryKey, cache lifecycle, staleTime vs gcTime

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.3.9, 4.3.1 (server state)

**Definition:** TanStack Query (formerly React Query) is a *server-state* cache for asynchronous data. It owns: a keyed cache of query results, in-flight request deduplication, staleness with `staleTime`, background revalidation, garbage collection with `gcTime`, retries with backoff, mutations with a lifecycle, and derived/cache state via `select`. Its central abstraction is a **QueryKey**: a stable, serialisable, structural descriptor of a request, hashed to identify a cache entry.

**The mental model to say out loud in an interview.** A query has four states, not two: `pending` (no data yet, fetching), `error` (no data, failed), `success` (data, possibly re-fetching), and `refetching`/`fetching` (data present, network in flight). `isLoading` in v5 means `isPending && isFetching` — i.e. *no data yet*. A "refetch" on visible cached data is `isFetching && !isPending`, and that is exactly what `isFetching` is for.

**`queryKey`: a structural contract, the most important 10 lines you will write.**

```tsx
import { useQuery, useQueryClient, QueryClient, queryOptions } from '@tanstack/react-query';

type Filters = { q?: string; status?: 'open' | 'paid'; page?: number };

// (a) Put the key in ONE exported factory. Never hand-write the same array in two components.
export const invoiceKeys = {
  all: ['invoices'] as const,
  lists: () => [...invoiceKeys.all, 'list'] as const,
  list: (filters: Filters) => [...invoiceKeys.lists(), { ...filters }] as const,   // object, not positional args
  details: () => [...invoiceKeys.all, 'detail'] as const,
  detail: (id: string) => [...invoiceKeys.details(), id] as const,
};
// partial matching: invoiceKeys.lists() matches every list key; invoiceKeys.detail(id) matches one.

// (b) Keys are hashed *structurally*, so key order and object key order do not matter:
//   ['list', {a:1, b:2}] === ['list', {b:2, a:1}]   -> same hash.
//   ['list', {a:1}] !== ['list', {a:'1'}]           -> different (values are typed by JSON).
//   ['list', {a:undefined}] vs ['list', {}]          -> {a:undefined} is normalised away by JSON hashing: treat as EQUAL.
//     => Always normalise your key with defaults + omit undefined, or you will create phantom entries.

// (c) queryOptions gives you a typed, reusable "query definition" (key + fn + options) — v5 idiom.
const invoiceList = (filters: Filters) =>
  queryOptions({
    queryKey: invoiceKeys.list(filters),
    queryFn: ({ signal }) => api.get<InvoicePage>(`/invoices?${new URLSearchParams(clean(filters))}`, { signal }),
    staleTime: 30_000,
    placeholderData: keepPreviousData,        // paged filter changes keep the old page on screen
  });

export function useInvoiceList(filters: Filters) {
  return useQuery(invoiceList(filters));
}

// (d) select: transform in the cache-read path without breaking sharing. MUST be pure and
//     must return a stable value, or you cause an infinite render loop.
const useFirstInvoice = (filters: Filters) =>
  useQuery({
    ...invoiceList(filters),
    select: (data) => data?.invoices[0] ?? null,          // returns one object: stable across refetches
  });

// (e) subscribe narrowly: the component re-renders only when the fields it names change.
const { invoices, totalCount, isPending, isFetching, isError, refetch } = useQuery({
  ...invoiceList(filters),
  select: (d) => ({ invoices: d?.invoices ?? [], totalCount: d?.totalCount ?? 0 }),  // object literal: safe ONLY because
  // useQuery memoises select output against the raw data reference; the raw data is stable between refetches.
  // (That memoisation is why select is safe and useStore(selector) in a plain store is not.)
});

// (f) enabled: the declarative "not yet" — no fake filter values, no effect.
const useInvoice = (id: string | null) =>
  useQuery({ ...invoiceKeys.detail(id ?? ''), queryFn: () => api.get<Invoice>(`/invoices/${id}`), enabled: !!id });
```

**The cache lifecycle — the whole thing on one page.**

```
                    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
                    │  cache entry for key K                                       │
  fetch() ─────────▶│  state: pending(no data) | error(no data) | success(data)     │
  (mount, key       │  dataUpdatedAt ───┐                                        │
   change, invalid, │  isFetching ──────┼── background refetch does NOT reset     │
   focus, interval) │  errorUpdatedAt ──┘   `isPending` (data survives)            │
                    │  observers: Set<QueryObserver>   ← refCount                 │
                    └───────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                │ gcTime with 0 observers → entry is deleted (data gone)
                                ▼
  invalidateQueries(key)  → mark stale + refetch all *active* observers now
  resetQueries(key)       → mark stale, keep data, refetch on next mount (no refetch now)
  removeQueries(key)      → delete cache entry now
  setQueryData(key, v)   → write optimistically (does NOT mark fetching)
  getQueryData(key)       → synchronous cache read (perfect for event handlers)
```

**`staleTime` vs `gcTime` — the distinction interviewers always probe.** They answer different questions:

| | `staleTime` | `gcTime` (`cacheTime` pre-v5) |
|---|---|---|
| Question it answers | "Is this data *fresh* enough to serve without asking the server?" | "How long do we keep this data after nobody is looking at it?" |
| Default | `0` (stale immediately → refetch on every mount/focus) | `5 min` |
| Effect of `0` | Always considered stale; the cached data still displays while a background refetch runs | — |
| Effect of a big number | No refetch on mount/focus/reconnect until it ages out; fewer requests, staler data | Fewer re-fetches when remounting, more memory retained |
| Set it when | the data is expensive, changes rarely, or is per-user (profile, permissions, reference lists) | you remount routes quickly and want warm caches |
| Set it to `Infinity` when | the data only changes via your own mutations (then invalidation is your only update path) | — |

The **practical consequence** most people miss: with `staleTime: 0`, navigating back to a list re-fetches *even though you have data* — this is the correct default for a feed and the wrong one for a country list. And `gcTime` only starts counting when the **last observer unmounts**; a mounted component's data never gets garbage collected, so a screen left open for hours holds its cache entry.

```tsx
// One place for global policy. This is the highest-leverage 20 lines in a data layer.
export const queryClient = new QueryClient({
  defaultOptions: {
    queries: {
      staleTime: 30_000,           // 30s: pages, lists, anything user-visible
      gcTime: 10 * 60_000,         // keep it around a bit for back-navigation
      retry: (failureCount, error) =>
        (error instanceof ApiError && error.status < 500 ? false : failureCount < 2),  // never retry a 4xx
      retryDelay: (attempt) => Math.min(1000 * 2 ** attempt, 30_000) * (0.5 + Math.random() / 2),  // backoff + jitter
      refetchOnWindowFocus: true,   // but only matters if data is stale
      refetchOnReconnect: true,
      refetchOnMount: 'always',     // true = only if stale (default); 'always' ignores staleTime
      throwOnError: (error) => error instanceof ApiError && error.status === 401,   // route to login
    },
    mutations: { retry: 0, networkMode: 'online' },   // mutations do NOT auto-retry: duplicate writes
  },
});
```

**The other features worth knowing in one line each.**
- **Infinite queries** (`useInfiniteQuery`, `getNextPageParam`) — 4.4.5.
- **Suspense**: `useSuspenseQuery` throws a promise; combine with `<Suspense>` for a route-level skeleton instead of per-hook `if (isPending)`.
- **`queryClient.ensureQueryData` / `prefetchQuery`** — await the first, fire-and-forget the second; call both in a router loader for a warm navigation.
- **`dehydrate`/`hydrate`** — move a server cache into the client at hydration, killing a duplicate first fetch.
- **`structuralSharing: false`** — disables deep-equal reuse of *unchanged* parts between refetches; turn it off for very large or non-plain (class/Map) data.
- **`select` vs `useMemo`**: `select` runs inside the cache subscription (narrower); `useMemo` runs after re-render (wider).
- **`notifyOnChangeProps: 'tracked'`** — only notify observers of fields actually used in render.
- **`placeholderData: keepPreviousData`** — instant page transitions on filter/pagination changes (avoids a skeleton flash).
- **`initialData`/`initialDataUpdatedAt`** — hydrate from SSR; set `initialDataUpdatedAt` to the server timestamp or the client will consider it instantly stale and refetch.

**When to use TanStack Query and when not to.** Use it for: anything fetched from an API in a client-rendered app; anything needing cross-component sharing, caching, retries, or invalidation. Do not use it for: data only the server has (use a loader/RSC — 4.2), local UI state (4.3.1), a truly static bundle (import it), or a WebSocket-fed stream you are already storing yourself (4.4.6). Do not run it *and* SWR *and* RTK Query in one app.

**Trade-offs.** You get an enormous amount of correct behaviour for ~13 kB gz and a 10-line config, plus DevTools showing every key, its observers, and its timings. You pay a dependency, a learning curve around key design (the #1 source of bugs), cache-invalidation design work, and the fact that a *badly keyed* cache produces subtle duplicate-entity bugs that are hard to see. Compared to SWR: TanStack Query is more explicit and more feature-complete (mutations, infinite queries, devtools with mutation timeline); SWR is smaller, has a simpler mental model, and treats data as immutable-with-mutate (which is closer to React's model).

**Production and operational.**
- **Invalidation discipline**: every mutation must declare what it invalidates; grep for `useMutation` and check each has an `onSettled`/tag/affected-keys story.
- **Per-tenant keys**: include the account/org id in every key, or you get cross-tenant cache leakage when a user switches accounts without a full reload (`queryClient.clear()` on tenant switch).
- **Cache caps**: if a query key contains a user-typed search term and you use `staleTime: 0` + no `gcTime` tuning, the cache grows unboundedly. Either don't cache search (`enabled: term.length > 2` and short `gcTime`) or cap it.
- **SSR**: `dehydrate` the loader's data into `window.__RQ__` and pass it to `hydrate()`; otherwise you fetch twice on first paint.
- **Monitoring**: `queryClient.getQueryCache().getAll().length` and total cache size; alert on sustained growth (a leak in your key design).
- **Error semantics**: 4xx = terminal (no retry, show a message with an action); 5xx/network = retryable with backoff; 401/403 = re-auth and clear the cache; 404 = remove the entry.

**Common mistakes.**
- Hand-writing the key array in each component (inconsistent → duplicate requests for the same data).
- Non-normalised keys: `['list', page, q || undefined, status || undefined]` producing two entries for the same logical query.
- `invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['todos'] })` being a no-op because your keys start with `'todosList'`.
- `refetchOnMount: 'always'` everywhere, turning navigations into request storms.
- Retrying mutations (double charges).
- Forgetting `signal` in `queryFn` so cancelled queries still consume bandwidth.
- Using `staleTime: Infinity` on everything and then wondering why the UI is stale — infinity means *you* own invalidation, completely.
- `select` returning a new array each call inside `selectFromResult` (loops).

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "Explain `staleTime` vs `gcTime`, and what happens with defaults."
- "How do you ensure two components with the same data make one request?"
- "How do you design keys for a list with filters?"
- "Why should mutations not retry?"
- "How do you share a cache between server and client?"
- "How do you prevent cache growth from a search box?"

**Mistakes:** treating a query as `useState` with extra steps; keys containing component names; invalidating on mount; ignoring `signal`.

---

## 4.4.3 SWR and the compare-and-swap revalidation model

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.4.1

**Definition:** SWR is a data-fetching hook built on **stale-while-revalidate**: serve cached data immediately, then revalidate in the background; accept a newer response only if it is actually newer (compare-and-swap). Its API surface is three functions — `useSWR`, `mutate`, and `useSWRConfig` — and its philosophy is that data is immutable and cache writes are explicit, which matches React's model closely.

**Stale-while-revalidate, mechanically.** Every `useSWR(key, fetcher)` render does:

```
key changed?  → revalidate (unless keepPreviousData semantics say otherwise)
no cache?     → status='loading', fetch, then render with data (or Suspense if configured)
cache fresh?  → render from cache. If stale (older than revalidateIfStale) → render AND refetch.
refetch completes → compareAndSwap: only overwrite the cache if the incoming `data` is not older
                     than what's already there (protects against out-of-order responses).
```

`revalidateOnMount`, `revalidateOnFocus`, `revalidateOnReconnect`, `dedupingInterval` (default 2000 ms — two components mounting within 2 s share one request), `refreshInterval` (polling, 4.4.6), `revalidateIfStale`, `keepPreviousData`, `errorRetryCount`/`errorRetryInterval` are the policy knobs. They map almost one-to-one onto TanStack Query's options; the difference is philosophy and surface area.

```tsx
import useSWR from 'swr';
import useSWRInfinite from 'swr/infinite';
import { compareAndSwap } from 'swr/_internal';   // the CAS primitive SWR itself uses internally

export const fetcher = async <T,>(url: string): Promise<T> => {
  const res = await fetch(url, { credentials: 'include' });          // SWR does not pass a signal to the fetcher by default:
  if (!res.ok) {                                                     // use a key with a fetchKey or accept no cancellation
    const err = new Error(`HTTP ${res.status}`) as Error & { status?: number };
    err.status = res.status;
    throw err;                                                      // SWR's retry policy keys off this
  }
  return res.json() as Promise<T>;
};

// One request for many components — same key means same cache entry and in-flight dedupe.
const { data, error, isLoading, isValidating, mutate } = useSWR<User>(`/api/users/${id}`, fetcher, {
  revalidateOnFocus: true,
  revalidateIfStale: true,
  dedupingInterval: 2000,
  keepPreviousData: true,             // when `id` changes, keep showing the old user (avoids a skeleton flash)
  fallbackData: undefined,             // SSR seed; also use `fallback: { [key]: data }` for a page of data
  onErrorRetry: (err, key, _cfg, revalidate, { retryCount }) => {
    if (err.status === 404) return;                                   // terminal: stop
    if (retryCount > 2 || (err.status && err.status < 500)) return;
    setTimeout(() => revalidate({ retryCount }), 1000 * 2 ** retryCount);   // backoff
  },
  isPaused: () => typeof document !== 'undefined' && document.hidden === true,  // optional: pause on hidden tab
});
```

**The compare-and-swap model — the part that makes SWR correct under races.** The global `mutate(key, data)` is SWR's "write to the cache" primitive, and internally it compares the incoming data against the cached value before committing:

```ts
// (1) Optimistic write: set the cache WITHOUT triggering a revalidation.
//     This is SWR's equivalent of an optimistic update, and it is why you rarely see a loader.
const { mutate } = useSWR<User>(`/api/users/${id}`, fetcher);

async function rename(name: string) {
  await mutate(
    async (current) => {                             // async function => optimistic data, then revalidate on resolve
      if (!current) return current;
      return { ...current, name };                    // optimistic value shown immediately
    },
    {
      optimisticData: (current) => (current ? { ...current, name } : current),  // v2 explicit form
      rollbackOnError: true,                         // restore the previous value if it throws
      revalidate: false,                              // CAS write: do NOT refetch, we just wrote the truth
      populateCache: true,
    },
  );
}

// (2) Manual write with CAS semantics on a key you may not be mounted on (global mutate):
import { mutate as globalMutate } from 'swr';
await globalMutate(`/api/users/${id}`, async (current: User | undefined) => {
  const updated = await api.patch(`/users/${id}`, { name });
  // compareAndSwap guards the cache from being overwritten by an older in-flight response
  compareAndSwap(`/api/users/${id}`, current, updated);
  return updated;
}, { revalidate: false });

// (3) Pessimistic-but-fast (the common case): mutate the fetcher, SWR revalidates on settle.
const save = async (patch: Partial<User>) => {
  await mutate(api.patch(`/users/${id}`, patch), { revalidate: false });
  // or: await mutate();   // SWR re-fetches after the mutation resolves
};
```

The mental model: **`mutate` is the only way to write the cache, and it takes an optional async function whose *return value becomes the next cache entry*.** TanStack Query's `setQueryData` + `invalidateQueries` splits the same idea into two calls; SWR's `mutate(fn, { revalidate })` keeps it as one, which is why SWR code is shorter and why SWR has fewer moving parts to get wrong.

**Conditional fetching and dependent keys.** SWR's key can be a function, which is how you express "not yet":

```tsx
const { data } = useSWR(
  orgId ? `/api/orgs/${orgId}/members` : null,     // null key => do not fetch (SWR's `enabled`)
  fetcher,
);
const { data: perms } = useSWR(me ? (perms_needed(me) ? `/api/me/permissions` : null) : null, fetcher);
```

**Pagination and infinite queries in SWR** — the `getKey` function is the whole API:

```tsx
const getKey = (pageIndex: number, previousPageData: User[] | null) =>
  previousPageData && previousPageData.length === 0 ? null   // end of list => stop
  : `/api/users?page=${pageIndex + 1}`;                     // null terminates the chain
const { data, size, setSize, isLoading, isValidating, mutate } = useSWRInfinite(getKey, fetcher);
const users = data?.flat() ?? [];
// setSize(n) loads pages until you have n; `isValidatingMore` tells you the tail is loading; `isReachingEnd` (v2)
// or data?.[size-1]?.length === 0 tells you the end.
```

**Comparison for choosing.**

| | SWR | TanStack Query |
|---|---|---|
| Philosophy | Immutable cache, explicit writes via `mutate` | Server state with a status machine; cache writes + invalidation are separate |
| API surface | Tiny; `useSWR`, `mutate`, `useSWRConfig`, `useSWRInfinite` | Large: `useQuery`, `useMutation`, `useInfiniteQuery`, `useQueries`, `prefetch`, `dehydrate`, devtools, persisted cache |
| Mutations | A first-class part of `useSWR`'s contract (optimistic + rollback built in) | `useMutation` with a full lifecycle (`onMutate`/`onError`/`onSettled`) |
| Suspense | `useSWR` has a suspense variant (`useSWRSubscription` for live streams) | `useSuspenseQuery` |
| Optimistic updates | One call (`optimisticData`, `rollbackOnError`) | Snapshot/restore/invalidate — more code, more explicit control |
| Devtools | Good, small | Excellent (query + **mutation** timeline, which is how you debug invalidation) |
| Multiple keys / dependencies | Manual | `useQueries`, `useSuspenseQueries` |
| Bundle | ~4 kB gz | ~13 kB gz |
| Best for | Small-to-medium apps, teams that like small surfaces, `useSWRInfinite`-style feeds | Apps with heavy mutation/invalidation needs, persisted cache, query/mutation devtools |

Interview phrasing that scores well: *"SWR and TanStack Query solve the same problem with different defaults. SWR's default is 'data is immutable and I write the cache myself'; TanStack Query's default is 'each query has a status machine and mutations declare what they invalidate'. If my team's dominant pain is mutation correctness and cache invalidation, I take TanStack Query; if it is 'keep the app small and let components fetch', SWR."*

**When to use SWR.** Small-to-medium apps; teams that like one concept; live subscriptions (`useSWRSubscription` is excellent for WebSockets/SSE, 4.4.6); `useSWRInfinite` feeds. Not when: your app has heavy cross-slice invalidation workflows, you need a mutation timeline for debugging, or your organisation has standardised on TanStack Query (consistency beats per-app preference at team scale).

**Trade-offs.** SWR's small surface is its main feature *and* its main limitation: fewer escape hatches means "I need to know exactly when this refetches" sometimes requires reaching into internals (`swr/_internal`), which is a smell you should name in an interview rather than do casually. SWR's implicit revalidation on focus can also surprise you (three tabs on the same app all refetch on focus). TanStack Query's explicitness costs more code but makes invalidation *reviewable* — a real advantage at scale and in code review.

**Common mistakes.**
- `revalidateOnFocus: false` globally "to reduce requests" and then shipping stale data on every tab switch.
- Mutating a cached object in place (`data.name = x`) instead of going through `mutate` — no re-render, no cache coherence.
- Not passing `keepPreviousData` on a param change, so every id change flashes a skeleton.
- `useSWRInfinite`'s `getKey` returning a URL for `pageIndex` before checking `previousPageData` (an extra request and a flash of undefined).
- Expecting `mutate(fn)` to be a rollback mechanism for arbitrary errors (it is a CAS write; `rollbackOnError` is the option you want).
- Assuming SWR aborts in-flight fetches — it dedupes but cancellation needs `AbortController` in the fetcher keyed off a request id.

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "Explain stale-while-revalidate in two sentences."
- "What does compare-and-swap protect against, and where is it in the SWR source?"
- "How do you do an optimistic update in SWR? In TanStack Query? What are the trade-offs?"
- "Why does SWR dedupe requests?"
- "How would you pause revalidation when a modal is open?"

**Mistakes:** treating SWR's cache as mutable; importing from `swr/_internal` without acknowledging it; conflating deduping with cancellation.

---

## 4.4.4 Mutations, invalidation, optimistic updates, and rollback

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.3.9, 4.4.2

**Definition:** A mutation is an intentional write to the server. Unlike a query (implicitly re-fetched by anything), a mutation only runs when you ask for it, and its aftermath — which caches are now wrong, and what the user sees while it runs — is an explicit design decision. In TanStack Query that decision is the `useMutation` lifecycle; in Redux it is `createAsyncThunk` + invalidation; in a router it is an `action`.

**The mutation lifecycle, and where each decision lives.**

```
mutate(vars)
  └─ onMutate(vars)         async · BEFORE the request. Cancel in-flight reads, snapshot, write
                            optimistic data. RETURN a context object. ← the only place rollback data comes from
  └─ mutationFn(vars)       the request. Receives { signal }. Must be idempotent OR accept an idempotency key.
  └─ onSuccess(data, vars, ctx)   authoritative result lands in the cache here
  └─ onError(err, vars, ctx)      restore the snapshot from ctx
  └─ onSettled(data?, err?, vars, ctx)  always runs: invalidate, log, hide the pending state
  └─ onMutate settled → observers notified → UI updates
```

The three invariants most teams break: (1) **`onMutate` must return the snapshot**, or `onError` cannot roll back; (2) **`onSettled` must invalidate**, or a failed mutation leaves a lying cache; (3) **`onSuccess` writes the server's copy**, not your predicted copy, because the server normalises (slug, id, `updatedAt`, permissions).

```tsx
// The complete, correct mutation. Copy this shape.
export function useArchiveInvoice(id: string) {
  const qc = useQueryClient();
  const toast = useToast();

  return useMutation({
    // Idempotency key: the server dedupes retries and double-clicks. Same key for the same logical intent.
    mutationFn: async ({ reason }: { reason: string }) =>
      api.post<{ id: string; status: 'archived' }>(`/invoices/${id}/archive`, { reason }, { idempotencyKey: crypto.randomUUID() }),

    onMutate: async ({ reason }) => {
      await qc.cancelQueries({ queryKey: invoiceKeys.detail(id) });
      await qc.cancelQueries({ queryKey: invoiceKeys.lists() });
      const ctx = {
        prevDetail: qc.getQueryData<Invoice>(invoiceKeys.detail(id)),
        prevLists: qc.getQueriesData<InvoicePage>({ queryKey: invoiceKeys.lists() }),
      };
      qc.setQueryData<Invoice>(invoiceKeys.detail(id), (old) => old && { ...old, status: 'archived', _pending: true });
      for (const [key, page] of ctx.prevLists) {
        if (page) qc.setQueryData<InvoicePage>(key, (p) => p && { ...p, invoices: p.invoices.map((i) => (i.id === id ? { ...i, status: 'archived' } : i)) });
      }
      return ctx;
    },
    onSuccess: (server) => {
      qc.setQueryData(invoiceKeys.detail(id), server);
      toast.success(`Invoice ${server.id} archived`);
    },
    onError: (err, _v, ctx) => {
      if (ctx?.prevDetail) qc.setQueryData(invoiceKeys.detail(id), ctx.prevDetail);
      if (ctx?.prevLists) for (const [key, page] of ctx.prevLists) if (page) qc.setQueryData(key, page);
      toast.error(err instanceof ApiError ? err.userMessage : 'Could not archive. Your change was not saved.');
    },
    onSettled: () => {
      void qc.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: invoiceKeys.detail(id) });
      void qc.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: invoiceKeys.lists() });
    },
    // Global hooks (all mutations in one place) — analytics, error reporting, a global pending counter.
    // mutationScope: { id: 'invoice' } lets unrelated invoice mutations run in parallel while
    // only one runs per scope (networkMode: 'serial' default) — useful for a save button.
  });
}

// Using it:
const archive = useArchiveInvoice(invoice.id);
<Button onClick={() => archive.mutate({ reason })} disabled={archive.isPending}>
  {archive.isPending ? 'Archiving…' : 'Archive'}
</Button>
```

**Scoped serialisation.** Two mutations that touch the same entity must not race; two that touch different entities should not be blocked. `mutationScope` gives you exactly that:

```ts
// In the client config: networkMode 'serial' within a scope, parallel across scopes.
const m = useMutation({
  mutationKey: ['invoice', id],
  mutationScope: { id: `invoice-${id}` },     // serialized per invoice
  mutationFn: save,
});
// And explicit cancel-previous if "last write wins" is the product rule:
const save = useMutation({ mutationFn: save, scope: { id: 'editor' } });
// with networkMode: 'serial', a new call cancels the in-flight one and starts after it settles.
```

**Invalidation strategies — pick by blast radius, not by habit.**

| Strategy | Mechanism | Correct when | Fails when |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Broad invalidation** | `invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['invoices'] })` | One entity type, small app, correctness-first | You have 12 filters of invoices; every write refetches 12 caches |
| **Exact-key invalidation** | `invalidateQueries({ queryKey: invoiceKeys.list(filters) })` | You know exactly which view shows the change | The change affects a view you forgot exists |
| **Tag invalidation** (RTK Query) | `providesTags` / `invalidatesTags` | Many components depend on the entity in different shapes | Tags are coarse ('LIST') and over-invalidate |
| **Predicate invalidation** | `invalidateQueries({ predicate: (q) => q.queryKey[0] === 'invoices' })` | Structured keys, need flexibility | Hard to read; easy to get the shape wrong |
| **Push/cache write only** | `setQueryData` and no invalidation | You *know* the server's response is authoritative and complete | You get one field wrong and it never corrects |
| **Cache lifetime as policy** | `staleTime: Infinity` + explicit `invalidateQueries` from the mutation's `onSettled` | Admin tools with well-known mutation points | A mutation path forgets to invalidate → data is wrong forever |

**The gold standard** is: *optimistic for the immediate UX, precise invalidation for correctness, and a broad invalidation on the failure path.* A useful practical rule: invalidate precisely on success and broadly on error — because after a failure you do not know what state the server is in.

**Rollback correctness under concurrency** — the subtle part people get wrong. If two mutations touch the same list and both snapshot, the second snapshot may already include the first's optimistic value. Rolling the second back restores that value (correct), but if the *first* then fails, its snapshot predates the second, so the rollback reverts the second's change too. Three mitigations: (1) serialise per entity with `mutationScope`; (2) on rollback, invalidate rather than restore (correct and simple, costs a refetch); (3) make the mutation idempotent and idempotent-keyed so a retry converges.

```tsx
// Mitigation 2, the honest one: on failure, just throw the cache away for the affected keys.
onError: (_e, _v, _ctx) => { void qc.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['invoices'] }); },
```

**Common mistakes.** `invalidateQueries` with a key prefix that matches nothing (silent no-op — a favourite, and it is why you should log invalidations in dev). Forgetting `cancelQueries`. Rolling back to a snapshot taken *after* another optimistic write. Treating 4xx like 5xx: a validation error should show the server's field errors, not "something went wrong", and must not roll back to a state that is now obviously wrong. No idempotency key on a payment. Optimistic update on a resource other users read (you are now lying to your own cache about shared data). Firing `mutate` from a `useEffect` (an event-driven operation belongs in an event handler).

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "Walk me through `onMutate`/`onError`/`onSettled` and why each exists."
- "How do you roll back when two mutations touch the same data?"
- "How do you decide what to invalidate?"
- "What is `mutationScope` for?"
- "How do you prevent double submissions?"

**Mistakes:** skipping `cancelQueries`; using `onSettled` only for the success case; invalidating nothing and hoping; treating a 422 as a network error.

---

## 4.4.5 Pagination: offset vs cursor, infinite queries, bidirectional scroll

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.4.2, 4.3.9 (dedupe by id)

**Definition:** Pagination is the policy for reading a bounded window of a large collection. Two families exist: **offset pagination** (`?page=2` / `?offset=50&limit=20`, computed by the database) and **cursor (keyset) pagination** (`?after=<opaque>` / `?before=<opaque>`, walking a sort key). Infinite scroll is a UI pattern that repeatedly extends a cursor-paginated collection; infinite *queries* are the cache structure that supports it.

**The mechanics and the failure each family has.**

```
OFFSET                              CURSOR (keyset)
GET /posts?offset=40&limit=20       GET /posts?limit=20&after=<cursor>
SELECT … LIMIT 20 OFFSET 40         SELECT … WHERE (createdAt, id) < (:ts, :id) ORDER BY createdAt DESC, id DESC LIMIT 20

+ page numbers, "jump to page 57"   + stable under inserts/deletes
+ trivially random-access            + O(log n) with a composite index
+ total count available               + no skipped/duplicated rows when the data mutates
- OFFSET 400000 is a full scan       - no page numbers ("next/prev" only)
- inserts shift rows: you see row 41  - cursor is opaque, must be encoded server-side
  twice or skip one entirely         - no total count without a second query
```

Concretely, in a feed where someone posts at the top between your page 1 and page 2, offset 20 now returns what you already saw as row 20, and one row is never shown. Cursors cannot do that, which is why *feeds, comments, and activity streams use cursors* and *admin tables with page numbers use offsets*.

```ts
// Cursor: opaque, encoded, tamper-evident, and stable.
type Page<T> = { items: T[]; nextCursor: string | null; prevCursor: string | null };
// Server: sort by (createdAt DESC, id DESC). The cursor encodes BOTH, because a non-unique sort
// key makes the cursor ambiguous ("rows with the same timestamp" → duplicates/skips).
const encode = (c: { ts: string; id: string }) => Buffer.from(JSON.stringify(c)).toString('base64url');
const decode = (s: string) => JSON.parse(Buffer.from(s, 'base64url').toString());
// SQL: WHERE (created_at, id) < ($1, $2) ORDER BY created_at DESC, id DESC LIMIT $3
```

```tsx
// TanStack Query infinite queries: the whole pattern, forward and backward.
import { useInfiniteQuery, useQueryClient } from '@tanstack/react-query';

type FeedPage = { items: Post[]; nextCursor: string | null; prevCursor: string | null };

export const useFeed = (feedId: string) =>
  useInfiniteQuery({
    queryKey: ['feed', { feedId }],
    queryFn: ({ pageParam, signal }) => api.get<FeedPage>(`/feeds/${feedId}`, { signal, cursor: pageParam }),
    initialPageParam: null as string | null,
    getNextPageParam: (last) => last.nextCursor,        // undefined/null stops the chain
    getPreviousPageParam: (first) => first.prevCursor,  // backwards, for "load older" on scroll-up
    maxPages: 7,                                          // ← the memory bound; keep only 7 pages in cache
    staleTime: 15_000,
  });

// Consuming it (forward). isFetchingNextPage vs isFetchingPreviousPage are different states; use both.
function Feed({ feedId }: { feedId: string }) {
  const q = useFeed(feedId);
  const pages = q.data?.pages ?? [];

  // Merge + dedupe: a post can appear in two pages if the cursor window overlaps after an insert.
  const items = useMemo(() => {
    const seen = new Set<string>();
    return pages.flatMap((p) => p.items).filter((i) => (seen.has(i.id) ? false : (seen.add(i.id), true)));
  }, [pages]);

  const { ref, inView } = useIntersection({ rootMargin: '600px' });      // prefetch before visible: 600px of margin
  useEffect(() => { if (inView && q.hasNextPage && !q.isFetchingNextPage) void q.fetchNextPage(); }, [inView, q]);
  // A pull-to-load-older button uses fetchPreviousPage(); render its pages *before* the current ones.

  return (
    <ul>
      {items.map((p) => <li key={p.id}><PostRow post={p} /></li>)}
      <li ref={ref} aria-hidden>
        {q.isFetchingNextPage ? <FeedSkeleton /> : q.hasNextPage ? null : <EndOfFeed />}
      </li>
    </ul>
  );
}
```

**Bidirectional scroll — the part most implementations skip.** To support "load older messages when the user scrolls up" you need three things: a `prevCursor` from the server, `getPreviousPageParam`, and *reverse* rendering order so older pages go above:

```tsx
// Scroll container approach: keep the newest at the bottom, append older content above it,
// and preserve the viewport by measuring the scrollHeight delta before/after the prepend.
function usePrependPreserveScroll(deps: unknown[], prepend: () => void) {
  const ref = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null);
  const prev = useRef({ height: 0, top: 0 });
  const first = useRef(true);
  useLayoutEffect(() => {
    const el = ref.current;
    if (!el) return;
    if (first.current) { first.current = false; prev.current = { height: el.scrollHeight, top: el.scrollTop }; return; }
    const delta = el.scrollHeight - prev.current.height;
    if (delta !== 0) el.scrollTop = prev.current.top + delta;      // undo the jump the prepend caused
    prev.current = { height: el.scrollHeight, top: el.scrollTop };
  }, deps);
  return ref;
}

// Accessibility and control-flow requirements for a feed:
// · The sentinel is not keyboard-reachable -> provide a real "Load more" button that is focusable and
//   calls fetchNextPage() too (infinite scroll must never be the ONLY way to reach content).
// · Announce results politely: <p aria-live="polite" class="sr-only">{items.length} posts loaded</p>
// · If the sentinel is unreachable by keyboard, that is a WCAG failure no scanner catches.
```

**`maxPages` and the memory model.** Without it, an hour of scrolling accumulates thousands of posts in one cache entry. With `maxPages: 7`, TanStack Query keeps the 7 most recent pages and drops the others as you scroll — which means scroll restoration by item id (4.5.7) must be able to *re-fetch* the page containing the anchor. Two acceptable designs: (a) keep `maxPages` generous and virtualise the list (4.6.11.2); (b) keep a small window and treat the scroll position as "the id at the top of the viewport", restoring by fetching backwards until the anchor exists.

**Offset pagination done properly**, for admin tables where page numbers are a requirement:

```tsx
// Server: total count + page metadata. Keep the query key aligned with the URL (4.3.8) so pages are shareable.
const page = Number(params.get('page') ?? 1);
const { data, isPending, isFetching, placeholderData } = useQuery({
  queryKey: ['users', { page, q, sort }],
  queryFn: () => api.get(`/users?page=${page}&limit=25&q=${q}&sort=${sort}`),
  placeholderData: keepPreviousData,      // ← no skeleton flash while the next page loads; rows dim instead
});
// Then: disable Next when `!data.hasMore`, show "1–25 of 1,234" from data.total, and keep page ≤ totalPages
// after a delete reduces the total (clamp and navigate with replace).
// Stable sorting is mandatory: without a tiebreaker column in ORDER BY, rows can repeat across pages.
```

**When to use which.** Cursor: feeds, comments, chat history, activity logs, anything that mutates while you read it, and infinite scroll. Offset: admin tables with page numbers, "jump to page", small mutable datasets, and search results where total count matters. Keyset-only ("load more"): search autocomplete, browse facets where jumping to page 400 is meaningless.

**Trade-offs.** Cursor pagination is the correct choice for correctness and index efficiency, and it costs you page numbers and total counts (both need either a second query or an approximate count — say "1,200+" rather than an exact number, which is what good products do). Infinite scroll removes pagination UI but harms: find-in-page, returning to a position, the back button, and accessibility (no page boundaries). The senior answer is that infinite scroll is a *UI* decision that should be paired with (a) a URL-representable anchor for restoration, (b) a keyboard-reachable "Load more", and (c) `maxPages` + virtualisation so memory is bounded. Server-side aggregation for huge tables beats client pagination entirely: if a user only sees 25 rows, never fetch 100k.

**Production and operational.**
- Every cursor must be validated server-side and scoped to the filter set (a cursor from filter A is nonsense for filter B) — encode the filter hash into the cursor and reject mismatches, or you get leaked pages.
- Log the number of pages fetched per session; a long-tail tail means `maxPages` is wrong or the sentinel margin is too small.
- Watch the DB: `OFFSET 400000` shows up as a sequential scan; a missing composite index on `(created_at, id)` shows up as slow cursor queries. Cursor pagination is only fast if the index exists *in the sort order*.
- Track "duplicate row seen" rate in analytics as a data-integrity signal — offset pagination on a mutating table shows up in product metrics as engagement oddities nobody can explain.

**Common mistakes.** Infinite scroll as the only path to content. No `maxPages`. Forgetting to dedupe by id. Offset pagination on a mutating feed. Cursor sort key not being unique. No "Load more" button. Restoring scroll by pixel offset after a prepend (it jumps). Using the sentinel with `rootMargin: '0px'` and a fast scroll, causing request storms — use a margin and a `isFetching` guard.

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "Offset vs cursor: give the exact failure of each."
- "How do you handle a post inserted while the user pages?"
- "How do you load older messages when scrolling up?"
- "What is `maxPages` and why does it matter?"
- "How would you make infinite scroll accessible?"
- "What index does cursor pagination need?"

**Mistakes:** assuming infinite scroll is free; `keepPreviousData` forgotten on page changes; a cursor that leaks filter state; no dedupe.

---

## 4.4.6 Polling vs WebSockets vs SSE for live data

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.4.1, 4.3.8 (multi-tab)

**Definition:** Three ways to learn that remote data changed. **Polling** asks on a timer. **Server-Sent Events (SSE)** is a one-way server→client stream over HTTP. **WebSockets** are a bidirectional, persistent, upgradeable protocol with framing. The choice is about *push capability vs* complexity, cost, and failure modes — and it should be driven by how fresh the data must be.

**The comparison, honestly.**

| | Polling | SSE | WebSocket |
|---|---|---|---|
| Direction | client → server requests | server → client | both |
| Transport | ordinary HTTP(S) | HTTP response with `text/event-stream` | HTTP upgrade, then frames |
| Setup cost | ~5 lines | ~10 lines client + 10 server | ~40+ lines client + server infra |
| Infra | none | none (any HTTP server, most CDNs pass through) | needs a WS-aware server; sticky sessions or a broker at scale (Redis/NATS/Kafka fanout) |
| Reconnect | free (next tick) | **automatic** in the browser (`EventSource`) with `Last-Event-ID` | **you implement it**: backoff, jitter, heartbeat, resync |
| Backpressure / ordering | n/a | TCP-level, one stream | app-level |
| Binary payloads | yes | text only (UTF-8) | yes |
| Auth | normal cookies/headers | normal cookies/headers (same-origin) | custom (query token is a leak in logs; use a cookie or a subprotocol) |
| Proxies/CDNs | universal | usually fine; must disable buffering (`X-Accel-Buffering: no`) | often breaks unless configured |
| Cost at 10k idle clients | 10k × (60/interval) requests/min — the expensive one | cheap (a sleeping connection) | cheap, but needs broker capacity |
| Right for | near-real-time not needed; dashboards; low value per event | notifications, live feeds, progress, presence (server→client only!) | chat, collaborative editing, multiplayer, bidirectional RPC |

```tsx
// 1. POLLING — the cheapest correct answer, and usually the right one.
const { data } = useQuery({
  queryKey: ['orders', { status: 'open' }],
  queryFn: () => api.get('/orders?status=open'),
  refetchInterval: (q) => (q.state.error ? 30_000 : 5_000),   // back off on failure (5s is aggressive)
  refetchIntervalInBackground: false,                          // default: STOP when the tab is hidden (huge win)
  staleTime: 4_000,                                             // don't also refetch on focus/mount within the window
});
// Rules: interval >= staleTime; stop on hidden tab; pause on error and back off; a visible "live" indicator;
// jitter if you have thousands of clients polling the same endpoint, or align your polls with a server-friendly cadence.

// 2. SSE — one-way live updates. ~30 lines, all of it worth memorising.
export function useEventStream<T>(
  url: string,
  onEvent: (e: { type: string; data: T; id?: string }) => void,
  { enabled = true }: { enabled?: boolean } = {},
) {
  const qc = useQueryClient();
  const handler = useRef(onEvent);
  handler.current = onEvent;                                   // keep the latest without reconnecting
  const [connected, setConnected] = useState(false);

  useEffect(() => {
    if (!enabled) return;
    const es = new EventSource(url, { withCredentials: true }); // EventSource reconnects ITSELF on error
    es.onopen = () => setConnected(true);
    es.onerror = () => setConnected(false);                     // do NOT close it: the browser retries with backoff
    es.onmessage = (ev) => {
      const parsed = JSON.parse(ev.data) as { type: string; data: T; id?: string };
      handler.current(parsed);
      // Turn the event into a cache write: this is the whole architecture — the stream is an
      // invalidation signal, and the cache stays the single source of truth for rendering.
      switch (parsed.type) {
        case 'order.updated': qc.setQueryData(['orders'], (old: any) => patchOrder(old, parsed.data)); break;
        case 'order.created': qc.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['orders'] }); break;
      }
    };
    // Named events: the server sends "event: ping"; listen per-name: es.addEventListener('ping', ...)
    return () => es.close();                                    // unmount/nav: close, or you leak a connection per visit
  }, [url, enabled, qc]);

  return { connected };
}
// Server side (Express): res.writeHead(200, { 'Content-Type':'text/event-stream', 'Cache-Control':'no-cache, no-transform',
//   Connection:'keep-alive', 'X-Accel-Buffering':'no' });  then res.write(`event: ping\ndata: {}\n\n`) every 25 s
// (comment lines keep proxies from closing an idle connection) and `id: <seq>` per event so a reconnect
// replays via Last-Event-ID.
// Cross-tab: BroadcastChannel + localStorage 'ping' to fan one connection out to all tabs.

// 3. WEBSOCKET — bidirectional. The reconnect + resync loop is the actual engineering.
export function useSocket(url: string) {
  const [status, setStatus] = useState<'connecting' | 'open' | 'closed'>('connecting');
  const [lastSeq, setLastSeq] = useState(0);
  const qc = useQueryClient();

  useEffect(() => {
    let ws: WebSocket | null = null;
    let attempt = 0;
    let heartbeat: number | undefined;
    let cancelled = false;

    const connect = () => {
      ws = new WebSocket(url);
      ws.onopen = () => {
        attempt = 0; setStatus('open');
        ws!.send(JSON.stringify({ type: 'subscribe', channel, since: lastSeq }));   // RESYNC: ask for what we missed
        heartbeat = window.setInterval(() => ws?.send(JSON.stringify({ type: 'ping' })), 25_000);  // heartbeat
      };
      ws.onmessage = (ev) => {
        const msg = JSON.parse(ev.data as string);
        if (msg.type === 'pong') return;
        if (msg.seq) setLastSeq(Math.max(lastSeq, msg.seq));    // monotonic sequence = the resync cursor
        qc.setQueryData(['messages', { channel }], (old: Msg[] | undefined) => dedupeById([...(old ?? []), msg], 'id'));
      };
      ws.onclose = () => {
        setStatus('closed');
        clearInterval(heartbeat);
        if (cancelled) return;
        const delay = Math.min(30_000, 500 * 2 ** attempt++) * (0.5 + Math.random() / 2);   // exponential + JITTER
        setTimeout(connect, delay);                                                     // jitter avoids a thundering herd
      };
      ws.onerror = () => ws?.close();                        // error is always followed by close: reconnect there, not here
    };
    connect();
    return () => { cancelled = true; clearInterval(heartbeat); ws?.close(); };
  }, [url, channel, lastSeq]);   // ← do NOT put lastSeq in deps: it changes on every message and reconnects constantly.
  // (Use a ref for lastSeq instead — this dependency-list bug is the #1 WebSocket bug.)
  return { status };
}
```

**Choosing.** The decision order that a senior engineer uses:

1. Does the user need to *see* changes without doing anything? If no → **no live data at all**; use invalidation on focus (4.4.11). Most "live" requirements are actually "when I come back, it is correct".
2. Is the staleness tolerance measured in minutes? → **polling** with an interval above the tolerance, paused when hidden. 99% of dashboards, admin panels, and order lists.
3. Seconds, and one-way (server tells client)? → **SSE**. Notifications, job progress, live scores, presence, "someone commented".
4. Seconds, and the client must talk (typing indicators, subscriptions per entity, presence)? → **WebSocket**, and accept the operational cost.
5. Multi-user concurrent editing → WebSocket + CRDT/OT (4.6.11.5), and acknowledge this is a multi-month project, not a feature.

**Trade-offs, stated honestly.** Polling is trivially reliable and CDN/proxy-friendly, but its cost scales as `clients × (60 / interval)` requests and it cannot express "this row changed"; at 5 s across 10k clients that is 120k requests/min — at which point you want SSE plus a change-feed endpoint. SSE is nearly free at idle and reconnects itself, but it is one-way, text-only, and depends on proxy configuration (buffering is the classic bug: you send events and nothing arrives until the buffer fills). WebSockets are the most capable and the most expensive to operate: connection draining on deploy, sticky sessions behind a load balancer or a broker for fan-out, per-connection memory, heartbeats to detect half-open connections, and mobile networks that drop connections constantly — so reconnect-with-backoff and a resync query are mandatory, not optional.

**Production and operational.**
- **Every transport needs a resync path.** Polling: none (the next tick is the resync). SSE: `Last-Event-ID` replay. WebSocket: `since=lastSeq` on reconnect plus a `GET /messages?since=` reconciliation. Never assume the stream is complete.
- **Detect half-open connections**: ping/pong for WS, a comment frame for SSE, and a server-side idle timeout. A TCP connection that looks open can be dead for hours behind a NAT.
- **Broadcast from the server layer, not from request handlers**: after a transaction commits, publish to Redis/NATS/Kafka; the WS/SSE gateway subscribes. Otherwise you need N socket servers (one per app instance) and users on different instances see different data — the classic "works locally, stale in production" bug.
- **Rate-limit the *outbound* stream per connection** (coalesce updates: a row changing 100×/s should send the latest value every 100 ms, not 100 events).
- **Drain before deploy**: stop accepting, send a `bye` frame, close after a grace period, and rely on client reconnect. Track reconnect rate as a health metric — a spike means a bad deploy or a broker problem.
- **Accessibility and battery**: pause polling/streaming when `document.hidden`; respect `prefers-reduced-data` where the platform offers it; a live region announcement is required if the update matters to a screen-reader user.

**Common mistakes.** `EventSource` closed in `onerror` (you turn a self-healing stream into a dead one). `lastSeq` in the effect deps (constant reconnects). No jitter on reconnect (thundering herd on recovery). Assuming SSE works behind nginx without disabling buffering. Polling with an interval *below* `staleTime` (double requests). Treating a WS message as truth and rendering it directly, bypassing the cache (you now have two sources of truth and no invalidation).

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "Polling, SSE, or WebSocket — how do you decide?"
- "What must a WebSocket client implement that `fetch` gives you for free?"
- "How do you avoid a thundering herd on reconnect?"
- "How does `EventSource` handle reconnection, and what does it not do?"
- "How do you scale live updates to 50k connections?"

**Mistakes:** "WebSocket for everything"; closing an EventSource in `onerror`; no resync after reconnect; polling in a hidden tab.

---

## 4.4.7 React Hook Form and Formik: uncontrolled-first philosophy

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.2.4 (actions), 4.4.8 (schemas)

**Definition:** A form library's job is to own values, validation, submission, error mapping, and the *pending* state, with as few re-renders as possible. React Hook Form (RHF) is **uncontrolled-first**: it reads values from the DOM through refs on submit (and on demand), so typing causes **zero React re-renders**. Formik is **controlled-first**: every change is a `setState`, so typing re-renders the form (it mitigates this with `validateOnChange` toggles and deferring validation).

**The trade-off, in one table.**

| | React Hook Form | Formik |
|---|---|---|
| Model | Uncontrolled (`register`) + controlled for special cases | Controlled state object |
| Re-renders on typing | **Zero** (until you enable a watched field) | One per keystroke |
| Validation | Schema-based, at submit (and on change per field if asked) | `validate` functions or Yup, on change / blur / submit, all configurable |
| Async validation | Built-in (`async` resolver, cancels stale) | Awkward (a race unless you guard it) |
| Field arrays | `useFieldArray` (stable keys, append/remove/move/swap/insert) | `FieldArray` (immutable, more ceremony) |
| Errors from the server | `setError(name, { message })` — first-class | Manual state merging |
| Nested/dependent fields | `watch`/`useWatch` for a subset, subscription per field | Full re-render by default |
| Bundle | ~9 kB gz (+ resolver) | ~11 kB gz (+ Yup) |
| Status in 2026 | The default choice in new React apps | Maintained but quiet; still fine, lots of existing code |

**RHF, in full — the shape you should be able to write without thinking.**

```tsx
import { useForm, useFieldArray, useWatch, Controller } from 'react-hook-form';
import { zodResolver } from '@hookform/resolvers/zod';
import { z } from 'zod';

const schema = z.object({
  email: z.string().min(1, 'Required').email('Enter a valid email'),
  displayName: z.string().min(2, 'At least 2 characters').max(40),
  plan: z.enum(['free', 'pro', 'enterprise']),
  seats: z.coerce.number().int().min(1).max(500).default(1),
  seats: z.number(),                        // if you must keep the number typed (below)
  billingSameAsOwner: z.boolean().default(false),
  tags: z.array(z.string()).max(5).default([]),
  acceptTos: z.literal(true, { errorMap: () => ({ message: 'You must accept the terms' }) }),
});
type FormValues = z.infer<typeof schema>;

export function SignupForm({ onSubmit }: { onSubmit: (v: FormValues) => Promise<void> }) {
  const {
    register, handleSubmit, control, formState: { errors, isSubmitting, isValid, dirtyFields },
    setError, clearErrors, reset, getValues, setValue, watch,
  } = useForm<FormValues>({
    resolver: zodResolver(schema),
    mode: 'onTouched',        // validate a field after it is touched, not on every keystroke (the sane default)
    reValidateMode: 'onChange', // after a failed submit, re-check live so the error clears as they fix it
    defaultValues: { plan: 'free', seats: 1, tags: [], billingSameAsOwner: false, acceptTos: false as true },
    criteriaMode: 'firstError',     // stop at the first failed rule per field (faster, less spam)
    shouldFocusError: true,          // focus the first invalid field on a failed submit (a11y + UX)
    values: undefined,               // 'values' (not defaultValues) REPLACES values when props change — use it to reset on prop change
  });

  const tags = useFieldArray({ control, name: 'tags' });     // stable ids, correct keys
  // Subscribe to exactly the fields you need in this component — a watched field re-renders the component.
  const plan = useWatch({ control, name: 'plan' });

  return (
    <form noValidate onSubmit={handleSubmit(async (values) => {
      await onSubmit(values);
      reset(values);                                            // or reset(defaults) after a successful save
    }, (validationErrors) => {
      // Server-side field errors from a 422, merged without clobbering client errors:
      for (const [field, message] of Object.entries(mapServerErrors(validationErrors))) {
        setError(field as keyof FormValues, { type: 'server', message });
      }
    })}>
      <label htmlFor="email">Email</label>
      <input id="email" type="email" aria-invalid={!!errors.email} aria-describedby={errors.email ? 'email-err' : undefined}
             {...register('email')} />
      {errors.email && <p id="email-err" role="alert">{errors.email.message}</p>}
      {/* errors.email is a FieldError: { type, message, ref }. errors.root is for form-level messages. */}

      <input type="number" inputMode="numeric" {...register('seats', { valueAsNumber: true })} />   {/* no re-render */}
      <input type="checkbox" {...register('acceptTos')} />

      {tags.fields.map((f, i) => (
        <div key={f.id} className="row">                          {/* f.id, NOT f.index */}
          <input {...register(`tags.${i}.name`)} />               {/* RHF uses paths as field names: 'tags.0.name' */}
          <button type="button" onClick={() => tags.remove(i)}>Remove</button>
        </div>
      ))}
      <button type="button" onClick={() => tags.append({ name: '' })}>Add tag</button>

      {/* Controlled ONLY where you need React: rich text, a combobox, a mask, a date picker. */}
      <Controller name="plan" control={control} render={({ field }) => (
        <select {...field}>
          {['free', 'pro', 'enterprise'].map((p) => <option key={p} value={p}>{p}</option>)}
        </select>
      )} />

      <button type="submit" disabled={isSubmitting}>
        {isSubmitting ? 'Creating…' : 'Create account'}
      </button>
      <button type="button" onClick={() => reset()}>Reset</button>
    </form>
  );
}
```

**The philosophy, stated precisely.** RHF treats the form as a *DOM-controlled object graph* with a registry: `register` attaches a `ref`, so the value lives in the input; the form reads it on submit, on `trigger()`, or on a blur event it subscribes to. Validation is a *pull* operation (submit, or `trigger('email')`), not a push on every keystroke. Result: typing in a 200-field form costs zero renders, and validation cost is proportional to how much the user actually interacts. The controlled mode exists for integration points that need React state (see `Controller`), and `watch`/`useWatch` reintroduce re-renders *deliberately* and narrowly.

**Formik, for comparison — controlled state, with the honest trade-offs.**

```tsx
import { Formik, Form, Field, ErrorMessage, useField, useFormikContext, type FormikProps } from 'formik';
import * as Yup from 'yup';

const schema = Yup.object({ email: Yup.string().email('Enter a valid email').required('Required') });
type Values = { email: string };

export function SignupFormik({ onSubmit }: { onSubmit: (v: Values) => Promise<void> }) {
  return (
    <Formik<Values>
      initialValues={{ email: '' }}
      validationSchema={schema}
      validateOnChange={false}          // ← the important knob: don't validate on every keystroke
      validateOnBlur={true}
      onSubmit={async (values, { setSubmitting, setFieldError, setTouched, setStatus }) => {
        setStatus(undefined);
        try {
          await onSubmit(values);
        } catch (err) {
          if (err instanceof FieldValidationError) {
            // server-side field errors, per field
            for (const [f, m] of Object.entries(err.fields)) setFieldError(f, m);
            setTouched(Object.fromEntries(Object.keys(err.fields).map((f) => [f, true])), false);
          } else setStatus('Could not create the account. Try again.');
          // MUST reset isSubmitting on the error path, or the form hangs forever.
        } finally { setSubmitting(false); }
      }}
    >
      {({ isSubmitting, errors, touched, status, values }) => (
        <Form>
          <Field name="email">
            {({ field, meta }) => (
              <>
                <input {...field} onBlur={(e) => { field.onBlur(e); void handleBlur(e); }} />
                {touched.email && meta.error && <p role="alert">{meta.error}</p>}
              </>
            )}
          </Field>
          <ErrorMessage name="email" component="p" role="alert" />
          <button type="submit" disabled={isSubmitting}>{isSubmitting ? 'Saving…' : 'Save'}</button>
          {status && <p role="alert">{status}</p>}
        </Form>
      )}
    </Formik>
  );
}
```

**What both libraries give you that hand-rolled forms do not** (and why hand-rolling is usually a mistake at scale): one submit handler that validates then submits; a single place for server field errors (`setError` / `setFieldError`); focus-the-first-error; dirty/untouched tracking per field so you know what to actually validate; a pending state that disables the submit button and guards double submission; array field operations with stable keys; and a reset path that is not `location.reload()`.

**When to use which, and when neither.** RHF for anything non-trivial, especially large forms, dynamic arrays, and unstyled components. Formik when the codebase already uses it, when you need its `<Field>` render-prop style heavily, or when a wrapper component in your design system is built on it. **Neither** when: the form is one input and a button (a Server Action, or a plain `<form action>`), when you want native validation and progressive enhancement (`required`, `type="email"`, `min`, `pattern` — free, accessible, works without JS), or when the form is a search/filter box (put it in the URL instead, 4.3.8). In general: **always render the native attributes** (`required`, `type`, `min`, `maxLength`, `inputMode`, `autoComplete`) even if JS validation is authoritative — they give mobile keyboards, browser autofill, and a11y for free. `noValidate` only when your schema covers everything the native rules would have.

**Trade-offs and production concerns.**
- **Validation timing is the real decision.** `mode: 'onChange'` is hostile (errors while typing); `onBlur` is decent; `onTouched` is the best default; `onSubmit` is the most accessible (errors appear after an explicit attempt) and is what progressive-enhancement forms do. Use `mode: 'onBlur'` + `reValidateMode: 'onChange'`.
- **Async validation needs cancellation** — a uniqueness check on email. RHF handles it; hand-rolled does not, and you get a stale "email is taken" for the previous value.
- **Server errors are not client errors.** A 422 body of `{ email: "already taken" }` must be mapped to field errors, not shown as a form-level toast. Put the mapping in one function (above) and test it.
- **Never disable submit based on `isValid` in RHF** without knowing `isValid` means "validated at least once" — with `mode: 'onTouched'` a pristine form reports `false`, so the button is disabled until the user touches a field, which looks broken. Use `isSubmitting` for the disabled state and let validation handle correctness.
- **Password/autofill:** `autoComplete="new-password"`, `autoComplete="email"`, correct `inputMode`, and `type="email"` — this is a conversion and accessibility problem that no library solves for you.
- **Unsaved-changes warning**: `isDirty && !isSubmitting` → `beforeunload` guard plus a router navigation blocker.
- Multi-step forms: one schema per step (partial via `.pick()`), keep all values in one form instance, and validate only the current step's fields with `trigger(fields)`; a wizard is a great XState candidate if steps have guarded transitions (4.3.7).

**Common mistakes.** Controlled-everything forms with a re-render per keystroke on a 200-field form. `noValidate` with no replacement for the native affordances. Server errors overwriting client state or vice versa. `setSubmitting(false)` missing in Formik's catch path. Keys by index in `useFieldArray` (`f.id` exists for a reason). Losing focus on every validation change because the whole form remounts (missing `key`). Clearing a field's error before the user has interacted with it.

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "Why is uncontrolled better than controlled for forms?"
- "How do you map server validation errors to fields?"
- "When is `Controller` justified?"
- "How do you do async uniqueness validation without races?"
- "Why shouldn't you disable submit on `isValid`?"
- "What is `useFieldArray` and why do the ids matter?"

**Mistakes:** reaching for a library to validate one input; re-rendering the form on every keystroke; treating client validation as a security boundary.

---

## 4.4.8 Schema validation with Zod and Yup, shared client/server schemas

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.4.7, 3.x (input validation basics)

**Definition:** A schema is a declarative, executable specification of the shape of valid data that also (a) validates at runtime, (b) infers a TypeScript type, and (c) can produce a human-readable error *path*. Zod and Yup are the two dominant implementations. The high-value pattern is **one schema module imported by both the client form and the server handler**, so the contract cannot drift.

**Zod vs Yup, mechanically.**

| | Zod v3/v4 | Yup v1 |
|---|---|---|
| Type source of truth | `z.infer<typeof schema>` — the schema *is* the type | `InferType<typeof schema>` — same idea |
| Parse | `schema.parse(x)` throws `ZodError` | `schema.validate(x)` returns a promise (rejects with `ValidationError`) — hence the `await` everywhere |
| Safe parse | `.safeParse(x)` → `{ success, data \| error }` | `.validate(x, { abortEarly: false })` + try/catch |
| Errors | `error.issues[]` with `path: (string\|number)[]`, `code`, `message` | `error.errors[]` with `path`, `message`, `type`; `error.inner[]` for all errors |
| Coercion | First-class (`z.coerce.number()`, `z.coerce.boolean()`, `z.preprocess`) | `yup.number().transform()` / `.cast()` — exists but is opt-in and confusing |
| Strictness | Unknown keys stripped by default in v3 (`z.object`), `z.strictObject` to error | `noUnknown` / `.noUnknown()`; `stripUnknown` to remove |
| Optional/null | `z.string().optional()` = `string \| undefined`; `.nullable()` for `\| null` | `.optional()` / `.nullable()` — similar, but `null` vs `undefined` confusion is a common Yup bug |
| Refinements | `.refine(fn, msg)`, `.superRefine` with `ctx.addIssue({ path })` for cross-field | `.test(name, msg, fn)` with `test` context, or `.when()` for conditionals |
| Async refinements | `schema.parseAsync` / `refine(async)` | `.validate()` is async by design |
| Union/discriminated union | Excellent (`z.discriminatedUnion`) | Good (`yup.mixed().oneOf`) |
| Size/perf | ~14 kB, sync-first (newer: core split for smaller bundles) | ~16 kB, promise-based |
| Ecosystem default in 2026 | RHF resolver, tRPC, Hono, drizzle-zod, OpenAPI generators | React Native/Formik heritage; still widely used |

```ts
// shared/contracts.ts — imported by BOTH the form and the handler. This is the whole point.
import { z } from 'zod';

export const SignupSchema = z.object({
  email: z.string().trim().min(1, 'Email is required').email('Enter a valid email'),
  password: z
    .string()
    .min(12, 'Use at least 12 characters')
    // Server-only refinement, attached conditionally so the client doesn't leak the policy:
    .refine((v) => !COMPROMISED.has(v), 'That password appears in a breach list'),
  displayName: z.string().trim().min(2, 'At least 2 characters').max(40),
  role: z.enum(['member', 'admin', 'owner'], { errorMap: () => ({ message: 'Unknown role' }) }),
  seats: z.coerce.number().int('Whole numbers only').min(1).max(500),
  billingAddress: z.object({ line1: z.string().min(1), city: z.string().min(1), country: z.literal('US'), postcode: z.string().regex(/^\d{5}$/) }),
  acceptTos: z.literal(true, { errorMap: () => ({ message: 'You must accept the terms' }) }),
})
  .strict()                                  // reject unknown keys => a client bug (typo'd field) fails loudly
  .superRefine((val, ctx) => {
    if (val.role !== 'member' && val.seats > 100) {           // cross-field rule with a precise path
      ctx.addIssue({ code: 'custom', path: ['seats'], message: 'Admin roles are limited to 100 seats' });
    }
  });

export type Signup = z.infer<typeof SignupSchema>;
```

```ts
// Server: Express handler. parse BEFORE touching the database, and return issues as field errors.
app.post('/api/signup', async (req, res) => {
  const parsed = SignupSchema.safeParse(req.body);
  if (!parsed.success) {
    return res.status(422).json({
      error: 'validation_failed',
      // Send only messages you are happy to show a user; keep codes for your own logic.
      fields: parsed.error.issues.map((i) => ({ path: i.path.join('.'), message: i.message, code: i.code })),
    });
  }
  const user = await createUser(parsed.data);        // parsed.data is typed Signup — no `any` downstream
  res.status(201).json(user);
});
```

```tsx
// Client: map the 422 back into field errors, one function, used by every form.
function mapServerErrors(issues: { path: string; message: string }[]) {
  return Object.fromEntries(issues.map((i) => [i.path, i.message]));
}
// Yup equivalent — path is already dotted; `error.inner` holds every issue when abortEarly:false.
try { await schema.validate(values, { abortEarly: false }); }
catch (e) {
  if (e instanceof yup.ValidationError) return Object.fromEntries(e.inner.map((i) => [i.path!, i.message]));
  throw e;
}
```

**When to validate, in which layer.** Four gates, and each has a job:

| Gate | Schema | On failure |
|---|---|---|
| **HTML attributes** | `required`, `type`, `min`, `maxlength`, `pattern`, `inputMode` | Browser-native bubble; free mobile keyboard + autofill + a11y. Never remove without a replacement. |
| **Client (submit / onTouched)** | the shared schema via `zodResolver` | Inline field errors; prevents a pointless round trip; UX only |
| **Server boundary** | the *same* schema, always | 422 with per-field errors; this is the only security boundary (PHASE 7) |
| **Database / downstream** | constraints, NOT NULL, foreign keys | 500 → map to a friendly message; never surface raw driver errors |

**Why the client copy is not a security control.** Anyone can `curl` the endpoint. Client validation exists to give fast feedback and to avoid wasted requests; the server is the authority. Interviewers like this framing because most candidates assume otherwise.

**Structural rules you must encode deliberately**, and the defaults that surprise people:

1. **`z.object` strips unknown keys** (v3) — a client sending `{ role: 'owner', isAdmin: true }` has `isAdmin` silently dropped. That is usually *desirable* (mass-assignment defence), but use `.strict()` when you want a loud failure instead of silence.
2. **Coercion is a security-relevant decision.** `z.coerce.number()` turns `""` into `0` and `null` into `0`. For numbers that must be present, use `z.number()` and validate the string first, or use `z.preprocess` with an explicit empty check. Same for `z.coerce.boolean()`: `"false"` coerces to **`true`** in JS. This is a classic real bug.
3. **`z.string()` does not trim.** `.trim()` is a transform; order matters (`z.string().trim().min(1)`).
4. **Optional vs nullable** are different types. `z.string().optional()` accepts `undefined` but not `null`; `.nullable()` accepts `null`. Omitting the modifier is the most common source of "why does my optional field fail validation".
5. **Dates**: validate as strings with a format check at the boundary, then convert to `Date`/`Temporal` inside — never accept a `Date` from `JSON.parse` (it arrives as a string).
6. **Enums vs literals**: `z.enum([...])` gives a union type and rejects unknown values; that is exactly what you want for a `role`.
7. **`partial()` vs a new schema**: `.partial()` is for updates where every field is optional; then remember that `undefined` means "don't change" while `null` must be a separate path.
8. **File and URL fields**: validate on the *server* with content sniffing and size limits; a client `File` type check is a hint, not a control.

**Async refinements belong on the server** (uniqueness, "is this a valid coupon"): `await SignupSchema.parseAsync(req.body)` with `.refine(async v => ...)`. On the client they need cancellation, which RHF handles and you should not hand-roll.

**Trade-offs and where the boundary belongs.** One shared schema means one place to change the contract, but it also couples your client bundle to the server contract (a heavy schema with a big enum costs bundle size) and it forces you to decide what *not* to share (password policy details, rate limits). The pragmatic split: share *shape* and *user-facing* messages; keep *policy* (rate limits, permission, uniqueness) on the server. Zod v4 improved tree-shaking via a modular core (`zod/mini`) and added better error maps; if bundle size matters, check the current docs rather than assuming v3 behaviour — and the same for `z.email()` (v4 made top-level string formats, `z.string().email()` is deprecated in v4). Always verify against the version you have installed.

**Production and operational.** Enforce schemas at **every** trust boundary: HTTP handlers, route params, query strings, WebSocket messages, uploaded file metadata, and anything from `localStorage`/`postMessage` (all untrusted). Return machine codes (`code: 'too_small'`) plus messages, and drive analytics off codes. Log validation failures with a fingerprint so a client typo or an attacker's probe shows up as a metric, not just a 422 in the logs. Never echo the submitted value back in the error message (reflected XSS risk if you ever render it as HTML) and never include internal reasons ("foreign key violation on org_members").

**Common mistakes.** Validating on the server with a *different* shape than the client (the drift this pattern exists to prevent). `z.coerce.boolean()` silently inverting `"false"`. Using `.optional()` where the API sends `null`. Only validating in the client. Showing a generic "invalid input" instead of mapping field errors. A schema per component so the same field is validated three ways. Leaking server policy details to the client and then treating them as enforced.

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "How do you keep client and server validation in sync?"
- "Why is client validation not a security control?"
- "What is the difference between `optional` and `nullable`?"
- "When do you use `safeParse` vs `parse`?"
- "How do you return field errors to a form?"
- "Tell me about a schema default that surprised you." (`z.coerce.boolean()` on `"false"`, `.strict()` vs stripping, coercion of `""` to `0`.)

**Mistakes:** two schemas that drift; coercion without checking; stripping unknown keys when you wanted rejection; schema errors surfaced as one big red banner.

---

## 4.4.9 File upload UX: progress, previews, multipart, presigned URLs

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.4.7 (forms), 4.4.6 (progress events), PHASE 7 (file upload security)

**Definition:** File upload is not a form field; it is a **resumable, observable, retryable, cancellable transfer** that a user is watching. The design problem has five parts: the UX states (idle → validating → uploading with progress → processing → done/failed), the transport (single POST vs multipart vs direct-to-object-storage), the preview, the queue (concurrency), and the failure/retry path.

**Why you cannot use `fetch` for progress.** The Fetch spec has no upload progress event; progress is observable via `XMLHttpRequest.upload.onprogress`. This is still true and is the single most common "why is my progress bar stuck at 0% with fetch" answer.

```tsx
// The XHR path: the only way to get real byte-level upload progress in a browser today.
export function uploadWithProgress(
  url: string,
  file: File,
  { fields = {}, headers = {}, onProgress, signal }: {
    fields?: Record<string, string>; headers?: Record<string, string>;
    onProgress?: (loaded: number, total: number, pct: number) => void; signal?: AbortSignal;
  },
): Promise<{ url: string; key: string }> {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    const form = new FormData();
    for (const [k, v] of Object.entries(fields)) form.append(k, v);
    form.append('file', file, file.name);                    // the third arg preserves the filename
```
