```tsx
    const xhr = new XMLHttpRequest();
    xhr.open('POST', url);
    for (const [k, v] of Object.entries(headers)) xhr.setRequestHeader(k, v);
    // (Do NOT set Content-Type: the browser must add the multipart boundary.)

    xhr.upload.onprogress = (e) => {
      if (e.lengthComputable) onProgress?.(e.loaded, e.total, Math.round((e.loaded / e.total) * 100));
      // Note: for chunked/resumable uploads, aggregate progress yourself across parts (see below).
    };
    xhr.onload = () => {
      if (xhr.status >= 200 && xhr.status < 300) resolve(JSON.parse(xhr.responseText));
      else reject(new UploadError(xhr.status, safeParse(xhr.responseText)));
    };
    xhr.onerror = () => reject(new UploadError(0, 'Network error'));
    xhr.onabort = () => reject(new DOMException('Aborted', 'AbortError'));

    signal?.addEventListener('abort', () => xhr.abort(), { once: true });
    xhr.send(form);
  });
}
```

**The transport decision — this is the real engineering.**

```
1. SINGLE POST through your server
   client → app server → storage
   + simple, authz and virus-scan in one place, works behind any firewall
   − double bandwidth through your server, memory/disk pressure, request timeouts, no resume,
     big files break serverless limits (6 MB Lambda, 32 MB / 100 MB GCP), no progress granularity you control
   Use for: < ~10 MB, needs server-side processing immediately

2. MULTIPART / CHUNKED upload
   client → app server (part by part) or → S3 multipart
   + resumable, per-part retry, parallel parts, aggregates to ~99.99% reliability
   − you must implement the state machine (init → upload parts → complete) and ETag collection
   Use for: 10 MB – 5 GB, unreliable networks, desktop app uploads

3. PRESIGNED (direct-to-storage) PUT
   client → ask app server for a signed URL → client PUTs straight to S3/GCS → client confirms
   + your server never proxies bytes: cheapest, fastest, scales horizontally for free,
     survives server restarts, supports multipart presigned parts
   − the URL is a bearer capability for a short window: scope it (key prefix, content-type,
     max size via `Content-Length` policy, exact key), keep it single-use where possible,
     and remember the object exists only after you confirm and move it out of a quarantine prefix
   Use for: everything > ~5 MB, and video/large-file apps — this is the default in 2026
```

```ts
// Presigned single PUT (server: mint the URL, never proxy bytes).
app.post('/api/uploads/presign', requireUser, async (req, res) => {
  const { name, size, contentType } = UploadRequest.parse(req.body);      // validate with a schema (4.4.8)
  if (size > 25 * 1024 * 1024) throw new HttpError(413, 'Max 25 MB');
  const key = `quarantine/${req.user.id}/${crypto.randomUUID()}-${sanitiseFilename(name)}`;
  const command = new PutObjectCommand({
    Bucket: BUCKET, Key: key,
    ContentType: contentType,
    ContentLength: size,                                  // pins the size: the client cannot upload more
    Metadata: { uploadedBy: req.user.id },
  });
  const url = await getSignedUrl(s3, command, { expiresIn: 300 });       // 5 minutes, not an hour
  res.json({ url, key, headers: { 'content-type': contentType } });
});

// Server-side confirmation: verify the object exists with the expected size, then scan it and promote it.
// THIS STEP IS NOT OPTIONAL — an attacker can call presign with a mime type of image/png and upload a script.
app.post('/api/uploads/:key/confirm', requireUser, async (req, res) => {
  const head = await s3.send(new HeadObjectCommand({ Bucket: BUCKET, Key: req.params.key }));
  assertBelongsToUser(req.params.key, req.user.id);                       // ownership from the key namespace
  if (head.ContentLength !== req.body.size) throw new HttpError(400, 'Size mismatch');
  const scan = await virusScan(await streamToBuffer(head));               // or async: quarantine + webhook
  if (!scan.clean) { await s3.send(new DeleteObjectCommand({ ... })); throw new HttpError(422, 'File rejected'); }
  const finalKey = req.params.key.replace('quarantine/', 'final/');
  await s3.send(new CopyObjectCommand({ Bucket: BUCKET, CopySource: `${BUCKET}/${req.params.key}`, Key: finalKey }));
  await s3.send(new DeleteObjectCommand({ Bucket: BUCKET, Key: req.params.key }));
  res.json({ key: finalKey, url: publicUrl(finalKey), contentType: head.ContentType, size: head.ContentLength });
});
```

```ts
// Multipart, client side: resumable, parallel, with aggregate progress and per-part retry.
import { Upload } from '@aws-sdk/lib-storage';

const upload = new Upload({
  client: s3Client, params: { Bucket, Key, Body: file },
  queueSize: 3,                    // 3 concurrent parts
  partSize: 8 * 1024 * 1024,       // 8 MB parts (S3 requires >= 5 MB except the last)
  leavePartsOnError: false,        // clean up so you do not pay for orphaned parts
});
const totalParts = Math.ceil(file.size / 8 * 1024 * 1024);
upload.on('httpUploadProgress', (p) => {
  // p.loaded is across parts; multiply by part count to get whole-file progress including completed parts
  onProgress?.(p.loaded ?? 0, file.size, Math.min(99, Math.round(((p.loaded ?? 0) / file.size) * 100)));
});
await upload.done();               // { Location, ETag, Key }
// Persist the multipart uploadId + parts in IndexedDB so an interrupted upload RESUMES rather than restarts.
```

**Previews — the part that looks easy and is not.**

```tsx
function usePreview(file: File | null) {
  const [url, setUrl] = useState<string | null>(null);
  useEffect(() => {
    if (!file || !file.type.startsWith('image/')) return;
    const objectUrl = URL.createObjectURL(file);        // cheap: no file read, no memory copy
    setUrl(objectUrl);
    return () => URL.revokeObjectURL(objectUrl);        // ← the leak everyone forgets (4.6.6 scenario 9)
  }, [file]);
  return url;
}
// Mechanics to know:
// · createObjectURL is O(1) and does not read the file. For a 200 MB video this is instant.
// · Downscale for display instead of rendering the original: create an ImageBitmap or use a canvas, or
//   send the thumbnail to the client separately. Rendering a 6000×4000 photo as <img> costs ~96 MB decoded.
// · HEIC: Safari uploads HEIC; <img> cannot render it everywhere. Convert on the client (heic2any) or show a generic icon.
// · SVG uploads: do NOT render an untrusted SVG in an <img> *and* do not render it inline — script in SVG
//   executes when rendered as a document; sanitise with DOMPurify's SVG profile if it must be shown.
// · EXIF contains GPS: strip it server-side on confirmation, or you leak location metadata.
```

**The upload queue UX.** A single <input type="file" multiple> produces a *queue*, and the queue is the product:

```tsx
type UploadItem = { id: string; file: File; status: 'queued' | 'uploading' | 'processing' | 'done' | 'error'; pct: number; error?: string; key?: string };

// Concurrency 2–3 (more starves the network and increases failure rate), FIFO, pause/resume, retry,
// cancel (AbortController per item), and a "Retry all failed" affordance. Show per-file progress AND
// an aggregate ("3 of 8 · 248 MB of 512 MB"). Keep failed items with their error message inline —
// never silently drop them. Announce progress politely, not per percent: aria-live="polite" on
// "Uploading 3 of 8", and no live region on the percentage itself (screen readers would spam).
// Drag-and-drop needs a keyboard equivalent (the file input is always present, visually styled).
// Validate before upload: size, type by extension AND magic bytes, count (max 10 files),
// and duplicate detection (name + size + lastModified). Do it again on the server.
```

**Trade-offs and production considerations.**
- **Direct-to-storage saves the most money of anything in this section.** A 2 GB/day video product that proxies uploads pays double egress and needs serverless with huge timeouts; presigned PUTs make the app server stateless.
- **Presigned URLs are capabilities**: short expiry, exact key, exact content type, pinned content length, and a `quarantine/` prefix that only your worker can promote. Never let the client choose the key freely (path traversal, overwriting other users' objects, serving XSS from your domain).
- **Timeouts and proxies**: an upload through your server dies at 30–60 s on most ingress. That alone is the reason to move large uploads off your server.
- **Resumability** requires persisting `{ key, uploadId, parts: [{ PartNumber, ETag }] }` in IndexedDB; on resume, call `ListParts` and upload only what is missing. This is a real feature users notice on mobile.
- **Never trust `File.type`** — it is whatever the OS reported, and on some platforms it is empty. Sniff magic bytes server-side (`file-type`), re-encode images, strip EXIF, and transcode video (an uploaded "video" can be a 4 GB decompression bomb).
- **Serving uploads**: serve user content from a *different domain* than your app (so an XSS in an HTML upload cannot touch your session), with `Content-Disposition: attachment` for non-media, a strict `Content-Type`, and `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`.
- **Cost/UX**: for large files, show an estimated time, run uploads while the user does something else, and support paste-from-clipboard (`onPaste`) and drag-from-Finder.
- **Mobile**: `capture="environment"` for camera, `accept="image/*"` for the picker (accepts HEIC on iOS), and remember iOS may downscale the video before it reaches you.

**Common mistakes.** `fetch` for a progress bar (no progress events). Revoking an object URL while it is still displayed. Trusting `file.type` server-side. Presigning without ownership scoping or with a 24-hour expiry. Uploading through the app server for multi-GB files. No concurrency limit (20 parallel 100 MB uploads). Announcing every percent to a live region. Losing the queue on refresh.

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "Why can't you get upload progress with `fetch`?"
- "Presigned vs multipart: when and why?"
- "What must the server do after a presigned upload, and why?"
- "How do you make a 2 GB upload resumable?"
- "How do you avoid leaking memory with previews?"
- "What are the security risks of client uploads?"

**Mistakes:** `URL.createObjectURL` without `revoke`; unbounded concurrency; no server-side verification of the uploaded object; assuming a presigned PUT means the file is validated.

---

## 4.4.10 Loading, empty, and error states as a first-class design problem

🟢 Beginner · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.6.1 (design systems), Phase 3 (a11y)

**Definition:** Every asynchronous surface has at least **five** states, not two: *initial loading* (nothing on screen yet), *refreshing* (data present, new data coming), *empty* (the request succeeded and there is nothing), *partial* (some parts failed / some data missing), and *error* (the request failed). UIs that only handle loading + data are incomplete, and the missing states are what users report as bugs.

**The five (really seven) states, and what each must look like.**

| State | Trigger | Must include | Must NOT include |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Initial loading** | no cached data, request in flight | a skeleton that matches the final layout's shape (avoids CLS), or a spinner for < 300 ms | a full-page spinner for a small region; a layout that jumps when data lands |
| **Refreshing** | data present, refetch in flight | subtle indication (dimmed rows, a thin progress bar, `aria-busy`), interactions still allowed | clearing the data; disabling the whole page; a modal spinner |
| **Empty (first run)** | success, zero items, user has never created one | an explanation + the primary action + an illustration | "No data" with no CTA; a broken-image icon |
| **Empty (filtered)** | success, zero items, filters applied | "No results for *{query}*" + a "Clear filters" action | the first-run illustration (wrong context) |
| **Error** | request failed | what failed in user terms, a retry that works, a way to get help, the error code for support | a raw exception; a silent console log; a retry that retries the same thing |
| **Partial / degraded** | some of N requests failed | the data that did load, plus an inline notice for the part that didn't | blanking the entire page because one widget failed |
| **Stale** | data shown, revalidation failed | the data, marked as possibly out of date, with a retry | hiding good data because a refresh failed |

```tsx
// The component shape that gets all of them, with the two details people forget:
// (1) isFetching ≠ isLoading once you have data; (2) empty vs error-first ordering.
type AsyncBoundary<T> =
  | { status: 'loading' }
  | { status: 'error'; error: Error; retry: () => void }
  | { status: 'success'; data: T; isRefreshing: boolean; isStale: boolean };

export function AsyncSection<T>({
  query, skeleton, empty, errorView, children, isEmpty,
}: {
  query: UseQueryResult<T, Error>;
  skeleton: ReactNode; empty: ReactNode; errorView?: (e: Error, retry: () => void) => ReactNode;
  children: (data: T, meta: { refreshing: boolean }) => ReactNode;
  isEmpty?: (data: T) => boolean;
}) {
  // Error BEFORE data: a query that has stale data AND an error should show the data + a notice,
  // which we handle below; a query with NO data and an error must show the error view.
  if (query.isPending) return <>{skeleton}</>;
  if (query.isError && query.data === undefined) {
    return <>{errorView ? errorView(query.error, query.refetch) : <DefaultErrorView error={query.error} onRetry={query.refetch} />}</>;
  }
  if (query.data === undefined) return null;
  if (isEmpty?.(query.data)) return <>{empty}</>;

  return (
    <section aria-busy={query.isFetching}>
      {query.isError && <StaleNotice error={query.error} onRetry={query.refetch} />}   // degraded, not blank
      {query.isFetching && !query.isPending && <RefreshBar />}                        // subtle, non-blocking
      {children(query.data, { refreshing: query.isFetching })}
    </section>
  );
}

export function DefaultErrorView({ error, onRetry }: { error: Error; onRetry: () => void }) {
  const status = error instanceof ApiError ? error.status : 0;
  const { title, body, retryable } = useMemo(() => {
    if (status === 0)     return { title: "You're offline", body: 'Check your connection and try again.', retryable: true };
    if (status === 401)   return { title: 'Please sign in again', body: 'Your session has expired.', retryable: false };
    if (status === 403)   return { title: "You don't have access", body: 'Ask an admin for permission.', retryable: false };
    if (status === 404)   return { title: 'Not found', body: 'It may have been deleted or renamed.', retryable: false };
    if (status >= 500)    return { title: 'Something went wrong on our side', body: `Reference: ${error.correlationId}`, retryable: true };
    return { title: 'Check your input and try again', body: 'Some of the values were rejected.', retryable: true };
  }, [status, error]);
  return (
    <div role="alert">
      <h2>{title}</h2><p>{body}</p>
      {retryable && <button onClick={onRetry}>Try again</button>}
      <a href="/support">Contact support</a>
    </div>
  );
}
```

**Loading strategies, and the threshold that decides between them.** The perception rule: **an operation under ~100 ms should have no indicator at all; 100 ms–1 s needs a subtle indicator (button spinner, inline shimmer); over 1 s needs a skeleton; over 10 s needs progress and the ability to leave the page.** Showing a spinner for a 40 ms request creates a *flash*, which feels slower than doing nothing — this is the most common UX defect in data-heavy apps.

```tsx
// Delay the loading state by 150 ms so fast requests never flash a skeleton.
function useDelayedPending(isPending: boolean, ms = 150) {
  const [shown, setShown] = useState(false);
  useEffect(() => {
    if (!isPending) { setShown(false); return; }
    const t = setTimeout(() => setShown(true), ms);
    return () => clearTimeout(t);
  }, [isPending, ms]);
  return isPending && shown;
}
// Use it ONLY for a boolean/spinner. For Suspense you cannot avoid the fallback appearing (which is one
// reason Suspense needs a transition — 4.1.12).

// Skeletons must match the final layout, or you trade a spinner for CLS (a Core Web Vitals regression).
export function InvoiceRowSkeleton() {
  return (
    <li className="grid grid-cols-[2rem_1fr_6rem_6rem] gap-3 p-3" aria-hidden>
      <div className="size-4 rounded bg-neutral-200" /><div className="h-4 rounded bg-neutral-200" />
      <div className="h-4 rounded bg-neutral-200" /><div className="h-4 rounded bg-neutral-200" />
    </li>
  );
}
// The skeleton is aria-hidden and the LIST carries the status instead: <ul aria-busy> + a visually hidden
// "Loading invoices" — a screen reader must be told once, not forty times.
```

**Empty states have three flavours and they are different products.** (1) *First-run empty* — the user has never done the thing; the job is to teach and give them one click to start. (2) *Filtered empty* — the job is to explain the filter and offer to clear it. (3) *Error-as-empty* — the request failed; the job is to say so, never to show an empty state (which reads as "you have nothing" and is actively misleading). Writing copy for all three is a design task, not a code task.

**Common mistakes.** Only handling loading + data. Treating `isLoading` as "spinner" when data already exists (it wipes good data). Showing the empty state on an error. A full-page spinner for a 200 ms sidebar. `role="alert"` on the loading container (announces constantly). Skeletons with a different layout from the content. A retry button that refetches with the same broken parameters. No error correlation id shown to support.

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "How many states does an async UI have? Name them."
- "Why not show a spinner immediately?"
- "How do you handle partial failure?"
- "What belongs in an empty state copy?"
- "How do skeletons affect CLS and INP?"

**Mistakes:** two-state thinking; skeleton layout mismatch; error states that leak exceptions; live-region spam.

---

## 4.4.11 Cache invalidation strategies and the stale-write problem

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.3.9, 4.4.4, 4.5.9

**Definition:** Invalidation is the policy that decides *which cached reads become wrong after a write, and how they learn about it*. The **stale-write problem** is the specific failure where two writes race — or where a write succeeds but the cache still shows the old value — and it is the reason "just invalidate everything" is a temporary answer, not a strategy.

**The four sources of cache invalidation.**

```
1. YOUR OWN WRITE        the user just did something on this screen          → invalidate precisely
2. ANOTHER USER'S WRITE  a colleague changed the same data                    → server event (SSE/WS) or short staleTime
3. THE DATA CHANGED      a background job, a cron, a webhook                 → poll, or a server push, or a version check
4. THE VIEW CHANGED      the user's own context changed (filter, tenant, role)→ key includes it; or clear()
```

**A strategy matrix.**

| Strategy | Mechanism | Cost | Correct when |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Broad invalidation** | `invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['invoices'] })` | One write → N refetches across every filter combination | Small app; correctness-first; < ~5 active queries per entity |
| **Exact-key** | `invalidateQueries({ queryKey: invoiceKeys.list(currentFilters) })` | Zero extra requests; requires you to enumerate dependents | You control both write and read paths (single-team monolith) |
| **Tag graph** (RTK Query) | `providesTags`/`invalidatesTags` | Coarse tags over-invalidate; fine tags need discipline | Many dependent reads with different shapes; you want a declarative graph |
| **Cache write-through** | `setQueryData` from the mutation response, no invalidation | Zero requests; wrong if the response is incomplete | Single-entity CRUD where the response is authoritative and complete |
| **Stale-while-revalidate** | `staleTime` short + revalidate on focus/mount | Steady background traffic | Data that is *eventually* consistent and cheap to read (notifications, dashboards) |
| **Push invalidation** | server sends `{ type:'invoice.updated', id }` → `invalidateQueries` by id | Needs SSE/WS + a broker (4.4.6) | Multi-user editing; anything where a user must not act on stale data |
| **Version/epoch** | server returns `dataVersion`; client refetches when it changes | Requires a cheap endpoint | Read-heavy dashboards where you want one small request instead of N refetches |
| **Cache lifetime as policy** | `staleTime: Infinity` + explicit invalidation from every mutation | Zero background traffic; any missed invalidation = permanent wrongness | Admin tools with few, well-audited write paths |

**The stale-write problem, precisely.** There are four distinct failure modes, and they have different fixes:

```ts
// (1) WRITE-WRITE RACE — two mutations on the same entity, responses arrive out of order.
// Fix: serialise per entity (mutationScope) OR make writes idempotent + include a version/If-Match.
const save = useMutation({
  mutationFn: (v: Values & { version: number }) =>
    api.put(`/docs/${v.id}`, v, { headers: { 'if-match': `"${v.version}"` } }),  // 412 if someone else wrote
  onSuccess: (server) => qc.setQueryData(['doc', id], server),                  // always adopt the SERVER's version
});

// (2) ROLLBACK STALENESS — an optimistic rollback restores a snapshot that was already stale.
// Fix: on failure, invalidate rather than restore (a refetch is cheap; being wrong is not).
onError: () => qc.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['doc', id] }),

// (3) MISSED DEPENDENTS — the write updated a list, a detail, a count, and a summary widget;
//     you invalidated the list but the count is now wrong until someone hard-refreshes.
// Fix: enumerate dependents explicitly and invalidate all of them; test the enumeration.
function useRenameProject() {
  const qc = useQueryClient();
  return useMutation({
    mutationFn: ({ id, name }) => api.patch(`/projects/${id}`, { name }),
    onMutate: async ({ id, name }) => {
      await qc.cancelQueries({ queryKey: ['projects'] });               // covers detail AND every list AND the count
      const prev = qc.getQueriesData({ queryKey: ['projects'] });
      // ...optimistic write across every affected entry...
      return { prev };
    },
    onError: (_e, _v, ctx) => ctx?.prev.forEach(([key, data]) => qc.setQueryData(key, data)),
    onSettled: () => qc.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['projects'] }),
  });
}

// (4) CACHE KEY BLIND SPOT — the data is keyed by a subset of what actually determines it.
//     /api/teams/{teamId}/projects and /api/teams/{teamId}/projects?archived=true are DIFFERENT entries,
//     so archiving a project leaves the archived view stale. Same for role-filtered data.
// Fix: put every input that changes the response in the key, and prefer ONE endpoint that takes filters
//      as parameters over N endpoints with different shapes.

// (5) THE SUBTLE ONE — optimistic write then a *read* of a query that was fetched BEFORE the write
//     and has a long staleTime, so it never revalidates: it holds a value that predates your own write.
// Fix: cancelQueries before optimistic writes (4.3.9) AND include the mutation's entities in invalidation.
```

**Making staleness visible and bounded.** Whatever the strategy, users need to trust the data. Techniques: show "updated 2 min ago" with a manual refresh; show a stale banner when a refetch fails; use a `staleTime` matched to how wrong the data can be (a stock price: 0; a country list: a week); and for the cases that must not be stale at all (money, permissions, availability), do not cache — read on every interaction, or push (4.4.6).

**A concrete, defensible policy you can present in an interview.**

```
Reads:
  entity detail          staleTime 30s, gcTime 10m, revalidate on focus
  search/autocomplete    staleTime 60s, enabled only for length >= 2, gcTime 2m (don't grow the cache)
  reference/config data  staleTime 24h, invalidateTags on mutation (near-zero traffic)
  permissions/entitlement staleTime 0 + refetchOnMount always + push on change (stale authz = incidents)
  anything in the URL     key must include every URL-derived input, or back/forward shows wrong data

Writes:
  create                 setQueryData into the first page (dedupe by clientId) + invalidate the list
  update                 setQueryData detail + patch every cached list containing it + invalidate that family
  delete                 remove from every cached list immediately + invalidate
  bulk                   invalidate the family; do NOT try to patch N entries optimistically
  failure                invalidate (never only roll back)

Cross-user:
  push events by entity id -> invalidateQueries({ predicate: q => affectedIds.includes(q.queryKey.at(-1)) })
```

**Trade-offs.** Broad invalidation is simple and correct and costs a request storm that grows with your app — it is a *deferred architecture bill*. Precise invalidation is efficient and requires a mental model of every dependent read, which is real ongoing cost and is invisible in code review (a new component with a new query key silently becomes a missed dependent). Push invalidation is the most correct and the most operationally expensive (4.4.6). `staleTime: Infinity` is the best performance and the worst failure mode (permanent wrongness from a missed invalidation) — reserve it for data you fully own.

**Production and operational.**
- **Log invalidations in development**: wrap `invalidateQueries` in a helper that `console.log`s the key and the caller. Half of "my data is stale" bugs become obvious immediately.
- **Test the invalidation graph**: for each mutation, a unit test that asserts the expected keys are invalidated and that an unrelated key is *not*. This is the cheapest high-value test in a data layer.
- **Alert on refetch volume** (requests per user-minute). A sudden rise usually means a refetch loop: `refetchInterval` too low, an `invalidate` inside an effect, or `staleTime: 0` with `refetchOnFocus` on a route users tab between constantly.
- **Clear the cache on identity changes**: logout, tenant switch, permission downgrade (`queryClient.clear()`), and on a `401` broadcast from another tab (via `BroadcastChannel`).
- **Bound the cache**: track `queryCache.getAll().length` and total serialised size; set `gcTime` deliberately for high-cardinality keys.
- **Never trust the client cache for authorisation.** It is a UX optimisation over server checks (4.6.9).

**Common mistakes.** Invalidating with a key that matches nothing. Invalidating in a `useEffect` (an infinite refetch loop). Not cancelling queries before an optimistic write. `staleTime: Infinity` plus a new read path that forgets to invalidate. Forgetting that a `queryKey` missing a filter makes two cache entries for one logical view. React Query's `refetchOnMount` default being `true` while assuming it means "always refetch".

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "After a rename, which queries need invalidating, and how would you know?"
- "What is the stale-write problem and how do you fix it?"
- "Broad vs precise invalidation: give the trade-off and the failure mode of each."
- "How do you prevent a refetch loop?"
- "When would you push invalidation instead of polling?"

**Mistakes:** `invalidateQueries` from an effect; no key discipline; treating the cache as authoritative for permissions; rolling back without refetching.

---

# 4.5 Routing Strategies

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.1.16 (portals), 4.2 (meta-frameworks), 4.3.8 (URL state)

**Framing.** A router is four things at once: (1) a **matcher** turning a URL into a component tree, (2) a **navigation model** with history semantics, (3) a **boundary system** for code splitting, error handling, and data loading, and (4) a **contract with the server** (what a deep link must render, what a crawler sees, what a 404 is). Most routing bugs are in (2) or (4) — history semantics and the server contract — while most routing *discussion* is about (1), which is the easy part.

---

## 4.5.1 Client routing and the History API

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.1.5

**Definition:** Client routing intercepts navigation, renders a different component tree without a full document load, and keeps the URL bar in sync using the **History API** — `history.pushState(state, '', url)`, `history.replaceState(state, '', url)`, and the `popstate` event. It is what makes a single-page app feel like a multi-page app while only loading the shell once.

**The mechanics, precisely.**

```js
// pushState / replaceState: change the URL and push a history entry WITHOUT loading a document.
// Crucially they do NOT fire popstate — the app must update its own UI synchronously.
history.pushState({ scrollY: 0, modal: true }, '', '/invoices?page=2');   // adds an entry; use for "forward" navigation
history.replaceState({ scrollY: 0 }, '', '?page=3');                        // replaces the current entry; use for filters/replace

window.addEventListener('popstate', (e) => {
  // Fired on BACK and FORWARD only. e.state is the object passed to the last push/replace for that entry.
  // It is null for entries created before your app loaded, and for cross-document navigations.
  console.log(location.pathname, e.state);
});

window.history.scrollRestoration = 'manual';   // take control (4.5.7)
// Constraints you must respect:
// · pushState THROWS if the URL is cross-origin. Same-origin only.
// · The URL is limited (browsers allow ~ 64 KB); use the state object for large data, not the query string.
// · state must be structured-cloneable: no functions, no class instances (they serialise to plain objects),
//   no DOM nodes, no Symbols. (Anything non-cloneable throws in some browsers.)
// · pushState cannot trigger a page load, so you own: code splitting, data fetching, scroll position,
//   document.title, and any SEO-relevant <head> updates. That is why frameworks abstract it.
```

```tsx
// What a router's navigation actually does, in order — this sequence is worth being able to recite:
// 1. User clicks a link (or popstate fires).
// 2. Intercept: don't let the browser navigate (preventDefault / the framework's Link handler).
// 3. Parse the URL into a route match (static segments, dynamic, splat, ranking by specificity).
// 4. Resolve lazy chunks for the matched route; start them downloading in parallel.
// 5. Run data loaders for the matched routes (may be async; may render a pending boundary meanwhile).
// 6. Update history (push vs replace) so the URL bar is right and Back works.
// 7. Render the new tree; run layout effects; manage focus and scroll.
// 8. Fire "route change" effects (analytics, cleanup of the old route, polling reset).
```

**The three navigation sources, all of which a router must handle.**

| Source | Fires | Needs a render update? | History entry? |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Link`/`<a>` click | your code | Yes, you drive it | Yes, push (or replace) |
| `popstate` (Back/Forward) | browser | Yes, driven by the event | No — the browser already moved |
| Programmatic (`navigate()`) | your code | Yes | Depends on options |
| Initial load / deep link | browser request | Yes, first render | n/a |

The classic bug: a router that updates the UI in the `Link` handler but forgets to re-render on `popstate` (Back does nothing). The other classic: `replaceState` for everything, so Back leaves the app entirely.

**Link semantics.** Always render a real `<a href>` and enhance it. Reasons: middle-click, right-click → open in new tab, ctrl/cmd-click, screen-reader link affordance, and users who see the destination URL. Intercept only plain left-clicks on the same origin:

```tsx
export function Link({ to, replace, onClick, ...rest }: LinkProps) {
  return (
    <a
      href={to}
      onClick={(e) => {
        onClick?.(e);
        if (e.defaultPrevented) return;
        if (e.button !== 0 || e.metaKey || e.ctrlKey || e.shiftKey || e.altKey) return;  // let the browser do new-tab things
        if (isModifiedEvent(e) || isExternal(to)) return;
        e.preventDefault();
        navigate(to, { replace });
      }}
      {...rest}
    />
  );
}
// Also handle: `target="_blank"` links are external by definition; download links; and same-origin absolute URLs.
```

**Trade-offs.** Client routing gives instant transitions, preserved app state (a query cache, a scroll position, an open composer), and no full reload. It costs: a real dependency, an initial bundle that must include the router, SEO and first-paint concerns for public pages (solved by SSR/SSG — 4.2.2), history-semantics bugs, and the requirement that a **server rewrite every unknown path to `index.html`** so a deep link works (the single most common deployment mistake: `GET /invoices/42` → 404 because the server does not know the app is an SPA).

**Production and operational.** Configure the fallback rewrite (`try_files $uri /index.html` in nginx, `rewrites` on Vercel/Netlify, `ErrorDocument` on S3+CloudFront) and **exclude real asset paths** or you will serve HTML for `main.js` (a spectacular failure mode). Set `Cache-Control: no-cache` on `index.html` and long immutable caching on hashed assets, or users get a new app shell referencing deleted chunks (the `ChunkLoadError` from 4.6.6 scenario 4). Handle `ChunkLoadError` on a new deploy with a one-time reload prompt, guarded against loops. Track route changes with analytics including a "navigation type" (push/replace/pop) so you can measure whether Back works. Set `document.title` per route and manage `<meta>` (4.2.11) — the router is the natural owner.

**Common mistakes.** Forgetting the server rewrite. Not handling `popstate`. `pushState` with unserialisable state. Intercepting modified clicks. An SPA fallback that swallows `/api/*` or asset requests. Using `replace` everywhere. Losing the previous scroll position on Back.

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "What are the constraints on `pushState`?"
- "Walk me through what happens on a Back navigation."
- "Why must a real `<a href>` be rendered?"
- "What must the server be configured to do for deep links?"
- "What state can you put in `history.state`, and what can you not?"

**Mistakes:** preventing default on every click; SPA fallback over `/api`; no popstate handler; non-serialisable state.

---

## 4.5.2 Hash vs history mode

🟢 Beginner · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.5.1

**Definition:** Two URL schemes. **History mode** uses real paths (`/invoices/42`) with the History API. **Hash mode** puts the route inside the fragment (`/#/invoices/42`), which is never sent to the server and fires `hashchange` instead of `popstate`.

```
History mode                             Hash mode
https://app.example.com/invoices/42      https://app.example.com/#/invoices/42
  ├─ server sees the full path             ├─ server sees ONLY /  (the fragment is never transmitted)
  ├─ needs SPA fallback rewrite            ├─ no rewrite needed: every route is the same document
  ├─ needs server cooperation for auth/    ├─ auth in the fragment is invisible to the server
  │  redirects (fragment arrives late)     │
  ├─ SEO: crawlers see real URLs           ├─ SEO: poor; modern crawlers partly cope, but the canonical
  │                                          URL is /#/, so unique-content indexing is unreliable
  ├─ pretty, shareable, cacheable          ├─ ugly, but bulletproof
  └─ popstate                             └─ hashchange
```

**The decisive technical differences (beyond aesthetics).**

1. **Server visibility.** The fragment is never sent in an HTTP request. So: no server-side auth check on `#/admin`, no server-side redirect of `#/login?next=/billing`, no per-route HTTP caching, no ability to give a deep link its own server-rendered payload. If you need any of these, hash routing is the wrong tool.
2. **When the fragment is available to client code.** It arrives *after* the document loads, so a hash router needs a hydration-safe read (`useEffect`/`useLayoutEffect`, not render) or you get a flash of the wrong route. History mode has the path from the first byte.
3. **Failure mode.** History mode fails loudly if the server rewrite is missing (a 404 on every deep link). Hash mode "just works" anywhere — which is why it dominated the pre-2015 era and why some internal tools still use it.
4. **Analytics.** `pageview` tools that read `location.pathname` see one page forever in hash mode; you must parse the hash yourself and fire synthetic pageviews.

```tsx
// Reading hash state without a hydration flash (and building the base path correctly).
function useHashRoute(): string {
  const [path, setPath] = useState('/');
  useEffect(() => {
    const read = () => setPath(window.location.hash.slice(1) || '/');
    read();
    window.addEventListener('hashchange', read);
    return () => window.removeEventListener('hashchange', read);
  }, []);
  return path;
}
// hash links: <a href="#/invoices/42"> — and NEVER a hash for in-page anchors in the same app
// ("#section" and "#/route" collide). Pick one or the other, or use ?section=.
```

**Where hash routing is still the right answer.**
- **Non-SPA contexts where you cannot configure the server**: opening `file://` pages, embedding inside an iframe served by another team, a static host where the fallback is fixed, a browser extension, a desktop app using a custom protocol.
- **Fragments used as *data* or *state*, not as routes**: OAuth implicit-grant return values (`#access_token=...` — legacy, do not build new flows on this; use PKCE, PHASE 7), or a client-side deep-link target for a native app (`myapp://open/#/invoice/42`).
- **In-page anchors** (`#section`) — the ordinary, boring, correct use of the fragment.

**When it is the wrong answer.** Public or marketing pages that must be indexed or share preview cards (Slack/Discord unfurls read the URL). Anything with server-side authentication or per-route redirects. Any multi-page app where URLs appear in support tickets, emails, or QR codes (a hash URL with an escaped token is unauditable and ugly in print). Anything behind a corporate proxy that strips fragments from logs.

**Trade-offs summary.** History mode: the correct default. Hash mode: less infrastructure coupling, worse semantics. The migration from hash to history is usually "never", because URLs are already in the wild — plan for a redirect map if you ever migrate (`/#/invoices/42` → `/invoices/42` via a small script on the hash page).

**Common mistakes.** Using hash routing "because it's simpler" on a public app. Expecting the server to see `#` values. Hash URLs for routing *and* anchors in the same app. Missing the hydration flash when reading `location.hash` during render.

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "What does the server see in a hash URL?"
- "When would you legitimately choose hash routing?"
- "What breaks with SEO and with auth redirects?"
- "How do you avoid the flash when reading the hash?"

**Mistakes:** hash routing for a public app; reading `location.hash` during render; assuming server-side auth works with a fragment.

---

## 4.5.3 Dynamic, optional-catch-all, and nested segments

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.5.1, 4.2.1 (nested layouts)

**Definition:** The path-pattern vocabulary: **static** segments (`/settings`), **dynamic** segments (`/invoices/:id`), **splat/catch-all** (`/files/*path`), **optional catch-all** (`/docs/:lang?/*path`), and **nested** segments that render a parent layout with an `Outlet` for the child. Optional catch-all segments exist so `/docs` and `/docs/getting-started/install` match one route without an ambiguous second definition.

**The matching algorithm, and why route order matters.** A router matches *all* the candidate routes, ranks them by specificity, and picks the most specific. Ranking rules: more static segments beat dynamic, dynamic beats splat, longer static prefixes beat shorter, and an optional segment makes a route *less* specific than its non-optional twin.

```ts
// React Router 6/7 route config. Dynamic = ':id'; splat = '*'; optional catch-all = ':lang?/*path'.
const routes = [
  { path: '/', element: <Home /> },
  { path: '/invoices', element: <InvoiceList /> },
  { path: '/invoices/new', element: <NewInvoice /> },                 // static wins over /invoices/:id
  { path: '/invoices/:id', element: <InvoiceDetail />, loader: invoiceLoader },   // params.id
  { path: '/invoices/:id/edit', element: <EditInvoice /> },
  { path: '/invoices/:id/attachments/*', element: <Attachments /> },   // params['*'] = 'a/b/c.pdf'
  { path: '/docs', element: <DocsIndex /> },
  { path: '/docs/:lang?/*path', element: <Docs /> },                   // matches /docs AND /docs/fr/getting-started
  { path: '/files/*', element: <FileBrowser /> },
  { path: '*', element: <NotFound /> },                                // LAST, or it eats everything
];

createBrowserRouter(routes);
```

```
Ranking table (React Router ranks and sorts; you rarely rely on order, but you should understand it):

/invoices/new         static        1 dynamic-less segment, fully static      → wins over /invoices/:id
/invoices/:id/edit    3 segments with 1 dynamic
/invoices/:id         2 segments with 1 dynamic
/invoices/:id/*       splat
/:lang?/docs          optional dynamic
/*                    catch-all      → always least specific
```

**Why optional catch-all exists.** Without it, `/docs` and `/docs/a/b/c` need two route definitions whose patterns overlap, so `/docs` renders through the catch-all route with `path === ''`. With `:lang?/*path`, one route handles both, and `path` tells the component whether it is a directory index (`''`) or a document (`'getting-started/install'`). Next.js's equivalent is an optional catch-all folder `[...slug]`; Remix/React Router's is `:lang?/*path`.

```tsx
// Reading params, including splats, and validating them at the boundary (4.4.8).
import { useParams, useMatch, useLocation } from 'react-router-dom';
import { z } from 'z';

const InvoiceParams = z.object({ id: z.string().uuid('Not an invoice id') });

export function InvoiceDetail() {
  const params = useParams();
  const parsed = InvoiceParams.safeParse(params);
  const location = useLocation();

  if (!parsed.success) return <BadRequest issues={parsed.error.issues} />;   // a malformed URL is a 400, not a crash
  const { id } = parsed.data;

  // Splat: '/invoices/x/attachments/2024/q3/report.pdf' → params['*'] === '2024/q3/report.pdf'
  // Decode each segment yourself; do not assume the server encoded it.
  const segments = (params['*'] ?? '').split('/').filter(Boolean).map(decodeURIComponent);

  // Nesting depth via useMatch: how deep am I? Useful for breadcrumbs and analytics.
  const matches = useMatch('/invoices/:id/attachments/*');   // null if not on that route
  return <h1>Invoice {id}</h1>;
}
```

**Nesting mechanics.** Two distinct kinds of nesting, often confused:
- **Layout nesting** (shared chrome persists): a parent route element renders `<Outlet />`. In Next.js App Router this is `layout.tsx`; the parent does **not** re-render on child navigation, which is the performance win.
- **Path nesting** (the child path is *relative to* the parent): `children: [{ path: 'edit', ... }]` inside `path: '/invoices/:id'`. The child's full path is concatenated.

```tsx
// React Router: nesting with a layout route and pathless layout routes (the "grouping without a URL" trick).
{
  path: '/',
  element: <Root />,
  children: [
    { element: <MarketingLayout />, children: [                    // pathless layout: adds chrome, no URL segment
        { index: true, element: <Landing /> },
        { path: 'pricing', element: <Pricing /> },
    ]},
    { path: 'app', element: <AppLayout />, children: [             // /app/* with sidebar chrome
        { index: true, element: <Dashboard /> },
        { path: 'invoices', element: <InvoiceList /> },
        { path: 'invoices/:id', element: <InvoiceDetail /> },
        { path: 'settings', element: <Settings /> },
        { errorElement: <RouteError /> },                          // errors in children bubble to the nearest boundary
        { lazy: async () => ({ Component: (await import('./Settings')).Settings }) },   // lazy route (4.5.6)
    ]},
  ],
}
```

**When to nest and when to flatten.** Nest when the layout is genuinely shared and should persist across child navigations (a sidebar that must not re-render or lose scroll, a wizard shell, a modal frame). Flatten when the "layout" is only styling (a `<div className="p-4">` — a wrapper component is clearer than a layout route). Do not nest five levels for five URLs: each layout level is a remounting boundary in some routers and a complexity cost everywhere. Prefer *composition* (a layout component that receives `children`) over nesting routes when the child is not a route.

**Trade-offs and production notes.**
- **Ranked matching** means you rarely get ordering wrong — but ambiguity still hurts readability and some routers (Next.js) *do* rely on filesystem layout rather than ranking, where `page.tsx` and `[id]/page.tsx` in the same folder is an error. Know which model your router uses.
- **Params are strings and untrusted.** Validate them at the boundary (above) before using them in a query key or a fetch. `:id` in the URL is user input (4.6.9: IDs in URLs are untrusted; authorisation is per object).
- **Optional catch-all and `generateStaticParams`**: with SSR, `[...slug]` and `[slug]` generate different `params` shapes (`{ slug: ['a','b'] }` vs `{ slug: 'a' }`), and forgetting to handle both is a 500 on a deep link.
- **Splats are a security surface**: a splat param feeds filesystem lookups, path joins, or globs. Normalise (`..`, duplicate slashes, absolute paths) and never pass it to `fs` unchecked.
- **`basename`** for apps served from a subpath (`/admin/app`) — set it once on the router; forgetting it breaks every asset-relative URL.

**Common mistakes.** Catch-all before specific routes. Trusting `:id` without validation. Confusing layout nesting with path nesting. Deeply nested layouts "for structure". Forgetting `basename`. A splat passed straight to a filesystem call.

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "Explain `:id`, `*`, and `:lang?/*path` and how they rank."
- "What is a pathless layout route for?"
- "What are the two kinds of nesting in App Router?"
- "How do you validate route params?"
- "When is a splat segment dangerous?"

**Mistakes:** ordering-dependent matching; unvalidated params; nesting for styling.

---

## 4.5.4 Data loaders and actions in the routing layer

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.4.1, 4.5.3, 4.2.4

**Definition:** A **loader** is a function the router runs *before* rendering a route, receiving the request (params, search, request object) and returning data for that route. An **action** is the same contract for mutations: it receives submitted data, performs the write, and returns a result. Both are the router's data layer, and both are the place where "fetch before render" becomes a framework feature instead of a `useEffect`.

**The loader contract, end to end.**

```
user submits <Form method="post"> → router collects FormData
   → action(request) runs  → returns data (or a Response/redirect/throw)
   → router (re)validates the loaders affected by the action, in parallel
   → the tree re-renders with fresh data; no fetch code in the component

navigation → loader(request) for each matched route, in parallel
   → data available at first render (no waterfall, no loading-state code in the component)
   → `defer` streams slow promises behind <Suspense>/<Await>
```

```tsx
// React Router 6.4+ / 7 data router: loaders + actions, with types end to end.
import { createBrowserRouter, redirect, data, useLoaderData, Form, useNavigation, Await, defer } from 'react-router-dom';

async function invoiceLoader({ params, request }: LoaderFunctionArgs) {
  const { id } = invoiceParams.parse(params);            // validate at the boundary (4.4.8)
  const url = new URL(request.url);
  const tab = (url.searchParams.get('tab') ?? 'summary') as 'summary' | 'history';

  // Loaders are request-scoped: everything you need must come from the request, not from a closure.
  const invoice = await api.server.get<Invoice>(`/invoices/${id}`, { headers: authHeaders(request) });
  if (invoice.status === 'gone') throw data({ id }, { status: 410 });   // a Response-shaped throw
  return { invoice, tab };                                             // → typed by typeof loader, available via useLoaderData
}

async function invoiceAction({ request, params }: ActionFunctionArgs) {
  const formData = await request.formData();
  const intent = formData.get('_intent');
  switch (intent) {
    case 'archive': {
      const parsed = ArchiveSchema.safeParse(Object.fromEntries(formData));
      if (!parsed.success) return data({ errors: parsed.error.flatten() }, { status: 422 });   // form reads errors
      await api.server.post(`/invoices/${params.id}/archive`, parsed.data, { headers: authHeaders(request) });
      return redirect(`/invoices/${params.id}?tab=history`, { status: 303 });   // 303 so the browser follows with GET
    }
    case 'delete': {
      await assertCanDelete(request, params.id!);        // AUTHZ IN THE ACTION, not in the UI (4.5.5)
      await api.server.del(`/invoices/${params.id}`, { headers: authHeaders(request) });
      return redirect('/invoices', { status: 303 });
    }
    default:
      throw data({ message: 'Unknown intent' }, { status: 400 });
  }
}

const router = createBrowserRouter([{
  path: '/invoices/:id',
  loader: invoiceLoader,
  action: invoiceAction,
  Component: InvoiceRoute,
  ErrorBoundary: InvoiceErrorBoundary,
}]);

function InvoiceRoute() {
  const { invoice, tab } = useLoaderData<typeof invoiceLoader>();    // typed from the loader's return
  const navigation = useNavigation();                                 // global pending state for the form
  const actionData = useActionData<typeof invoiceAction>();           // typed 422 errors from the action

  return (
    <Form method="post">                                              {/* no JS required: works without React */}
      <button name="_intent" value="archive" disabled={navigation.state === 'submitting'}>Archive</button>
      {actionData?.errors?.title && <p role="alert">{actionData.errors.title}</p>}
    </Form>
  );
}
```

**Deferred loading (streaming), because loaders should not block the whole route.**

```tsx
export const loader = ({ params }: LoaderFunctionArgs) =>
  defer({
    // Fast, critical: awaited implicitly. Slow, secondary: streamed.
    invoice: invoiceLoader({ params } as LoaderFunctionArgs),
    attachments: fetchAttachments(params.id!).then((data) => ({ data })),   // a promise of { data } is deferred
  });

function InvoiceRoute() {
  const { invoice, attachments } = useLoaderData<typeof loader>();
  return (
    <>
      <InvoiceHeader invoice={invoice} />                 {/* renders as soon as it is ready */}
      <Suspense fallback={<AttachmentsSkeleton />}>
        <Await resolve={attachments}>                    {/* streams in later, in place */}
          {(r) => <Attachments items={r.data} />}
        </Await>
      </Suspense>
    </>
  );
}
// The UI shell is available in ~200ms instead of ~1.2s. Same idea as RSC streaming (4.2.6) and Suspense for data.
```

**Subtle but important mechanics.** (1) `useNavigation()` gives you a *global* pending state (`idle | loading | submitting | redirecting`) — use it for buttons and a subtle progress indicator, and understand that it does not tell you *which* route is loading. (2) Loaders re-run on every navigation to the route by default, which is why `shouldRevalidate` exists: return `false` to skip a revalidation when only the URL changed, or use `defaultShouldRevalidate` for form submissions (always true). (3) A loader throwing a `Response` becomes the nearest route `ErrorBoundary`'s `error` prop. (4) Loaders can `redirect()` — including in a loader, which is how you redirect after a failed auth check. (5) Loaders share data through the same request scope; use `defer` or nested routes to keep waterfalls out.

```tsx
export const shouldRevalidate: ShouldRevalidateFunction = ({ currentUrl, nextUrl, formMethod, defaultShouldRevalidate }) => {
  if (!defaultShouldRevalidate) return false;                    // no submission, no revalidation requested
  if (currentUrl.search === nextUrl.search) return false;         // only the pathname changed → reuse data
  return true;                                                    // a filter changed → refetch
};
```

**Next.js equivalents** (for comparison, since 4.2 covered them): a Server Component that awaits data is a loader; a Server Action is an action; `loading.tsx` is the Suspense boundary; `error.tsx` the boundary; `revalidatePath`/`revalidateTag` the invalidation API; `redirect()` from `next/navigation`. The conceptual map is identical — that is the point worth making in an interview.

**When to use the router's data layer.** Use it when the data is *needed to render the route* and *should be fetched as part of navigation* (a detail page, a list page, an authenticated shell). Do not use it for: a component-level widget fetch deep in the tree (a query library with its own cache is better — you do not want a widget re-render to re-run a route loader), anything that should keep a client-side cache across navigations (queries do that better), or background/live updates (4.4.6). The two layers coexist: **loaders for the route's initial data, query cache for everything the user interacts with afterwards.**

**Trade-offs.** Loaders: explicit, no waterfall, SSR-capable, progressive enhancement, and impossible to render with the wrong data (fetch-before-render is a *guarantee*, not a convention). Costs: you must learn a second data model; loaders run on *every* navigation unless you tune `shouldRevalidate`; data is not shared with non-router components (a modal rendered outside the route tree cannot use `useLoaderData`); and client-side-only caching of loader data requires extra work (`dehydrate`/`hydrate`, 4.4.2). Effects/queries: better caching and sharing, worse guarantees. The honest senior position: *use the router's loader for the route's data (it is the only thing that can SSR and stream it correctly), and use a query cache for incremental and shared client-side data.*

**Production and operational.** Loader failures must map to real HTTP statuses (404 vs 403 vs 500) or your server logs and monitoring are useless. Always pass through the incoming `Cookie`/`Authorization` headers when calling your own API from a loader — otherwise you lose the session and get an infinite redirect loop (a very common bug). Set explicit timeouts on loader fetches (`AbortSignal.timeout(3000)`) so a hanging dependency cannot hang the route. Instrument loader duration per route and alert on p95 regressions; a loader that turns into a waterfall is invisible otherwise. Cache loaders where safe (headers-based caching in the router) and be explicit that a loader is *per navigation*, so it is not a cache.

**Common mistakes.** Calling your API without forwarding auth headers (redirect loops). Using `useEffect` + fetch inside a route component anyway. Expecting `useNavigation().state` to identify which route. Revalidating on every query-string change. Loaders that ignore `shouldRevalidate` and hammer the API. Throwing `new Error` instead of a `Response` for expected states (everything becomes a 500).

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "What is the difference between a loader and an action?"
- "How do loaders avoid waterfalls?"
- "What is `defer` for?"
- "When does a loader re-run, and how do you stop it?"
- "Why would you use a query library inside a route that has a loader?"

**Mistakes:** mixing the two data models without a rule; unauthenticated loader fetches; no `shouldRevalidate` tuning.

---

## 4.5.5 Route guards and protected routes with auth (server-side enforcement included)

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.5.4, 4.2.8 (middleware), PHASE 7 → Auth Mechanisms

**Definition:** A route guard is code that decides whether the current navigation may proceed. The critical, security-relevant distinction: **client and router guards are UX; enforcement is server-side.** A guard can stop a user from seeing a screen; only the server can stop them from *doing the thing*. Interviewers who probe this distinction are looking for whether you understand that the ID in the URL is attacker-controlled input.

**Three layers, and what each one can do.**

```
Layer 1 — SERVER (edge/worker/middleware): reject or redirect before any HTML/JS is served.
          Can: prevent disclosure of data, enforce authn for the whole subtree, set edge-cache-safe redirects.
          Cannot: know which tab/field the user was editing. Also: server frameworks differ (Next middleware runs
          before rendering and can only read headers/cookies, not rendered data).

Layer 2 — ROUTER (loader / beforeEnter / layout guard): runs during navigation, before the route renders.
          Can: fetch the current user, redirect to /login?next=…, render a 403, avoid flashing protected content,
          and block the client router from ever requesting it.
          Cannot: be bypassed — the JS is on the user's machine.

Layer 3 — COMPONENT: render a "you don't have access" state or hide a control.
          Can: improve UX. Cannot: protect anything.
```

```tsx
// Layer 2, the real implementation. Guard in the LOADER, not in the component.
export async function requireUser(request: Request): Promise<{ user: User; token: TokenClaims }> {
  const token = await getSessionFromCookie(request.headers.get('cookie'));   // PHASE 7: signed, HttpOnly, SameSite
  if (!token) {
    const next = encodeURIComponent(new URL(request.url).pathname + new URL(request.url).search);
    throw redirect(`/login?next=${next}`, { headers: { 'Set-Cookie': await clearSessionCookie() } });
  }
  if (token.exp * 1000 < Date.now()) throw redirect('/login?reason=expired');
  return { user: await loadUser(token.sub), token };
}

// Per-object authorisation. NOT a role check: this is the check that stops IDOR (4.6.9).
export async function requirePermission(request: Request, permission: Permission) {
  const { user } = await requireUser(request);
  if (!user.permissions.includes(permission)) throw data({ permission }, { status: 403 });
  return user;
}

// Usage: guard the layout so EVERY nested route is protected by construction, not by remembering per page.
{
  path: '/app',
  loader: async ({ request }) => { await requireUser(request); return null; },
  Component: AppLayout,                          // renders <Outlet/> + nav + <AuthProvider user={...}/>
  ErrorBoundary: ForbiddenBoundary,              // a 403 becomes a real boundary, not a blank page
  children: [
    { path: 'settings', loader: async ({ request }) => { await requirePermission(request, 'settings:write'); return null; }, Component: Settings },
    { path: 'billing',  loader: billingLoader, Component: Billing },   // billingLoader itself calls requirePermission
  ],
}
```

```tsx
// Layer 1 — edge/server. Next.js middleware: cheap, pre-render, cookie-based, no data access.
import { NextResponse } from 'next/server';

export function middleware(request: NextRequest) {
  const { pathname } = request.nextUrl;
  const session = request.cookies.get('__session');                  // presence check only — NOT verification
  const isPublic = pathname === '/login' || pathname.startsWith('/public');

  if (!session && !isPublic) {
    const url = request.nextUrl.clone();
    url.pathname = '/login';
    url.searchParams.set('next', pathname + request.nextUrl.search);  // validate `next` on the login page!
    return NextResponse.redirect(url);
  }
  if (session && pathname === '/login') return NextResponse.redirect(new URL('/app', request.url));

  const response = NextResponse.next();
  response.headers.set('Cache-Control', 'private, no-store');       // never CDN-cache an authenticated page
  response.headers.set('Vary', 'Cookie');                             // 4.6.5 scenario 9
  return response;
}

export const config = { matcher: ['/app/:path*', '/billing/:path*', '/login'] };   // narrow; a broad matcher taxes every request
```

```tsx
// Layer 3 — component. Guard the *rendering of a control*, never the authorisation.
function DeleteProjectButton({ project }: { project: Project }) {
  const { user } = useAuth();
  const canDelete = user.permissions.includes('project:delete') && project.ownerId === user.id;   // UX only
  if (!canDelete) return null;                                        // the server checks again on DELETE
  return <ConfirmButton onConfirm={() => api.del(`/projects/${project.id}`)}>Delete</ConfirmButton>;
}
```

**The `next` parameter — the vulnerability everyone forgets.** `/login?next=https://evil.com` turns your login page into an open redirect used for phishing (and worse, some flows leak the session token into the URL). Validate it as a *relative path* on the client and again on the server:

```ts
function safeNext(raw: string | null): string {
  if (!raw) return '/app';
  // Same-origin relative path only: no scheme, no host, no protocol-relative "//evil.com", no backslash tricks.
  if (!raw.startsWith('/') || raw.startsWith('//') || raw.includes('\\')) return '/app';
  try { const u = new URL(raw, 'https://app.internal'); return u.origin === 'https://app.internal' ? u.pathname + u.search : '/app'; }
  catch { return '/app'; }
}
```

**The client router must also not render the protected component.** A guard that redirects *after* the route module is loaded still downloads the code and may flash. Use a lazy layout guard plus `ErrorBoundary`, and — importantly — do not rely on the guard to prevent the *fetch*: loaders must call `requireUser` themselves (Layer 2 above) because a child route can be navigated to directly by URL.

**Token storage, briefly (full treatment in PHASE 7 and 4.6.9).** `HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Lax` cookie is the default for browser apps: it is not readable by JS (so XSS cannot steal it), it is sent automatically (so it survives deep links and parallel requests), and `SameSite` gives you CSRF protection for free. A `Authorization: Bearer` token in `localStorage` is readable by any XSS and requires you to attach it manually to every request, including the ones you forget. If you must use a client-held token: short-lived access token + rotating refresh token in an `HttpOnly` cookie, with the token in memory only (never `localStorage`).

**Trade-offs.** Edge guards are fast and prevent disclosure but are limited by what they can read (cookies/headers, no DB), and a broad matcher adds latency to every request. Router guards are precise and can make authorisation decisions with full context, but only after the app is loaded. Component guards are pure UX. The correct architecture uses all three with one rule: **the server check is the only one that matters; the other two reduce wasted requests and avoid embarrassing flashes.**

**Production and operational.**
- Centralise: one `requireUser`/`requirePermission` per route module, and a lint/review rule that a route with data access must have a loader that calls it. Route-level guards are the most commonly forgotten security control in SPAs because there is no compile error when you skip one.
- `Cache-Control: private, no-store` and `Vary: Cookie` on authenticated responses; a CDN-cached authenticated page is a data breach (4.6.5 scenario 9).
- Guard the *server actions* and *API handlers* too — a route guard protects the UI, not the endpoint. This is Broken Access Control / IDOR territory in OWASP (PHASE 7).
- Session expiry mid-session: an API layer that detects a 401 broadcasts to all tabs (`BroadcastChannel`) and triggers one redirect, rather than N tabs each redirecting.
- Log denials with user + resource + permission: 403 spikes are the signal for a privilege-escalation attempt or a broken role mapping.
- Test: for every protected route, an integration test that asserts (a) unauthenticated → redirect, (b) authenticated but unauthorised → 403, (c) authorised → data, and (d) *authenticated as user A requesting user B's resource → 403* — that last one is the test most teams do not write and it is the one that catches real breaches.

**Common mistakes.** Guarding only in components; a role check where an object check was needed; validating `next` insufficiently; caching authenticated HTML; not guarding the server action/API; redirect loops from a loader that fetches without cookies.

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "You hid the admin button. Is the app secure?" (No — explain the three layers and where enforcement lives.)
- "How do you guard a route in Next.js, React Router, and your own SPA?"
- "How would you stop user A reading user B's invoice by changing the ID in the URL?"
- "What is an open redirect and how do you prevent it?"
- "Where do you store the access token, and what breaks in production if you get it wrong?"

**Mistakes:** component-only guards; broad middleware matchers; no object-level checks; unvalidated `next`; authenticated HTML in a shared cache.

---

## 4.5.6 Lazy loading routes and code splitting at the route boundary

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.1.12 (transitions), 4.5.4

**Definition:** Route-level code splitting is the discipline of making each route's *component module* a separate bundle, downloaded when that route is first needed. It is the single highest-leverage performance technique in a routed SPA, because routes are the natural boundaries of both code and data.

**How lazy routes actually work (and the two things that must be right).**

```
1. The route table itself must be statically analysable — that is why framework routers use a config
   file or a filesystem convention, and why a *dynamically computed* route table cannot be split.
2. Before rendering, the router must (a) start the import, and (b) render SOMETHING — a fallback boundary,
   or keep the previous route on screen (a transition). If you await() inside a component, you block
   rendering and you have thrown away concurrent rendering.
```

```tsx
// Option A — the loader's `lazy` property (React Router 6.4+): split AND defer the loader.
{
  path: 'reports/:id',
  // `lazy` runs when the route is MATCHED (at navigation time), not at import time.
  lazy: async () => {
    const [{ Component, loader }, { loader: reportLoader }] = await Promise.all([
      import('./routes/report/ReportRoute'),
      import('./routes/report/loader'),
    ]);
    return { Component, loader: reportLoader };      // also: action, ErrorBoundary, shouldRevalidate, handle
  },
}
// What it buys: the component chunk, the loader, AND the dependencies they import are all out of the entry bundle.
// Note: `lazy` must be declared on a route you can match WITHOUT evaluating the module — so no top-level
// imports of that route's dependencies in the route table, or the graph is pulled back in.

// Option B — React.lazy + Suspense, when you are not using a data router.
const Report = lazy(() => import('./Report'));     // must have a DEFAULT export
function App() {
  return (
    <Routes>
      <Route path="/reports/:id" element={
        // A Suspense boundary per route: only the loading route shows a fallback.
        <Suspense fallback={<RouteSkeleton />}>
          <Report />
        </Suspense>
      } />
    </Routes>
  );
}

// Option C — prefetching so the split is invisible (this is what makes route splitting free of UX cost).
// (a) On hover / on intent, above.
// (b) On idle, after the app is interactive, for routes the user is likely to need next.
// (c) <link rel="modulepreload"> in the document head from the server for the LCP route's chunk.
function useRoutePrefetch() {
  useEffect(() => {
    const idle = window.requestIdleCallback ?? ((cb) => setTimeout(cb, 1));
    idle(() => { void import('./Report'); void import('./Settings'); });   // low priority, after first paint
  }, []);
}

// Wrapping the lazy render in a transition keeps the OLD page visible instead of a spinner.
function ReportsPage() {
  const [isPending, startTransition] = useTransition();
  const navigate = useNavigate();
  const [Report, setReport] = useState<React.ComponentType | null>(null);
  const open = (id: string) => startTransition(async () => {
    const m = await import('./Report');            // awaited inside a transition: React keeps showing the list
    setReport(() => m.default);
    navigate(`/reports/${id}`);
  });
  return isPending ? <List dimmed /> : Report ? <Report /> : <List onOpen={open} />;
}
```

**Where to put the boundaries (and where not to).**

| Split here | Because |
|---|---|
| One bundle per route | Natural boundary; large win on multi-page apps; the framework's default |
| Heavy, rarely-used features (admin, editor, charts, PDF viewer) | Biggest ratio of bytes saved to code complexity |
| Route *layouts* per section (`/app/settings`, `/marketing`) | Keeps section chrome out of the entry bundle |
| Vendor libraries used by one route (a map SDK, a spreadsheet engine) | Route-level `import()` keeps them out of the shared chunk |
| Individual *components* (a rich text editor, a modal gallery) | Use `lazy` + Suspense at the component boundary, not per leaf |
| **Do not** split per micro-component | Thousands of tiny chunks: many requests, worse compression, harder debugging |
| **Do not** split the app shell / first route | Delays LCP; the shell is needed immediately |
| **Do not** split what is needed for hydration | Hydration must match the server's output; a lazily-rendered above-the-fold component produces a mismatch |

**The chunk-loading failure modes in production.** A deploy deletes chunks that a user's open tab still references: navigating later throws `ChunkLoadError: Loading chunk 42 failed`. You must handle it, and you must handle it *once*:

```tsx
// One-time recovery, guarded against loops. Put it at the app root.
class ChunkErrorBoundary extends Component<{ children: ReactNode }, { error: Error | null }> {
  state = { error: null as Error | null };
  private reloaded = false;
  static getDerivedStateFromError(error: Error) { return { error }; }
  componentDidCatch(error: Error, info: ErrorInfo) {
    if (/Loading chunk .* failed|Importing a module script failed|error loading dynamically imported module/i.test(error.message)) {
      if (!this.reloaded) { this.reloaded = true; window.location.reload(); }   // the new shell points at the new chunks
      return;                                                                  // recover silently
    }
    Sentry.captureException(error, { extra: { info } });
    this.setState({ error });
  }
  render() { return this.state.error ? <FatalError /> : this.props.children; }
}
// And in the server: keep the previous assets for a grace period (stale-while-revalidate on index.html,
// retain old hashed assets for ~24h) so an in-flight user never asks for a chunk you deleted.
```

**Verification.** Measure, do not assume: `vite build --mode production` → bundle report → confirm which routes pulled which vendors; use `<link rel="modulepreload">` for the LCP route; check the waterfall in the Network panel on a hard reload with cache disabled; and track real-user metrics (LCP, INP) with a release tag so a splitting change that regressed anything is visible (4.6.8).

**Trade-offs.** Route splitting reduces initial bytes and improves time-to-interactive, at the cost of: one more round trip on first navigation to each route (mitigated by prefetch), the `ChunkLoadError` class of bugs, and complexity around shared chunks (a bug-fix to a shared dependency invalidates every chunk that uses it, so you want long-lived vendor chunks and immutable hashed filenames). The failure mode of over-splitting is worse than under-splitting on a fast connection and better on a slow one — so measure on the slowest network your users actually have (4.6.8 RUM, not lab).

**Common mistakes.** `lazy` on the shell. Splitting above-the-fold content (hurts LCP). No prefetch, so every route navigation shows a spinner. No `ChunkLoadError` handling. Forgetting to include route-level *loaders* in the split. Suspending without a boundary or with a boundary at the root (one slow route blanks the app). `manualChunks` splitting so aggressively that a shared import is duplicated.

**What an interviewer probes.**
- "Where do you put code-splitting boundaries in a routed app?"
- "Why can you not split a dynamically computed route table?"
- "What is `ChunkLoadError` and how do you handle it?"
- "How do you avoid a spinner on every route navigation?"
- "Why can `lazy` break hydration?"

**Mistakes:** splitting the shell; no prefetch; no chunk-error recovery; a root-level Suspense boundary.

---

## 4.5.7 Navigation hooks, programmatic navigation, and scroll restoration

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.5.1, 4.5.3

**Definition:** Navigation hooks are the router's imperative API for programmatic navigation (`navigate`), the current location (`useLocation`), params (`useParams`), and navigation state (`useNavigation`, `useNavigationType`). Scroll restoration is the policy of putting the user back where they were when they hit Back — including the hard cases where the content was virtualised or lazily loaded and the saved offset no longer means anything.

**The hook surface, and when each is right.**

| Hook | Returns | Use for |
|---|---|---|
| `useNavigate()` | `(to, { replace, state, relative }) => void` | after a submit succeeds, on a redirect from a loader, on a cancel, on "open in new tab" logic |
| `useLocation()` | `{ pathname, search, hash, state, key }` | reading query state, analytics, effect dependencies |
| `useParams()` | route params (object) | the current resource identity |
| `useSearchParams()` | `[URLSearchParams, setSearchParams]` | URL state (4.3.8) — prefer this over `navigate` for filters |
| `useNavigation()` | `{ state, location, formMethod, formData }` | global pending indicator, disabling submit |
| `useNavigationType()` | `'POP' \| 'PUSH' \| 'REPLACE'` | scroll policy: only auto-scroll on PUSH, never on POP |
| `useNavigate` (relative) | `'..'`, `'../settings'`, `'.', 'x'` | nested-route-relative navigation (survives a URL restructure) |

```tsx
// Programmatic navigation, with the cases people get wrong.
const navigate = useNavigate();

async function onSubmit(values: Form) {
  await api.post('/invoices', values);
  // (a) after a mutation, PUSH: Back should return to the list the user came from.
  navigate(`/invoices/${res.id}`, { state: { from: location.pathname + location.search } });
  // (b) replace when the current entry should NOT be reachable by Back (login → app, post-login redirect).
  navigate('/app', { replace: true });
  // (c) relative: '..' goes up one route level, NOT one URL segment. In a nested router this is what you want.
  // (d) state is for UI context only (a return-to-hint, a modal origin). It is NOT data and it is NOT durable.
  // (e) NEVER navigate in an effect on mount without a guard — that is an infinite redirect loop:
  useEffect(() => { if (!user) navigate('/login', { replace: true }); }, [user, navigate]);
  // (f) replace the whole search string while keeping the pathname:
  setSearchParams(next, { replace: true });     // the correct tool for filters (4.3.8)
}
```

**Scroll restoration — the four cases, and only one is easy.**

```
1. PUSH (forward navigation)   → scroll to top, or to the new page's #anchor. The default users expect.
2. POP (Back/Forward)          → RESTORE the previous position. This is the hard one.
3. REPLACE (filters, modals)   → do NOT scroll at all.
4. Hash target present         → scroll to the element (after it exists, see below).
```

```tsx
// The straightforward case: browser-native restoration, turned off when you take over.
window.history.scrollRestoration = 'manual';

// Case 1 & 3: on PUSH scroll to top (or to the hash target once it exists).
function ScrollToTopOnPush() {
  const { pathname } = useLocation();
  const navType = useNavigationType();
  useLayoutEffect(() => {
    if (navType !== 'PUSH') return;
    if (location.hash) {
      // Scroll to the anchor AFTER the element renders. If it is inside a Suspense boundary, wait for it:
      const el = document.getElementById(decodeURIComponent(location.hash.slice(1)));
      if (el) el.scrollIntoView({ block: 'start' });
      else document.addEventListener('hashchange', () => {}, { once: true });  // or retry on data arrival
    } else {
      window.scrollTo({ top: 0, left: 0, behavior: 'instant' as ScrollBehavior });  // 'instant' avoids a smooth-scroll animation on a route change
    }
  }, [pathname, navType]);
  return null;
}

// Case 2: save the position keyed by `location.key` (a unique id per history entry), restore on POP.
function useScrollRestorationByKey(storage: 'session' | 'local' = 'session') {
  const location = useLocation();
  const navType = useNavigationType();
  const positions = useRef(new Map<string, number>());

  // Save the outgoing entry's position before it can change.
  useLayoutEffect(() => {
    const save = () => positions.current.set(location.key, window.scrollY);
    window.addEventListener('scroll', save, { passive: true });
    return () => { save(); window.removeEventListener('scroll', save); };
  }, [location.key]);

  useLayoutEffect(() => {
    if (navType === 'POP') window.scrollTo({ top: positions.current.get(location.key) ?? 0, behavior: 'instant' as ScrollBehavior });
    else if (navType === 'PUSH' && !location.hash) window.scrollTo({ top: 0, behavior: 'instant' as ScrollBehavior });
    // REPLACE: deliberately do nothing — a filter change must not move the user.
  }, [location.key, navType, location.hash]);
}
```

**Why pixel offsets fail, and the id-anchored fix.** Offset `1200px` is meaningless if the list's earlier items were deleted, the container resized, or the content is virtualised (only ~40 rows exist in the DOM, so row heights ≠ document heights). The robust approach anchors to a *content identity*:

```
On leaving the route:  record the id of the item at the top of the viewport + its pixel offset within that item
                      (store in sessionStorage keyed by location.key, or in history.state).
On POP back:          ask the server for the page(s) containing that id (fetch backwards if needed),
                      render until the anchor exists, then measure the anchor's real offset and scroll so
                      the anchor sits at the same viewport-relative position.
Fall back to:         the stored offset (best effort) — but re-apply it once content height stabilises
                      (after the last page loads / after images load), or the user lands at the wrong row.
For virtualised lists: restore the *scroll offset in item units* via the virtualiser's scrollToIndex, which
                      requires the item to be present in the data — hence fetching backwards first.
```

```tsx
// Anchor-based restoration, the part you can write in an interview.
function useAnchoredScrollRestore(items: { id: string }[], listRef: RefObject<HTMLDivElement | null>, anchorId: string | null) {
  useLayoutEffect(() => {
    if (!anchorId || !listRef.current) return;
    const node = listRef.current.querySelector<HTMLElement>(`[data-id="${CSS.escape(anchorId)}"]`);
    if (!node) return;                                   // not rendered yet: the caller must load more and re-run
```
