Why: cursors give consistency, not liveness — liveness needs a push (4.4.6) or a refetch.

**Drill 12 — Offset pagination with a deletion: user is on page 3 (rows 41–60) and 5 rows are deleted.**
Trace: `?page=3&limit=20` now returns what were rows 36–55. The user sees 5 rows they have already seen and never sees the last 5. Total count drops, so page 3 may be empty → clamp and `navigate('?page=2', { replace: true })`.
Why: the exact failure that makes feeds use cursors.

**Drill 13 — `useSearchParams` with `push` vs `replace` for a search box.**
Trace: typing "a", "ab", "abc" with `push` → three history entries; Back walks `ab` → `a` → the previous page, and the input re-renders each time (correctly, because URL is the source of truth). With `replace` and a 250 ms debounce → one entry, one fetch. Trace also: the debounce is on the **write**, not the read — the input stays responsive.
Why: history semantics are a product decision, and only Back/Firefox-style `popstate` reveals it.

**Drill 14 — Back button after a modal opened with `navigate('/photo/42')` and closed with `navigate('/gallery', { replace: true })`.**
Trace: entry `/gallery` → push `/photo/42` → replace `/gallery`. The stack is now `/gallery`, `/gallery`. Pressing Back exits the app instead of returning to the previous page. Correct close: `navigate(-1)`.
Why: replace destroys the user's Back path; this is why intercepted-modal routes close with `router.back()` (4.5.8).

**Drill 15 — Route matching trace for path `/invoices/42/attachments/2024/q3.pdf`.**
Input routes: `/invoices/:id`, `/invoices/:id/attachments/*`, `/invoices/new`, `/`.
Trace: `/invoices` static-matches (1st segment), `:id` dynamic-matches `42` (2nd), `attachments` static-matches (3rd), `*` splat-captures `2024/q3.pdf` → `params = { id: '42', '*': '2024/q3.pdf' }`. Ranking mattered only if two routes could both match — `/invoices/new` beats `/invoices/:id` because static scores higher.
Why: splat must be last and must capture the remainder joined with `/`, each segment decoded exactly once.

**Drill 16 — Loader auth trace: a signed-out user deep-links to `/app/billing`.**
Trace: middleware (cookie presence) misses → 302 `/login?next=%2Fapp%2Fbilling`. Login posts → `redirect('/app/billing')` → **loader** runs → `requireUser` throws a 302 (session was cleared by the 401 broadcast in another tab) → back to `/login` → loop.
Trace the fix: (a) add a `?reason=` marker so the second redirect is not silent, (b) do not fetch in a loader without forwarding the `Cookie` header — that is the actual cause of most of these loops, (c) guard the redirect with a "have we already redirected for this navigation" flag.
Why: the loop is almost always a missing `credentials`/`Cookie` forward, not a broken session.

**Drill 17 — A form with `mode: 'onTouched'` and 12 fields.**
Trace: typing in `email` → **zero** RHF re-renders (uncontrolled). Blur `email` → `trigger(['email'])` → one error, one re-render of the field's `FieldError` consumer only. Submit with `email` empty → full-schema validation → 3 errors → `shouldFocusError` moves focus to the first invalid field → server returns 422 on the second attempt → `setError('email', { type: 'server' })` → the client error is **not** overwritten on the next `trigger`, and clearing the field removes the client error but leaves the server one until re-submission.
Why: the last part is the bug people hit — client and server errors have different lifetimes and must be stored separately.

**Drill 18 — Two tabs, one localStorage write.**
Trace: tab A calls `setValue` → writes to `localStorage` → **no `storage` event in tab A**. Tab B receives `storage` with `{ key, newValue }`, parses, validates, and calls `setValue` → one re-render in B. Tab A's in-memory state is already correct, so no event is needed. Trace the failure: if A's write is skipped when the tab is hidden (or vice versa), B and A diverge until a reload; and if B's parse throws on a half-written value, the event must be ignored rather than clearing to the default.
Why: `storage` fires only in *other* tabs — a fact that breaks most hand-rolled cross-tab sync code.

---

# 4.3-4.5 DEBUGGING SCENARIOS (12)

Each follows **Symptoms → Hypotheses → Investigation → Root cause → Fix → Prevention.** The skill being demonstrated is forming the hypothesis from the *timeline* (what request, what render, what history entry, what release) rather than from reading code top-down.

## Scenario 1 — The list re-renders on every keystroke in an unrelated field

**Symptoms:** a 400-row table view becomes janky when typing in a filter input; the Profiler shows the row component committing on every keystroke even though no row data changed. No warnings, no errors.

**Hypotheses:** (a) a context provider above the table has `value={{ filters, rows }}` recreated per render; (b) the store selector returns a new array/object (`s.rows.filter(...)`) so `useSyncExternalStore` sees a new snapshot every notification; (c) the filter state lives in the same context as the rows, so filtering legitimately re-renders the list; (d) `React.memo` rows receive an inline `style={{...}}` or a callback prop; (e) a parent re-render creates new element props for every row.

**Investigation:** DevTools React panel → enable "Highlight updates when components render" → type one character → watch which component flashes. Turn on the Profiler with "Record why each component rendered" and read the recorded reason (`Props changed: 'onSelect'` vs `Context changed`). Grep for `value={{` and for selectors returning `.filter(`/`.map(`. Log `useSyncExternalStore` snapshot identity: `console.log(s === prevSnapshot)` — if false on a store notification with unchanged data, the selector is allocating.

**Root cause:** the filter state and the row data shared one context/store subscription, and the row selector returned a fresh array, so every keystroke invalidated 400 memoised rows.

**Fix:** split the contexts by update frequency (filters context vs data context), memoise the row selector (`createSelector` on `byId` + `ids`, with the filter applied outside the memo or inside a memised selector), pass stable callbacks (`useCallback` or a row id with a lookup), and mark rows `React.memo` with a custom `areEqual` if they carry derived fields.

**Prevention:** a render-count assertion test (`expect(renderCount).toBe(1)` after an unrelated state change), a lint/codemod rule against `useSelector(s => s.x.filter(...))`, and a documented rule that a component subscribes to the narrowest slice and that memoised boundaries require stable inputs.

## Scenario 2 — Infinite redirect loop between `/app` and `/login`

**Symptoms:** users with a valid session bounce between `/app` and `/login`; a handful of them land in a blank page after the loop; the server logs show a steady GET storm on both URLs. Started right after a release that added an authenticated layout route.

**Hypotheses:** (a) the new loader calls your API **without forwarding the `Cookie`/`Authorization` header**, so the API returns 401 and the loader redirects to login; (b) a permission check requires a permissions query that itself is guarded, creating mutual recursion; (c) `next` is dropped so the user lands on `/app` instead of the original URL; (d) a session cookie is set on the redirect response but with a path/domain that excludes the API path; (e) a middleware `matcher` catches `/login` and redirects logged-in users to `/app`, which loops with the API check.

**Investigation:** reproduce in a private window with DevTools open and "Preserve log" on; look at the failing XHR/fetch — is it sent with credentials? Check the request headers for `Cookie`/`Authorization`. `curl -sI -H 'Cookie: __session=…' https://staging.example.com/app` and the same against `/api/me`. Read the server access log for the *exact* repeating sequence and the status of every request in it (the 401 is the tell). Check the login redirect code for `next` preservation and for a `?reason` marker.

**Root cause:** the layout loader fetched the current user without forwarding the request's auth headers, so it always saw "anonymous" and always redirected.

**Fix:** pass `request.headers.get('cookie')` (or build an `authHeaders(request)` helper) into every loader fetch; assert `credentials: 'include'` for cookie-based APIs; add `?reason=session-expired` so the second hop is distinguishable; add a "do not redirect twice for the same navigation" guard so a genuine failure lands on an error page instead of looping.

**Prevention:** a helper that makes forwarding the *hard* path (a typed `serverFetch(request, url)` that always reads auth from the request); an integration test that deep-links to a protected route with a valid session and asserts a 200; an alert on repeated 3xx chains in the access log; and never put a permission check in a layout that a child loader also needs to fetch.

## Scenario 3 — Duplicate requests for the "same" data

**Symptoms:** the network panel shows two identical `GET /api/invoices?page=2` requests on every page change, and four on the first load of the invoice detail page. Response times are fine; nothing errors.

**Hypotheses:** (a) two components build the key differently (`['invoices', 2]` vs `['invoices', { page: 2 }]` vs `['invoices', 'page=2']`); (b) one uses `useQuery` and the other `useSWR` (two caches); (c) the list uses a `fetch` in a `useEffect` *and* a query; (d) `select` returns a new array, causing a re-render that re-mounts the hook (unlikely, but check); (e) StrictMode double-invoking effects (dev only — verify whether this is production); (f) an SSR pass fetches on the server and the client refetches because `staleTime`/`initialDataUpdatedAt` was not set.

**Investigation:** log every key: `queryClient.getQueryCache().getAll().map(q => q.queryKey)` and inspect for near-duplicates. In DevTools filter by "Fetch/XHR" and compare the full URLs *and* the initiator stack. Search the codebase for hand-written `fetch(` calls inside components. Check whether the duplicated requests appear in production (StrictMode) or only in dev. Look for `hydrate()`/`initialData` and confirm `initialDataUpdatedAt` matches the server timestamp.

**Root cause:** two callers used structurally different keys for the same request, so the cache treated them as distinct entries; a third came from an effect-based fetch.

**Fix:** centralise the keys in one factory (`invoiceKeys.list(page)`), remove the effect fetch, use one data library per app, and set `initialDataUpdatedAt` (or `staleTime`) so the hydrated cache is not immediately stale.

**Prevention:** key factories in a shared module; a lint rule banning `useQuery([` / `useSWR(` with an inline array literal; a smoke test that asserts request counts with a request counter (Playwright `page.on('request')`); and one data library per app as an architectural rule.

## Scenario 4 — The "infinite spinner" on a route that should have 403'd

**Symptoms:** a restricted page shows an infinite skeleton for users without permission. Network shows the request 403s within 40 ms. The user can see the route in the nav (so they know it exists) but can never load it.

**Hypotheses:** (a) the component only handles `isLoading` and `isError` is being treated as `isPending` (TanStack Query v4→v5 `isLoading` rename confusion); (b) the loader `throw data(...)` is caught but the error boundary is only at the root and the root's boundary also spins; (c) the query has `retry: (n) => n < 3` on a 403, so it retries forever with a spinner; (d) `enabled` is false because the permissions query has not resolved, and nothing re-enables it; (e) the error state is rendered but visually identical to the skeleton.

**Investigation:** print `query.status`, `isPending`, `isFetching`, `isError` in the component; check the request log for repeated identical requests (retry storm); inspect the nearest `ErrorBoundary` in the tree (React DevTools highlights which boundary caught it); in v5 confirm you migrated `isLoading` → `isPending` everywhere (a common upgrade bug: `isLoading` now means "pending AND fetching", so a failed query has `isLoading: false` but many components wrote `!isLoading ? <Error/> : <Spinner/>`).

**Root cause:** the route had no per-route error boundary and the component treated a terminal 403 as a still-loading query, so it never left the pending branch.

**Fix:** render the error state from `isError` first; make `retry` return `false` for 4xx; add an `errorElement`/`ErrorBoundary` at the route; and return a real 403 from the loader with a Forbidden view, not a thrown generic error.

**Prevention:** a shared `<AsyncBoundary>` that handles loading/error/empty/partial in one place (4.4.10); a default `retry` that never retries 4xx; per-route error boundaries in the route table; and an e2e test that visits the restricted route as an unprivileged user and asserts the 403 view.

## Scenario 5 — Data is correct in dev, stale in production for ~3% of users

**Symptoms:** a settings page shows the old plan for a small subset of users. Reproducible only in production; it resolves after a hard refresh. Release correlation: it started the day a new CDN rule was added.

**Hypotheses:** (a) the authenticated HTML/JSON is being CDN-cached without `Vary: Cookie`/`private` — one user's response served to another; (b) the query cache is persisted (`persistQueryClient`) with a long TTL and the hydration path skips revalidation; (c) a service worker serves a cached API response; (d) the flag/cohort cookie is cached and the server-rendered variant disagrees with the client; (e) `staleTime: Infinity` with a missed invalidation.

**Investigation:** `curl -sI https://app.example.com/settings -H 'Cookie: session=userA'` and inspect `Cache-Control`, `Vary`, `Age`, `x-cache`. Fetch the *exact* HTML for two different cookies and diff it — if they are identical, you have a cache-key bug. Check whether the query client is persisted and what `buster`/`maxAge` it uses. Check the service worker registration and its cache names. Compare the affected rate with the rollout percentage and with cache-age distribution.

**Root cause:** the CDN cached an authenticated page keyed only by URL, so a fraction of users received another user's (or an older) render.

**Fix:** `Cache-Control: private, no-store` (or at minimum `Vary: Cookie` plus a cache-key that includes the identity) on authenticated responses; purge the affected cache paths; add the identity to the cache key for anything user-specific.

**Prevention:** a rule that authenticated responses are never publicly cached, enforced in the CDN config and asserted by an automated check (request with a cookie, assert `no-store`); `Vary` headers in the response helper; no persisted query cache without a documented TTL and a logout `clear()`; and a synthetic test that requests an authenticated route with two different cookies and asserts the bodies differ.

## Scenario 6 — Optimistic like disappears when a second user also likes

**Symptoms:** user A likes a post (instant), user B likes the same post a second later; A's UI snaps back to "not liked" for a few seconds and then returns to "liked". No errors.

**Hypotheses:** (a) A's mutation is invalidated by a push event for the *same* entity from B, and the refetch response was generated before A's write committed (read-after-write inconsistency); (b) the refetch replaced the whole post object with a stale server copy; (c) a `useEffect` that syncs props to state overwrote the optimistic value; (d) the like count is a separate query that got invalidated and refetched with a cached intermediate value; (e) double-submission: two mutations for one click, second one 409s and rolls back.

**Investigation:** reproduce with two browsers against staging; enable the mutation timeline in the query devtools (or add `onMutate/onSuccess/onError/onSettled` logs with timestamps and the incoming payload); check the server response for the refetch and compare `likedBy` with what the UI shows; look for two mutation calls per click (a form submit plus a click handler both firing).

**Root cause:** the invalidating refetch raced the mutation's own write (no read-your-writes guarantee) and its response overwrote the optimistic cache entry.

**Fix:** ensure read-your-writes (the mutation's `onSuccess` writes the authoritative entity *after* any in-flight refetch, or cancel + refetch *after* settle); make the refetch a `POST`-invalidate if the API supports it; and guard against double submission with `isPending`.

**Prevention:** the `cancelQueries` → optimistic → `onSuccess` → `onSettled` invalidate ordering, enforced by a shared mutation helper; an idempotency key so a double click is one write; and a two-browser e2e test for concurrent likes.

## Scenario 7 — The feed loses items when scrolling back up

**Symptoms:** a cursor-paginated feed, `maxPages: 7`. Users report that scrolling up sometimes loads the same items twice and sometimes jumps a screen; on a fast scroll some items are skipped.

**Hypotheses:** (a) dedupe is applied per page instead of at merge time; (b) `getNextPageParam` is called with a stale `lastPage` due to a closure in the effect; (c) `maxPages` sliding shifts indices so the saved cursor array no longer matches the page it does; (d) the sentinel effect fires twice concurrently (no `isFetching` guard) and two `fetchNextPage` calls interleave; (e) the server's cursor is not scoped to the filter set, so changing a filter mid-scroll mixes two cursors.

**Investigation:** log every request URL plus the returned `nextCursor` and item ids; in the devtools, step through a slow scroll and watch for two concurrent requests for the same cursor; assert the merged id list has no duplicates by adding a dev-only invariant `assert(new Set(ids).size === ids.length)`; check whether `getPreviousPageParam` is implemented at all (if not, "scroll up" is doing nothing while `maxPages` still drops pages).

**Root cause:** no `isFetchingNextPage` guard plus per-page rather than merged dedupe, with `maxPages` trimming pages whose cursors were still needed.

**Fix:** guard the sentinel with `hasNextPage && !isFetchingNextPage`; dedupe in one `useMemo` over the flattened pages; keep cursors keyed by page index **plus** trim only on the far end; scope cursors to the filter set server-side and reset the query when filters change.

**Prevention:** a dev invariant for duplicate ids; an e2e test that scrolls 5 screens up and down and asserts the id sequence is strictly monotonic with no duplicates; and `staleTime`/`resetKeys` discipline so a filter change starts a fresh chain.

## Scenario 8 — Flash of wrong theme on first paint

**Symptoms:** users with dark mode enabled see a white flash for 150–400 ms on hard load, only on first navigation of the session. No hydration warnings in the console.

**Hypotheses:** (a) the theme is read from `localStorage` in a `useEffect`, so the first client render uses the default; (b) the theme is in Context with `value={{ theme }}` created in a client component that mounts after paint; (c) a CSS `prefers-color-scheme` media query is used alongside an explicit toggle, and they disagree; (d) the `<html>` class is set by an effect on the client only; (e) the blocking head script exists but the cookie-based path is not used for SSR.

**Investigation:** `curl -s https://app.example.com/ | grep -i 'class='` on `<html>` — the server output must already carry the theme. Disable JavaScript and check the rendered colours. Use the Performance panel and look at the first paint vs the first contentful paint; `chrome://media-internals`/emulation for `prefers-color-scheme`. Look for two sources of truth (media query + stored preference).

**Root cause:** the theme is applied after hydration because it is client-read state, so the first paint uses the default palette.

**Fix:** resolve the theme before first paint: store it in a cookie the server can read, render `<html class="dark">` on the server, and add a tiny blocking inline script in `<head>` that sets the class from `localStorage` when no cookie exists (accepting the CSP cost of a nonce, 4.6.9); gate React rendering on a `hydrated` flag so there is no mismatch.

**Prevention:** theme resolution in one module used by the server and the client; a visual test in both themes; and `suppressHydrationWarning` only on the `<html>` element, never globally.

## Scenario 9 — `Maximum update depth exceeded` after adding a store selector

**Symptoms:** a screen throws "Maximum update depth exceeded" within a second of mount. It started when a component switched from `useSelector(s => s.count)` to `useSelector(s => ({ a: s.a, b: s.b }))`.

**Hypotheses:** (a) the selector returns a new object → `useSyncExternalStore`'s snapshot differs on every call → React re-subscribes in a loop; (b) an effect that dispatches state on every render, and the dispatch notifies the same subscriber; (c) `setState` in the store called during render; (d) a selector that reads a mutable object whose identity is stable but whose contents the component mutates during render.

**Investigation:** read the error stack for the repeated component. Log the snapshot identity inside `getSnapshot`: `console.log(next === prev)`. Check the store's `setState` for an equality bail-out. Verify whether the dispatch is in an effect with an unstable dependency.

**Root cause:** object-literal selector output defeats `useSyncExternalStore`'s snapshot identity check.

**Fix:** return a primitive, use `useShallow`/a shallow-equality option, or memoise with `createSelector`.

**Prevention:** a lint rule for inline object/array returns in selectors; a `useSelector` wrapper in `store/hooks.ts` that documents the rule; and a dev-only warning when a selector output is not `Object.is`-stable across two calls with the same state.

## Scenario 10 — Route guard bypass: user changes the id in the URL

**Symptoms:** QA reports that a user can read another team's invoice by editing `/invoices/123` to `/invoices/124`. The nav is correct and the button is hidden, but the request returns 200 with data.

**Hypotheses:** (a) the API endpoint trusts the id and only checks authentication, not authorisation; (b) the loader calls `requireUser` (authentication) but not an ownership check (authorisation); (c) the check exists but is a role check (`admin` vs `member`) rather than an object check; (d) the query is not scoped by tenant in the `WHERE` clause; (e) an RSC/loader path bypasses the middleware.

**Investigation:** `curl -H 'Cookie: session=userA' https://api.example.com/invoices/124` — a 200 is the finding. Read the handler's query: does it filter `orgId: session.orgId`? Compare with the route guard: what does it assert? Grep the route table for every dynamic route and check each has a loader that calls the *ownership* helper.

**Root cause:** authentication was enforced; object-level authorisation was not (classic IDOR / broken access control, OWASP A01).

**Fix:** scope every query by tenant **and** owner in the data access layer, return 404 (not 403) when the object is outside the caller's scope so existence is not leaked, and add a shared `requireOwnership` helper that must be called by every loader/action/handler that touches a user-owned resource.

**Prevention:** a per-route table mapping every dynamic segment to its authorisation helper, enforced in review and by a CI grep; integration tests for every route with a "user A accesses user B's resource → 404" case; authorisation enforced in the data-access layer (so a new endpoint inherits it) rather than in handlers; and log denials for alerting.

## Scenario 11 — Analytics shows fewer page views than server logs, correlated with slow pages

**Symptoms:** client pageview counts are ~12% below server request counts, and the gap grows with page load time. Events sent in the last second before a navigation never arrive.

**Hypotheses:** (a) `fetch` without `keepalive` is aborted on unload (the 4.6.6 scenario 6 cause); (b) flush on `beforeunload` instead of `visibilitychange` (unreliable on mobile, and skipped when the bfcache is used); (c) the payload exceeds `sendBeacon`'s size cap; (d) an ad blocker kills the collector domain silently; (e) SPA navigations never fire a pageview because the collector listens to `load` only.

**Investigation:** DevTools with "Preserve log" and throttled network; watch the pending request during a navigation and look for `(canceled)`; compare counts per page type; test the beacon path and see the gap close; check `navigator.userAgent`/extensions; count SPA navigations separately from full loads.

**Root cause:** navigation-aborted `fetch` calls plus no `visibilitychange` flush, so events in the last moments of a session are lost.

**Fix:** `sendBeacon` on `visibilitychange`/`pagehide` with a `fetch(..., { keepalive: true })` fallback, batched under the size cap; fire a synthetic pageview on router navigations with the navigation type.

**Prevention:** a "no event lost on navigation" integration test; a nightly reconciliation dashboard comparing client events to server truth with an alert above 5% divergence; and the collector contract documented in the analytics spec.

## Scenario 12 — Upload hangs at 0% then fails at 60 seconds

**Symptoms:** file uploads over ~40 MB show 0% progress, then fail with a gateway timeout at exactly 60 s. Smaller files succeed. Users report "it just stops".

**Hypotheses:** (a) uploads are proxied through the app server and the ingress timeout is 60 s; (b) the progress bar is `fetch`-based, which never fires upload progress (so it reads 0% until completion); (c) `Content-Length` is not pinned and the server cannot enforce a max size; (d) the client does not chunk, so a retry restarts from zero; (e) memory: the server buffers the whole file.

**Investigation:** reproduce with a 100 MB file and watch the request timing (TTFB vs download) and the response status/headers (`504`, `client_max_body_size`, `413`). Check `nginx client_max_body_size`, the platform's request size/time limits, and the Lambda/API Gateway payload caps. Look at the client code for `xhr.upload.onprogress` (absent ⇒ (b)) and for the storage layer.

**Root cause:** the app server was the transport, so a 60 s ingress timeout killed large uploads, and `fetch` gave no upload progress to show.

**Fix:** switch to presigned direct-to-storage `PUT` for anything above ~5 MB (with `XHR` only for the progress bar if a single PUT suffices, or S3 multipart for resumability); pin `Content-Length` and `Content-Type` in the presign so the client cannot upload more or a different type; confirm server-side afterwards (ownership, size, magic bytes, malware scan) before promoting from `quarantine/` to final.

**Prevention:** a documented upload-size policy per route (a 25 MB server cap and a 5 GB direct cap); client-side validation with a clear message; a multipart resumable path with per-part retry; and a dashboard of upload failure rates by size bucket to catch regressions in the presign path.

---

# 4.3-4.5 INTERVIEW Q&A (36 questions with model answers)

Answers are written to be *reasoned aloud*: state the decision rule first, then the mechanism, then the failure you are avoiding. That structure is what senior interviewers are listening for.

**Q1. When do you reach for a global store, and when is Context enough?**
Context is enough when the value is stable-ish and the consumers are few: theme, locale, an injected API client, the current router. The defining property is that the value changes rarely, so a broadcast invalidation costs nothing. I reach for a store when the value changes at interaction frequency, when many independent components read *different slices*, or when I need middleware — cross-cutting behaviour like analytics, persistence, or a cancellable workflow. The test I actually apply: if the value changes more than once per user interaction, Context's broadcast will show up in INP, and a store with per-component selectors is the right shape. And the first question is always whether it is server state, because if it is, none of these apply — it belongs in a query cache.

**Q2. Explain a Context performance incident you would have caused, and how you fixed it.**
A settings page became unresponsive after we added a live character counter to the page header. We had one `AppContext` with `{ user, theme, filters, rows }`, and the provider did `value={{ user, theme, filters, rows }}`. Because that object is new on every render, every consumer re-rendered whenever anything changed — and the whole app was a consumer through the nav. I would diagnose it with the Profiler set to "record why each component rendered" and see `Context changed` on components whose props never changed. The fix was threefold: split the context by update frequency (identity, preferences, and data are three providers, not one), memoise each value, and move the hot data to a query cache so the provider no longer held it. The lasting change was a rule plus a render-count test, because the failure mode is invisible in code review — nothing looks wrong.

**Q3. Redux Toolkit or Zustand — how do you decide?**
Not on bundle size; on the number of writers and whether anyone will need to debug it. Redux Toolkit earns its cost when state transitions are order-dependent or non-trivial, when actions need to be logged and replayed, when several teams extend the same store, and when I want middleware — `createAsyncThunk` plus listener middleware gives me cancellation, debouncing and cross-slice cascades with an explicit, reviewable surface. Zustand earns its cost when the app is smaller, the state is a handful of independent slices, and the team values brevity over a DevTools timeline. In practice I reach for Zustand plus TanStack Query for a new mid-size app and keep Redux Toolkit where it exists and works, because migration cost dominates. The one non-negotiable in both cases: server state never goes in the store.

**Q4. What is normalized state and what bug does it prevent?**
Normalized state stores each entity once — `byId: Record<Id, T>` plus `ids: Id[]` — and references entities by id everywhere else. The bug it prevents is duplicated copies of the same fact: with `{ posts: [{ author: { name } }], comments: [{ author: { name } }] }`, a name change needs three writes and a missed one leaves the comments page showing the old name forever. It also fixes identity: two copies of the same user are different object references, so a selector comparing them never matches and a subscription on one never fires for the other. On top of that, per-entity subscription becomes O(1) — `byId[id]` — instead of scanning an array with `find` on every store notification. `EntityAdapter` gives me the shape and the reducers; `normalizr` is for flattening responses from an API I do not control.

**Q5. What does reselect memoise, and when does it go wrong?**
A memoised selector caches its output against the values returned by its *input selectors*, using reference equality. So it recomputes only when one of those inputs changes identity. It goes wrong three ways: the wrong dependency list (you forget an input, so the output is stale forever); creating the selector inside a component, which means a new cache every render and therefore no memoisation at all; and returning a fresh object from a *plain store* selector, where nothing memoises it, so `useSyncExternalStore` sees a new snapshot each call and you get an infinite render. Reselect is a performance mechanism, not a correctness one — if your output is wrong, reselect made the wrong answer sticky.

**Q6. Thunk, saga, or listener middleware?**
`createAsyncThunk` for request/response: it gives me pending/fulfilled/rejected, `condition` for dedupe, `rejectWithValue` for my own error messages, and an injectable `extraArgument` so it is testable. Listener middleware for "when this action happens, do that": analytics, invalidation, toasts, and cancellable debounced side effects, because it runs *after* the reducer and gets `getState`, `dispatch`, `delay`, `signal` and `cancelActiveListeners`. A saga only when the flow has multiple concurrent long-lived processes with cross-cancellation — a websocket loop, a multi-step orchestration with retries and timeouts — because a saga is a pausable program, which is the one thing the others cannot do. My rule: one mutation flow is a thunk, action reactions are listeners, and a saga is a deliberate choice with a named reason.

**Q7. How do you decide if something is server state?**
I ask who can change it without this browser being involved. If another user, a background job, or a deploy can change it, it is server state and this client holds a *cache* of it — so it needs staleness, deduplication, invalidation and garbage collection, which means a query cache. If only this tab's interactions can change it, it is client state and `useState` is correct. The practical tell is a bug: if you have ever written a `useEffect` that copies a fetch result into state and a second place that has to keep it fresh, you have misclassified it as client state. And the corollary matters as much: server state in a store means you now own a cache with no invalidation policy, which is the most common way these libraries get misused.

**Q8. What is the status of Recoil, and what would you use instead?**
Recoil was archived by Meta in 2023 — the repository is read-only, with no new features or bug fixes — so I would not propose it for new work. I can still read it, because plenty of large codebases run it, and it has two genuinely nice ideas: an atom graph with automatic dependency tracking, and `atomFamily` for per-id caches. Its practical problems are a large bundle, a history of friction with React 18 concurrent features and StrictMode, and awkward TypeScript (`RecoilState<T>` per atom). My recommendation would be Jotai if the design is atom-shaped — same author, same philosophy, actively maintained — or Zustand if the team wants one conventional store. Saying "Recoil is archived and here is the migration path" is a much stronger answer than quietly avoiding it.

**Q9. When is a state machine better than a set of booleans?**
When the interesting logic is in the *transitions* rather than the values. Booleans multiply: five booleans is 32 combinations, maybe six of them real, and every new feature adds a combination you have to think about. One state value with six states has six. A machine is worth it when I need to answer questions like "what is legal right now?" or "ignore a double click while submitting", when cleanup must be tied to a state's lifetime, or when the action is money-adjacent and I want the logic unit-testable without rendering. I would *not* use one for a preferences object, a single flag, or CRUD filtering — a reducer is smaller and clearer there. The failure mode of overusing machines is real: an FSM for a dropdown is pure overhead, so the decision is about transition complexity, not state count.

**Q10. What state goes in the URL, and what does not?**
Anything a user might want to share, bookmark, or survive a refresh with — filters, sort, page, active tab, selected entity, and anything that changes what the server returns. That last clause is the one people forget: if `?page=3` does not change what the server sends, a shared link renders page 1 and the URL is a lie. What does not go in the URL: server data (it is a cache), secrets (query strings leak into logs, `Referer`, and screenshots), and transient interaction state like hover, open panels, or which field is focused. The mechanic that matters is `push` versus `replace` per interaction — filters replace so Back leaves the page, navigation pushes — and canonicalisation, so defaults are omitted and every view has exactly one URL.

**Q11. How do you make `localStorage` safe?**
Four things. First, never render from it during the first client render — read it in an effect and gate on a `hydrated` flag, or the client output will not match the server. Second, treat it as untrusted input: validate with a schema on read, because a user can edit it in devtools and a schema change will otherwise throw on load. Third, version it — the key carries a version, and there is a migration table, so a shape change does not brick returning users. Fourth, never store a token there: any XSS can read it for the life of the token, and it is not scoped or revocable the way an `HttpOnly` cookie is. For cross-tab behaviour I also add a `storage` listener, remembering it fires only in *other* tabs, so a naive implementation never updates the writer.

**Q12. Walk me through an optimistic update, including failure.**
Five steps. Predict the server's result; snapshot the exact cache entries I am about to change — deeply, because the cache may hand back a live reference; `cancelQueries` on those keys so an in-flight refetch cannot overwrite my optimistic value; apply the value so the UI responds immediately; then reconcile — on success overwrite with the server's authoritative copy, on failure restore the snapshot *and* invalidate, because the snapshot may already have been stale. The failure people hit is rollback-without-invalidate, which leaves the cache confidently wrong. And on creation I send a client-generated id and reconcile by it, so a retry after a timeout cannot create two rows. I would not be optimistic about payments or anything irreversible; I would show a pending state and wait.

**Q13. What does RTK Query give you that `createAsyncThunk` does not?**
Caching, request deduplication by key, a status state machine per query, background revalidation with `refetchOnMount`/`Focus`/`Reconnect`, garbage collection of unused entries, loading and error state per query, and a declarative invalidation graph through `providesTags`/`invalidatesTags`. A thunk gives me pending/fulfilled/rejected and nothing else — caching, sharing between components and staleness are mine to build, and everyone builds them slightly wrong. So the honest framing is that `createAsyncThunk` is for imperative *workflows* — multi-step orchestration with cancellation and error mapping — and RTK Query is for *reads and writes to the API*. Having both a hand-rolled fetch thunk and RTK Query in one app means two caches with different staleness policies, which is a bug factory.

**Q14. How would you migrate an app that uses `useState` everywhere?**
Incrementally, in an order chosen by value. Step one is an inventory: classify every state with the taxonomy, which alone surfaces the derived-state duplication bugs. Step two is moving all server data to a query layer, because it is the biggest win and the least invasive — components keep the same `data`/`isLoading`/`error` surface, so almost no call sites change. Step three is URL-ifying shareable view state. Only then do I introduce a store for what genuinely remains, one slice at a time behind a flag, and delete the old path when the last consumer moves. What I refuse to do is the big-bang "rewrite the state layer" PR: it is unreviewable, it stalls feature work for weeks, and it never lands.

**Q15. What is the waterfall problem and how do you fix it?**
A waterfall is when request B cannot start until request A has resolved, resolved a component, caused a render, committed, and run B's effect. With effects, dependent requests serialise because the effect is the fetch. Three fixes: start independent requests together in one effect with `Promise.all` (and `allSettled` when one panel failing should not blank the page); use the router's loader model or a query library so sibling fetches are concurrent by construction and each can render behind its own Suspense boundary; and prefetch on intent, so the request starts before the navigation rather than during it. The one thing that never fixes a waterfall is making the first request faster.

**Q16. `staleTime` vs `gcTime`?**
`staleTime` answers "is this fresh enough to serve without asking the server?" and `gcTime` answers "how long do we keep this after nobody is looking at it?". With `staleTime: 0`, which is the default, returning to a list refetches even though you have data — correct for a feed, wrong for a country list; a five-minute `staleTime` there removes a request on every navigation. `gcTime` starts counting only when the *last observer unmounts*, so a screen left open holds its entry indefinitely, and `gcTime: 0` means no cache between screens and a skeleton on every navigation. Setting `staleTime: Infinity` is a real choice with a real obligation: you own invalidation completely, and one missed invalidation is wrong forever.

**Q17. How do you design query keys?**
One exported factory per entity, so nobody hand-writes an array in two places. The key includes every input that changes the response — which means a filter belongs in the key, not just in the request function. I use objects rather than positional arguments so the key is self-describing, and I omit undefined values because the hash normalises them away and phantom entries are how you get two caches for one logical view. I keep prefix levels (`['invoices','list']`, `['invoices','detail',id]`) so invalidation can be coarse or precise. And I include tenant and user-identifying inputs, because a shared cache across an account switch is a data leak. The test: if two different screens can show different data from the "same" query, the key is wrong.

**Q18. How do you decide what to invalidate after a mutation?**
Broad invalidation is correct and cheap to implement, and it gets expensive exactly linearly with the number of live cache entries under the prefix — one write, N refetches, one per active filter combination. Precise invalidation requires a mental model of every dependent read, which is invisible in review: a new component with a new key silently becomes a missed dependent. So my practical policy is: optimistic write for the immediate UX, precise invalidation for the specific keys I know, and *broad* invalidation on the failure path, because after a failure I do not know what state the server is in. Where the dependency graph is wide, RTK Query's tags express it declaratively. And I test it: for each mutation, assert the expected keys are invalidated and that an unrelated key is not.

**Q19. Offset vs cursor pagination, and what does infinite scroll break?**
Offset pagination gives you page numbers and random access but breaks under concurrent mutation: insert a row at the top between page 1 and page 2 and you will show one row twice and skip another, because offsets are positions, not identities. Cursor pagination is stable, O(log n) with a composite index on the sort key, and immune to that — at the cost of no page numbers and no cheap total count. So feeds, comments and chat use cursors; admin tables that need "jump to page 57" use offsets with a unique tiebreaker in the sort. Infinite scroll adds its own costs: memory (hence `maxPages`), find-in-page, the back button, and accessibility — so it needs a real "Load more" button, a cursor-free anchor for restoration, and an announcement of newly loaded items.

**Q20. Polling, SSE, or WebSocket?**
I ask three questions. Does the user need to see changes without doing anything? If not, I use no live data at all — invalidation on focus is usually the correct answer and the cheapest. If staleness tolerance is minutes, I poll above that tolerance, pause when the tab is hidden, and back off on error. If it is seconds and one-way, SSE: nearly free at idle, reconnects itself, needs no new infrastructure, and only needs buffering disabled on the proxy. WebSockets only when the client must talk — typing indicators, per-entity subscriptions, chat — and then I accept the cost of implementing reconnect with exponential backoff and jitter, heartbeats, and a resync path, because the stream can never be assumed complete. I have also seen this scale problem: at 10k clients on a five-second poll you have 120k requests a minute, and the answer is a change-feed plus SSE, not a faster poll.

**Q21. Why uncontrolled forms, and RHF vs Formik?**
Uncontrolled means the input owns its value and the form reads it on submit, so typing causes zero re-renders; controlled means every keystroke is a `setState` and a render of the form. On a two-field form that is irrelevant; on a 200-field form it is the difference between smooth and unusable. RHF is uncontrolled-first with a `register`-based registry, stable field-array ids, first-class server-error mapping, and per-field subscription through `useWatch` for the rare places you need React state. Formik is controlled-first, with a promise-based `ValidationError`-based Yup schema, and its main lever is `validateOnChange={false}`. I use RHF for anything non-trivial. For either, I keep the native attributes — `required`, `type`, `inputMode`, `autoComplete` — because mobile keyboards, autofill and accessibility are free there, and I remove `noValidate` only when my schema genuinely covers what the native rules would have caught.

**Q22. How do you keep client and server validation in sync?**
One schema module, imported by both the RHF resolver and the server handler, with the type inferred from it (`z.infer`). The handler calls `safeParse` before touching the database and returns a 422 whose body is a list of `{ path, message }`; the form maps that onto `setError(path, { message, type: 'server' })` through one shared function. The subtle part is that the client copy is UX, never enforcement — anyone can `curl` — and the split I use is to share *shape* and user-facing messages while keeping *policy* (rate limits, permissions, uniqueness, breach-list checks) on the server. Where the schema must be strict about untrusted input I use `.strict()` rather than silently stripping unknown keys, so a client typo fails loudly instead of dropping a field.

**Q23. What is a schema default that has bitten you?**
`z.coerce.boolean()`: JavaScript's `Boolean("false")` is `true`, so a payload of `"false"` coerces to `true` — a "disable this setting" request that enables it. Related: `z.coerce.number()` turns `""` into `0`, so an empty optional field silently becomes zero. And `z.object` in Zod v3 *strips* unknown keys by default, which is good for mass-assignment defence and bad for debugging, because a typo'd field vanishes with no error. My rule: coerce only where an empty string genuinely means the same thing as the number, and use `z.preprocess` with an explicit empty check everywhere else. I also always check `.optional()` versus `.nullable()`, since that mismatch accounts for a large share of "the form won't submit".

**Q24. How would you design uploads for large files?**
Direct to object storage with a short-lived presigned URL, so my server never proxies bytes: that removes the ingress timeout that kills large uploads (a 60-second gateway timeout is the single most common cause of "uploads over 40 MB always fail"), removes double egress, and makes the app server stateless. The presign is scoped — exact key under a `quarantine/` prefix, pinned content type and content length, five-minute expiry — and then the client PUTs straight to storage, with `XMLHttpRequest.upload.onprogress` for a real progress bar because Fetch has no upload progress event at all. Crucially, after the upload the server confirms: `HeadObject` for size and type, a magic-byte check rather than trusting `File.type`, a malware scan, and only then promotion out of quarantine. Above ~100 MB I use multipart with per-part retry and a persisted `uploadId` so an interrupted upload resumes instead of restarting. Never trust the client's content type, and serve user content from a different domain than the app.

**Q25. How many states does an async UI have?**
At least five: initial loading, refreshing, empty, error, and partial. Empty splits into three products — first-run (teach and give one click), filtered (explain and offer to clear), and never error-as-empty, because an empty state on a failed request tells the user they have no data when you simply do not know. The mechanics people miss: `isFetching` is not `isPending` once you have data, so a refetch must not clear the screen; and a failed *refetch* must show the stale data with a notice rather than blanking it. I also delay the loading indicator by ~150 ms, because a spinner on a 40 ms request is a flash and flashes feel slower than nothing, and skeletons must match the final layout or I have traded a spinner for CLS.

**Q26. What is the stale-write problem?**
It is the family of bugs where a write succeeded but the cache is wrong, or two writes raced. Four shapes: write-write race (two mutations on one entity, responses arriving out of order — fix with per-entity serialisation or an `If-Match` version); rollback staleness (an optimistic rollback restores a snapshot that was itself stale — fix by invalidating instead of restoring on failure); missed dependents (you updated a list but not the count widget — fix by enumerating dependents and testing the enumeration); and cache-key blind spots, where the key omits an input that changes the response, so `/projects` and `/projects?archived=true` are two entries and archiving updates only one. The general fix is that invalidation is a *declared* responsibility of the write, and it should be unit-tested per mutation.

**Q27. What are the constraints on `pushState`?**
Same-origin only or it throws; the URL is length-limited so large payloads belong in the state object, not the query string; the state must be structured-cloneable, so no functions, class instances or DOM nodes; and it does not fire `popstate`, so the app must update its own UI synchronously. Two more consequences people miss: you now own everything a document load would have done — code splitting, data fetching, `document.title`, meta tags, scroll — which is exactly why frameworks abstract it; and the server must be configured to rewrite unknown paths to your shell, or every deep link 404s. That rewrite must exclude real asset paths, or you serve HTML for your JavaScript.

**Q28. Hash routing vs history routing?**
The decisive difference is that a fragment is never sent to the server. So with hash routing the server cannot authenticate the route, cannot redirect it, cannot render it, and cannot cache it separately — which means no SEO value, no per-route SSR, no auth redirects, and no link unfurls. History mode is the right default for anything public or shared. Hash routing still has legitimate uses: contexts where you cannot configure the server at all — a static host with a fixed fallback, an iframe served by another team, a browser extension, a desktop app — and fragments used as *data* rather than routes. I would also note the flash: the fragment arrives after the document loads, so a hash router must read it in an effect, not during render.

**Q29. How do you protect a route, and what does hiding a button achieve?**
Nothing, by itself — a hidden button is UX. Enforcement has to be three layers. The edge or server middleware rejects or redirects before serving anything, using a cookie presence check, with a narrow matcher and `private, no-store` on the response. The router loader does the real check before the route renders: verify the session, then authorise, and redirect or throw a 403. And the server-side data access enforces it again, per object — which is the layer that actually stops IDOR, because the id in the URL is attacker-controlled. I guard the *layout* so nested routes inherit it by construction rather than by remembering per page, I validate `next` against open redirects, and I write the test nobody writes: authenticated as user A, request user B's resource, expect 403/404.

**Q30. How do you code-split routes, and what breaks?**
Split per route, and let the router own the boundary: a route's `lazy` returns its component *and* its loader, so the code and the data logic leave the entry bundle together. Split at section layouts and at heavy rare features — an editor, a chart library, a PDF viewer — and never split the shell or above-the-fold content, because that delays LCP, and never split something needed for hydration, because the client output will not match. To keep it invisible I prefetch on intent (`defaultPreload: 'intent'` prefetches on hover and focus), so navigation usually hits a warm chunk. The thing that breaks in production is `ChunkLoadError`: a deploy deletes chunks an open tab still references, so you need a one-time reload in a root error boundary, guarded against loops, plus keeping old assets around for a grace period.

**Q31. How do you implement scroll restoration properly?**
First, `scrollRestoration = 'manual'`, because native restoration plus your own gives you the worst of both. Then branch on navigation type: on push scroll to top or to a hash anchor; on pop restore; on replace do nothing, because a filter change must not move the user. The naive implementation stores a pixel offset keyed by `location.key` and restores it, which is fine for a stable list and wrong for everything else: if items were deleted, the container resized, or the list is virtualised with only forty rows in the DOM, `1200px` no longer refers to the same content. So the robust version anchors to content identity — record the id of the item at the top of the viewport plus its offset within that item, then on restore fetch backwards until that id is in the data, render it, measure its real position and scroll there. And all of it is pointless without focus management: move focus to the new page heading, restore it on pop, and never remove the focused element.

**Q32. What are parallel and intercepting routes for?**
Parallel routes render independent slots into the same layout — a feed and an authenticated sidebar that load and refresh separately, each with its own boundary, so one slow widget does not block the page. Each slot needs a `default` fallback or soft navigations break. Intercepting routes render one route *in place of* its normal position when the navigation came from a specific origin, which is how you get a photo lightbox that feels instant while the URL is still `/photos/42` — shareable, crawlable, and correct on refresh, with the full page as the hard-navigation fallback. The rules that make it work: close with `back()`, not `push`, so the user's Back path is intact; the underlying slot must not refetch; and there must be a real full-page equivalent, because otherwise you built a URL nobody but your router can render. I would not use interception for a confirm dialog — that is a component and a portal.

**Q33. How do you design URLs?**
Identity in the path, view state in the query, ephemeral UI state nowhere. So `/teams/acme/projects/42/tasks?status=open&sort=-due&page=3`. The URL must decide what the server returns, or a shared link shows page 1. Canonicalise: omit defaults so `/tasks` and `/tasks?page=1` are one URL, use a fixed key order, one spelling per filter forever, and 301 the non-canonical form. Treat URLs as a public API — they appear in emails, tickets, QR codes and print, so they must be readable, short, stable and secret-free. Every URL-derived input belongs in the query cache key, or Back/Forward shows data that does not match the URL. And because every URL you ship is permanent, changing one means adding a redirect map you keep forever.

**Q34. React Router, TanStack Router, Next.js App Router, or Remix?**
The first question is whether I need SSR/SEO/CDN caching at all; if yes, I am in a framework, and if no, I am in a library. Among frameworks, if we are on Next.js I stay unless there is a named, measurable blocker, because migration cost is re-deriving caching semantics, auditing every data access, retraining the team, and re-tuning Core Web Vitals — that cost only pays off for a capability we need and cannot get. Among libraries, TanStack Router wins on type safety: routes, params, and validated search params are inferred end to end, so `navigate({ to: '/invoices/$id', params: { id } })` is a compile error if the route does not exist, and it prefetches on intent. React Router is the choice when I want the smallest, most composable surface and I am rendering my own HTML. Remix is the choice when I want explicit loaders and actions with progressive enhancement and no implicit caching.

**Q35. Tell me about a production incident caused by a state bug.**
The shape of the answer matters more than the story: symptoms, timeline, hypothesis from the timeline, root cause, the systemic fix, and the prevention with a mechanism. For example: a settings page served the wrong plan for ~3% of users; the correlation was a same-day CDN rule; `curl` with two different cookies returned byte-identical HTML; the cause was a publicly cacheable response on an authenticated route; the fix was `private, no-store` plus `Vary: Cookie` and a purge; the prevention was an automated check that requests an authenticated route with two cookies, asserts the bodies differ, and asserts the `Cache-Control` header. The lesson worth stating out loud: I did not fix it with `cache: no-store` sprinkled on one handler; I made the mistake impossible at the layer that produced it and added a test that fails if it returns.

**Q36. Design the data layer for a product with live collaboration.**
I would separate the three layers explicitly. Reads go through a query cache keyed by entity, with cursor pagination and a finite `maxPages`. Real-time transport is a WebSocket for bidirectional presence plus SSE or the same socket for fan-out, and — critically — the stream is only ever an *invalidation signal*, never a direct write to render from, because that keeps the cache the single source of truth. The connection publishes from the broker, not from request handlers, or users on different app instances see different data. Every transport gets a resync: reconnect with exponential backoff and jitter, then `GET /changes?since=lastSeq` to reconcile, because the stream can never be assumed complete. Writes are optimistic with a client id and an idempotency key, version-checked server-side. Finally, the collaborative editing of the actual document is a separate problem — OT or a CRDT with its own conflict policy — and I would scope it as its own project rather than pretending the query layer solves it.

---

# 4.3-4.5 RAPID-FIRE (60)

One line each. If you cannot answer a line in one sentence, that is the topic to study next.

1. **Context vs store:** Context broadcasts to every consumer; a store subscribes per slice — use Context for rare changes, a store for interaction-rate changes.
2. **Context re-render trigger:** a new `value` object identity on every provider render, even with the same contents.
3. **Context split rule:** one provider per update frequency (identity, preferences, data) so a hot change cannot invalidate cold consumers.
4. **`useSyncExternalStore` contract:** `getSnapshot` must be pure and return an `Object.is`-stable value, or React re-renders forever.
5. **Infinite render cause:** a selector returning a fresh object/array literal on every call.
6. **Selector fix:** primitives, or `useShallow`, or a memoised `createSelector` with a complete dependency list.
7. **`createSlice` bonus:** generates action creators, action types and a reducer from one `reducers` object, with Immer inside.
8. **`configureStore` defaults:** auto-batched, DevTools integration, and a middleware chain (thunk → devtools).
9. **`createAsyncThunk` helpers:** `condition` for dedupe, `rejectWithValue` for your own errors, `extraArgument` for injected dependencies.
10. **`createSelector` default:** `memoizeOptions` with an equality function, or `createSelectorCreator`.
11. **`createEntityAdapter`:** normalised `ids` + `entities` plus `selectAll/selectById/selectTotal`, with `sortComparer` for stable order.
12. **`normalizr`:** flattens nested API responses into `{ entities, result }` using `schema` definitions.
13. **Zustand shape:** `create` a hook, keep `set` shallow (`set({ a })`) or `replace` semantics explicit, and read with selectors to subscribe narrowly.
14. **Zustand outside React:** `useStore` with a selector plus `subscribeWithSelector`, which is what makes it usable outside components.
15. **Jotai vs Recoil:** Jotai is the maintained atom library from the same author; Recoil is archived since 2023.
16. **`atomFamily`:** one derived atom per parameter, so `atomFamily(id)` is memoised per id instead of allocating per render.
17. **`useReducer` vs `useState`:** reducer when transitions depend on previous state or have named actions; state for isolated values.
18. **Action shape:** `{ type, payload }` with a namespaced `type` string (`domain/thing`), and never a payload containing a function.
19. **Server state:** owned by the server; the client holds a cache — treat it with a query library, not a store.
20. **Query cache default `staleTime`:** 0, so returning to a view refetches — override it for slowly changing reference data.
21. **`staleTime` vs `gcTime`:** freshness for serving vs retention after the last observer unmounts.
22. **`gcTime: 0` consequence:** no cache between screens, so every navigation shows a skeleton.
23. **`staleTime: Infinity` obligation:** you own every invalidation; one miss is wrong forever.
24. **`placeholderData` vs `initialData`:** `placeholderData` shows data while fetching and is dropped on success; `initialData` is treated as real cache data and is `stale` from the start.
25. **`keepPreviousData`:** renders the prior key's data during a param change, so pagination and filters do not flash a skeleton.
26. **`select`:** transforms server data into view data once per key; must be pure or memoised.
27. **Query key shape:** hierarchical array, prefix first, every response-varying input included, undefined omitted, tenant scoped.
28. **Key factory rule:** one exported factory per entity so nobody hand-writes a divergent literal.
29. **`invalidateQueries`:** marks entries stale so active ones refetch; a mutation should invalidate what it changed, not the world.
30. **`setQueryData`:** writes cache directly — use it for optimistic updates and websocket-driven updates, never as a silent replacement for a fetch.
31. **`cancelQueries`:** the required first step of an optimistic update so no in-flight refetch overwrites the predicted value.
32. **`removeQueries`:** deletes an entry (logout, tenant switch) — use it for cache isolation, not for "refetch".
33. **`persistQueryClient` risk:** hydrates a stale cache at start; needs `buster`, a TTL, and a logout `clear()`.
34. **Cache isolation:** tenant/user in the key *or* `queryClient.clear()` on identity change, or you leak one user's data to another.
35. **Waterfall:** request B blocked on A's render/effect; fix with `Promise.all`, a query layer, a router loader, or intent prefetch.
36. **Waterfall from Suspense:** `await` a cached query is fine, but `await` a never-cached one throws a promise that no component is suspended on, so nothing retries it.
37. **`useSuspenseQuery`:** removes the effect waterfall entirely; you must supply a Suspense boundary and an `errorElement`.
38. **Prefetch vs `staleTime`:** prefetch with `staleTime: 0` to warm; prefetch with `staleTime: Infinity` to also make it serve-without-fetch.
39. **`onMutate` order:** snapshot → `cancelQueries` → apply optimistic value.
40. **`onSettled`:** invalidate after both success and failure; it is the only step that guarantees convergence.
41. **Rollback rule:** restore the deep snapshot *and* invalidate, because the snapshot may already be stale.
42. **Server snapshot:** `getServerSnapshot` supplies the SSR value; it must never return undefined (it makes the store client-only).
43. **TanStack Router vs React Router:** typed routes, params and search params, and built-in loaders/prefetch vs a smaller composable surface.
44. **Route `lazy`:** returns component + loader together, so code and data leave the entry bundle as one unit.
45. **`defaultPreload: 'intent'`:** fetch route code and data on hover/focus, making navigation feel instant.
46. **Hash vs history:** the fragment never reaches the server, so no SSR, no auth redirects, no SEO, no per-route caching.
47. **`popstate`:** fires only on Back/Forward; a `pushState` must update the UI synchronously or navigation appears frozen.
48. **Deep-link 404:** the server must rewrite unknown paths to the shell, excluding real asset extensions.
49. **Guard layers:** edge/middleware → loader/action → data access; the last one is what stops IDOR.
50. **Hiding a button:** UX only; authorisation is enforced server-side per object, since the URL id is attacker-controlled.
51. **`next` param:** must be validated against an allowlist or same-origin check, or it becomes an open redirect.
52. **Scroll restoration branches:** push → top/anchor, pop → restore, replace → do nothing (a filter change must not move the user).
53. **Offset vs cursor:** offsets give page numbers but duplicate/skip rows under concurrent inserts; cursors are stable but have no page numbers.
54. **`maxPages`:** bounds memory in an infinite query by dropping the oldest pages — which means the cursor for "load previous" is gone unless you keep it.
55. **Dedupe rule:** dedupe the flattened merge, not per page, and guard `fetchNextPage` with `isFetchingNextPage`.
56. **Form state:** uncontrolled keeps the input as the source of truth (zero re-renders per keystroke); controlled routes every keystroke through React.
57. **RHF vs Formik:** registry/uncontrolled-first and per-field subscription vs controlled-first with `validateOnChange={false}` as its main lever.
58. **Shared schema:** one Zod module, `z.infer` for types, `safeParse` server-side returning `{ path, message }` mapped onto `setError(path, { type: 'server' })`.
59. **Zod traps:** `z.coerce.boolean("false") === true`, `z.coerce.number("") === 0`, unknown keys stripped by default, and `optional` ≠ `nullable`.
60. **Five async states:** initial loading, refreshing, empty, error, partial — and `isFetching` is not `isPending` once data exists, so a refetch must not clear the screen.

---

# 4.3-4.5 SECTION READINESS CHECKLIST

Per section. A section is ready only when you can tick its own boxes without relying on a neighbouring section's material.

## 4.3 — State Management Architecture

### 4.3.1 The state taxonomy
- [ ] I can name all five categories (local UI, derived, server, URL, global client) and give one real example of each.
- [ ] I can apply the "who else can change it" test to sort a new feature's state into server vs client.
- [ ] I can identify derived state that is being duplicated into the store and delete it.
- [ ] I can state which of my app's URLs would break if the query string were lost.
- [ ] I have a written default for a new project, not an implicit one.

### 4.3.2 Context: right use, wrong use, performance cliff
- [ ] I can explain why a new `value` identity re-renders every consumer even when contents are equal.
- [ ] I can fix it with `useMemo` and decide when to split one provider into several by update frequency.
- [ ] I can list what belongs in Context (identity, theme, locale, i18n, injected clients) and what never does (server data, hot UI state).
- [ ] I can compose two Contexts without re-rendering the union of their consumers.
- [ ] I can diagnose a Context re-render in the Profiler and say `Context changed` vs `Props changed` out loud.

### 4.3.3 Redux Toolkit: store shape, slices, createSlice, createAsyncThunk, RTK Query
- [ ] I can scaffold a typed store and export `RootState`/`AppDispatch` hooks that the whole app uses.
- [ ] I can explain what `createSlice` generates and what Immer buys the reducer.
- [ ] I can write `createAsyncThunk` with `condition`, `rejectWithValue`, and an injected `extraArgument`.
- [ ] I can map an RTK Query endpoint with `providesTags`/`invalidatesTags` and optimistic update args.
- [ ] I can order the middleware chain deliberately and say why devtools goes last.

### 4.3.4 Selectors, reselect, memoisation, normalized state
- [ ] I can use `createSelector` and debug its dependency list; I know what a wrong dependency list does.
- [ ] I can explain the cost of creating a memoised selector inside a component body.
- [ ] I can write `createSelector` with a custom equality function via `createSelectorCreator`.
- [ ] I can use `createEntityAdapter` with `sortComparer` and `selectAll/selectById/selectTotal`.
- [ ] I can flatten a nested API response with `normalizr` schemas.
- [ ] I compute derived values in selectors, not by storing a second copy.

### 4.3.5 Middleware: thunk vs saga vs listener middleware
- [ ] I can give the one-line decision rule for each of the three and a case where I would pick the non-default.
- [ ] I can write a listener middleware effect with `cancelActiveListeners`/`delay`/`takeLatest`.
- [ ] I can justify a saga only for concurrent long-lived processes with cross-cancellation.
- [ ] I know the middleware order and what breaks when it is wrong.
- [ ] I can unit test a thunk with an injected API and no network.

### 4.3.6 Zustand, Jotai, MobX, Recoil: comparison and selection criteria
- [ ] I can compare all four on bundle, DevTools, learning curve, and migration cost from Redux.
- [ ] I can use `useStore` + `subscribeWithSelector` to read Zustand state outside React.
- [ ] I can explain `atomFamily` and when a derived atom beats a store selector.
- [ ] I know Recoil is archived (2023) and can name two Jotai ideas it carried forward.
- [ ] I can explain why shallow-equality subscriptions are load-bearing in every one of these libraries.

### 4.3.7 XState and state machines
- [ ] I can model a submit flow as states plus guarded transitions and test an illegal transition.
- [ ] I can show how five booleans produce 32 combinations and which are reachable.
- [ ] I can tie cleanup to a state's exit and cancel a stale request on transition.
- [ ] I can justify an FSM for one flow and argue against it for a preferences object.
- [ ] I know the testing story: pure reducer-like machine logic, no rendering required.

### 4.3.8 URL as state, localStorage persistence, hydration-safe storage
- [ ] I can decide what goes in the URL (filters, sort, page, tab, selected entity) and what never does (secrets, server data).
- [ ] I can choose `push` vs `replace` per interaction and explain the Back-button consequence.
- [ ] I can canonicalise URLs: omit defaults, one spelling per filter, one key order.
- [ ] I can persist safely: versioned key, schema-validated read, hydration gate, logout `clear()`.
- [ ] I can sync across tabs with the `storage` event and handle write conflicts.
- [ ] I never store tokens in `localStorage`, and can say why (XSS reach, no scoping, no revocation).

### 4.3.9 Optimistic updates and rollback
- [ ] I can run the five steps: snapshot → cancel → apply → success → rollback+invalidate.
- [ ] I can explain why the snapshot must be deep and why rollback without invalidation is wrong.
- [ ] I can reconcile a create by client-generated id so a retry cannot duplicate.
- [ ] I can serialise concurrent writes to one entity or use an `If-Match` version.
- [ ] I can name a case where I would *not* be optimistic (payments, irreversible side effects).

### 4.3.10 The state-management decision flowchart
- [ ] I can state the decision order out loud, starting with "is this server state?".
- [ ] I can apply the flowchart to three unfamiliar features and justify each terminal answer.
- [ ] I know the exit criteria for each layer and what "too many layers" looks like.
- [ ] I can defend my current project's default choice in 30 seconds.

## 4.4 — Data Fetching, Caching, and Form Handling

### 4.4.1 The data-fetching model: loaders vs effects, and the waterfall problem
- [ ] I can explain why effects serialise dependent requests and how loaders/`useSuspenseQuery` do not.
- [ ] I can fix a waterfall with `Promise.all`/`allSettled`, a query layer, a loader, or intent prefetch.
- [ ] I can implement abort with `AbortController` so a superseded request cannot resolve later.
- [ ] I can implement one retry with exponential backoff and jitter, for idempotent requests only.
- [ ] I can add correlation ids and structure errors as `{ status, code, fieldErrors }`.

### 4.4.2 TanStack Query: queryKey, cache lifecycle, staleTime vs gcTime
- [ ] I can design keys with an exported factory per entity, including every response-varying input.
- [ ] I can explain `staleTime` vs `gcTime` and set both deliberately for a resource.
- [ ] I can use `placeholderData`/`keepPreviousData` so a param change does not flash a skeleton.
- [ ] I can implement a stale-while-revalidate cache and a bounded LRU+TTL cache by hand.
- [ ] I know `placeholderData` vs `initialData` vs `initialDataUpdatedAt` and what each does to staleness.

### 4.4.3 SWR and the compare-and-swap revalidation model
- [ ] I can explain SWR's cache-first-then-revalidate model and `dedupingInterval`.
- [ ] I can use `mutate` with `optimisticData`, `rollbackOnError`, `populateCache`, and `revalidate`.
- [ ] I can implement compare-and-swap mutation concurrency (ignore a response older than the last applied) and say why it matters under rapid typing.
- [ ] I can implement `useSWRInfinite` with `getKey` and a cursor `persistSize`.
- [ ] I know when SWR's smaller surface is the right choice and when I need TanStack Query's mutations and cache API.

### 4.4.4 Mutations, invalidation, optimistic updates, and rollback
- [ ] I can run the five-step optimistic flow in both TanStack Query and RTK Query.
- [ ] I can choose precise vs broad invalidation and defend the choice per mutation.
- [ ] I can invalidate on *both* success and failure (`onSettled`) so the cache converges.
- [ ] I can handle idempotency keys and double submission with `isPending`.
- [ ] I can implement an undo affordance by snapshotting before a destructive write.

### 4.4.5 Pagination: offset vs cursor, infinite queries, bidirectional scroll
- [ ] I can explain the offset duplicate/skip failure and fix it with a unique tiebreaker.
- [ ] I can implement cursor pagination with a composite-index-friendly sort.
- [ ] I can configure `maxPages`, dedupe the merged list, and guard `fetchNextPage` with `isFetchingNextPage`.
- [ ] I can restore scroll by content anchor (id + offset) rather than pixel offset.
- [ ] I can provide a "Load more" fallback and announce newly loaded items to screen readers.

### 4.4.6 Polling vs WebSockets vs SSE for live data
- [ ] I can decide "no live data at all" for most screens and justify invalidation-on-focus instead.
- [ ] I can configure `refetchInterval` with hidden-tab pause and error back-off.
- [ ] I can pick SSE for one-way seconds-scale data and state the proxy requirement (`proxy_buffering off`).
- [ ] I can implement WebSocket reconnect with exponential backoff, jitter, heartbeat, and a resync (`?since=seq`).
- [ ] I treat every stream message as an invalidation signal, not a direct render write.

### 4.4.7 React Hook Form and Formik: uncontrolled-first philosophy
- [ ] I can explain why uncontrolled inputs remove a render per keystroke and when controlled is still correct.
- [ ] I can wire RHF: `register`, `handleSubmit`, `formState` (`isDirty`/`isSubmitting`), `setError`, `watch`/`useWatch`.
- [ ] I can map a server 422 onto field errors with `criteriaMode: 'all'`.
- [ ] I know Formik's controlled model, `validateOnChange={false}`, and `ValidationError`.
- [ ] I keep native attributes (`required`, `type`, `inputMode`, `autoComplete`) and handle browser validation explicitly.

### 4.4.8 Schema validation with Zod and Yup, shared client/server schemas
- [ ] I can write one schema module, `z.infer` the type, and use it as an RHF resolver *and* in the server handler.
- [ ] I can call `safeParse` server-side and return `{ path, message }` field errors with a 422.
- [ ] I can state the coercion traps: `z.coerce.boolean("false") === true`, `z.coerce.number("") === 0`.
- [ ] I choose `.strict()` vs strip deliberately, and I never confuse `optional` with `nullable`.
- [ ] I keep shared *shape* on the client and *policy* (rate limits, permissions, uniqueness) on the server.

### 4.4.9 File upload UX: progress, previews, multipart, presigned URLs
- [ ] I can implement a real progress bar with `XMLHttpRequest.upload.onprogress` and say why Fetch has none.
- [ ] I can do a presigned direct-to-storage `PUT` and explain how it removes the 60 s proxy timeout.
- [ ] I can pin content type and length in the presign, then verify server-side and quarantine before serving.
- [ ] I can implement multipart with per-part retry and resumption from a persisted `uploadId`.
- [ ] I validate size, declared type *and* magic bytes, and can explain why `File.type` is attacker-controlled.

### 4.4.10 Loading, empty, and error states as a first-class design problem
- [ ] I render all five states: initial loading, refreshing, empty, error, partial.
- [ ] I use `isFetching` vs `isPending` so a refetch never clears existing data.
- [ ] I distinguish first-run empty from filtered empty and never render empty on error.
- [ ] I delay the loading indicator ~150 ms and make skeletons match the final layout.
- [ ] I show stale data with a notice when a *refetch* fails, instead of blanking the screen.

### 4.4.11 Cache invalidation strategies and the stale-write problem
- [ ] I can name the four stale-write shapes: write-write race, rollback staleness, missed dependents, cache-key blind spots.
- [ ] I can serialise writes per entity or use version/`If-Match` checks.
- [ ] I can enumerate dependents for one of my own mutations and unit-test that enumeration.
- [ ] I know `invalidateQueries` vs `resetQueries` vs `removeQueries` vs `setQueryData`.
- [ ] I enforce cache isolation on identity/tenant change — a key-scoped cache *or* `queryClient.clear()` — and can prove it with a test.

## 4.5 — Routing Strategies and Implementation

### 4.5.1 Client-Side Routing Fundamentals
- [ ] I can explain what `pushState`, `replaceState`, and `popstate` do and when each fires.
- [ ] I can implement a minimal router with a route table, params, and link interception.
- [ ] I know the constraints: same-origin, URL length, cloneable state object.
- [ ] I can configure the server to rewrite deep links to the shell, excluding asset paths.

### 4.5.2 Route Configuration and Dynamic Segments
- [ ] I can define nested routes, layouts without a URL segment, index routes, and pathless layouts.
- [ ] I can parse/validate params and search params and derive them from the type.
- [ ] I can redirect legacy paths permanently and keep a redirect map.
- [ ] I can build a canonical URL policy: omit defaults, fix key order, one spelling per filter.

### 4.5.3 Nested Routes and Layout Composition
- [ ] I can share a layout across children so the layout does not remount on child navigation.
- [ ] I can place `Outlet`/children correctly and keep scroll and focus per navigation.
- [ ] I can nest loaders so a parent provides data to children without refetching.
- [ ] I can explain when a layout should load data vs when a child should.

### 4.5.4 Route Guards and Access Control
- [ ] I can implement the three layers: edge/middleware, loader/action, and data access.
- [ ] I can write a guard that preserves `next` safely (allowlisted, same-origin).
- [ ] I can write the negative test: user A requests user B's resource and gets 403/404.
- [ ] I know a hidden button is UX only and that the URL id is attacker-controlled.

### 4.5.5 Code Splitting for Routes
- [ ] I can lazy-load route components and their loaders as one unit.
- [ ] I can prefetch on intent and choose what must never be split (shell, above-the-fold, hydration).
