- [ ] I can handle `ChunkLoadError` with a guarded one-time reload.
- [ ] I can keep old assets for a grace period after a deploy.

### 4.5.6 Scroll Restoration and Navigation State
- [ ] I set `scrollRestoration = 'manual'` and branch on push/pop/replace.
- [ ] I can restore by content anchor (id + offset) rather than a raw pixel offset.
- [ ] I move focus to the new heading on push and restore it on pop.
- [ ] I keep a non-scrolling "Load more" path and an anchor for restoration.

### 4.5.7 Route Transitions and Loading UX
- [ ] I can show a pending state on the *pending* navigation without a full-page fallback.
- [ ] I can keep stale content visible while a loader runs (deferred/streaming, `keepPreviousData`).
- [ ] I can avoid a spinner flash on fast navigations with a delay and a minimum display time.
- [ ] I can distinguish a navigation timeout from a data error and give each its own boundary.

### 4.5.8 Route-Based Code Organization
- [ ] I organise by feature/route, not by file type, so a feature's loader, component and tests sit together.
- [ ] I keep route config declarative and typed, and derive param/search types from it.
- [ ] I can audit bundle boundaries per route and attribute a dependency to the right chunk.
- [ ] I can add a new route without touching shared routing infrastructure.

### 4.5.9 Error Boundaries and Route Error Handling
- [ ] I can place error boundaries per route and per lazy chunk so one failure is contained.
- [ ] I can distinguish 403/404/500 with distinct UI and correct HTTP status.
- [ ] I can log route errors with a correlation id and enough context to reproduce.
- [ ] I can implement a root boundary that resets on navigation and does not loop.

### 4.5.10 SSR, SSG, and Hybrid Rendering Strategies
- [ ] I can define SSR, SSG, ISR, and streaming and the caching/SEO/freshness trade-off of each.
- [ ] I can implement streaming with Suspense and know what the shell must contain.
- [ ] I can avoid the hydration-mismatch class of bugs (theme, viewport, `Date.now()`, random ids).
- [ ] I can explain how server rendering changes the data layer (loader data, no client fetch waterfall) and how to cache it safely.

# PHASE 4, PART 3: Frontend Architecture, Ecosystem, and System Design

Phase 4 Part 1 was React mechanics; Part 2 was React patterns and ecosystem. This part zooms out: how a frontend codebase is *organised*, how it is *shipped consistently across teams*, how it is *observed* and *controlled* in production, how it is *kept safe*, and how you *design* the user-facing systems an interviewer will hand you.

The through-line of this part: **frontend architecture is mostly about making change cheap and failure visible.** Every technique below either reduces the cost of the next change (design systems, structure, modularity) or increases the speed at which you notice the last change broke something (error monitoring, flags, analytics, tracing).

---

# 4.6.1 Design systems (🔴)

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.1, 4.2

**Definition:** A design system is a versioned, cross-product collection of decisions — tokens, primitives, components, patterns, documentation — plus the governance that keeps those decisions consistent. Canonical entry for design principles and visual language: PHASE 1 → Communication; here we talk engineering.

**What a design system is**
- A **single source of truth** for visual and interaction decisions, encoded as code where possible (tokens, components, lints).
- A **contract** with a versioning policy: consumers declare which major version they are on and get a migration path.
- A **product of an organisation**: it exists because five teams were solving the same modal focus-trap bug five different ways.
- An **accessibility enforcement point**: primitives are where keyboard and screen-reader behaviour is proved once.

**What a design system is NOT**
- Not a Figma file with a colour palette. Designers' files are an input, not the deliverable — code is where behaviour lives.
- Not a "component library with nice buttons". It includes *patterns* (how components combine to accomplish a task), *content guidelines* (empty states, error copy), and *governance*.
- Not a monolith of 200 exported components. A system that nobody can navigate is a system nobody adopts.
- Not a marketing term for "we use Tailwind". Tailwind is a styling mechanism; a design system is the decisions plus the contract.

**The anatomy: token → primitive → component → pattern → page**

```
┌─ Token          --color-brand-600 = oklch(0.55 0.17 258)      "a decision with no opinions about use"
│   └─ Primitive  --blue-500 = <value>                            "a raw palette"
│       └─ Component   <Button variant="primary">                "a single UI element, one job"
│           └─ Pattern  <Form> = <Field> + <Input> + <ErrorText> + <Actions>  "a task the user wants to do"
│               └─ Page  Checkout = <AppHeader/> + <Stepper/> + <Form/> + <Summary/>  "a route"
```
Rule of thumb that interviewers like: **a primitive has no knowledge of your product; a pattern knows what a task is; a page knows your domain.** Leaking upward knowledge is how design systems rot — the moment `Button` learns about "invoice status", it is no longer reusable.

**Design tokens**

Three tiers, and the distinction is the whole game:

| Tier | Example | Stability | Consumed by |
|---|---|---|---|
| Primitive / base | `--blue-500`, `--space-4`, `--font-size-14` | Changes rarely; a palette shift breaks many things | primitives only |
| Semantic / alias | `--color-action-bg`, `--color-text-muted`, `--color-border-focus` | Changes when the design language changes | components, product code |
| Component | `--button-padding-inline`, `--dialog-max-width` | Local to one component | that component's CSS |

The rule: **product code may only reference semantic tokens, never primitives.** If a feature team writes `background: var(--blue-500)` they have bypassed the theming layer, and their screen will be wrong in dark mode or for the second brand. This is enforceable — a stylelint rule or a CI grep is the only way it actually holds:

```
// stylelint.config.mjs — enforce the tier boundary mechanically
{
  "rules": {
    "declaration-property-value-disallowed-list": {
      "/^background(\\-color)?$|^color$|^border(\\-.*)?-color$|^box-shadow$/": [
        { "message": "Use semantic tokens (--color-*) in product code, not primitives" }
      ]
    }
  }
}
```
Plus a check that no source file (outside `tokens/`) contains a hex literal or an `oklch(` call:
```js
// scripts/check-tokens.mjs — run in CI
import { readdir, readFile } from 'node:fs/promises';
const SKIP = new Set(['node_modules', 'dist', 'build', 'coverage']);
const HEX = /#[0-9a-f]{3,8}\b/i, FN = /\b(oklch|oklab|color-mix|rgb|hsl)\(/;
let bad = 0;
async function walk(dir) {
  for (const e of await readdir(dir, { withFileTypes: true })) {
    if (SKIP.has(e.name) || e.name === 'tokens') continue;
    const p = `${dir}/${e.name}`;
    if (e.isDirectory()) { await walk(p); continue; }
    if (!/\.(css|scss|tsx|ts)$/.test(e.name)) continue;
    const src = await readFile(p, 'utf8');
    if (HEX.test(src) || FN.test(src)) {
      console.error(`raw color in ${p}`); bad++;
    }
  }
}
await walk('src');
process.exit(bad ? 1 : 0);
```

**Token naming.** Use a flat, dot-free `--group-role-modifier` scheme so tooling can diff it: `--color-bg-surface`, `--color-bg-surface-hover`, `--color-fg-default`, `--color-border-default`, `--space-3`. Modifiers stack (`--color-fg-default-muted`). Avoid encoding the value (`--color-greyish-blue`) because the value changes and the name lies.

**One source of truth and the pipeline.** Tokens are authored once (JSON or DTCG-style JSON), then *compiled* per platform:

```
tokens.json (DTCG format)
   ├─→ CSS custom properties     (web)
   ├─→ TS/JS object              (canvas, native, email)
   ├─→ iOS/Android resources
   ├─→ Figma variables           (via a plugin)
   ├─→ SVG sprite / icon font
   └─→ docs table (for humans)
```
A build step that emits all of them from one file is what makes "one source of truth" real. Interview framing: *tokens are the only place where a raw value is allowed to exist; everything else is a reference.*

**Theming with CSS custom properties**

Custom properties beat preprocessor variables (`$brand`) because they cascade, can be changed at runtime without a rebuild, and can be scoped to a subtree.

```css
/* tokens/light.css — the default palette */
:root {
  color-scheme: light;
  --color-bg-canvas:      oklch(99% 0.004 250);
  --color-bg-surface:     oklch(100% 0 0);
  --color-bg-surface-alt: oklch(97.5% 0.006 250);
  --color-fg-default:     oklch(22% 0.02 260);
  --color-fg-muted:       oklch(48% 0.02 260);
  --color-border-default: oklch(90% 0.01 260);
  --color-action-bg:      oklch(55% 0.17 258);
  --color-action-fg:      oklch(99% 0.01 258);
  --color-danger-bg:      oklch(55% 0.19 27);
  --radius-md: 8px;
  --shadow-1: 0 1px 2px oklch(0% 0 0 / 0.08), 0 1px 3px oklch(0% 0 0 / 0.06);
  --ring-focus: 0 0 0 2px var(--color-bg-canvas), 0 0 0 4px var(--color-action-bg);
}
/* dark: only override the semantic layer */
[data-theme="dark"] {
  color-scheme: dark;
  --color-bg-canvas:      oklch(18% 0.015 260);
  --color-bg-surface:     oklch(23% 0.018 260);
  --color-bg-surface-alt: oklch(27% 0.02 260);
  --color-fg-default:     oklch(96% 0.005 260);
  --color-fg-muted:       oklch(72% 0.02 260);
  --color-border-default: oklch(36% 0.02 260);
  /* action bg is LIGHTER in dark mode — brand contrast flips direction */
  --color-action-bg:      oklch(70% 0.15 258);
  --color-action-fg:      oklch(20% 0.03 258);
  --shadow-1: 0 1px 2px oklch(0% 0 0 / 0.5);
}
```
Component CSS consumes **only** the semantic layer:
```css
.button {
  background: var(--color-action-bg);
  color: var(--color-action-fg);
  border: 1px solid transparent;
  border-radius: var(--radius-md);
  padding: var(--space-2) var(--space-4);
}
.button:focus-visible { outline: none; box-shadow: var(--ring-focus); }
```

**Runtime switching and multi-brand.** Because custom properties inherit, a theme is just a scope:
```js
function applyTheme(theme) {
  document.documentElement.dataset.theme = theme;   // triggers [data-theme=dark] rules
  document.documentElement.style.colorScheme = theme;
  localStorage.setItem('theme', theme);            // re-applied before paint in 4.6.10/4.5 SSR notes
}
function applyBrand(brandId) {
  // Per-brand overrides live in a stylesheet loaded once; only the primitives differ.
  for (const [k, v] of Object.entries(brandTokens[brandId])) {
    document.documentElement.style.setProperty(k, v);
  }
}
```
Multi-brand rule of thumb: **brands override the primitive palette only**; the semantic layer and every component stay identical. If a brand needs a unique `--color-action-bg`, that is a theming bug, not a feature.

**`color-mix()` and modern colour spaces.** `oklch()` is perceptual-lightness: `oklch(L C H)` where L is 0–1 perceptual lightness, C is chroma, H is hue. Its practical wins:
- **Contrast is predictable**: equal `L` reads as equal lightness, so you can generate a passing ramp automatically.
- **Tints/shades are one-liners** without a colour-mixing library.
- `color-mix(in oklab, var(--brand) 20%, transparent)` gives translucent variants that stay perceptually even (no grey mud in sRGB).

```css
.card {
  background: color-mix(in oklab, var(--color-action-bg) 8%, var(--color-bg-surface));
  border: 1px solid color-mix(in oklab, var(--color-border-default) 70%, transparent);
}
/* Transparent in sRGB darkens; in oklab it keeps hue: this is why you mix in a perceptual space */
.scrim { background: color-mix(in oklab, black 45%, transparent); }
```
Caveats to state in an interview: `oklch()` needs a fallback declaration for older browsers (`background: #1a73e8; background: oklch(55% .17 258);`), and hue interpolation can be non-intuitive (`in oklch shortest hue` vs `longer`).

**Contrast in light and dark is not symmetric.** Pure-white on pure-black fails WCAG AA for body text (1:1 vs the required 4.5:1... in practice you need ~4.5:1, and white-on-black is 21:1, so the *dark* end is easier; the hard cases are mid-greys and saturated brand colours). The failures that show up in audits:
- Muted text at `#999` on white (2.8:1) — fails.
- Placeholder text that is lighter than the border.
- Disabled controls (exempt from contrast minimums, but still must be perceivable).
- Focus rings that only appear in one mode.
Test with computed styles in both themes; a design that passes in light can fail in dark because you lightened the foreground and darkened the surface simultaneously.

**Component API design**

The API is the design system's most-used surface, so design it like a public interface (Phase 11 → SOLID/adapters applies).

| Decision | Rule | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Controlled vs uncontrolled | Support **both**: pass `value`+`onChange` for controlled use; if `value` is undefined, keep internal state and expose the default | Enables both simple usage and form-library integration |
| Composition over configuration | Prefer `children`/slots + context over new boolean props | Boolean props multiply: 6 booleans → 64 states, 15% of which you never test |
| Boolean-prop explosion | If you have `primary secondary danger`, replace with `variant: 'primary' \| 'secondary' \| 'danger'`; never accept `primary` **and** `color` | Contradictory states become unrepresentable |
| Escape hatch | Always provide `className`/`style` (styled systems) or `data-*`+CSS vars (headless) | You cannot predict every consumer; if they can't escape, they fork your component |
| Polymorphic `as` | Let consumers swap the rendered element *and* retype props | `<Button as="a" href>` vs `<Button as={Link} to>` — one component, correct semantics |
| Ref forwarding | Forward refs to the **DOM node**, not your wrapper | Refs are the escape hatch; a ref that points at your abstraction breaks `focus()`, measurement, and third-party libs |
| Data attributes for styling | Expose `data-state="open"`, `data-disabled`, `data-side="top"` | Styling hooks that are stable, inspectable in devtools, and don't collide with utility-class specificity wars |
| Compound components | `Dialog.Root / .Trigger / .Content / .Title` with implicit shared context | Multi-part widgets (dialog, tabs, menu, combobox) read better and are individually replaceable |
| Constrained unions for content | `icon?: ReactNode` — not `icon?: string \| ReactNode \| IconComponent` | Union sprawl returns as runtime bugs |

```tsx
// A well-shaped headless primitive: composition, polymorphism, ref, data attrs, escape hatch
type ButtonOwnProps = {
  variant?: 'primary' | 'secondary' | 'danger';
  size?: 'sm' | 'md' | 'lg';
  loading?: boolean;                 // loading is state, not a variant
  iconStart?: ReactNode;             // nodes, never strings or component refs
};
type ButtonProps<T extends React.ElementType> =
  ButtonOwnProps & {
    as?: T;
    children?: ReactNode;
    className?: string;
  } & Omit<React.ComponentPropsWithoutRef<T>, keyof ButtonOwnProps | 'as' | 'className'>;

function Button<T extends React.ElementType = 'button'>({
  as, variant = 'primary', size = 'md', loading = false,
  iconStart, children, className, disabled, ...rest
}: ButtonProps<T>) {
  const Tag = as ?? 'button';
  return (
    <Tag
      {...rest}
      disabled={Tag === 'button' ? disabled || loading : undefined}
      aria-busy={loading || undefined}
      data-variant={variant} data-size={size} data-loading={loading || undefined}
      className={cx('btn', className)}
    >
      {loading && <Spinner aria-hidden />}
      {iconStart}
      {children}
    </Tag>
  );
}
```
Notes you should say out loud: `disabled` alone removes the element from the tab order, so a `disabled` primary submit often needs `aria-disabled` + a focusable wrapper so screen-reader users can discover why it's unavailable; `loading` must set `aria-busy` and keep the label width stable to avoid CLS.

**Accessibility is baked into primitives, not added later**
- Every interactive primitive supports **keyboard** operation with the documented pattern (Phase 3.7): tabs with arrow keys and roving tabindex, menus with type-ahead, dialogs with focus trap + restore, combobox with `aria-activedescendant`.
- **Focus visible** on every focusable state, in every theme, with a token (`--ring-focus`) so it is consistent.
- **Names**: icons-only controls need `aria-label`; the primitive should refuse an accessible-name-less icon button at build time via `tsd`/`expect-type` or a lint rule.
- **Motion**: respect `prefers-reduced-motion` inside the primitive so every consumer inherits it.
- Automated a11y tests run on primitives as unit tests (fast, always), plus manual AT testing per release. Canonical detail: PHASE 3.7.

```tsx
// The primitive owns reduced motion — consumers get it for free
const reduce = usePrefersReducedMotion();
<Transition duration={reduce ? 0 : 200}>{children}</Transition>
```

**Versioning, deprecation, migration**
- **Semantic versioning applies to the public API surface.** A visual tweak is a patch; a new optional prop is a minor; removing a prop, renaming a token, changing a prop's type, or changing focus/keyboard behaviour is a **breaking** change → major.
- Ship **codemods** for mechanical migrations (renamed props, restructured tokens) using jscodeshift or ts-morph. Hand-written migration guides rot; codemods do not.
- Emit **runtime deprecation warnings in dev only** — a console warning with the replacement and a link-free migration note:
```tsx
if (process.env.NODE_ENV !== 'production' && (legacyProps as any).onClickItem) {
  console.warn('[ui] <Button onClickItem> is deprecated — use onClick on the item slot of <ButtonList>.');
}
```
- Support **long-lived majors in parallel** (`@acme/ui@4` and `@acme/ui@5`) with a shared codemod path, because a design system that only offers "upgrade everything now" gets abandoned by the teams that can't.
- **Deprecation policy**: announce → codemod → warn → measure adoption → remove only when usage hits zero. Track usage with a build-time usage report: scan consumer lockfiles for the package version, or emit an opt-in usage beacon. Removing something still in use is the fastest way to lose trust.

**Adoption: monorepo vs multiple repos**

| Dimension | Monorepo (packages/ui) | Multi-repo |
|---|---|---|
| Change propagation | PR touches primitives + consumers; CI builds all | N PRs, N merges, version bumps per repo |
| Contributor onboarding | One clone, one install | Clone the repo you need |
| Release | Coordinated, can auto-publish from CI | Manual, cross-repo version hell |
| Tooling cost | Needs remote caching, task graphs, path filters (Phase 3.8) | Simple CI per repo |
| Access control | Coarse by default | Fine-grained possible |
| Isolation | Apps can import each other's internals accidentally | Enforced by the network/package boundary |

Practical middle ground used widely: **monorepo for authoring + published packages with restricted exports**, plus `CODEOWNERS` so the design-system team must approve changes to `packages/ui`, and changesets for version bumps and changelogs. In a multi-repo world you must also solve the "how does an app get the new fix" problem: a Renovate/Dependabot-style automated bump PR is the minimum viable answer.

**Documentation and contribution**
- Docs must be **executable**: usage examples are the same stories Storybook renders (cross-ref 4.6.3). Prose-only docs rot within two releases.
- Document the **decision**, not just the usage: why this spacing scale, why `variant` and not `color`, which patterns are stable vs experimental.
- **Contribution model**: RFC template for new components/patterns (proposal → a11y plan → token usage → perf cost → adoption evidence), design review with the design-systems group, and an explicit "we said no and here is why" record. Rotating maintainers prevents a single person becoming a bottleneck and becoming a political target for feature requests.
- Publish a **component status** (stable / beta / experimental / deprecated) so consumers know what they may depend on. This is the same contract idea as semver, applied to maturity.

**Measuring adoption and quality**

| Metric | How | Target intuition |
|---|---|---|
| Coverage | % of UI rendered by system primitives (instrument via `data-ui` attributes + RUM sample, or codemod inventory) | Trend up; report per surface |
| Inconsistency count | Grep for raw colors, hard-coded paddings, one-off modals | Should be monotonically decreasing |
| Accessibility | axe violations per primitive run in CI; manual AT audit per release | Zero known criticals |
| Bundle impact | Bundle diff per release (`size-limit` / bundle analyzer in CI, Phase 3.8) | Guardrail, e.g. ≤ +2 kB per release |
| Adoption of changes | How long until a deprecation reaches zero usage | The metric that predicts whether deprecation works |
| Escape-hatch usage | Count of `className` overrides in consumer code | High = the API is missing something |
| PR friction | Time-to-merge for system changes; # of consumers blocked | Leading indicator of governance health |

**Cost, and when not to build one**
Real cost: a design system is 1–3 engineers' ongoing time for a medium system, plus a designer, plus governance overhead. The failure mode is a library nobody adopts. Signals you should **not** build yet:
- One product, fewer than ~3 teams, all shipping from the same component set.
- Product velocity is the top constraint; the UI is changing weekly by design.
- Your components would be ~10 primitives and no patterns.
Instead: use a well-maintained third-party primitive set (4.6.2), add a thin `ui/` layer in your repo to hold tokens and the few wrappers you actually need, and revisit when the third team appears. The trigger for a real system is **cost, not aesthetics**: the third time two teams independently build the same thing.

**Questions**
1. What is a design system, and what is explicitly not one?
2. Explain the token → primitive → component → pattern → page anatomy with an example.
3. Primitive vs semantic vs component tokens: why does the distinction matter operationally?
4. How do you enforce "product code never touches primitive tokens"?
5. How do you theme with CSS custom properties? Why not Sass variables?
6. Multi-brand and dark mode at the same time — where do the overrides live?
7. What does `oklch()` give you over hex, and what are the caveats?
8. Why is contrast not symmetric between light and dark themes? Give failing examples.
9. Controlled vs uncontrolled components: why support both?
10. How do you prevent boolean-prop explosion? Show the API you would design.
11. Escape hatches: what do you expose and why is removing them harmful?
12. Polymorphic `as`: what problems does it solve and what does it cost?
13. Where do data attributes fit in a component API?
14. When do you use compound components over slots?
15. How is accessibility a build-time concern rather than a QA concern?
16. Your breaking change is a renamed prop: describe the deprecation process end to end.
17. What goes in a codemod, and what does not?
18. Monorepo vs multi-repo for a design system: what breaks in each?
19. How do you measure whether the design system is working?
20. When is a design system not worth building? Give concrete triggers.

**Model answer, Q16:** "Context: we renamed `Button`'s `type` prop to `intent` because `type` collided with the native attribute. Decision: semver-major, codemod plus a dev-only runtime warning. Reasoning: `type` was load-bearing in roughly 400 call sites, and a silent break at runtime for consumers whose props are spread is worse than a type error. So: publish `v5` alongside `v4`, ship a jscodeshift codemod that rewrites `type=` to `intent=` on our component imports only, emit a dev console warning for the legacy prop for one minor cycle, and run a build-time usage scan to report which repos still pass it. Trade-offs: the parallel-major approach costs double maintenance for us and slows the v5 rollout; the alternative — a hard cut — is faster for us and much riskier for consumers, and this component is inside a checkout flow. Failure scenario: a consumer spreads props from their own API object, so the prop name is dynamic; our codemod misses it and it breaks in production, where the dev warning does not fire — which is exactly why the usage scan and an integration test on the real consumer are both required. Alternative: keep `type` as a deprecated alias for one major, deprecate rather than remove, and delete it only when measured usage is zero."

**Mistakes:** calling Figma the design system; letting product code reference primitives; assuming `dark:` class variants scale past a handful of components; adding props instead of slots; deprecating without a codemod or a migration deadline; treating accessibility as a checklist at the end; versioning by "how big the diff looks" instead of by API compatibility; measuring adoption by "did we write it" instead of "is it used".

**Mini task:** build a token pipeline: author 40 tokens in JSON, emit CSS custom properties + a TS object + a markdown table; define a light and a dark theme; write the tier-boundary lint; then implement `Button`, `Input`, and `Dialog` consuming only semantic tokens, with axe passing and a `--brand` switch at runtime with no rebuild. Measure the CSS output size for one theme and justify whether you shipped both or generated one at build time.

---

# 4.6.2 Component libraries: shadcn/ui, Radix, MUI, Ant Design, Chakra

🔴 Advanced · **Nice-to-have** (asked in "how do you build at scale" and library-comparison rounds) · Prerequisites: 4.6.1

**The landscape.** These tools answer different questions, and mixing them up in an interview answer is the fastest way to sound uninformed.

| Library | Distribution model | Styling | A11y baseline | Bundle | Customisation cost | Theming | Maintenance burden on you |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Radix UI** (headless primitives) | npm package, unstyled behaviour | You bring CSS / utility classes | Excellent (focus, keyboard, ARIA are the product) | Small per-primitive, tree-shakes | Low–medium: you own the CSS | Via your own CSS vars | Low: you own styles, they own behaviour |
| **React Aria** (Adobe) | npm, headless + hooks | Tailwind via plugin, or your CSS | Strongest semantics, plus internationalisation and keyboard handling | Medium (per-component hooks) | Low with Tailwind; higher with bespoke CSS | Theme object + CSS vars | Low–medium |
| **Ark UI** (Chakra/Zag) | npm, headless state machines | You style | Good (Zag state machines, focus management) | Small–medium | Low | CSS vars | Low |
| **MUI** | npm, styled, opinionated | `sx`, emotion, theme object | Good but historically noisy (`Menu`, `Tooltip` focus bugs) | Medium–large (many barrel-import pitfalls) | Medium: `sx`, theme overrides, `styled()` | Theme object with `palette`, `typography`, spacing (4px default) | Medium: emotion caching, theme overrides, SSR setup |
| **Ant Design** | npm, styled, very opinionated | Less tokens; CSS-in-JS + token file | Good, locale-complete | Largest of the set; heavy global CSS-in-JS | High if you fight it, low if you adopt it | `ConfigProvider` + Less token file | Low if you follow it, high if you customise |
| **Chakra UI** | npm, styled | Props-first, emotion | Good | Medium | Medium | Semantic tokens, `defineConfig` | Medium (provider setup, v2→v3 migration) |
| **shadcn/ui** | **Code copied into your repo** (CLI-scaffolded, you own the files) | Tailwind + `cn()`, CSS vars | Radix behaviour underneath | Only what you import | Low in the file, but every change is *your* change | CSS vars in a theme file | **High** — you now own accessibility fixes |
| **Headless UI** | npm, headless | You style | Good | Tiny | Low | Your CSS | Low |

**Headless vs styled.** The honest summary: *headless* gives you behaviour (keyboard, focus, ARIA, positioning) and lets you own pixels, at the cost of writing CSS and handling visual states yourself. *Styled* gives you behaviour + pixels + a default look that looks finished on day one, at the cost of learning a theme/escape system and paying bundle and opinionation costs. Neither "wins"; the question is whether your product needs to look like the library.

**Why "copy the source into your repo" changed the ecosystem.** Before, a component library's release cycle was your release cycle: a fix waited for an upstream release, a bump, a QA pass, and a deploy; you could not patch a single component; your customisations piled up as `!important` overrides. shadcn/ui inverted this: the CLI copies real component source into `components/ui/`, so *you* own the file, edit it freely, and only take upstream changes by re-running the CLI and reviewing the diff. The consequences, which are excellent interview material:
- **Customisation cost drops to near zero**, because customisation is a diff you control.
- **No runtime abstraction**: fewer layers between your code and the DOM, better tree-shaking, no version conflicts.
- **You inherit responsibility**: accessibility fixes, React 19 compatibility, security patches, dependency updates are now *your* backlog. The MIT license makes this legal; the engineering cost is real.
- **The trade-off that people gloss over**: with 60 copied components you now have 60 files to keep in sync with React and Radix. Mitigations: keep the copied set small, keep a `components/ui/` convention, and re-run the CLI deliberately during dependency upgrades rather than pretending files are a package.

**License and maintenance realities.** Check the actual license of whichever you name (MUI, Radix, Ant Design, and shadcn's copied files are all permissively licensed in different ways; some enterprise-adjacent libraries are not). The interview-relevant point is: *a permissively licensed dependency is a business decision you inherit when someone leaves.* Ask: who patches this when the upstream project is archived, and does the license allow modification and redistribution of the modified files? For copied-source libraries, modification is the whole model — read the license before you adopt that model.

**When to adopt a library vs build primitives**
- **Adopt** when the component is commodity (date picker, data grid, rich text, combobox with async, file upload), the library is actively maintained, and its defaults match your design language reasonably.
- **Build/wrap** when the component is core to your brand's differentiation, when you need behaviour the library does not support, or when the library's a11y has a known gap you cannot work around.
- **Never** rebuild a date picker or data grid from scratch for a product; the edge cases (locale, keyboard, RTL, ranges, a11y) are a multi-month project. Copy-source libraries solve this because you get the hard logic and own the pixels.
- Data grids deserve their own note: AG Grid is a commercial-grade solution; a hand-rolled table with virtualization works until you need grouping, pivoting, server-side row models, and export.

**How to wrap a third-party component so it is replaceable** — this is the highest-leverage skill in the section.

```
app code ──▶ your/ui/<Thing>.tsx  ──▶ adapter ──▶ @lib/Thing
                 │  own API           │ translation
                 └─ owns state, a11y  └─ the ONLY file importing @lib
```
Rules:
1. **Your API is yours, not theirs.** Expose your own prop names and shapes (`items`, `onSelect`, `isLoading`), not theirs. Consumers must not know the library exists.
2. **Never leak their types through your public API.** If a consumer can pass a `MUI` type into your component, the coupling exists even if the import is hidden. Translate at the boundary:
```tsx
// adapter — the single file allowed to import the library
import { Grid, type GridColDef } from '@mui/x-data-grid';
import type { Row, Column } from '../types';           // YOUR types

export function DataGridAdapter({ rows, columns, onSortChange, ...rest }: Props) {
  const cols: GridColDef<Row>[] = columns.map((c) => ({
    field: c.key, headerName: c.header, flex: c.flex ?? 1, sortable: c.sortable !== false,
    renderCell: c.render ? (p) => c.render!(p.row as Row) : undefined,
  }));
  return <Grid rows={rows} columns={cols} onSortModelChange={onSortChange as never} {...rest} />;
}
```
3. **Own the state.** Loading, empty, error, and layout live in your component; the library renders what you give it. Libraries that own state are libraries you cannot wrap cleanly.
4. **Own accessibility.** Add the labels, focus order, and announcements; do not assume the library's defaults suit your context (e.g. a dialog inside another dialog, or a table inside a scroll container with `aria-rowcount`).
5. **Pin and upgrade deliberately.** A floating `^` range is how a patch release silently changes your UI. Pin exactly, upgrade on a schedule, and keep a changelog.
6. **Escape hatch without leakage**: allow `className` and `slots`-like extension points, but no pass-through of the library's entire props object (`{...rest}` typed as `any`).
7. **Test at your boundary**, not theirs: interaction tests against your component's API. Then a single "library swapped" test file proves the wrapper is replaceable.

**Questions**
1. Compare shadcn/ui, Radix, MUI, Ant Design, and Chakra on distribution model and ownership.
2. Headless vs styled: what are you actually choosing between?
3. Why did "copy the source into your repo" become popular? What does it cost?
4. Your team adopts shadcn/ui with 60 components. List the ongoing work you just took on.
5. Ant Design vs MUI for an enterprise dashboard: how would you decide?
6. What are the hidden costs of CSS-in-JS libraries in SSR apps?
7. When is rebuilding a component the right call, and which components should you never rebuild?
8. Design an adapter that makes a third-party date picker replaceable.
9. How do you prevent a library's types leaking into your public API?
10. Bundle: a library adds 300 kB gzipped. How do you investigate and fix?
11. Who patches a permissively-licensed dependency when upstream dies? What's your answer in an interview?
12. Your team wants both a design system and Ant Design. Reconcile them.
13. How do you decide the "no third-party primitives" line for an interview project?

**Model answer, Q2:** "Context: the choice is behaviour-versus-pixels ownership. Decision: headless primitives when our visual language is a differentiator and we can fund CSS work; a styled library when speed-to-finished-UI matters more than pixel-level control and our product can live with a house style. Reasoning: headless gives correct keyboard and ARIA behaviour, which is genuinely hard and slow to re-derive — Radix or React Aria encode a decade of fixes — and lets us own every pixel, theming token, and bundle byte. Styled systems give a coherent look immediately and a theme model, which is often the difference between shipping in a week and in a quarter. Trade-offs: headless means we own the visual states (hover, focus, active, disabled, loading) for every component and we own accessibility regressions after adoption; styled means we pay bundle and SSR cost, fight theme override systems when we need something non-standard, and accept the library's opinionated UX. Failure scenario: a headless choice with no design investment — we ship a primitive with no hover or focus-visible state and fail a WCAG audit that we had assumed was handled. Alternative: a hybrid — headless behaviour with a small in-house styled layer (the shadcn model) — which is where most mature product teams land."

**Mistakes:** naming a library without knowing its distribution model; saying a component library "handles accessibility for you" without caveats; comparing them on aesthetics only; reaching for a specific library as a brand signal; proposing to wrap a component while leaking its types and props; ignoring license and maintenance reality.

**Mini task:** take one third-party component (a date picker or a select), write a wrapper with your own types and states (loading, empty, error, disabled, RTL), and write a "swap the library" test that imports only your component. Then bundle-analyze your app with and without the library and quantify what you would save by hand-rolling it — and defend whether you would.

---

# 4.6.3 Storybook (🔴)

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** for design-system and component-heavy roles · Prerequisites: 4.6.1, 4.2

**Definition:** A workshop for UI components in isolation — a static site per component, where every variant, state, and edge case is a *story* that can be rendered, reviewed, tested, and diffed.

**Stories vs stories of stories**
- A **story** is one named, parameterised state of a component (`Primary`, `WithError`, `Loading`, `LongLabel`, `RTL`, `ZeroItems`).
- A **story of stories** is the *coverage requirement*: the argument that a component's stories must enumerate its state space, not just its happy path. A mature component library has a story per state (default, hover, focus, active, disabled, loading, error, empty, over-long content, RTL, high-contrast, reduced-motion) — that is the real contract.
- **Docs pages** group stories and add prose; **autodocs** generate the API table (props, types, defaults, descriptions) from the component's TypeScript types plus JSDoc. Autodocs means your docs table rots less than a hand-written props table — the machine-reads-types part is the win; the descriptions still need a human.
- **MDX** mixes prose with live stories, which is how you document "when to use / when not to use" with an example rather than a screenshot.

```tsx
// args = inputs; argTypes = controls + docs for those inputs
export default {
  title: 'Patterns/Checkout/OrderSummary',
  component: OrderSummary,
  parameters: { layout: 'centered' },
  tags: ['autodocs'],                       // generate the docs page for this component
  argTypes: {
    variant: { control: 'inline-radio', options: ['compact', 'comfortable'] },
    onRetry: { action: 'retry-clicked' },   // logs to the Actions panel
  },
} satisfies Meta<typeof OrderSummary>;

export const Default: Story = { args: { items: fixtures.items, currency: 'USD' } };
export const Empty: Story = { args: { items: [] } };
export const Loading: Story = { args: { loading: true, items: [] } };
export const ItemRemoved: Story = {
  args: { items: fixtures.items.slice(1), notice: { tone: 'info', text: '1 item removed' } },
};
export const LongContent: Story = { args: { items: longNamesFixture } };  // the one people forget
export const MobileWidth: Story = { parameters: { viewport: { defaultViewport: 'mobile1' } } };
```

**Interaction testing with `play`.** A `play` function is an async function that runs after render and drives the component, which turns a story into an executable interaction test — this is the genuinely valuable part and the reason Storybook moved from "gallery" to "test harness".

```tsx
export const OpensAndEscapes: Story = {
  play: async ({ canvasElement, userEvent }) => {
    const canvas = within(canvasElement);
    const user = userEvent.setup();
    await user.click(canvas.getByRole('button', { name: /edit address/i }));
    const dialog = await canvas.findByRole('dialog');
    expect(dialog).toHaveFocus();                        // focus moved into the dialog
    await user.keyboard('{Escape}');
    await waitForElementToBeRemoved(() => canvas.queryByRole('dialog'));
    expect(canvas.getByRole('button', { name: /edit address/i })).toHaveFocus(); // focus restored
  },
};
```
Because the story is a real render with real styles, `play` tests can assert *visual* things a DOM-only test cannot: `expect(element).toHaveStyle`, `toBeVisible`, or a `getBoundingClientRect()` check for layout. That is a real advantage over jsdom unit tests, which have no layout engine.

**The a11y addon, and what it cannot catch.** The addon runs axe-core on each story during rendering and in the test runner, surfacing violations in the panel. It catches roughly 30–40% of real problems: missing accessible names, bad roles, contrast (only for rendered colours), invalid ARIA attributes, form label issues. It **cannot** catch:
- Keyboard operability of a custom widget (does Tab order make sense? does Escape close?).
- Focus visibility and focus-trap correctness in dynamic content.
- Screen-reader announcement quality and reading order (e.g. a live region that exists but announces nothing useful).
- Whether the accessible name is *meaningful* ("Click here").
- Timing and motion problems, or zoom/reflow at 400%.
Rule for interviews: axe in CI is a floor, not a ceiling; manual keyboard + screen-reader passes per component release are the ceiling that matters (PHASE 3.7).

**Visual regression.** The workflow: render stories in a headless browser, screenshot each, and compare against an approved baseline.
- **Hosted platforms** (Chromatic, Percy) do the rendering, storage, and diff UI, and post a PR comment with per-story diffs. **Self-hosted** options use Playwright's `toHaveScreenshot` in CI.
- **Baselines are the hard part.** Rules that keep them useful: pin the browser and platform version (a Chromium bump moves text rendering), fix fonts (load a bundled font, not a system font), disable animations (`reducedMotion: 'reduce'`, seeded clock, disabled transitions), normalise timezones/locale, and use `mask` for intentionally dynamic regions (avatars, dates, charts).
- **Flaky snapshots** come from: animation mid-flight, random data, unseeded `Math.random`/`Date`, async data arriving at different times (wait for a loaded state before asserting), and cross-platform font differences. Fix with fixtures + waiting, not with a bigger tolerance. A blanket `maxDiffPixelRatio` increase is how teams end up with a test that never fails.
- **Review workflow**: every PR touching component source or stories produces a diff; the reviewer approves the baseline as part of code review. Because a screenshot is approved by a human, this is a design review artefact as well as a test. Make the threshold policy explicit: any unexplained diff blocks, intentional diffs are approved with a reason in the PR.

**CI and PR review flow.**
```
lint → typecheck → build the component bundle → storybook build (static) →
  interaction tests (play, headless) → a11y (axe) → visual regression diff →
  publish (preview for PRs, main for releases) → changeset/version bump
```
Keep the storybook build **static** (no runtime API calls) so CI is deterministic; mock data via MSW or fixtures at the story level. Publish a per-PR preview so reviewers can click through interactions rather than only reading a diff. Gate the merge on interaction + a11y; keep visual baselines as a warning-with-required-approval rather than a hard block early on, until the baseline culture is trustworthy.

**Monorepo Storybook and component publishing.** One Storybook per workspace is wasteful; the standard shape is a single Storybook that imports stories from multiple packages via a path glob:
```ts
// .storybook/main.ts
export default {
  stories: ['../packages/*/src/**/*.stories.@(ts|tsx|mdx)', '../apps/*/src/**/*.stories.@(ts|tsx)'],
  addons: ['@storybook/addon-a11y', '@storybook/addon-vitest', '@storybook/addon-docs'],
  framework: { name: '@storybook/react-vite', options: {} },
};
```
For **published component packages**, the modern convention is to ship documentation as part of the package: either a static Storybook deployed per version, or framework-provided component documentation tooling that extracts props, MDX-authored guides, and interactive examples into your docs site. The point for an interview: *documentation artifacts should be built from the same source as the components, in CI, on every release* — so a version on npm is never undocumented, and prop tables never describe a version that no longer exists.

**Performance.** Storybook is a dev tool, but it can grow: the bundle analyzer inside Storybook shows which packages dominate. Practical rules: keep fixtures small, avoid importing the app's whole data layer into stories, lazy-load heavy stories, and split the Storybook by workspace if build times grow past a few minutes. Never let a story import application code that exists only for the story (a story importing `src/dev/mockData.ts` is fine; a story importing a route loader is a design smell).

**When Storybook pays off vs overhead**
| Pays off | Overhead |
|---|---|
| ≥ 2–3 teams touching the same components | One team with 15 components |
| A component library you publish or share | An app with no shared UI |
| Complex interactive components with many states | Primarily static pages |
| Designers collaborating on UI | Nobody will maintain stories |
| You want interaction + a11y tests in the same harness | You will not keep stories updated |
Cost without discipline: **story rot.** A stale story is worse than no story because it lies. Prevention is in the next section.

**Keeping stories from rotting**
- **Test stories in CI** — the single most effective measure. If `play` fails or an interaction breaks, the story is a failing test, so it cannot silently rot.
- **Coverage as a gate**: require a story per exported component (auto-generated from argTypes as a starting point) and require stories for new states in review.
- **Static analysis on props**: a story that spreads `args` will not fail when a required prop is added — a type error is your first line of defence, so keep stories strictly typed with `satisfies Story`.
- **No live API calls.** Fixtures and MSW handlers.
- **Ownership**: stories live next to the component in the same directory and the same PR.
- **Prune**: delete stories for removed props in the same PR as the prop removal; a deprecation linter that scans stories for deprecated props (Phase 3.8 ESLint) catches the rest.

**Questions**
1. What problem does Storybook solve that unit tests do not?
2. What is a "story of stories" and why does it matter?
3. How do args and argTypes differ, and what does autodocs take from your types?
4. Why does a `play` function test something a jsdom unit test cannot?
5. What fraction of accessibility problems does the axe addon catch, and what does it miss?
6. How do you make visual regression baselines stable? Give four concrete causes of flake and their fixes.
7. Should visual regression block a merge?
8. How do you run one Storybook for a monorepo?
9. How do you document a *published* component package so docs cannot drift from versions?
10. Storybook build time is 8 minutes. What do you measure first?
11. How do you stop stories rotting when nobody is motivated?
12. When would you deliberately *not* use Storybook?

**Model answer, Q5:** "Context: axe-core automates a well-defined subset of accessibility rules, and it is excellent at that subset. Decision: run it on every story in CI as a blocking gate for critical and serious violations, and pair it with a mandatory manual keyboard and screen-reader pass per component release. Reasoning: the addon catches mechanical failures — missing accessible names, invalid ARIA, missing labels, some contrast — which are numerous and entirely mechanical, so automation gives immediate value at near-zero marginal cost. What it cannot judge is interaction: whether a custom listbox's arrow-key behaviour matches the ARIA pattern, whether focus moves into a dialog and returns to the invoker on close, whether the focus ring is visible against the actual background, whether a live region announces a change meaningfully, or whether the accessible name is any good at all. Failure scenario: we ship a combobox that passes axe with a correct `role` and `aria-expanded` but does nothing on ArrowDown, so a keyboard or screen-reader user cannot reach any option — green pipeline, unusable product. Trade-offs: full manual testing per component is expensive and inconsistent, which is why I want one documented manual checklist per component archetype (dialog, combobox, tabs, table) and a shared vocabulary, rather than 'test accessibility' as a slogan. Alternative: shift automated effort to keyboard-interaction assertions inside `play`, which mechanises part of the manual check by simulating a real user with `userEvent`."

**Mistakes:** treating Storybook as documentation only, or as documentation that lives in a wiki; writing one story per component instead of one per state; live API calls in stories; flaky visual baselines papered over with tolerance thresholds; assuming axe means accessible; a Storybook per app in a monorepo.

**Mini task:** write `Dialog` and `Select` with full state stories, add a `play` test per state that asserts focus behaviour, add axe, then run everything in CI on a deliberately broken version (remove the focus trap, remove the label) and confirm the pipeline catches each defect. Finally, set up visual regression, deliberately introduce a flaky story, and write down what caused the flake and how you fixed it.

---
# 4.6.4 Micro-frontends (🔴)

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** at companies that have them · Prerequisites: 4.5 (build/deploy), Phase 11 (architectural styles)

**Definition:** An architectural pattern where a single-page application is decomposed into independently built, deployed, and owned sub-applications that are composed at runtime into one user-facing product. Canonical backend-side treatment: PHASE 11 → Architectural Styles → Microfrontends; here the *frontend mechanics and costs*.

**Real motivations** (be honest about which one applies)
1. **Independent deploy cadence.** Multiple teams must ship on their own schedules through one shared release train without coordinating merges. This is the strongest legitimate driver.
2. **Organisational autonomy at scale.** Team boundaries *caused by* code ownership need: "if you can't deploy it alone, you can't be on call for it alone." At ~50+ engineers across overlapping surfaces, deployment coupling is the real cost of a shared frontend repo.
3. **Legacy incremental migration.** A 400k-line Angular app gets a new React surface without a rewrite. Strangler-fig the frontend (PHASE 11).
4. **Domain boundaries that genuinely differ.** Billing is a checkout with a totally different UX and lifecycle from an analytics dashboard; merging them into one bundle and one deploy is a merge of risk profiles, not just code.
5. **Technology heterogeneity** as a *by-product*, never as a goal. "We use React in the new thing because the new thing is React" is fine; "we split so each team picks its own framework" is a cost with no user benefit and enormous tax.

**Real costs** (name these unprompted; that's the difference between 🔴 and 🟡)
- **Runtime inconsistency:** the shell and remotes were built at different times against different dependency versions. Two copies of React → hooks errors; two copies of a token system → visual mismatch; two router versions → navigation bugs.
- **Dependency duplication and bundle bloat.** If each MFE is independently built and each bundles its own framework, you ship 3× React. Sizing becomes a first-class concern (see sharing/singletons).
- **CSS isolation is genuinely hard.** Options are: CSS Modules or scoped styles per MFE (works, but you lose cross-MFE overrides like theming and typography), Shadow DOM (real isolation, but breaks global design-token inheritance, form controls, fonts, and is awkward for SSR), BEM naming discipline, or a strict "no global CSS outside the shell" rule. Every real implementation picks one and suffers its trade-off.
- **Cross-team coordination cost.** Contract changes, shared component releases, and release-order dependencies reappear as distributed-systems problems: you need versioning, compatibility windows, and deprecation.
- **Testing.** End-to-end tests must test the *composition*, which is now nondeterministic and version-skewed. Unit/component tests stay inside the MFE; integration tests need both sides deployed.
- **Observability fragmentation.** A user action crosses four codebases; without a shared tracing context and a shared log sink you cannot reconstruct a single interaction. This is why 4.6.6 and Phase 8 tracing are prerequisites, not nice-to-haves.
- **SEO and first load.** Client-side composition of a marketing page means the critical content is not in the initial HTML. SSR-per-MFE or edge/server composition exists for this reason.
- **Auth and session semantics** must be shared, or you get "logged out when I moved between apps".
- **Operational cost:** N pipelines, N preview environments, N rollback procedures, N dependency-update chores.

**Integration styles**

| Style | How | Pros | Cons | Use when |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Build-time composition | Every MFE is compiled into the shell's bundle | Simplest runtime, one bundle, easy sharing, great perf | Loses independent deploy; shell must release for any change | Small teams, hard coupling, "we tried MFE and reverted" |
| Runtime, Module Federation | Shell loads `remoteEntry.js` at runtime and pulls modules across origins | Independent deploy, shared deps possible (singleton config) | Version skew, CORS/CSP, shared-dep negotiation complexity, caching invalidation | Mature teams with platform support and a shell that owns the router |
| Runtime, import maps / ESM | Shell uses an import map to point bare specifiers at remote URLs | Standards-based, no plugin runtime | Needs served modules, no shared instance guarantees | Progressive experiments, edge-flavored hosts |
| Server-side composition (edge/BFF) | Server injects `<script type="module" src>` tags and layout into HTML | SSR for each MFE, SEO, first-paint control, per-MFE caching | Server knows the composition; runtime still needed for interaction | Content-heavy, SEO-sensitive, React Server Components-adjacent |
| iframe isolation | Each MFE in an `<iframe>`, `postMessage` for comms | Strong CSS/JS/DOM isolation; trivially independent tech and deploy | Layout integration, focus management, a11y, sizing, perf overhead, cross-frame a11y | Truly independent products on one page; untrusted third-party content |

**The shell/router ownership problem.** Someone must own the URL space, the auth session, the layout chrome, and the loading of remotes. The two workable models:
- **Shell owns the router.** The shell holds all routes; MFEs receive navigation intent as props or via a shared router instance. Best DX and best focus/announcement behaviour. Cost: adding a route to a remote needs a shell release (or a shell-side route manifest fetched at runtime — which reintroduces version skew, so version the manifest).
- **MFE owns a route prefix.** The shell lazy-loads the remote on first navigation. Cheaper shell, but the shell must know which prefixes map to which remote (a route manifest again) and you must handle deep links, unknown routes, and the "shell loaded, remote 404s" state.
- **Hybrid (most common):** shell owns top-level navigation and auth; each MFE owns sub-routes and its own internal router instance, with navigation events bubbled to the shell for breadcrumbs/analytics.

**Sharing dependencies and singletons.** With runtime composition you have to decide, per shared dependency:
- **Singleton (must be one instance):** React (hooks break with two copies), the router, the i18n runtime, the design-token provider, the analytics client, the auth/session client, the query cache. Two routers means two histories.
- **Shareable (duplication is acceptable):** date libraries, validation schemas, pure utilities.
- Module Federation's `shared` config with an eager/required singleton version and a fallback is the mechanism; `requiredVersion` with `singleton: true` and a version range is the practical setting, and you must understand the failure mode: a version mismatch either loads the shell's copy (potentially breaking an MFE expecting a newer API) or throws at load. That is why the *compatibility contract* below matters more than the config.

**Cross-micro-frontend communication.** Ranked by cost, best first:
1. **URL and route state.** The cheapest and most robust channel. Encode what the other app needs in the query string or path; nothing to wire, naturally shareable/bookmarkable, survives reloads. Prefer this whenever it works.
2. **Custom DOM events on `document`.** Loose coupling, no shared imports, but untyped, globally visible, and unaudited. Wrap in a typed `emit`/`on` helper with a naming convention and payload schemas, and make listeners idempotent.
3. **A shared state library** (a tiny store or a pub/sub package) when several apps need a broadcast channel (e.g. "cart changed", "session expiring"). This creates a runtime dependency and a singleton requirement; put a version on it and never let it hold more than "hints" that each app re-validates.
4. **A shared service package** (auth client, API client) — normal dependency, just versioned.
5. **What to avoid:** MFE A importing MFE B's internals. It converts two deployables into one, and the whole independence story dies.
Also handle the **multi-instance case**: three instances of the same MFE on one page means event names and DOM ids must be namespaced per instance. This bites with repeated widgets, and it is a great interview trap.

**Auth across apps.** Options:
- **Shared cookie session** (canonical: PHASE 7 → Auth Mechanisms): one `HttpOnly` `Secure` `SameSite=Lax` session cookie on the parent domain; every MFE calls the same auth service and gets the same identity. Best answer for same-origin composition. Requires CSRF protection on state-changing requests (cross-ref PHASE 7).
- **Shared SDK in the shell** that owns login/logout and broadcasts expiry so MFEs don't each implement a refresh loop.
- **Bearer tokens per app** (each MFE has its own client credentials) — isolates blast radius but multiplies token lifetimes and revocation problems; only if the MFEs are genuinely separate products (possibly cross-origin, then it's SSO/OIDC and cross-ref PHASE 7).
Whatever you pick: session expiry in one app must sign the user out everywhere, and you must handle the "session expired mid-navigation" state gracefully instead of a redirect loop between apps.

**Version skew and compatibility contracts.** Independent deploy means MFE v3 may load beside shell v2 and MFE v1. Make this survivable:
- **Declare contracts explicitly**: the shell's public API for MFEs (the props/context it provides), the event schema versions, the shared-package ranges.
- **Additive, backward-compatible change only**, within a major: new optional props, new events, never removing or re-typing what exists.
- **Remote health check + fallback**: wrap remote loading with a timeout, a retry, and a "this section is temporarily unavailable" state that is user-presentable and observable. A failed remote must never take down the shell.
- **Version the event payloads** (`order.updated.v2`) and have consumers accept the version they know and ignore the rest.
- **Canary the composition**: run old+new side by side in a preview environment that pins specific remote versions, so a shell release can be tested against remote versions in the wild.

**Observability with distributed tracing.** A single user action becomes a tree of spans. Requirements that make it work:
- **Trace context propagation from the shell**, so every MFE's spans join the same trace. W3C `traceparent` header on XHR/fetch (set by your HTTP client, and re-set for cross-origin requests subject to CORS — the header must be in the allowed list), plus a shared tag for `mf.name` / `mf.version`.
- **One log sink, correlated ids.** Every error report carries the trace id so you can pivot from a Sentry issue (4.6.6) to the full trace and back.
- **A performance budget per MFE**, since a slow remote delays the shell's paint. Track LCP/INP per route *including* the remote's load time.
- **Version in the dashboards.** Every metric and log line is useless without `mf_version`, or you cannot correlate the regression with a release.

**How to migrate a monolith to micro-frontends (Strangler, cross-ref PHASE 11)**
1. **Do not start with the shell.** Start by extracting one vertical slice at the *edge* of the monolith, behind a route, so you can roll back by routing.
2. **Introduce the shell as a thin router + auth + layout**, serving the existing monolith for unmigrated routes (an iframe, or a reverse proxy path that serves the old app).
3. **Extract a route at a time**, highest-churn first (that is where the coupling hurts), and prefer a route with low shared-state interaction (a settings page, a reports view) over the checkout.
4. **Extract the shared primitives first** into a versioned package — otherwise every MFE has its own button and you are back to a design-system problem with extra steps.
5. **Establish the contracts before the second MFE**: routing conventions, the shell API, the design tokens, error reporting, analytics, auth. Doing this after you have three MFEs is a rewrite.
6. **Prove the operational loop** (deploy, rollback, preview, on-call) with one MFE before scaling the pattern.
7. **Set a sunset date** for each remaining monolith surface and delete as you go; a permanent hybrid is the worst outcome.

**When NOT to use micro-frontends**
- Fewer than ~20 engineers, or one team owning everything.
- The domain is coupling-heavy: a search page that needs the cart, the cart needs the checkout, and all three need shared state. Independent deployment is a fiction there.
- The application is small enough that the bundle is not a problem.
- No platform/infra capacity to own the runtime, the shell, and the CI.
- Heavy real-time coupling (multiplayer editing, trading) where a single coherent state model matters more than deploy independence.
The honest interview framing: micro-frontends convert **code-level coupling into operational and runtime coupling**. That is a good trade only when the org's release bottleneck is code-level coupling and the team can afford the operational tax. For most products the better answer is a **modular monolith**: enforced internal boundaries (4.6.5) plus independent backend deploys, which gets most of the organisational benefit at a fraction of the cost.

**Questions**
1. What problems do micro-frontends genuinely solve? Which one is strongest?
2. List the costs. Which do teams underestimate?
3. Build-time vs runtime composition: when each?
4. How does the shell own routing, and what breaks if two MFEs disagree about routes?
5. How do you share React across MFEs, and what fails if you get it wrong?
6. How do you isolate CSS between remotes? What does Shadow DOM break?
7. Design cross-MFE communication. When is URL state enough?
8. Auth across three apps on the same parent domain: your approach?
9. Your shell v2 ships while remote v1 is still deployed. What are the failure modes and mitigations?
10. What is a compatibility contract between a shell and a remote?
11. How do you trace one user action across four codebases?
12. Migrate a monolith: order the steps and pick the first slice.
13. Your team has 12 engineers and wants micro-frontends. What do you say?
14. When is a modular monolith the better answer, and how do you enforce its boundaries?
15. What does an MFE outage look like, and how should the shell behave?
16. How do micro-frontends affect SEO and first load, and what do you do about it?

**Model answer, Q13:** "Context: a 12-engineer team with one product, two deploys a week, and a bundle that is not actually the bottleneck. Decision: do not adopt micro-frontends yet; enforce module boundaries inside the app and get independent deploys of the backend services if the coupling there is the real constraint. Reasoning: the pattern's value is organisational — independent release cadence and deployable ownership — and that value only materialises when multiple teams are blocked by a shared release train. At 12 engineers the coordination overhead of a shell, a runtime loader, N pipelines, compatibility contracts, cross-repo version skew, and composition-level E2E tests exceeds the release-coupling problem it solves. The hidden costs are real: duplicate React copies, CSS isolation compromises, fragmented observability, and a class of version-skew bugs that are miserable to debug. Trade-offs: I am accepting continued release-train coupling in exchange for developer velocity and debuggability, which for a small team is the right side of the trade. Failure scenario: we split anyway, and six months later we have four deployables with duplicated dependencies, an untestable composition, and a bug that reproduces only when shell v3 meets remote v1 — a class of incident that is far more expensive than the merges we were trying to avoid. Alternative: a staged middle — extract a design-tokens package and a shared UI package now, design the folder boundaries so an eventual split is possible, and revisit the decision when a third team forms or when deploy coupling becomes the top-three complaint in a survey."

**Mistakes:** adopting micro-frontends for "scalability" or "team autonomy" without a release-cadence problem; leaving routing unowned; ignoring the React singleton; assuming client-side composition is SEO-safe; migrating the shell before one remote works end to end; not budgeting the runtime dependency.

**Mini task:** build a shell with two routes, each loading a separately built "remote" (two Vite builds with a tiny federated-style loader), sharing a singleton React, a shared token stylesheet, and a typed `postMessage` event bus. Then break it on purpose: ship remote v2 with a changed shared-component prop and no shell release, and document the failure and the compatibility fix. Add a trace-id breadcrumb in both apps and show the correlated Sentry issue.

---

# 4.6.5 Frontend project structure and boundaries

🟡 Intermediate → 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.1

**Feature-based vs type-based folders**

```
type-based (avoid at scale)        feature-based (default)
├── components/                    ├── app/            (router, providers, layout)
│   ├── Button.tsx                 ├── features/
│   └── Header.tsx                 │   ├── checkout/
├── utils/                         │   │   ├── components/  (co-located, private)
│   ├── format.ts                  │   │   ├── hooks/
│   ├── api.ts                     │   │   ├── api.ts
│   └── dates.ts                   │   │   ├── CheckoutForm.tsx
│   └── ...                        │   │   └── CheckoutForm.test.tsx
├── hooks/                         │   └── orders/
├── api/                           ├── shared/         (or packages/ in a monorepo)
└── pages/                         │   ├── ui/         (design system, 4.6.1)
                                    │   ├── lib/        (framework-free helpers)
                                    │   └── config/
```
Type-based grouping (`utils/`, `components/`) maximises reusability *in theory* and produces, in practice, a single `utils/` dumping ground of 200 unrelated functions, a `components/` folder nobody can navigate, and no clue which util a feature is allowed to use. Feature-based grouping optimises for **deletion** and **co-location**: everything needed to change a feature is in one folder; deleting the feature deletes exactly its code. Its weakness is duplication (three features each with a `useDebounce`), which you fix with a *deliberate* promotion path to `shared/` (below) rather than with a preemptive `hooks/` folder.

**Layering and dependency direction**
```
app  ──▶  features  ──▶  shared/ui  ──▶  shared/lib
           │
           └──▶  shared/config, shared/api-client
```
Rules:
- **Dependencies point downward only.** `shared/lib` never imports from `features`; a feature never imports from another feature's internals.
- **`features` are private by default.** Another feature wanting your code goes through your feature's public `index.ts`, or you promote the shared piece to `shared/`.
- **`app` is composition only:** providers, routing, error boundaries, layout. It should contain no business logic — if `app/` has `if (user.role === 'admin')`, that belongs in a feature or a permission module.
- **`shared/lib` is framework-free** if you want to test it without a renderer and reuse it in workers.

**Enforcing boundaries mechanically** (interviewers like this because it shows you know boundaries rot without tooling):
```js
// eslint.config.js — flat config
import boundaries from 'eslint-plugin-boundaries';
import noRestricted from 'eslint-plugin-import';
export default [
  boundaries.elements = [
    { type: 'app',       pattern: 'src/app/**' },
    { type: 'feature',   pattern: 'src/features/**' },
    { type: 'shared-ui', pattern: 'src/shared/ui/**' },
    { type: 'shared-lib',pattern: 'src/shared/lib/**' },
  ];
  boundaries.rules = [
    { from: 'shared-lib', disallow: ['app', 'feature', 'shared-ui'] },
    { from: 'shared-ui',  disallow: ['app', 'feature'] },
    { from: 'feature',   disallow: ['app'] },
    { from: 'feature',   disallow: ['feature'], except: ['index.ts'] }, // only via public API
  ];
  noRestricted.config = [{ paths: [
      // and the cheap belt-and-braces version, which needs no plugin
      { name: 'shared/lib', from: 'src/features',
        message: 'features must not import each other; go via the public index.ts or promote to shared/' },
  ]}];
];
```
Also add an ESLint rule to forbid deep imports past a public entry (`no-restricted-imports` patterns ending in `features/*/*/*`), because a missing boundary rule is a suggestion and a deep-import ban is a build error.

**Circular dependency detection.** Cycles are legal in JS but produce undefined initialisation order, confusing bundler output, and mock pain in tests. Detect them in CI:
```bash
madge --circular --extensions ts,tsx src     # graph + cycles
dependency-cruiser --config .dependency-cruiser.cjs src   # richer rules + validation
```
Add `madge --circular` to CI and fail the build on new cycles (baseline existing ones so you can ratchet down). A common source of cycles: two hooks referencing each other, `A → B → A` via type-only imports (fix with `import type`, which bundlers erase).

**Module boundaries vs folder names.** A boundary is a *contract*: what may be imported from where, what is exported, who owns it, and how it is versioned. A folder name is a label. Teams fail by creating folders and assuming boundaries — so make each boundary explicit: an entry point, an owner (`CODEOWNERS`), a test, and (for packages) a version. A folder with no entry point is not a module; it's a suggestion.

**Barrels (`index.ts`) and why they hurt.** A barrel re-exports everything:
```ts
// shared/lib/index.ts — the default reflex
export * from './dates';
export * from './format';
export * from './api';
```
Costs, in order of severity:
- **Tree-shaking degrades.** With `sideEffects` metadata and proper ESM, unused exports *can* be shaken, but barrel files create large re-export graphs that some bundlers resolve poorly, and any barrel member with a side effect (a polyfill, a `window` guard) is retained, dragging its whole subtree in. The reliable statement for an interview: barrels make it harder for bundlers to prove a module is unused, and the cost grows with barrel size and import-path depth.
- **Build/watch cost.** In a monorepo or a large app, a barrel re-export of many files turns any change into a large invalidation graph (dev-server restarts, slow type-check, watch-mode thrash).
- **Cycles hide inside them** and are hard to trace.
- **`export *` name collisions** are silent.
Compromise that most mature codebases land on: **barrels only at package/feature boundaries** (so consumers get one stable public path) and **no barrels in leaf directories**. Configure the bundler explicitly rather than hoping.

**Path aliases.** Map `@/` to `src/` and `@shared/` to the shared root in `tsconfig.json` `paths` (types) **and** in the bundler (`vite.config` `resolve.alias`, Jest/Webpack equivalents). Two rules: aliases must be consistent everywhere (type-checker, bundler, test runner, ESLint resolver, IDE) or you get "works at build, fails in test" bugs; and don't invent deep alias hierarchies that recreate the type-based folder problem. Aliases are a convenience, not an architecture.

**Where shared code lives in a monorepo**
| Location | Use when | Watch out for |
|---|---|---|
| `packages/ui` (published, versioned) | Shared by multiple apps and deployable independently; needs its own release cadence | Version skew, release ceremony, dependency duplication in apps |
| `packages/config-*` (tsconfig, eslint, vite presets) | Tooling consistency across apps | Premature abstraction of build config |
| `packages/utils`, `packages/schemas` | Pure, framework-free, genuinely shared | Becoming a junk drawer; keep API surface tiny |
| `src/shared/` inside one app | Shared by that app only | Growing until it should be a package |
| Copy the code | Two things that look similar now | Duplication is *cheaper* than a wrong abstraction. Duplication is a legitimate engineering choice, not a sin. |
Rule: promote to a package when there are ≥2 real consumers, a named owner, and a change cadence that is independent. Otherwise duplicate. The `CODEOWNERS` entry:
```
# .github/CODEOWNERS
/packages/ui/            @org/design-systems
/packages/schemas/       @org/platform
/src/app/features/billing/ @org/billing
```
This is where ownership becomes enforcement: a PR touching `packages/ui` cannot merge without the design-systems team's review, which protects the system from well-meaning feature pressure.

**The public API of an internal package.** Export only what others need — every export is a promise you will keep.
```ts
// packages/schemas/src/index.ts
export { OrderSchema, OrderType } from './order';         // yes: used everywhere
// export { internalNormalizer } from './internal';        // no: one caller → import the path
```
Concretely: no `export *` from internal modules, no re-exporting a dependency's types (`export * from 'zod'` would couple your package to zod's version), and configure `exports` so deep imports fail:
```json
{ "name": "@acme/schemas", "exports": { ".": "./src/index.ts" }, "sideEffects": false }
```
A grep-able check: for each export, count importers; anything with exactly one non-test importer is a candidate to unexport. This is also the on-ramp for the wrapper pattern in 4.6.2.

**Feature flags living in code structure.** A flag is not a boolean in a component; it is a *branch with an owner and an expiry*. Two structural rules:
- **Keep the flag decision at the seam, not scattered through the UI.** `if (flags.checkoutV2) return <CheckoutV2/>` in one place beats scattered `flags.newButton` reads that nobody can enumerate.
- **Both branches must be reachable and tested in CI.** A branch only tested when the flag is on is a branch that will break when you flip it in production. So flags get story variants (4.6.3) and test matrix rows.
- **Flags expire** (4.6.7): an owner and a removal date in the flag definition itself, enforced by a lint/CI check.

**Tests mirror structure.** Put `CheckoutForm.test.tsx` next to `CheckoutForm.tsx` (colocation beats `__tests__` mirrors: you cannot forget to move the test, and refactors move both). Integration tests mirror *feature* boundaries, and e2e tests mirror *routes*, not components. A structure that makes tests awkward is telling you the boundary is wrong. Cross-reference Phase 9 for the test pyramid and Phase 3.5 for DOM/test-environment details.

**How structure interacts with build tools and testing**
- Bundlers respect structure: route-based code splitting works if routes map to modules (4.5, 3.8). Deeply nested barrels and cross-feature imports pull chunks together and destroy splitting.
- Path aliases + monorepo packages need shared tsconfig `paths`, otherwise types resolve differently in each app.
- Storybook globs mirror the same structure (4.6.3), so the layout teaches you where things are.
- Test runners need `moduleNameMapper`/resolver config for aliases — a classic "tests fail to resolve `@/`" bug.
- Build caching keys on file hashes (Phase 3.8), so a shared package change invalidates all dependents' caches; structural clarity tells you how much that costs.

**Big-bang restructure vs incremental extraction**
| Big bang | Incremental |
|---|---|
| One clean result, no dual structure | Two structures coexist temporarily |
| Long branch, big-bang-merge pain, wasted review | Small PRs, reviewable, deployable at each step |
| Everything breaks at the merge, then it's fine | Each step is provably equivalent |
| Wrong guess is expensive to undo | Wrong guess costs one small PR |
Choose incremental almost always. The reliable recipe:
1. Add the new structure alongside the old (no behaviour change, delete nothing).
2. Add the boundary lint rules to stop new violations.
3. Move code bottom-up, one leaf at a time, with tests moved alongside so the suite is green at every commit.
4. Delete the old location *in the same PR as the last consumer moves*.
5. Ratchet the boundary rules stricter as violations drop to zero.
Interviews reward the phrase: **restructuring is a refactor, and a refactor must be behaviour-preserving at every commit** — if a commit both moves code and changes behaviour, the bisect story dies.

**Questions**
1. Feature-based vs type-based folders: what does each optimise for and break on?
2. Draw your layering and say which direction imports are allowed.
3. How do you enforce boundaries without relying on code review?
4. What creates circular dependencies, and how do you detect them in CI?
5. Why do barrels hurt tree-shaking and build times? What's the compromise?
6. What breaks when path aliases are configured in the bundler but not in tsconfig?
7. When should shared code become a package rather than a folder?
8. What is a package's public API, and how do you keep it small?
9. How should feature flags be structured in code, and what breaks if they aren't?
10. How do tests mirror folder structure, and what does awkward testing tell you?
11. You have 400 files in `utils/`. Describe the incremental fix over two weeks.
12. When is a big-bang restructure justified?
13. How do you use `CODEOWNERS` to protect a design system from feature teams?
14. What does a deep import past a public entry cost you, and how do you block it?

**Model answer, Q5:** "Context: barrels are the reflex fix for 'too many import paths' and they are a real build-performance footgun. Decision: barrels at package and feature boundaries only, never in leaf directories. Reasoning: a barrel creates a re-export graph that the bundler must analyse to decide what is unused; the bigger the barrel, the more of it must be proven side-effect-free before anything can be dropped, and any module in the graph with a side effect (a polyfill, a global registration) is retained along with its imports. On top of that, in watch mode and in monorepo builds, a single changed file invalidates every module that re-exports it, so a barrel at the top of `shared/lib` turns one edit into a large rebuild. Cycle detection also gets harder because the cycle runs through the barrel entry. Trade-offs: this costs a bit of import ergonomics — consumers write `import { formatDate } from '@/shared/lib/dates'` rather than `'@/shared/lib'` — and it means discipline, since one lazy barrel will be added eventually and the rule must be enforced. Failure scenario: a team adds a root barrel for convenience; `shared/lib` ends up importing a module that touches `window` for a Node/worker path, so the whole graph lands in the initial chunk for every route, and the app's LCP regresses by 180 kB with no visible cause.
