Alternative: keep the ergonomic barrel but split per-package with `exports` maps and `sideEffects: false`, and verify with the bundler's stats that the initial chunk actually dropped — measure, don't believe."

**Mistakes:** mixing type-based and feature-based conventions without a rule; adding barrels "for cleanliness"; relying on review instead of lint for boundaries; promoting a single-consumer helper to a shared package; a big-bang restructure on a live codebase; tests in a mirrored `__tests__` tree that drift.

**Mini task:** take an existing app (or scaffold one with 3 features), add feature-based folders, `eslint-plugin-boundaries` rules, a `madge --circular` CI step, and alias config across tsconfig/Vitest/ESLint. Then, one file at a time, move code into the new structure with the suite green at every commit, and record the wall-clock time the lint rule saved you versus the time the rules cost on new code.

---

# 4.6.6 Error monitoring (Sentry) (🔴)

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.5 (build/deploy), 3.8 (source maps)

**Definition:** A client/server error-reporting service that captures unhandled exceptions and (optionally) traces, enriches them with release/environment/user context, groups them into *issues*, and routes alerts. Canonical observability material: PHASE 8 → monitoring, logging, metrics; here is the frontend-specific craft.

**Setup, SDK choice, and the parts that actually matter**
- **Browser SDK** for client errors/traces; **server SDK** for your API; **framework/framework-agnostic wrappers** (React Router, Next.js, SvelteKit) add routing context and automatic route transaction names — use one, because free contextual data is better than manual breadcrumbs.
- **Source maps must reach Sentry.** They never go in the public bundle. Upload them from CI, per release, and **delete/ignore them for the deployed artifact**. Framework: `sentry-cli sourcemaps upload`.
- **Release and build identity.** Every build gets a unique release id (`$CI_COMMIT_SHA`, or `${semver}+${sha}`), reported by the SDK and written onto uploaded source maps. Without a matching release, stack traces are useless — this is the single most common integration failure. Also set a `dist` (or build id) so the Sentry release matches your asset fingerprint exactly.
```js
// vite.config.ts — release/dist wiring (hash must match what CI uploads)
export default defineConfig({
  plugins: [sentryVitePlugin({
    org: process.env.SENTRY_ORG, project: process.env.SENTRY_PROJECT,
    authToken: process.env.SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN,
    sourcemaps: { filesToDeleteAfterUpload: ['./dist/**/*.map'] },
    release: { name: process.env.RELEASE, dist: process.env.RELEASE },
    telemetry: false,
  })],
});
```
```yaml
# .github/workflows/deploy.yml — order matters: build, upload maps, THEN deploy
- run: npm ci && npm run build
- run: npx sentry-cli releases new "$RELEASE" --project "$PROJECT"
- run: npx sentry-cli sourcemaps upload --release "$RELEASE" ./dist
- run: npx sentry-cli releases set-commits "$RELEASE" --auto --repo "$GITHUB_REPOSITORY"
- run: npx sentry-cli releases finalize "$RELEASE"
```
In Docker/monorepo CI, cache the build by commit SHA and **upload maps exactly once per release**; a re-upload that misses a file yields unminified frames for that file only, which produces a confusing partial trace.

**What to capture**
| Signal | How | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Unhandled errors | Automatic (`window.onerror`, `unhandledrejection`) | The default baseline; noisy but essential |
| Handled errors | `captureException` at boundaries (error boundary `componentDidCatch`, route `onError`, `try/catch` at a service boundary) | This is the highest-signal data you will collect |
| Network failures | Automatic breadcrumbs, plus explicit `captureException` for 5xx on critical calls | A 500 with no breadcrumb trail is unactionable |
| Console output | Breadcrumbs (`console` integration) | Noise vs. context trade-off; cap the buffer |
| User actions | `addBreadcrumb` on meaningful interactions (nav, submit, payment step) | Do **not** log every click; log the 5 that precede a failure |
| Transactions/traces | Browser tracing with spans around route load, fetch, and critical interactions | This is what turns "the page was slow" into "this fetch was slow" |
| Session replay | Optional, with masking | Privacy-reviewed (below) |
The boundary rule: **catch at the boundary you can describe and re-raise with context, and do not swallow.** A swallowed exception with a `console.error` is invisible in production.

**Context that makes a report actionable**
Every event should carry: `release`, `environment`, `dist`, `user.id` (internal id, not email), `session.id`, `tags` (`route`, `browser`, `app.version`, `feature-flag-values` — the last one saves hours), `breadcrumbs`, and a scrubbed request/response summary. Two practices with outsized value:
- **Attach feature flag state to every event** (4.6.7). When a bug appears only for 5% of users, the flag values in the event are the difference between a five-minute and a five-hour investigation.
- **Attach the trace id** so you can jump to the trace (Phase 8) for the timeline of fetches and spans around the failure.

**Sampling and error quotas.** Error-based sampling (only capture a fraction of *repeats of the same issue*, e.g. 10% after the first 100 occurrences) keeps volume down while preserving visibility of new issues. Rate-limit per `release` and per environment so a storm cannot blow the quota; in dev, sample 100% of distinct issues but dedupe. `beforeSend` is the place to drop noise:
```js
Sentry.init({
  dsn: import.meta.env.VITE_SENTRY_DSN,
  release: import.meta.env.RELEASE,
  environment: import.meta.env.MODE,
  tracesSampleRate: 0.1,           // 10% of transactions traced
  tracesSampler: (ctx) => ctx.name === '/checkout' ? 1.0 : 0.05,  // spend samples where it matters
  integrations: [Sentry.browserTracingIntegration()],
  beforeSend(event) {
    if (event.request?.url?.includes('/health')) return null;              // drop infrastructure noise
    if (event.exception?.values?.[0]?.type === 'ChunkLoadError') return null; // handled by the retry boundary
    scrub(event);
    return event;
  },
});
```
State the cost model in an interview: traces are the expensive signal (payload and volume), errors are cheap; so sample traces aggressively, errors lightly, and route **100%** to a dev environment.

**Session replay and PII.** Replay is DOM-mutation video-ish capture: exceptionally useful for reproducing UI bugs, and a genuine privacy and compliance risk.
- **Mask by default**: all `input`, `textarea`, `select`, and `contenteditable` (set `maskAllText: true` and `blockAllMedia: true`, then unmask only specific selectors you have reviewed).
- Never replay: payment fields, national IDs, health data, free-text notes containing customer data, anything on a page behind authentication you have not reviewed.
- **Legal/privacy review is mandatory** before enabling, and record it (retention period, DPA, where data is stored, who can access). Cross-reference PHASE 7 → GDPR/privacy practices.
- **Sampling**: record a fraction of *sessions* (or only sessions that contain an error), which cuts cost and data volume while keeping the useful correlation ("replay the session where this happened").
- Treat replay as a debugging tool with **short retention** (7–14 days) and access controls, not as an analytics product.

**Performance tracing and Web Vitals.** Enable browser tracing to get route-level spans, and either rely on the SDK's Vitals integration or report your own:
```ts
import { onCLS, onINP, onLCP } from 'web-vitals';
const report = (m: any) => navigator.sendBeacon('/rum', JSON.stringify(m));
if (typeof onINP === 'function') onINP(report); else onCLS(report) /* old browsers */;
onCLS(report); onLCP(report);
```
Link field RUM to the trace id (via the request headers you already send) so a bad INP value leads directly to the slow span. Field data plus traces is the loop: CrUX/field data prioritises, traces diagnose. Cross-reference 3.6 (Core Web Vitals) and 4.6.8 (analytics collection).

**Issue grouping and fingerprints.** By default Sentry groups by the top frame's function/file, which is usually right and occasionally catastrophic: a shared utility (`formatError`, a router wrapper) produces one giant issue with a thousand unrelated causes.
- **Custom fingerprints** for known-noisy spots: `fingerprints: ['<%= ctx.function %>', '{{ default }}']` for an API call site, or route + error type.
- **Grouping by release/environment** so a regression in one release is visible.
- **Ignore list** for third-party noise and known-unfixable browser bugs, with a written justification per entry (an ignore list nobody reviews becomes a graveyard).
- **Merging/splitting** issues as you learn: an issue that is one bug per week is mis-grouped; split it.

**Alert routing and SLO integration.** Route by tag: `environment: production` + `release` + `owner` (`CODEOWNERS`-derived) → team channel with an owner ping, not a firehose. Integrate with your SLO: alerting on *every* new issue trains people to ignore alerts; alert on new issues **in a release you just shipped**, on issue *rate* exceeding a threshold, or on error-budget burn (Phase 8 → SLO/error budgets). A defensible rule: page only for high-severity, high-volume, user-affecting regressions; file tickets for the rest.

**What NOT to send**
- PII: email addresses, names, tokens, session ids, request/URL query strings containing ids or emails, full request bodies, and any user-entered text.
- **Bearer tokens, cookies, and API keys** — scrub `Authorization`, `Cookie`, `Set-Cookie`, and any `password`/`token`/`secret` field, recursively, in `beforeSend`.
- Full HTML/DOM snapshots of authenticated pages unless reviewed and masked.
- Secrets of any kind, including Sentry's own auth token (obvious, but it is on every CI log then).
Cross-reference 4.6.9 for the general principle: **anything in the browser is public.**

**Self-hosting vs SaaS.**
| Dimension | SaaS | Self-hosted |
|---|---|---|
| Time to first error | Minutes | Weeks (operate ingestion, storage, symbolication, retention) |
| Ops burden | None | Real: on-call, upgrades, scrubbing PII at rest, backups |
| Data residency | Vendor regions (check where) | Yours |
| Cost at volume | Per-event; spikes are expensive | Infra cost, spikes are your problem |
| Symbolicating/minified stacks | Built in | You run it |
Interview answer: start on SaaS, migrate only when a legal/data-residency requirement or a genuine volume problem forces it. Nobody has ever regretted a *late* self-hosting decision; several regret migrating to self-hosting while still needing vendor-grade ingestion reliability.

**Debugging workflow: from an alert to a fix**
1. **Triage in the issue:** release, environment, first-seen vs last-seen, affected users, top frames after symbolication, browser/OS distribution, and the flag values.
2. **Check correlation:** is it concentrated in one release (a regression), one flag cohort (an experiment), one browser (a compatibility bug), one route, one country (a geo/regulatory code path), one tenant?
3. **Pivot to the trace** via the trace id; find the failing request and its neighbours (was it slow? did a retry happen? did a flag evaluation differ?).
4. **Replay** the session if enabled and the flow is masked-safe.
5. **Reproduce locally** at that release: `git checkout <release-sha>`, install the exact lockfile, and if the build is behind a flag, toggle the exact flag values you saw in the event.
6. **Bisect** if it correlates with a release; write a regression test that fails before the fix; fix; add the missing context (breadcrumb/flag/tag) that made step 2 slow.
7. **Close the loop**: add to the ignore list only if unfixable, with a comment; link the fix PR from the issue so the timeline is a record.

**Questions**
1. What is the single most important Sentry integration detail, and why?
2. How do you wire release and source maps in CI? Why does order matter?
3. What is the difference between unhandled and handled error capture, and which is more valuable?
4. Where exactly should `captureException` live in a React app?
5. What context makes an error report actionable? Name five fields.
6. How do you sample without losing visibility of new issues?
7. What must be masked in session replay, and who approves it?
8. How do traces and Web Vitals connect to a user-reported "the page is slow"?
9. An issue has 4,000 events from six different bugs. Fix the grouping.
10. How do you decide what pages you?
11. What is the difference between `beforeSend` and an ignore list, and when is each right?
12. When do you alert on an error, and how do you connect that to SLOs?
13. What should never leave the browser in an error report?
14. SaaS vs self-hosted Sentry: how do you answer this?
15. Walk me through a bug you found in production using this tooling.

**Model answer, Q15:** "Context: monitoring alerted on a spike in `TypeError: undefined is not an object` with about 900 users over 40 minutes. Decision: work release-first rather than code-first. Reasoning: the alert carried a `release` tag, and correlating by release showed all events on the deploy completed at 14:20, so this was a regression rather than a long-tail issue. Grouping by browser showed it was Chrome-only at a specific version, and the flag values in the event showed all affected users had `checkout.v3` on — that gave me the cohort and the reproduction conditions in one step. From there I pivoted to the trace id, which showed the failing call was the address-validation request, and the neighbouring span showed the response arrived with an `errors` array instead of the documented shape, so the client-side defensive code was reading the old contract. Investigation: I checked out the release SHA with the exact lockfile, replayed the failing response from the trace, and confirmed the crash; the server change was a schema migration shipped without a compat window. Fix: make the client tolerant of both shapes (a normalising adapter at the API boundary), add a schema validation step at that boundary so the next mismatch fails loudly in dev instead of at runtime, and add a regression test with the exact payload from the trace. Prevention: I added API-contract fixtures from production traces to the test suite, required a `flags` tag on events so cohort analysis is always possible, and changed our alerting to page on new issues in a release within an hour of deploy — which is how this one arrived at all. Trade-off: the tolerant adapter hides future breakage, so it is bounded by strict boundary validation and I set a follow-up to remove it after the server fix is everywhere. Failure scenario I was watching for: the bug also reproduced on Safari but was filtered out by the browser-cohort filter, which I only noticed because I checked the full distribution instead of trusting the first filtered view."

**Mistakes:** deploying without uploading source maps for that exact release; no `release` tag so every stack is unminified; sending `Authorization` headers; enabling replay without masking and legal review; alerting on every new issue; relying on breadcrumbs you never added; "it only happens locally" treated as a monitoring gap rather than an environment difference.

**Mini task:** integrate Sentry into an app with release tagging, CI map upload, `beforeSend` scrubbing of headers and query params, flag tags, and a custom fingerprint on your API client. Then deliberately ship three distinct bugs (a type error on a rare flag path, a network 500, and a slow fetch) and verify each lands as a grouped, actionable issue with the right release, flags, and trace link. Then delete one issue from the noise and justify it.

---

# 4.6.7 Feature flags (🟡)

🟡 Intermediate → 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 4.6.6 (observability), 4.5 (build/deploy)

**Definition:** A remote configuration mechanism that lets you change runtime behaviour without shipping code. Cross-reference PHASE 8 → CI/CD → feature flags for the delivery side; here is the frontend correctness side.

**Types of flag, with the frontend consequence**

| Type | Purpose | Lifetime | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Release flag** | Roll a feature out progressively, then remove it | Days–weeks | `checkout.v3` for 5% of users |
| **Operational / kill switch** | Disable a feature instantly when it is broken | Long-lived, owned by ops/SRE | `disable.recommendations`, `payments.provider=braintree` |
| **Experiment** | Compare variants and measure an outcome | Weeks, ends in a decision | `pricing.table.layout` = A/B |
| **Permission / entitlement** | Plan- or account-based feature access | Long-lived | `feature.sso` for enterprise tenants |

The distinction is operational, not technical: **kill switches must not expire** (you need them during an incident) while **release flags must expire** (they are technical debt with branches). Interviewers like candidates who say this out loud.

**Targeting rules**
- **User id** (stable, precise, but only usable after login — so pre-login experiences need something else).
- **Tenant/organisation** (the usual choice for B2B: one rollout decision per customer).
- **Cohort/attribute** (country, plan, app version, device class) — needs a stable bucketing hash of a *stable* attribute; changing the attribute reassigns users and destroys your experiment data.
- **Percentage** (0–100%) via a **deterministic hash** of a stable id (user id, or anonymous id before login), not `Math.random()`:
```
variant = hash(stableId + flagKey) mod 100  <  threshold
```
  Because the salt includes the flag key, a user gets *independent* assignments across experiments (no correlated exposure across tests), while remaining sticky across sessions and devices (same id → same variant).
- **Gradual rollout**: ramp 1% → 10% → 50% → 100%, advancing on an SLO/metric gate rather than on a date.

**Evaluation point, and why it matters**
| Evaluate on | Good for | Bad for |
|---|---|---|
| **Client (runtime SDK)** | Instant changes without a deploy, per-browser experiments | Flash of wrong content, server unaware of the variant, `localStorage` staleness, anyone can tamper |
| **Server (render time)** | Correct HTML, cacheable per-variant, authoritative | Needs a deploy or a server config change; no per-user instant experiments |
| **Edge/middleware** | Authoritative and personalised close to the user | Latency, cold-start cache fragmentation, more moving parts |
Rules of thumb: **evaluate anything that changes markup or data server-side on the server** (otherwise the SSR HTML and the hydrated client disagree); evaluate *presentation-only* variants on the client; and treat every client-side flag as untrusted for anything security-relevant. For a rollout percentage that changes data shapes, do the check in the server so the API returns the right shape in the first place.

**Exposure events and experiment analysis.** The critical distinction: **assignment is not exposure.** Assignment happens when the flag is evaluated; exposure happens when the user actually *sees* the variant.
- Emit exposure once per (flag, variant, user/session) with a stable id, and only when the variant was actually rendered.
- Gate analysis on exposure, not assignment. Filtering on assignment dilutes results with users who never reached the UI — usually a large and *biased* fraction (they may have bounced early or errored).
- Sticky assignment plus a shared id across devices is what makes results trustworthy; without it, users flip variants between sessions and your metrics become noise.

**Flag lifecycle (the part teams skip)**
```
created ──▶ owner + expiry date + ticket required ──▶ ramp ──▶ 100% ──▶ cleanup ──▶ deleted
                                                └─▶ abandoned (experiment ended, no winner) ──▶ deleted
```
Rules that hold up in interviews:
- **Every flag requires an owner and an expiry date at creation.** No owner, no flag.
- **CI enforces expiry:** a job that fails when a flag is past its expiry and not yet removed, plus a report of flags by age and by usage.
- **Cleanup debt is real work.** A codebase with 300 flags has untested branches and a comprehension tax; the 100%-rolled-out branch should be deleted within days, not months.
- **Removing a flag you shipped** is a code change with a rollout plan: ship the flag defaulted to the winning value for a release, then delete the branch, then delete the config. Skipping the "default to new behaviour" step means the removal deploy can unexpectedly flip users back.

**Safe patterns**
1. **Default off** for anything risky, so a missing SDK/config fails closed into the known-good path (and never into the untested path).
2. **Expand/contract** for data and APIs: add the new field/table/endpoint, dual-write both formats, backfill, switch reads to the new one behind a flag, verify, then remove the old one. This is the Strangler pattern applied to flags and is the correct answer for migrations (cross-ref PHASE 11).
3. **Dual-write** with a reconciliation job so you can trust the new path before switching reads.
4. **Backfill before switch** — a flag does not migrate data.
5. **Flags wrap, they do not replace types:** keep both variants behind the same internal interface so the seam is one line.

```ts
// flags/definitions.ts — owner + expiry are data, so CI can enforce them
export const flags = defineFlags({
  'checkout.v3': {
    owner: '@team-checkout', expires: '2026-03-01', default: false,
    variants: [false, true],
  },
  'payments.provider': {
    owner: '@team-payments', expires: null, default: 'stripe', // kill switch: no expiry
    variants: ['stripe', 'braintree'],
  },
});
```

**Flag-induced bugs** (a great debugging-scenario source, see the scenarios section)
- **Client/server skew**: the server rendered variant A, the client evaluated B → hydration mismatch and a "flash of the wrong thing", or worse, a state machine that receives data for a variant it is not rendering.
- **Stale cached flags**: a flag cached in the bundle at build time (inlined env) or in a long-lived `localStorage` entry with a far-future TTL. Rule: never bake flag values into a build artifact unless that build *is* the rollout mechanism; use a short TTL and revalidate.
- **Toggling in production recreates the bug**: the two branches share mutable state and the "off" path is the stale one (classic: the old code cannot handle data written by the new code).
- **Flag sprawl**: 300 flags, nobody knows which are mutually exclusive. Symptoms: contradictory combinations, "works on staging" (staging has different defaults).
- **Nested flags** and untested combinations explode the state space (2^n). Rule: no more than one release flag per user journey in a single rollout.
- **Kill-switch abuse**: a switch used as a rollout in disguise never gets removed and becomes an undocumented configuration surface.

**Auditability.** Every flag change is a production code change and should be as reviewable as a deploy: who changed it, when, why, with what rollout, linked to a ticket. That means flags in Git (with PR review + CODEOWNERS for the feature team's flag namespace) rather than in a UI-only console, or at minimum, console changes exported to version control. Auditors and incident responders need to answer "what did production look like at 14:20" — a versioned flag config with history answers it; a mutable console does not.

**SSR/hydration and caching interactions**
- **Server-rendered HTML containing a variant**: the CDN cache key must include the variant, or users get a cached page from the other variant. Practically: `Vary` on the cookie/header you vary by, or bypass the HTML cache and cache the data API instead with the variant in the query. The *design* rule: prefer variants that do not change the HTML (client-rendered islands) or make the CDN key correct.
- **Hydration mismatch**: React will warn and re-render if the server HTML and first client render disagree. Compute the variant deterministically on both sides from the same inputs, and render the flag-dependent markup only after hydration if the inputs differ.
- **Caching of data APIs**: a flag that changes a response shape must be part of the cache key, or your CDN serves variant-B data to a variant-A client. Version the cache key with the flag set hash when a flag can affect payloads.
- **E2E tests must be deterministic**: pin flags per environment in test config, and have at least one test run against the alternative variant for each release flag.

**Questions**
1. What kinds of feature flags exist, and which ones should never expire?
2. How should percentage rollouts be bucketed so assignment is sticky and independent?
3. Where should a flag be evaluated, and what goes wrong if you evaluate on the wrong side?
4. Explain assignment vs exposure and why analysis must gate on exposure.
5. Describe the flag lifecycle, including the owner and expiry requirements.
6. What is expand/contract and why does a flag not migrate data?
7. Give three flag-induced bugs and their root causes.
8. Your CDN serves the wrong experiment variant. Diagnose.
9. How do you keep flags from becoming a configuration swamp?
10. When would you use a kill switch, and how do you test it?
11. A hydration mismatch appears only for 5% of users. Explain the mechanism.
12. How do you make E2E tests deterministic with flags in the picture?
13. Kill switch vs feature flag: what is the difference operationally?
14. Should flag config live in Git or a console? What does auditability require?
15. How do flags and caching interact?
16. When would you deliberately avoid flags?

**Model answer, Q4:** "Context: experiment analysis has to attribute an outcome to the variant a user actually saw. Decision: emit one exposure event per flag, variant, and user id at the moment the variant is rendered, and define the analysis population as users with an exposure event, not users with an assignment event. Reasoning: assignment happens at evaluation time — which might be at app boot, on a route the user never visits, on a payload the client discards, or in a code path that throws before render. Users in the assigned-but-unexposed group are systematically different from exposed users: they are the ones who bounced early, hit an error, or never reached the UI at all. Including them biases the experiment toward whatever the unexposed population does, and it dilutes effect size toward zero, so you can easily fail to detect a real win. Exposure also gives the denominator you actually need for funnel conversion. Trade-offs: exposure logging is extra client work and needs dedupe by (flag, variant, user) so page navigation does not inflate counts; it also means you must handle a variant that renders conditionally inside an error boundary, where an exposure may be logged for something the user did not perceive. Failure scenario: we ship a pricing-layout experiment, assign 50/50, log assignment at boot, and read a 2% conversion lift as real — but exposure logging shows only 20% of assigned users ever reached the pricing table, and the lift was entirely inside the wrong population; the team ships a change that does nothing. Alternative: define exposure at a well-chosen semantic point (the component that actually differs) rather than at the flag read, and validate the exposure rate per arm as a sanity check — an exposure rate near zero or near 100% means the measurement point is wrong."

**Mistakes:** using `Math.random()` for bucketing; logging assignment and calling it exposure; no owner or expiry; baking flag values into build-time env; caching HTML without the variant in the key; assuming client flags are trustworthy for access control; leaving 100%-rolled-out flags forever.

**Mini task:** implement a flag SDK over a static JSON config with deterministic hash bucketing, an `useFlag` hook, an exposure logger with dedupe, and typed definitions including `owner`/`expires`. Then write a CI script that fails on expired flags and prints a report of flags by age and usage, and reproduce the hydration-mismatch bug by evaluating a flag differently on server and client — then fix it.

---
# 4.6.8 Analytics (🟡)

🟡 Intermediate → 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 3.6 (RUM), 4.6.7 (flags)

**Definition:** The deliberate collection of product and performance events, with a governed taxonomy, that answers product questions you cannot answer from server logs alone.

**Client vs server event collection**
| | Client events | Server events |
|---|---|---|
| Sees | Interaction: clicks, scroll, playback, form abandonment, errors in the UI, CWV | Truth: orders, refunds, entitlement changes, job completion, latency |
| Strengths | Real user behaviour, fast iteration, no backend deploy | Authoritative, complete, works from any client, hard to spoof |
| Blind spots | Spoofable, lost on crash/navigation, missing server-side outcomes, ad blockers | Cannot see UX quality or intent; no CWV |
| Rule | Instrument **funnels on both sides** and reconcile | Never rely on client events for money, entitlement, or security |
The classic bug: a purchase button click event fires, the payment fails server-side, and the funnel says 4% conversion. Always join on a shared `order_id`/`transaction_id` and reconcile counts.

**Event taxonomy and naming**
- **Pick one convention and enforce it.** Two that work: past-tense object.action (`order_completed`, `report_exported`) or verb-noun (`completed_order`). Past-tense object.action reads better in SQL-ish pipelines and groups related events alphabetically; verb-noun is more common in the wild. Whatever you pick, consistency beats preference.
- **Properties**: `snake_case`, lowercase, documented, no PII, and **low-cardinality where you group by them** (a `user_email` property destroys your dashboards; a `plan` value is fine).
- **Versioned schemas.** Events change; consumers (dashboards, warehouses, downstream ML) do not deploy with you. Put a schema version in every event and never repurpose a name:
```
order_completed {
  schema_version: 2,
  order_id, item_count, currency, total_minor_units,   // money in minor units, integer!
  coupon_code_present: bool, channel, experiment_assignments: {…}
}
```
  Money as an integer in minor units with an explicit currency, never a float — the storage-layer decision that prevents a reconciliation nightmare.
- **Reserved property names**: common ones like `page_url`, `session_id`, `user_id`, `timestamp`, `app_version`, `release` are added by the collector so you never hand-write them inconsistently.

**The tracking plan (the part that makes analytics survive contact with an org)**
```
1. Question → which decision does it inform? If none, don't collect it.
2. Funnel definition → ordered steps, the event for each, and the population.
3. Event spec → name, properties (typed, documented), owner, schema version.
4. Governance → who approves new events; naming conventions; a data dictionary.
5. Storage/retention → warehouse sink, retention, access control.
```
The **data dictionary** (a versioned doc or generated from your TypeScript event types) is the single highest-leverage artefact: it turns "ask the data team" into a search. Practical enforcement: generate event types from one TypeScript module and derive both the runtime tracking helpers and the documentation, so an undocumented event cannot exist.

```ts
// analytics/events.ts — one source of truth for names, props, and docs
export const events = {
  'order_completed': { v: 2,
    props: { order_id: z.string(), item_count: z.number().int().nonnegative(),
             currency: z.string().length(3), total_minor: z.number().int().nonnegative() } },
  'checkout_step_viewed': { v: 1, props: { step: z.enum(['cart','address','payment','review']), attempt: z.number().int() } },
} as const;
type EventName = keyof typeof events;
```

**Client vs server duplication and identity.** The same logical event can be emitted from both sides; that is fine *if* each has a distinct name and a shared key. Prefer naming them differently (`checkout_cta_clicked` vs `order_created`) so you can compare intent against outcome instead of double-counting one fact.

Identity:
- **`anonymous_id`** assigned on first visit (persisted in a first-party cookie or `localStorage`), rotates with consent state.
- On login/identify, **stitch** by calling the platform's `identify(anonymous_id, user_id)`. Historical anonymous activity attaches to the user.
- Handle the awkward cases honestly: shared devices, logouts, multi-account households, and clearing cookies. These are real data-quality limits; say so in an interview rather than claiming a perfect identity graph.
- Use your **internal user id**, never email or phone (PII, and it changes).

**Consent and privacy**
- Nothing is collected until consent, except the strictly-necessary storage (consent record itself).
- Store the consent state in a **first-party** cookie/localStorage key with a timestamp and a policy version, and make consent **withdrawable** as easily as it is given.
- Honour opt-out in the collector, not just in the UI: a single `consent` gate in your tracking wrapper means no event can escape review.
- **`Do Not Track` reality:** the header is widely ignored, inconsistently implemented, and not legally sufficient in the EU. Treat it as one signal among several — a user agent that sends DNT plus a cookie or account-level opt-out is a clear intent signal worth honouring.
- GDPR/CCPA: consent for non-essential analytics, data-subject requests (access/delete) which require your identity graph and retention policy to be real, and a documented processor/DPA. Cross-reference PHASE 7 → GDPR and privacy practices.
```js
// one gate, so no call site can bypass consent
export function track(name, props = {}) {
  if (!consent.has('analytics')) return;           // hard gate
  queue.push({ name, props: scrub(props), ts: Date.now() });   // scrub: strip PII keys
  if (queue.length >= 20) flush();
}
```

**RUM: Core Web Vitals and custom marks**
```ts
import { onCLS, onINP, onLCP, onFCP, onTTFB } from 'web-vitals';
for (const m of [onCLS, onINP, onLCP, onFCP, onTTFB]) m(sendVital, { reportAllChanges: true });
function sendVital(m) {
  track('web_vital', {
    name: m.name, value: Math.round(m.name === 'CLS' ? m.value * 10000 : m.value),
    rating: m.rating, nav_type: m.navigationType,
    route: location.pathname,           // normalised route, not the raw URL with ids
    release: __RELEASE__,                // tie RUM to code, like Sentry (4.6.6)
  });
}
// Custom marks for product-specific timings
performance.mark('editor_ready');
performance.measure('editor_ttfi', { start: 0, end: performance.getEntriesByName('editor_ready')[0].startTime });
```
Definitions you must state if asked: **page view** = a navigation/route change, not a `load` event (SPAs never fire it); **session** = a 30-minute inactivity window (state it, because everyone defines it differently); **engaged session** = >10s or a conversion event; route normalisation strips ids and query strings so `/user/123` and `/user/456` are one page.

**Product analytics: funnels, retention, cohorts**
- **Funnels** need ordered steps, a defined population, and a time window; "conversion" without a window is meaningless.
- **Retention** is cohort-based: users grouped by first-activity date, measured on a returning-activity definition. Daily vs weekly retention changes the story completely.
- **Cohorts** need an identity that is stable across devices (server-side stitched identity) — client-only cohorts cannot see cross-device behaviour.
- Guard against **survivorship bias** in every funnel: users who errored early drop out of the client funnel and your conclusion gets inverted.
- **Server-side truth for revenue**: pipeline client events and server events into the same warehouse, joined by id, and treat the server record as authoritative for anything money-related.

**Sampling and volume control**
- Sample high-volume events by session or by user with a **deterministic** decision, so a sampled user is consistently sampled across events (otherwise funnels break).
- Do not sample low-volume, high-value events (errors, purchases, funnel steps in experiments).
- Cap properties per event and cap payload size; drop unknown properties server-side by schema so a rogue caller cannot inflate cost.
- Batch and send on a timer or when `visibilitychange` fires; the collector's quota is a product decision, not an accident.

**Warehouse / BI sink.** Client analytics platforms are for exploration and funnels; a warehouse (BigQuery/Snowflake/Redshift) plus a BI layer is what you use for serious analysis. Bridge them by forwarding events into the warehouse (server-side collection endpoint writing to a queue → warehouse load) so you get: raw immutable data, SQL joins with backend tables, and models that a BI tool consumes. This is the only way to answer "did the feature change retention among enterprise accounts who were also exposed to experiment X".

**Collector reliability and latency: never block the UI**
The collector must be fire-and-forget and must not affect interaction latency:
```js
function flush() {
  if (!queue.length) return;
  const body = JSON.stringify({ events: queue.splice(0) });
  if (navigator.sendBeacon) {                      // survives unload
    navigator.sendBeacon('/collect', new Blob([body], { type: 'application/json' }));
  } else {                                          // no beacon: keepalive, don't await
    fetch('/collect', { method: 'POST', body, keepalive: true,
      mode: 'cors', credentials: 'omit' }).catch(() => {});
  }
}
addEventListener('visibilitychange', () => { if (document.visibilityState === 'hidden') flush(); });
addEventListener('pagehide', flush);
```
Pitfalls worth naming: `fetch` **without** `keepalive` is cancelled on navigation (this is the #1 cause of "we lost the last event on every page"); `sendBeacon` has a size cap (~64 kB in practice) so batch small; an unhandled promise rejection from a failed collect call becomes a console error the Sentry SDK may capture — filter it; and blocking `await` on analytics in an event handler directly inflates INP (cross-ref 3.6, 4.6.6).

**Debugging without console logs**
- **Debug mode** driven by a query param or a `localStorage` flag that prints every event to the console and dumps the queue instead of sending (`?analytics=debug`).
- **An event debugger / validator** in dev that checks the event against your Zod/TS schema at call time and warns on unknown events, missing properties, or PII-shaped values. This is a real lint-on-steroids and catches 80% of instrumentation bugs before production.
- **Network tab** for the collector's payload; **session replay** correlated with the event (4.6.6) for "did the click actually happen?".
- **Server-side validation counts**: log how many events arrive with unknown names or invalid properties; a spike in unknown names means an old client is alive.
- **QA test IDs**: require a `data-analytics-event` attribute on interactive elements so tests can assert instrumentation rather than class names.

**Questions**
1. Client events vs server events: who owns which truth?
2. Pick and defend an event naming convention.
3. How do you version an event schema, and why must names never be repurposed?
4. What is a tracking plan and who signs off on it?
5. Explain identity stitching from anonymous to user id, and where it breaks.
6. What is the consent gate and why must it live in the collector?
7. What is the reality of `Do Not Track`?
8. How do you define a page view and a session in a SPA, and why does the definition matter?
9. Capture Core Web Vitals in a way that is useful in a dashboard.
10. Funnels vs retention vs cohorts: which question does each answer?
11. Why are client events insufficient for revenue, and how do you join the two sources?
12. Sampling: what must be sampled deterministically, and what must never be sampled?
13. Why did we lose events on navigation, and what is the fix?
14. How do you keep analytics from hurting INP?
15. What does a warehouse sink buy you over an analytics platform?
16. How do you debug missing events in production with no console access?
17. How do you prevent PII from entering events?
18. Retention numbers went up 20%. What are the first five hypotheses?

**Model answer, Q13:** "Context: the checkout funnel was losing roughly the last event on every navigation, so completion looked like 60% when it was 78%. Decision: move to `navigator.sendBeacon` on `visibilitychange`/`pagehide`, with a `fetch(..., { keepalive: true })` fallback. Reasoning: a normal `fetch` is tied to the document lifecycle and is aborted when the page unloads, and because we flushed on route-change *after* the navigation had already started, the request was cancelled mid-flight; the loss was systematic, not random, which is why it showed up as a consistent under-count rather than an occasional gap. `sendBeacon` is designed for exactly this — it is handed to the network stack at call time and is not tied to the page. We also batch, because the failure probability grows with the number of pending requests. Trade-offs: `sendBeacon` has a payload cap (roughly 64 kB in practice) and no response body, so we cannot rely on it for server-side validation feedback, and some strict CSP configurations block `connect-src` endpoints for beacon URLs — which we hit and had to allow-list separately. Failure scenario: users on a flaky mobile network with the app backgrounded within a second of the click; beacons are fire-and-forget, so those events are simply gone, and our mitigation is client-side deduplication plus a server-side reconciliation that reports a floor on conversion from authoritative order records. Alternative: a small service worker that owns the queue and flushes on `sync` events, giving retry semantics, at the cost of an extra moving part that can itself fail to install."

**Mistakes:** one-off event names with no registry; money as floats; relying on the `load` event for page views; awaiting analytics in event handlers; `fetch` without `keepalive` for critical events; collecting before consent; no sampling policy; treating client-side revenue as authoritative.

**Mini task:** build a consent-gated collector with `sendBeacon` + batching, a schema validator for 8 events, a debug mode, and a CI test that fails on unknown event names or PII-shaped properties. Then simulate a navigation right after an event and prove no loss; add a `visibilitychange` flush and measure the added INP impact (should be ~0).

---

# 4.6.9 Frontend security (🔴)

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: PHASE 7 (Web Vulnerabilities), 3.8 (build/env)

**Framing for the whole section:** canonical entries live in PHASE 7 → Web Vulnerabilities (XSS, CSP, CSRF, clickjacking, supply chain) and PHASE 7 → Auth Mechanisms (Token Storage). This section is the *frontend engineer's* angle: which framework APIs create the holes, what escaping does and does not buy you, and how the build pipeline turns "secrets" into public strings.

**The React escaping model — where it helps and where it stops**

React escapes all interpolated text and attribute values by default. It does **not** help you when you:
1. **Opt out of escaping** with `dangerouslySetInnerHTML`.
2. **Pass URLs to `href`/`src`/`action`/`formaction`** — React 16.9+ warns on `javascript:` URLs, but `data:` URLs, `blob:` and unusual schemes, or an unvalidated path are still your problem, and a `data:text/html` link is an XSS vector in some contexts.
3. **Spread user-controlled props onto DOM elements** (`<div {...userProps} />`), which can inject `dangerouslySetInnerHTML`, `onError`, `style`, or event handlers.
4. **Render third-party or user-authored rich content** (markdown, HTML comments, WYSIWYG output).
5. **Build DOM yourself** — `innerHTML`, `insertAdjacentHTML`, `document.write`, `eval`, `new Function`, template strings into `style`/`href`.
6. **Set `style` from user data** (CSS injection can exfiltrate via `background: url(//evil/?token)` in older engines, and can always do UI redress).
7. **Third-party embeds/widgets** that are, by design, script injection points.
8. **`postMessage` handlers** that trust the message's origin and payload shape.

```jsx
// The four realistic React XSS sinks, and the fix for each

// 1. HTML rendering → sanitize with an allowlist, in a known-good parser
import DOMPurify from 'dompurify';
<div dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: DOMPurify.sanitize(html, { USE_PROFILES: { html: true } }) }} />;

// 2. URLs → allowlist schemes, never trust a full user-supplied URL
const SAFE_SCHEME = /^(https?:|mailto:|tel:|\/|#)/i;
function safeHref(raw) {
  const u = String(raw ?? '').trim();
  return SAFE_SCHEME.test(u) ? u : undefined;      // undefined, not '#', so it's visibly broken
}
<a href={safeHref(user.url)} rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank">…</a>

// 3. Prop spreading → strip dangerous keys first
const OMIT = new Set(['dangerouslySetInnerHTML', 'innerHTML', 'ref', 'style']);
<div {...Object.fromEntries(Object.entries(userProps).filter(([k]) => !OMIT.has(k)))} />

// 4. Markdown → render to a sanitized AST; never re-parse sanitized HTML twice
import MarkdownIt from 'markdown-it';
const md = new MarkdownIt({ html: false, linkify: true });   // html:false is the important bit
md.renderer.rules.link_open = (t, i, o, _e, s) => // add rel on external links
  t[i].attrSet('rel', 'noopener noreferrer nofollow');
```
Two rules people forget: **sanitize at render time, not at write time** (content already in your DB may predate the sanitizer; sanitize on output so stored XSS gets fixed retroactively), and **sanitize the whole subtree once**, not fragments (browsers re-parse `innerHTML`, `noscript`, and SVG/MathML contexts can mangle it — known mXSS class). Prefer a real parser-based allowlist sanitizer, and never regex.

**DOM clobbering.** Named elements and form fields become global properties and can shadow what your code expects:
```html
<img name="currentPage">
<form id="location"><input name="href"></form>
<script>/* window.currentPage, window.location.href are now attacker/DOM-controlled */</script>
```
In `window` terms, `document.getElementById` and named access can be shadowed, and named access can return a *different* element than your code assumes. Mitigations: `no-undef`-style linting, avoiding reliance on globals, using explicit DOM APIs (`document.getElementById`), and CSP (a `<img name=...>` clobber that then feeds `location.href` is only dangerous if you read the clobbered global).

**`postMessage` and origin checks.** Every `message` listener must verify `event.origin` *and* validate the payload shape:
```js
const ALLOWED = new Set(['https://app.example.com']);
window.addEventListener('message', (e) => {
  if (!ALLOWED.has(e.origin)) return;                       // never `if (e.origin !== '*')`
  if (e.source !== iframeRef.current?.contentWindow) return; // who is talking?
  const msg = e.data;
  if (typeof msg !== 'object' || msg?.type !== 'theme:set' || typeof msg.value !== 'string') return;
  applyTheme(msg.value);                                    // treat input as untrusted
});
// Parent → child, always with an explicit target origin (never '*')
iframeRef.current?.contentWindow?.postMessage({ type: 'theme:set', value: 'dark' }, 'https://embed.example.com');
```
Real failures seen in interviews: wildcard `postMessage` targets (data leak), trusting `event.data` shape (prototype pollution or crashes), and using `postMessage` as an authentication channel (it is not — anyone with a reference to the window can post).

**CSP for a React app.** Content-Security-Policy is the structural defence that keeps an XSS bug from becoming a breach.
```http
Content-Security-Policy:
  default-src 'self';
  script-src 'nonce-{RANDOM_PER_RESPONSE}' 'strict-dynamic';
  style-src 'self' 'nonce-{RANDOM_PER_RESPONSE}';
  img-src 'self' data: https://cdn.example.com;
  connect-src 'self' https://api.example.com https://collector.example.com;
  font-src 'self';
  object-src 'none';
  base-uri 'none';
  frame-ancestors 'none';
  form-action 'self';
  upgrade-insecure-requests
```
Key mechanics:
- **`'unsafe-inline'` in `script-src` defeats the entire control** — because injected inline scripts are then allowed. It exists in React apps mostly because the framework or a library injects inline scripts; the fix is nonces or hashes, not weakening the policy.
- **Nonce-based strict CSP:** generate a cryptographically random nonce per response, put it in the CSP header, and set `nonce={nonce}` on every `<script>` you render. With `'strict-dynamic'`, scripts loaded by a trusted (nonced) script are allowed **even from a CDN you do not list**, which is how you keep third-party tags while blocking attacker-injected ones.
- **Hash-based CSP** (`'sha256-…'` per inline script) is more precise but brittle: any change to the inline script changes its hash, so it fights with bundler-injected runtime code (some bundlers/frameworks need a small build step to emit hashes).
- **Rollout discipline:** start with `Content-Security-Policy-Report-Only`, collect violations, fix, then enforce. Report violations to an endpoint you actually monitor (same collector discipline as 4.6.8) — an unenforced report-only policy in a log nobody reads is theatre.
- **Styles**: `style-src` needs `'unsafe-inline'` for inline `style` attributes in many React codebases; the cheap mitigation is to avoid inline styles and use nonces for `<style>`, or to move to a strict-ish `style-src-attr 'none'` and remove inline styles.
- **CORS on the report endpoint**, and never report full URLs containing PII.

**Third-party script risk.** Analytics tags, chat widgets, A/B tools, and payment iframes are arbitrary JS running with your origin's privileges: they can read the DOM, cookies (non-`HttpOnly`), and tokens in `localStorage`, and their supply chain can be compromised independently of yours. Mitigations, in order of strength: (1) don't load them; (2) load them in a **sandboxed cross-origin iframe** on a separate origin (a "tag manager container" pattern) with `postMessage` as the only channel; (3) allow-list them explicitly in CSP with nonces/`strict-dynamic`; (4) accept them with strict CSP, Subresource Integrity where you can pin versions, and monitoring of what they do (CSP report-only violations attributed to them). Also: SRI (`integrity` + `crossorigin`) only works for immutable, content-hashed assets — a tag-manager URL that changes content breaks SRI, which is a real reason third-party scripts cannot use it.

**Token storage.** Canonical entry: **PHASE 7 → Auth Mechanisms → Token Storage**. The frontend angle in one line: **`localStorage` is readable by any script on the origin, including every third-party tag and any XSS payload**; a non-`HttpOnly` cookie is sent to your server on every request and appears in server logs; `sessionStorage` is per-tab and cleared on close, which is a reasonable compromise for a short-lived token. Recommended shape: `HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Lax` session cookie for the browser app + CSRF token defence (cross-ref PHASE 7), with tokens in memory only for non-browser clients.

**Sensitive data in `localStorage`.** Never store: access/refresh tokens, passwords, PII you do not need client-side, secrets of any kind. If you must persist non-sensitive client state, prefer the minimum, encrypt-then-store only with a key that is *also* client-side (which makes it obfuscation, not security — be honest about that), and always support "clear all local data" on logout, because stale PII on a shared device is a real incident.

**Client-side authorization is UX, never security.** Hiding a button because `user.plan === 'pro'` is good UX. Security is the server refusing the request. Cross-reference **PHASE 7 → Web Vulnerabilities → Broken Access Control / IDOR**. State the interview line clearly: *every authorization decision must be enforced server-side; client checks are a hint for the UI and an attacker will simply call the API*. Two consequences to add: never ship the full record and hide fields client-side; and treat IDs in URLs as untrusted — enforce authorization per object, not per route.

**Clickjacking and framing.** `X-Frame-Options: DENY` is legacy; the modern control is `frame-ancestors 'none'` (or a specific allowlist) in CSP. For your own app, deny framing entirely; for embeddable parts, ship `frame-ancestors https://partner.example.com` and use `frame-ancestors 'self'` (not `*`) for the app shell. Also send `X-Frame-Options: SAMEORIGIN` as a belt-and-braces header for very old browsers, understanding that it cannot express an allowlist of origins.

**Iframe sandboxing.** Every third-party iframe gets the minimum:
```html
<iframe src="https://widget.example.com/embed"
  sandbox="allow-scripts allow-forms allow-same-origin"
  referrerpolicy="strict-origin-when-cross-origin"
  loading="lazy" allow="geolocation 'none'; camera 'none'; microphone 'none'"
  title="Support widget"></iframe>
```
Notes: `allow-scripts` **plus** `allow-same-origin` for a *cross-origin* frame is fine (the two combine into a same-origin restriction only within the frame's own origin), but never combine `allow-scripts allow-same-origin` on a frame that can navigate your top-level page or read your origin's storage. Always set `title` (accessible name) and `referrerpolicy`. If you need postMessage communication, validate origin and shape on both sides (above).

**Supply chain (frontend view)**
- **Typosquatting and dependency confusion**: typos of popular names, and *internal* package names that a public package can shadow (the fix: scope internal packages and use a lockfile with `npm ci` plus registry scoping/proxy config). Verify the maintainer, download count, and repo before adding a dependency; prefer a dependency with a low transitive count for anything you do not need.
- **Install scripts**: packages with `postinstall` can run arbitrary code on your machine and in CI. Audit them (`npm ls`, lockfile diff review); prefer `--ignore-scripts` where the dependency does not need them.
- **Lockfile integrity**: commit the lockfile, use `npm ci` (not `install`) in CI so installs are reproducible and match the reviewed tree; consider `npm audit signatures`/lockfile signature verification for higher assurance.
- **`npm audit` limitations**: high false-positive rate, transitive noise, and it tells you nothing about *reachability*. Real practices: allow-list exceptions with expiry, add a dependency-review step in CI, run SAST on your own code, and monitor for newly published advisories in your critical packages.
- **Lock down the pipeline**: pin GitHub Action versions by commit SHA (not tag), restrict which registries are reachable, and never expose a publish token to a fork PR (cross-ref PHASE 8 → secure engineering practices).

**Client-side rate limits are not rate limits.** Anything enforced in the browser (disabled buttons, debounce, captcha gating, quota counters in `localStorage`) is bypassed by calling the API directly. Enforcement must be server-side per identity/IP/token with a shared store so it survives multiple instances (PHASE 7 → Rate limiting, PHASE 11 → rate limiter case study). The frontend's jobs are *to avoid wasting the user's attempts* (disable the button, show a countdown, cache the captcha token) and to *handle 429 correctly*: read `Retry-After`, show a real message, queue the retry, and do not retry in a tight loop (which turns a rate limit into a self-inflicted outage).

**Security headers that matter to a frontend** (canonical detail and full semantics: PHASE 7 → Web Vulnerabilities → Security Headers)

| Header | Frontend-relevant effect |
|---|---|
| `Content-Security-Policy` | Structural defence against XSS and script injection; nonce + `strict-dynamic` for third parties (above) |
| `Strict-Transport-Security` | Forces HTTPS for your origin; include subdomains only if they are all HTTPS |
| `Referrer-Policy` | `strict-origin-when-cross-origin` stops paths/query (often containing ids) leaking to third parties |
| `Permissions-Policy` | Deny camera/microphone/geolocation/payment by default; only grant to the features that need them |
| `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` | Prevents MIME sniffing — blocks a served `.txt`/`.svg` being interpreted as script |
| `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy`, `Cross-Origin-Embedder-Policy` | Isolation hardening; required for `SharedArrayBuffer`/cross-origin isolation, and COOP isolates the tab from cross-origin popups |
| `X-Frame-Options` / `frame-ancestors` | Clickjacking (above) |
Also: `Set-Cookie` flags (`HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite`) and cookie `__Host-` prefix rules where applicable. Setting these is a server/CDN concern, but knowing what your frontend *needs* is a frontend skill — and shipping an app that breaks because you forgot `connect-src` for your API is a very common integration bug (that's CSP, not a browser bug).

**Build-time env vars are not secrets.** `import.meta.env.VITE_*`/`NEXT_PUBLIC_*`/`REACT_APP_*` values are **inlined into the public bundle at build time** and are readable by anyone who loads the page. Consequences: an API key in a public bundle is a public API key (scope it, restrict it server-side, rotate it, monitor usage); any "secret" you put there is already disclosed. Correct design: keep all secrets server-side; the frontend receives short-lived, narrowly scoped tokens for the specific operation it performs. Cross-reference 3.8 → build-time env vars.

**Questions**
1. What does React's escaping protect against, and where does it stop?
2. Your app renders user markdown. What is the safe pipeline?
3. `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` alternatives: what do you use when you cannot sanitize?
4. How do you safely handle a user-supplied URL in `href`?
5. What is DOM clobbering and how do you defend against it?
6. Write a `postMessage` listener that is actually safe.
7. Why does `'unsafe-inline'` defeat CSP?
8. Explain nonce-based CSP with `'strict-dynamic'`. How do you keep third-party scripts?
9. How do you roll out CSP without breaking production?
10. `style-src` is fighting your app. What are your options?
11. What is the risk profile of an analytics/chat widget, and how do you contain it?
12. Why is `localStorage` a bad place for tokens? What are the alternatives?
13. A teammate says "we hide the button, so it's secure". Your response?
14. What is the difference between `X-Frame-Options` and `frame-ancestors`?
15. Write the `sandbox` attribute set for a third-party iframe and justify each token.
16. Dependency confusion vs typosquatting: what are they and how do you prevent each?
17. What does `npm ci` buy you over `npm install` in CI?
18. What can `npm audit` not tell you?
19. Your API returns 429. What must the frontend do, and what must the backend do?
20. Which security headers matter for a frontend app and what breaks if you get them wrong?
21. A teammate puts an API key in `VITE_API_KEY`. Explain exactly why it fails.
22. What is the security review you would run before shipping a new third-party embed?

**Model answer, Q3:** "Context: we need to render a rich description field that users can format, and we cannot sanitize because we do not control all producers — some content is created by an internal tool and some is imported. Decision: never pass untrusted strings to `dangerouslySetInnerHTML`; render user-authored rich content through a parser that emits a constrained AST (markdown-it in CommonMark mode, or a ProseMirror/Tiptap schema where the source is the editor), map that AST to React elements, and drop anything outside the allowlist. Reasoning: `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` hands the string to the HTML parser, and once the parser is in play, the attacker controls an entire parser surface, including mutation XSS where a sanitized string re-parses differently in a different context (inside SVG, `noscript`, or an attribute). Escaping-by-default is the whole value of React and opting out of it throws that away. The AST approach keeps text as text, keeps attributes as props where React escapes them, and makes link handling (`rel`, scheme allowlist) a property of my renderer rather than of the content. Trade-offs: I lose arbitrary HTML — product has to accept the subset the renderer supports, which is a real product conversation — and markdown tables/embeds need explicit renderer support. I also need to normalise at render time rather than write time, because content already stored before the sanitizer existed must be safe on output, which costs CPU on read and means a stored-XSS cleanup job. Failure scenario: someone adds a 'feature' to the renderer that renders raw HTML for 'trusted' authors, and because trust was defined by an author flag rather than by role plus a sanitiser, one support-agent account turns into stored XSS on every customer page. Alternative: if a WYSIWYG HTML field is genuinely required, sanitize at render with a mature allowlist sanitiser, sanitise the entire subtree in one pass, and combine it with a strict CSP so a sanitiser bypass is not a full compromise."

**Mistakes:** sanitizing only some of a subtree; sanitizing on write instead of on output; trusting author/trust flags; regex "sanitising"; allowing `javascript:` and `data:text/html` URLs; `postMessage` with `'*'`; CSP with `unsafe-inline`; secrets in `VITE_`/`NEXT_PUBLIC_` env vars; treating hidden UI as authorization; no rate limiting on the server because the UI disables the button.

**Mini task:** build a component that renders user markdown with a safe pipeline, then attempt five XSS payloads against it (script tag, `javascript:` link, `data:` URL, SVG `onload`, mXSS via `noscript`, and a prop-spread attack) and show each failing safely. Then add a nonce-based CSP in report-only mode, run your app with a third-party tag, and record the violations you must fix before enforcing.

---

# 4.6.10 Vue / Angular / Svelte awareness + "Why React?" (🟡)

🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know** (asked at shops that use them, and in "would you pick X?" rounds) · Prerequisites: 4.1 (React depth)

**Purpose.** You are not going to be hired to write Angular at a React shop. You are being tested on whether your React knowledge is *framework knowledge* or *ecosystem trivia*, and on whether you can reason about a tool choice instead of arguing about it. The transferable core — semantic HTML, ARIA and focus management, forms and validation, HTTP and caching, state modelling, testing, performance — is Phase 3 and 4.1 and it is identical everywhere.

**Vue 3**
- **Reactivity:** `ref` for primitives, `reactive` for objects, both implemented with ES `Proxy`; reads inside a *reactive scope* (the render effect created by `watchEffect`/`computed`, which a component's render function is) are tracked, writes trigger re-runs. `computed` values are cached and lazily invalidated; `watch`/`watchEffect` are side-effect hooks with `flush` timing (`'pre'`, `'post'`, `'sync'`).
- **Effect scope / lifecycle:** `effectScope` collects effects for disposal (this is why component unmount is clean), plus `onMounted`/`onUnmounted`.
- **Template compiler:** SFC templates compile to render functions; the compiler tracks *static* vs *dynamic* nodes and hoists static content, so it knows at build time what can be skipped. `v-memo` is the escape hatch for expensive subtrees.
- **Fragmentation insight:** Vue re-renders a component when any reactive value it touched during render changed (a "dynamic dependency" set), not a top-down re-render of the subtree. This is finer-grained than React's default top-down re-render and needs no manual `memo`.
- **Data fetching:** `@tanstack/vue-query` is the community standard; there is no first-party framework data layer (contrast React Query, which is also third-party but React-first — be accurate).
- **Routing:** Vue Router (official). State: Pinia (official, store pattern).
- **Model:** single-file components; template + scoped styles + script; templates are the recommended way to write UIs (and the compiler's static analysis is part of the performance story).
- **SFC `<style scoped>`** rewrites selectors with a data attribute, which is a *build-time* isolation strategy — closer to "CSS Modules" than to Shadow DOM.

**Angular**
- **Reactivity/change detection:** zone-based change detection by default (the zone patches async APIs so the framework knows when to check), with `OnPush` components for targeted checks; the newer **signals** (`signal`, `computed`, `effect`, and signal inputs/outputs) give fine-grained graph-based updates and can be used to reduce reliance on zones. `markForCheck`/`detectChanges` are the manual escape hatches.
- **Dependency injection:** a first-class hierarchical injector (`providedIn: 'root'` tree-shakable singletons, `providers` arrays per component/module) — the most complete DI story of the three frameworks, and a genuine reason large Angular apps stay comprehensible.
- **Templates:** HTML-ish templates compiled to instructions; structural directives (`*ngIf`, `*ngFor`) desugar to property binding; change detection walks bindings and compares. `trackBy`/`@for (...; track item.id)` fixes the `ngFor` identity problem.
- **RxJS:** observables as the data model for async and event streams; `async` pipe + `toSignal` bridge to templates. Powerful and also the most common source of Angular complexity (subscribe leaks, nested subscribe, cold observables recreated per change-detection pass).
- **Forms:** template-driven and reactive forms, the latter with declarative validators — strong built-in story.
- **Router:** official, with route-level lazy loading, guards, and resolvers (a real architectural feature, not just URL matching).
- **Model:** components with lifecycle hooks, a module/standalone-component duality (now standalone-first), and a strong CLI that generates files (`ng generate`).
- **Versioning:** Angular's major cadence (one major roughly every six months) with a long support window for each — excellent for enterprises, and a real cost for small teams (migration work every 18 months).

**Svelte (and SvelteKit)**
- **Compile-time:** a Svelte file is compiled to direct DOM operations; there is **no virtual DOM** in the framework runtime, so updates are targeted mutations. Reactivity is compiled into the code rather than proxied at runtime — hence very small bundle and fast updates.
- **Runes (Svelte 5):** `$state`, `$derived`, `$derived.by`, `$effect` are compiler-tracked signals — explicit reactivity with compile-time knowledge of which values each update reads. Legacy `$:` reactive statements are replaced by runes in Svelte 5, which is itself a useful "what changed" talking point.
- **Component model:** very few lifecycle rules (props in, `$effect` for sync), small API surface, and the compiler enforces things React allows (e.g. state mutation is local, scoped styles by default, no implicit globals).
- **Data fetching:** load functions in SvelteKit (universal `load` on server and client) plus a fetch-based query layer; SvelteKit gives you SSR, routing, form actions, and endpoint routes *in the framework*, which is a batteries-included story closer to Next than to Vite+React.
- **Realistic limits:** the ecosystem and hiring pool are smaller; very large apps need discipline; the compiler is a constraint (no dynamic component patterns as free-form as React's); SSR/streaming features are younger.

| | React | Vue 3 | Angular | Svelte |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Core model | UI as functions, hooks, virtual DOM (optional with compiler) | SFCs, Proxy reactivity, compiler-optimised templates | DI + templates + zones/signals | Compile-time, runes, no VDOM |
| Async/data | Framework-agnostic; TanStack Query is the de facto standard | Vue Query; no first-party data layer | RxJS + `http` client; signals now | Fetch/load functions, SvelteKit |
| Routing | React Router / framework routers | Vue Router | Angular Router (guards, resolvers) | SvelteKit routing (filesystem) |
| State | Context + reducers + external stores | Pinia | Signals + services/stores | Runes/stores + context |
| Styling | CSS Modules, Tailwind, CSS-in-JS | Scoped SFC styles, Tailwind | Component styles (encapsulation), Tailwind | Scoped styles by default, Tailwind |
| Full-stack framework | Next.js, Remix/React Router | Nuxt | Angular SSR (`ng` platform-server) | SvelteKit |
| Sweet spot | Ecosystem reach, hiring, RN, product variety | Balanced simplicity + capability; strong in some geos | Large teams, strict conventions, enterprise | Lean apps, great DX, perf-sensitive small/medium |
| Main cost | Hooks/concurrency footguns, ecosystem churn, more decisions | Smaller hiring pool in some markets | Verbosity, build times, major-version churn | Smaller ecosystem, fewer escape hatches at scale |

**Honest reasons teams choose each** (say these instead of framework ideology)
- **Hiring pool and existing investment.** Usually the dominant factor. "We have 12 Angular devs and a working codebase" beats every technical argument.
- **Ecosystem fit.** Which libraries, integrations, and design systems exist; which auth/payment/analytics vendors document the framework; React Native leverage for mobile teams.
- **Performance profile.** Svelte and Vue for lean bundles and less runtime; Angular's compiler for large apps with heavy templates; React with the compiler or RSC for streaming SSR. In practice, most apps' performance is decided by data fetching and image strategy (Phase 3.6), not framework choice.
- **Learning curve and time-to-first-ship.** Vue/Svelte are usually gentler; Angular is steep but its conventions mean less local decision-making.
- **Governance/convention vs freedom.** Angular's batteries and strict tooling help consistency across 100 engineers; React's freedom means every team must build its own conventions (which is why design systems, 4.6.1, and structure, 4.6.5, matter more in React shops).

**Framework-agnostic React concepts that transfer (name these explicitly)**
- Semantic HTML and ARIA patterns (Phase 3.7): a combobox is a combobox in every framework.
- Forms, validation, and error messaging conventions; controlled input semantics.
- HTTP semantics, caching headers, optimistic UI, pagination strategies (4.6.11).
- State modelling: server state vs client state, normalised stores, immutability discipline.
- Testing pyramid: unit/component, integration, e2e; user-centric selectors (4.6.11, Phase 9).
- Performance reasoning: bundle size, code splitting, virtualisation, rendering cost, Core Web Vitals (3.6).
- Accessibility testing with axe + keyboard + screen reader.
- The security surface: escaping model, sanitisation, CSP, token storage (4.6.9).

**Model answer — "Why React?"**
"Context: the question is usually a probe for whether I have a position or just a preference. Decision: I choose React when hiring depth and ecosystem breadth matter, when the product spans web plus mobile, or when I want a small core library with maximal freedom to build our own conventions; I would not choose it purely on technical merit. Reasoning: React's advantages are largely *ecosystem* advantages. Unidirectional data flow with explicit state ownership gives a code base whose data flow you can trace by reading one component tree; the JSX-per-render model means UI is a pure function of state, which makes it very testable and makes rendering cost predictable. Its hooks ecosystem, router, query layer, form libraries, testing tools, and design-system primitives are the deepest in the industry, so for a team that needs to hire and to move fast, that is a real, quantifiable advantage. React Native extends the same model — components, hooks, and TypeScript types — to mobile, so a team can share logic and types across web and native rather than maintaining two languages. React's concurrent features (transitions, `useDeferredValue`, Suspense, and the compiler's automatic memoisation) are the direction of travel for keeping large apps responsive without hand-written memoisation discipline. Trade-offs and honest counter-cases: Vue's Proxy reactivity is finer-grained and simpler to reason about — no dependency-array footguns, no stale closures — and Vue/Svelte bundle less, which matters on the mobile web; Angular's DI and enforced conventions are genuinely better for very large organisations because decisions are made once by the framework rather than repeatedly by each team; and Svelte's compile-time model produces less runtime work with a smaller API surface, which is a legitimate reason to pick it for a small, perf-sensitive product. Failure scenario: we pick React for its ecosystem, then spend two years rebuilding conventions, an internal component library, a state layer, and a router abstraction — and the whole reason we chose the flexible option was to build our own abstractions. Mitigation: adopt an existing design system (4.6.2), TanStack Query and React Router rather than hand-rolling, and put boundaries in place (4.6.5) early. Alternative: pick Angular or Vue if the organisation already has that expertise; a 200-engineer shop with 12 senior Angular developers will ship faster in Angular than in React, and that is a better answer than any technical claim."

**How to answer "which would you pick for X?"**
1. **Start from requirements, not preference:** team size and existing skills, product surface (web only / web + mobile / embedded), performance constraints (bundle budget, low-end devices), ecosystem needs (vendors, hiring), SSR/SEO, and long-term ownership.
2. **State the constraint that dominates** (e.g. "we need SSR for SEO and a small runtime" or "we have to share code with a React Native app").
3. **Name the runner-up and why it lost** — this signals genuine analysis.
4. **State what would change your answer** (team growth, a specific library requirement, a framework deprecation).
Sample: "For a marketing site with a 90 kB bundle budget and a five-person team, I'd pick Svelte or Vue and skip React's ecosystem — none of that ecosystem is needed. For a product with a React Native app, staff augmentation needs, and four vendor integrations documented only for React, I'd pick React and standardise on its design system and query layer. For a 300-engineer regulated org with an existing Angular platform and a platform team, I'd stay on Angular and invest in its tooling, not migrate."

**Reading a framework-agnostic job posting.** Look for: the *shape* of the work (SPA vs SSR vs static), whether they mention a design system or component library, testing expectations (unit vs e2e, and what they call them), performance or Core Web Vitals ownership, accessibility expectations, CI/CD and release ownership, analytics/monitoring expectations, and how they describe state (store, context, "global state management"). Signal of a mature frontend team: mentions of error tracking, feature flags, design tokens, ADRs, and a component library — those are the same topics as this whole section. Signal of trouble: "pixel perfect from Figma" with no accessibility or performance requirements, or a rewrite-imminent warning without an incremental plan.

**Questions**
1. Explain Vue 3's reactivity in two sentences and when it re-renders.
2. What is `reactive` vs `ref`, and what breaks if you forget which to use?
3. What does `computed` guarantee that a plain function does not?
4. Explain Angular's change detection and what `OnPush` changes.
5. What does Angular's DI give you that React lacks?
6. Where does RxJS help and where does it hurt in Angular?
7. What are Svelte runes and why were they introduced?
8. Why does Svelte have no virtual DOM, and what do you trade away?
9. Which React concepts transfer to every framework, and which are React-specific?
10. Why does an Angular app have a different performance profile than a React app?
11. Why React? Give an honest answer with counter-cases.
12. When would you pick Vue? When Svelte? When Angular?
13. Our team knows Angular. Does that decide the framework? Argue it.
14. How do you learn a new framework's core in a week?
15. How do you read a job posting to infer frontend maturity?
16. What would make you migrate a React codebase to another framework?

**Model answer, Q11:** "Context: 'why React' is a test for whether my reasoning is evidence-based. Decision: React, framed as an ecosystem and hiring decision rather than a technical superiority claim, with the constraints that would flip my answer stated up front. Reasoning: unidirectional data flow gives traceable state ownership; the component-per-function model keeps rendering a pure function of props and state, which is what makes unit testing cheap; the breadth of mature libraries (routing, server state, forms, testing, RN) removes work we would otherwise own; React Native shares components, hooks, and types with the native app, which is a leverage argument no other web-first framework can make. React's concurrent model and the compiler are the trajectory for keeping large trees responsive without hand-tuning. Trade-offs I accept: React's escape hatches mean we must build and enforce our own conventions — a design system, boundary rules, a state strategy — which is real cost; the hooks model has genuine footguns (dependency arrays, stale closures) that require discipline; and the churn is real. Where React is the wrong answer: a small bundle-sensitive product (Vue/Svelte ship less runtime), a very large org that benefits from Angular's enforced conventions and DI, or a codebase whose only requirement is web with a small team that will be faster with less ceremony. Failure scenario: we choose React for ecosystem depth, inherit three state libraries and two routers from merge conflicts, and spend a year untangling conventions — the flexibility we bought is exactly the ambiguity we got. Mitigation: standardise on one of each at the start, adopt the community defaults, and enforce boundaries in CI. Alternative: if the team has 10 years of Angular and a working platform, staying on Angular is the professional answer and I would say so."

**Mistakes:** declaring a universal winner; arguing from personal taste; not knowing what `<script setup>`/runes/signals are; claiming React is "faster" without a workload; implying you could rewrite a production app in a different framework in a quarter; being unable to name what transfers framework-agnostically.

**Mini task:** implement the same accessible combobox (4.6.11 drill 1) in React, Vue 3, and Svelte, and compare bundle size, LOC, and the a11y behaviour you get from the platform's native primitives. Write one paragraph per framework on what the framework gave you for free versus what you reimplemented — this is the real answer to "why X".

---
# 4.6.11 Frontend system design (🔴)

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** (the most common senior frontend round) · Prerequisites: 4.6.5, 3.6, 3.7, 4.1

**How to run this section.** Every one of the six follows the same skeleton, and if you memorise nothing else, memorise the skeleton:

```
1. Clarify requirements + scale      6. Accessibility
2. Client state model                7. Caching
3. Key flows (happy + unhappy)       8. API design
4. Performance budget                9. Edge cases
5. Loading / empty / error states   10. What breaks at scale + follow-ups
```
The skeleton exists so that when the interviewer interrupts with "what about X?", you have a slot to put it in. Say the numbers out loud (back-of-envelope is Phase 12): how many users, how many keystrokes per second, how many items, what the latency budget is.

---

## 4.6.11.1 Typeahead / autocomplete
